 1 Introduction

1.1 Purpose and Scope of Chemical Stockpile Emergency
 Preparedness Program History Project
The History Project of the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP or the
CSEP Program) was initiated in 2010 to document CSEPP’s extraordinary efforts and
achievements. CSEPP was implemented to enhance emergency preparedness at U.S. Army
installations and their surrounding communities as the Army’s stockpile of chemical weapons
was being destroyed. As noted in an earlier program retrospective:

 When Congress mandated destruction of the U.S. chemical stockpile, it ordered
 that this be carried out with “maximum protection” for the public until the
 chemical agents are completely gone. Early studies of the communities near the
 stockpiles showed the need to improve existing emergency plans, training,
 equipment, and facilities. CSEPP was created to enhance the emergency response
 capabilities in these communities, recognizing that each site has its own particular
 needs for emergency preparedness. Program goals and metrics were established
 and tools were created to work more efficiently. These breakthroughs were the
 result of all the program stakeholders collaborating to identify program needs,
 solve problems, and advance program goals. Since CSEPP began, State and local
 emergency management officials have teamed with the U.S. Army and the
 Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) to protect the public in the
 unlikely event of a stockpile accident. This partnership among multiple Federal
 agencies, 10 states, 40 counties, one Tribal nation, volunteer agencies, and the
 private sector presented challenges to the newly formed program... [over the
 program’s history, however] many emergency management innovations have
 been discovered, planned, and implemented in CSEPP communities. These best
 practices and lessons learned should be collected and shared with all the CSEPP
 sites, as well as with other emergency management agencies that may benefit
 from CSEPP’s experience.1

By February 2012, 89% of the chemical stockpile had been destroyed, including 100% of the
stockpile at six of the eight original CSEPP sites. Completion took another 11 years, and
stockpile destruction at the final two sites was finished in 2023. As each installation completed
disposal, CSEPP was no longer necessary, and the program was shut down at that site. The
History Project sought to document the experiences and lessons learned from CSEPP before the
relevant personnel, institutions, and documentation dispersed.

The CSEPP History Project had two main deliverables: a report and a historical archive. This
document is the report. Presented in two volumes, the first volume summarizes the program as a
whole, and the second volume details specific lessons learned over the course of the program.

1
 FEMA, 2009, CSEPP: A History of Innovation, available at
 https://portalfiles.blob.core.usgovcloudapi.net/training/CSEPP_LegacyBrochure.pdf.

 1

The historical archive includes records pertaining to the program, including documents (e.g.,
reports, letters, agreements, guides, news clippings), photographs, video, software, and other
items. Several thousand electronic and paper records were collected, including materials from
each CSEPP site, FEMA, and Army CSEPP headquarters (HQs), and supporting contractors. The
archive includes transcripts of interviews with approximately 80 program staff from a cross-
section of organizations involved in the program. The archive has been turned over to the
Disaster Research Center at the University of Delaware for the benefit of emergency
management researchers.

1.2 Organization of This Report
Volume I of this report is organized into eight chapters:
 1. Introduction
 2. CSEPP Mission and Genesis
 3. Organization of the Program and Agency Roles
 4. Program Management and Budgeting
 5. Program Components and Innovations
 6. Program Closeout
 7. Perspectives from Program Staff
 8. Conclusions

Volume I also includes the following annexes:
 • Acronyms
 • CSEPP Glossary

Volume II of the report details program lessons learned. Each lesson learned contains a brief
description of the situation, the key issue, the action taken to resolve the issue, and an analysis of
how the lesson can be applied to other emergency management programs moving forward.
CSEPP lessons learned include aspects of program management and interagency cooperation,
emergency management techniques and doctrine, and supporting technologies.

Note: some referenced documents, particularly CSEPP reports and documents cited to the
website CSEPP Portal, may be available from the Disaster Research Center’s E. L. Quarantelli
Resource Collection (QRC) at the University of Delaware, which holds an archive of CSEPP
documents. One may contact the QRC office at elq-resource@udel.edu to arrange for archive
access. More information about the QRC can be found at https://www.drc.udel.edu/drc-
quarantelli-resource-collection/#about.

 2

 2 CSEPP Mission and Genesis

This chapter provides an overview of CSEPP and describes the foundations on which it was
based. The overview takes us from the program’s original legislative mandate, through the
process of quantifying the hazard and conceptualizing a response framework, to a brief
description of the program’s phases (i.e., initial development to program formalization, maturity,
sustainment/closeout). The bases and origins of the program are then described in more detail:
the Army chemical stockpile and the process for destroying it, the legislative and regulatory
context of CSEPP, and the antecedent programs on which much of the CSEPP program was
initially based (Army Chemical Surety and the FEMA/Nuclear Regulatory Agency (NRC)
Radiological Emergency Preparedness [REP] Program).

2.1 Program Overview
In 1985, Congress directed the U.S. Army to destroy the existing stockpile of lethal unitary
chemical warfare agents in the United States.2 The Army decided to expand an existing program
that was already addressing disposal of the M55 rocket stockpile, retaining Oak Ridge National
Laboratory (ORNL) to prepare the necessary documentation under the National Environmental
Policy Act (NEPA). CSEPP resulted from a series of public meetings held in the spring and
summer of 1986 to gather public input on the Draft Programmatic Environmental Impact
Statement (DPEIS). Hearings were held in each of the eight affected communities. Because the
DPEIS contained detail on risks of stockpile storage and disposal, including the potential
consequences of an accidental release, extensive public comment was received regarding the
need for enhanced emergency preparedness around the stockpile sites.

2.1.1 Definition of Hazard

The Army extensively analyzed the risks to the public in conjunction with preparation of the
Final Programmatic Environmental Impact Statement (FPEIS) for the disposal program. The
MITRE Corporation prepared a quantitative risk assessment (QRA) for the Army’s Program
Manager for Chemical Demilitarization (PMCD). The QRA was completed in seven volumes,
five of which were classified. It reported risks associated with each of the options being
considered: continued storage (“no action”), disposal on-site at each facility, and various options
involving transport of some or all of the weapons to other sites for disposal.3 Values were
calculated for the probability of one or more fatalities, the maximum number of fatalities, the
expected number of fatalities, person-years at risk, and expected plume area. The analysis also
looked at the effects of two levels of mitigation measures to reduce risk.

2
 U.S. Congress, 1986, DOD Authorization Act, Public Law (PL) 99–145.
3
 Fraize, W.E., R. Cutler, W. Duff, E. Hughitt, and J. Perry, 1987, Risk Analysis in Support of the Chemical
 Stockpile Disposal Program, the MITRE Corporation, Civil Systems Division, SAPEO-CDE-IS-87014,
 December. This analysis was founded on a number of previous hazard and risk analyses by Arthur D. Little,
 R.M. Parsons Co., Science Applications International Corporation (SAIC), Sandia National Laboratory, GA
 Technologies, MITRE Corporation, and others. The QRA was incorporated into the FPEIS as Appendix J.

 3

Among the complex results, a key finding was that the continued storage alternative yielded the
highest number of expected fatalities, whereas on-site disposal and regional disposal alternatives
yielded the lowest. For scenarios incorporating mitigation measures, though, the on-site disposal
option had the lowest probability of causing one or more fatalities.

The analysis showed that, while onsite storage could result in accidents with greater than
5,000 fatalities, such accidents would be extremely unlikely (with a probability of much less than
one in a million). For the on-site disposal option with no mitigation measures, the probability of
an accident involving one or more fatalities was about 7 in 1,000; with mitigation measures, the
risk dropped to about 3 in 10,000.

The hazard studies considered potential events that could occur during storage, during weapons
handling and transport, and within a disposal facility. Probabilities were assigned (where
possible) based on industrial experience with similar types of operations. External events such as
earthquakes and airplane crashes were also considered.

The studies also examined event chains: for example, whether detonation of one chemical round
would set off nearby rounds. The extent of the hazard area would depend on the amount of agent
released; the manner of release (e.g., a slow leak versus dispersion via explosion or fire); and
meteorological conditions (e.g., temperature and wind speed). In general, the worst-case
scenarios tended to involve fire; nerve agent; and slow, stable winds.4

2.1.2 Emergency Response Concept Plan

As a result of extensive public comment on the need for improved emergency preparedness, the
Army commissioned development of an Emergency Response Concept Plan (ERCP) for the
Chemical Stockpile Disposal Program (CSDP). The ERCP established a framework for
preparedness for the risks identified in the QRA. Completed in late 1987, the ERCP became
Appendix L to the FPEIS.

2.1.2.1 Geographic Zone-Based Approach

The ERCP described a geographic-zone-based emergency planning approach, with three
concentric zones at each site: an innermost Immediate Response Zone (IRZ), a Protective Action
Zone (PAZ), and the outermost Precautionary Zone (PZ). The IRZ was defined as a roughly 10-
kilometer (km) radius around the chemical storage area. If a chemical emergency were to occur,
this area would have extremely limited time to respond (less than 60 minutes) and would
experience the greatest risk of harmful concentrations of agent. The PAZ, defined as the area 10–
35 km from a potential chemical source, would have more time to respond (1 to 3 hours) and
would have less risk of harmful effects; however, in the worst-case scenarios, the PAZ might still
be subject to lethal concentrations. The PZ, more than 35 km away, would not be expected to
experience lethal exposures, and people in the PZ would have several hours to respond if an
emergency were to occur.
4
 The results of the risk analysis were later used to prioritize disposal operations. Weapons considered to present
 the greatest risk to the community, such as the M55 rockets, were destroyed first to reduce risk as quickly as
 possible.

 4

Use of these zones allowed jurisdictional departments and agencies, as well as special facilities
such as schools, to plan for and allocate resources for the range of possible scenarios and the
time required to counter them with protective action.5 As the CSEP Program developed and
matured, many aspects of the off-post program became a function of the IRZ and PAZ
demarcations. At the local level, location within the IRZ or PAZ became the most important
determinant of a jurisdiction’s level of involvement in the program. The zones were customized
at each site according to local conditions and jurisdictional boundaries, and site-specific ERCPs
were developed, including geographic, demographic, and economic characteristics; hazard and
risk analyses; and evaluation of protective action strategies based on local circumstances.
Figure 2.1 shows the IRZ and PAZ for the Anniston Army Depot in Alabama’s Calhoun County.

 Figure 2.1 Anniston Army Depot’s IRZ and PAZ

5
 For further information on how geographic zones were defined and used, see the Protective Action discussion in
 Section 5.5. See also the “Lesson Learned on Organizing Community Protective Action by Zones” in Volume 2
 of this document.

 5

2.1.2.2 Framework and Elements of Preparedness

The ERCP drew heavily upon experiences gained in the FEMA/NRC Radiological Emergency
Preparedness Program, at that time the most sophisticated and extensive fixed-site technological
hazard program in existence. The ERCP addressed the following topics and subtopics:

 • Emergency Response Management
 o Coordination of Emergency Response
 o Command and Control
 o Communications
 o Accident Assessment
 o Protective Action Decision-Making

 • Protective Actions and Responses
 o Protective Action Options
 o Public Alert and Notification (A&N)
 o Access and Traffic Control
 o Special Populations
 o Emergency Worker Protection

 • Emergency Resource and Information Management
 o Emergency Medical Services
 o Transportation
 o Community Resources
 o Public Information
 o Evacuee Support

 • Emergency Response Program Development and Implementation
 o Plan and Procedure Development
 o Training
 o Drills and Exercises
 o Program Implementation

 • Alternative Concepts for Transportation

The topics and subtopics addressed in the 1987 ERCP later evolved into national benchmarks
that the program used as an evaluation and management tool (see Section 4.2.2).

One of the most significant conclusions contained in the ERCP was that, given standardized
event consequences, emergency preparedness programs for eight fixed sites were likely to be
more effective than emergency preparedness programs addressing transportation corridors
extending over hundreds or thousands of miles. When the Army issued its final Record of
Decision in favor of constructing on-site disposal facilities, it noted that the risks arising from
on-site disposal and transportation to a central disposal facility were essentially the same, but
that emergency preparedness as a mitigation factor was likely to be more effective for fixed sites.
Emergency preparedness, then, became the primary deciding factor in the decision to build

 6

disposal plants at each of the eight existing stockpile sites located within the continental
United States (CONUS).

2.2 Program Phases
The history of CSEPP can be divided into four phases: initial development, program
development, program maturation, and sustainment/closeout.

2.2.1 Initial Development

Initial development extended from late 1986 through the early 1990s. The DPEIS was developed
by teams from ORNL and the PMCD. The ERCP was developed by a team from Schneider
Engineers (working under prime contractor Jacobs Engineering Group) with assistance from the
Army, ORNL, and FEMA. ERCP development began in November 1986, and a draft was
delivered to the Army in September 1987. The final generic ERCP was included in the FPEIS 6
when it was published in 1988.

The September 1987 draft ERCP was presented to Under Secretary of the Army David Ambrose.
In attendance at that meeting were numerous FEMA officials, including FEMA’s then-Director,
Julius Becton, Jr. Director Becton proposed that the U.S. Army and FEMA join forces to
implement the concepts discussed in the ERCP. This meeting initiated the process that led to a
1988 Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) between FEMA and the Army.

From 1986 through 1992, ORNL developed a series of technical studies for the Army that
addressed many of the concepts outlined in the ERCP, including protective action options and
effectiveness, emergency responder protection, rapid accident assessment and protective action
decision making, and warning system effectiveness.7 Following execution of the 1988
Army/FEMA MOU, ORNL developed a series of draft technical standards for critical program
areas. These draft standards were reviewed by the Joint Army/FEMA Steering Committee and
then were combined and released as “interim draft” program guidance for CSEPP in 1991. In the
same general timeframe, ORNL developed site-specific ERCPs for each of the eight
participating communities, applying the ERCP concepts to the unique nature of each community
and each installation’s chemical stockpile (ORNL Technical Memoranda 11092 – 11099, all
published in October 1989). In 1991, the Army published Department of the Army
(DA) Pamphlet 50-6, the Army’s implementing guidance for responding to chemical warfare
agent emergencies. The first CSEPP Policy Paper8 defined the Congressional Maximum
Protection mandate as “avoidance of fatalities to the maximum extent practicable” and was also
published jointly by FEMA and the Army in 1991.

6
 U.S. Army Program Manager for Chemical Demilitarization, 1987, Chemical Stockpile Disposal Program Final
 Programmatic Environmental Impact Statement. Army Chemical Materials Agency, Aberdeen Proving Ground,
 Md.

 A series of Policy Papers was issued over the next several years; see Section 4.3 for details.

 7

2.2.2 Program Formalization

The period from the early 1990s until 2000 can be called the “program formalization” phase.
During this time, management structures at FEMA and the Army went through a series of
changes: the program became more formalized; State, Tribal, and local CSEPP organizations
were established; and program components such as annual exercises were established.

This phase included evolving management structures at both FEMA and the Army. The early
Steering Committee structure, comprising multiple functional subcommittees, proved unwieldy
because of its size, and program policy became more centralized among FEMA and Army
management. There were many differences of opinion between the Federal partners during this
period, and growing pains were visible. In response, the participating states became better
organized and began meeting as a bloc to discuss program issues and to advocate for their
positions with Army and FEMA management. Also during this period, the U.S. Government
Accountability Office (GAO) issued a series of reports criticizing the program’s management
systems and its rate of progress. These reports resulted in a 1997 restructuring of the program to
address the GAO recommendations and confirm FEMA as the lead agency for off-post
preparedness, as prescribed in Section 141 of Public Law (PL) 105–261. One of the more
significant outcomes of this restructuring was the commitment to use both national and
community-level Integrated Process Teams (IPTs) to manage the program and resolve
fundamental issues. Section 4.2 further describes the evolution of CSEPP management practices
and IPTs.9

During this phase, the program heavily emphasized developing emergency plans; designing and
delivering training; hiring dedicated State and local staff; building infrastructure, including
automation systems, warning systems, communication systems, and emergency operations
centers (EOCs); and purchasing personal protective equipment (PPE) for responders.

2.2.3 Program Maturation

From 2000 until 2005, CSEPP was in a “program maturation” phase. New management at both
the Army and FEMA CSEPP offices produced a more team-oriented approach, and the
frequently adversarial relationship between the Federal partners became much more cordial. The
new program management initiated a “re-baselining” of CSEPP in early 2000, including
development of a new standardized CSEPP State-specific life-cycle cost estimate (LCCE)
process. CSEPP State agencies and counties took their annual cooperative agreement detailed
line-item budget requests and expanded them into multi-year LCCEs. Also in 2000, a CSEPP
Planning Conference identified unresolved program issues and established a series of working
groups to address them. These working groups ultimately evolved into functional, national-level
IPTs that developed landmark innovations, including the CSEPP Portal, automated shelter-in-
place (SIP) decision tools, reentry and recovery guidance, risk communication programs, and
other planning tools and performance measures. During this period, FEMA also initiated
development of an enhanced grants management software tool that ultimately evolved into a

9
 See also “Lesson Learned on Use of IPTs” in Volume 2 of this document.

 8

central web-based financial management tool used by FEMA and the off-post communities to
manage budgets, track grant expenditures, and report performance.

The terror attacks of September 11, 2001, highlighted the vulnerability of the U.S. chemical
warfare agent stockpile and led to renewed efforts to accelerate the disposal process. The
stockpile disposal program expedited plans to neutralize the bulk stockpiles at Aberdeen Proving
Ground in Maryland and Newport Chemical Depot in Indiana. In addition, several of the baseline
incineration sites received their operating permits and began disposal operations.

2.2.4 Sustainment and Closeout

In 2005, the program entered a phase of sustainment and closeout, with a focus on planning for a
smooth closeout while maintaining a high level of public safety and replacing obsolete systems
as required. The Aberdeen, Newport, Pine Bluff, Umatilla, Anniston, and Deseret stockpiles
were eliminated by February 2012, and the Pueblo and Blue Grass stockpiles were eliminated by
July and September 2023, respectively. Thus, the last 11 years of the program involved only the
two sites in Colorado and Kentucky. The program adapted by streamlining national-level
activities (including eliminating the annual national workshop) and focusing on how the two
remaining sites could support one another in planning, response, and exercising. Chapter 6
provides details of the program closeout for each site and nationally.

2.3 Army Chemical Stockpile
This section describes the chemical stockpile that is the subject of the CSEP Program. It includes
information about the chemical agents, how they were configured in the stockpile, and where and
how the different components of the stockpile were stored.

The description in this section is not a comprehensive summary of the U.S. chemical weapons
program; this section addresses only the stockpile that drove development of CSEPP. As
mentioned in Section 2.1.1, the potential hazard to the installation and the surrounding
community depended on the nature of the chemicals involved, how they were configured and
stored as part of the weapons stockpile, and the potential for their release into the environment.

The U.S. chemical stockpile consisted of two broad categories of items: bulk containers of
chemical agent and munitions incorporating chemical agent. Bulk chemicals were stored in ton
containers (steel cylinders 81 inches [in.] long and 30 in. in diameter holding up to
approximately 170 gallons [gal] or about 1,600 pounds (lb.) of liquid agent); the contents of
these containers accounted for the majority of the chemical agent in the stockpile (by weight) at
the beginning of the disposal program. The munitions incorporating chemical agent included
mortar cartridges, artillery projectiles, rockets, mines, spray tanks, and bombs. Most munitions
contained a few pounds of liquid agent and a small explosive charge to disperse it. Bombs,
containing 100 to 350 lb. of agent, were an exception, as were spray tanks designed to disperse
up to 1,350 lb. of agent from an aircraft.

 9

The term “chemical agent” generally refers to the chemical itself, while “chemical weapon” or
“chemical munition” usually refers to a delivery vehicle that contains chemical agent. However,
in popular usage “chemical weapons,” “chemical munitions,” and “chemical materiel” were
often used generically to refer to the chemical warfare stockpile regardless of the configuration.

Note that not all of the Army’s chemical weapons were included in the disposal program.10 The
Army, at various times, produced and stockpiled other types of chemical agents such as riot-
control agents (e.g., tear gas), defoliants, incendiaries, obscurants (i.e., smoke-producing
chemicals), hallucinogens, and binary lethal agents (i.e., two precursor chemicals incorporated
into a weapon, designed so the chemicals would combine to form a lethal chemical when the
weapon was fired). In this document, the terms “chemical agent,” “chemical weapon,” “chemical
munition,” and “chemical materiel” will refer to the stockpile of unitary lethal chemical agents
and munitions mandated for destruction as part of the CSDP (the Schedule 1 agents identified in
the Chemical Weapons Convention [CWC]). These are described in Sections 2.3.1 and 2.3.2.

2.3.1 Types of Chemical Agent

The chemical stockpile consisted of two main types of chemical
agent: nerve agent and mustard agent.11 Both are liquid at room
temperature and pressure.

Nerve agents were developed just before and during World War II
and are related chemically to organophosphate insecticides. The
principal nerve agents of concern to the CSEP Program were GB
(sarin) and VX (methylphosphonothioic acid). The “G” agents
(including GB) tend to be nonpersistent, whereas the “V” agents are
persistent. 12 At room temperature, GB is a comparatively volatile
liquid and therefore considered nonpersistent. VX is a relatively
nonvolatile liquid that does not hydrolyze readily and therefore is
persistent. Both GB and VX are toxic in small doses and are fast Figure 2.2 Container
acting. Respiratory lethal doses generally cause death within 1 to of Vesicant Agent HD
15 minutes. Their toxicity and rapidity of effect complicate
emergency preparedness efforts. They are toxic through respiration
10
 The program’s legal mandate provides for “the destruction of the United States’ stockpile of lethal chemical
 agents and munitions that exists on November 8, 1985.” The law also provides definitions of these terms: “[t]he
 term ‘chemical agent and munition’ means an agent or munition that, through its chemical properties, produces
 lethal or other damaging effects on human beings, except that such term does not include riot control agents,
 chemical herbicides, smoke and other obscuration materials.” And, “[t]he term ‘lethal chemical agent and
 munition’ means a chemical agent or munition that is designed to cause death, through its chemical properties, to
 human beings in field concentrations,” from 50 U.S.C. Sec. 1521, subsections (a) and (o).
11
 Note: the agents described in this section are the ones pertinent to CSEPP; the United States has manufactured
 and stored other chemical agents, although they were not part of the active stockpile during the CSEPP years.
 The general sources for the information in this subsection are Types of Chemical Weapons by the Federation of
 American Scientists, and Frederick R. Sidell, M.D., Management of Chemical Warfare Agent Casualties (1995).
 Further information about chemical agent characteristics and toxicity may be found in the chemical agent
 Material Safety Data Sheets (MSDSs).
12
 The term “persistent” refers to the length of time a chemical agent will remain on terrain, equipment, or other
 objects, or more simply, how quickly the agent evaporates.

 10

of agent vapor, absorption after skin exposure, or ingestion. Injection of atropine can counteract
some of the effects of nerve agent exposure.

Blister or vesicant agents in the stockpile included H, HD, and HT (Figure 2.2 above).
Sometimes referred to as mustard agents, they are persistent agents that were used extensively in
World War I. Munitions containing mustard agent are still being found on WWI battle sites.

While not as toxic as the nerve agents on a per-milligram basis, mustard agents are nonetheless
extremely toxic — through respiration, skin exposure, or ingestion — and there are no antidotes.
Vapor exposure can also damage the eyes. Exposure to mustard agents causes injury almost
immediately, but symptoms of exposure might not appear until hours later. Mustard agent is also
a known carcinogen.

The U.S. chemical weapons stockpile included a variety of munitions incorporating the chemical
agents described above. Figures 2.3–2.5 illustrate some of the munitions in the stockpile.

2.3.3 Stockpile Locations

At the beginning of the CSEP Program, the U.S. stockpile of lethal chemical weapons was stored
at eight U.S. Army installations within the CONUS. Table 2.2 lists the eight locations, including
which agents were present at each location, the configuration of the agents, and what percentage
of the stockpile (by weight) was stored at each location. This information, which was classified
at the beginning of the program, was declassified in 1996.16 Figure 2.6 shows the general
locations of the stockpile installations and quantities of agent stored at each.17

 Figure 2.6 Chemical Stockpile Sites and Agent Quantities19

Within each installation, the chemical weapons were segregated in chemical limited areas
(CLAs), behind additional security barriers and with additional access requirements. In the
CLAs, bulk agent in ton containers was stored either outdoors, indoors in a warehouse, or in
earth-covered igloos. Explosively configured munitions were always stored in igloos. An igloo is
an arch-shaped, reinforced concrete structure with a front wall made of reinforced concrete. Most
igloos are either 60 or 80 feet (ft) long.

To illustrate the capacity of an igloo, an 80-ft igloo could store up to 9,288 4.2-in. mortar
cartridges packed two per wooden box, with 36 boxes per pallet and 129 pallets per igloo. The
M23 VX-filled mines were stored three per steel drum, with 12 to 16 drums per pallet. A 60-foot
igloo could hold up to 95 pallets of 12 drums each, or 3,420 mines total. These storage
configurations affected the hazard analyses that drove emergency preparedness; for example, an
igloo might suffer a fire, causing the detonation of or damage to the munitions inside, but it was
not considered plausible that the fire or damage would spread to adjacent igloos, limiting the
amount of chemical agent released during such an event. Figure 2.7 shows earth-covered
chemical igloos at Pueblo Chemical Depot.

     January 2024.

2.4 Chemical Stockpile Disposal Program
Large-scale destruction of chemical weapons began as early as 1972, when the Army established
a program to dispose of leaking and obsolete chemical munitions, headed by a Program Manager
for Demilitarization of Chemical Materiel.21 By 1985, more than 7,000 tons of chemical agent
had been destroyed at the Rocky Mountain Arsenal, where much of the U.S. chemical agent
stockpile had been produced. The Army also built the Chemical Agent Materiel Disposal System
(CAMDS) facility at the TEAD in Utah to dispose of Tooele’s leaking and obsolete weapons and
to serve as a testbed for destruction technologies. CAMDS began operations in 1979 and ended
them in 2005.22 More than 100 tons of agent were neutralized and 78 tons were incinerated at
CAMDS, nearly 40,000 munitions in all.

In November 1985, Congress directed destruction of the remaining stockpile of lethal unitary
weapons in the DOD Authorization Act, 1986 (PL 99–145).23 That statute provided for an Army

20
 U.S. Army PEO ACWA Fact Sheet, Chemical Weapons Destruction at the U.S. Army Pueblo Chemical Depot,
 April 3, 2025. Accessed March 2026 at https://www.peoacwa.army.mil/2025/04/03/facts-chemical-weapons-
 destruction-at-the-u-s-army-pueblo-chemical-depot/. Igloos are also used to store conventional munitions.
21
 The information in this section derives primarily from Mauroni, A., 2003, Chemical Demilitarization: Public
 Policy Aspects, Praeger Publishers, Westport, Conn.
22
 In addition to the facilities and techniques described here, smaller quantities of chemical agents and munitions
 were also destroyed in various ways at various locations, as circumstance dictated, throughout the chemical
 weapons program. Methods included deep sea disposal, chemical neutralization, and explosive decomposition.
23
 Destruction of the stockpile was initially linked legislatively and programmatically to production of a new
 generation of chemical weapons, called binary weapons, which would purportedly be safer and easier to store
 and handle. Binary weapons were designed so that munitions contained two precursor chemicals that would react
 to produce a chemical agent en route to the target. Initially, the idea was that, as the old stockpile was destroyed,
 it would be replaced with the new generation of binary chemical weapons. While some binary weapons were
 produced, the binary weapons program was canceled after a few years, in 1990.

 16

organization to oversee the program, which was to be headed by a general officer. As noted in
Section 2.1, the Army contracted ORNL to develop a PEIS as part of the decision-making
process regarding the method for disposal. The ability to mitigate hazards through emergency
preparedness became a factor in the decision to perform destruction operations at each storage
site rather than shipping the stockpile to a central location.

The comprehensive destruction program began with construction of a pilot plant at Johnston
Island, a remote atoll in the Pacific to test weapons processing and incineration. Chemical
munitions were relocated to Johnston Island from installations in Germany. Construction of the
Johnston Atoll Chemical Agent Disposal System (JACADS) began in 1987. Operational testing
began in 1990, full-scale operations began in 1993, and operations were concluded in 2000. In
all, 2,000 tons of chemical weapons were destroyed there.

During construction and operation of JACADS, work began on several of the destruction
facilities in the United States. Table 2.3 lists milestones for the destruction facilities in the
disposal program. Figure 2.8 shows the Umatilla Chemical Disposal Facility.

2.4.1 Alternatives to Incineration

Technologies, cost, organizational oversight, permitting, and other aspects of the disposal
program are thoroughly documented elsewhere.27 The main impact on CSEPP was that changes
to disposal options increased the life of the disposal program, which, in turn, extended the life of
CSEPP. Opposition to the incinerator technology chosen in the final PEIS led to further study
and evaluation of alternatives. In 1992, Congress directed the Army to reevaluate the use of
alternative (non-incineration) disposal technologies and to use an alternative technology at the
low-volume sites, as long as the selected alternative was found to be safer and no more costly
than incineration.28 Low-volume sites were those with less than 5% of the total stockpile,
specifically, Aberdeen, Blue Grass, and Newport. Planning and construction of disposal facilities
was suspended at those sites and also at Pueblo.

A series of studies and assessments eventually led to the adoption of a chemical neutralization
technology for use at Aberdeen, Blue Grass, Newport, and Pueblo. The technology was
implemented relatively quickly at Aberdeen and Newport, both of which had only bulk chemical

26
 U.S. Army, accessed July 2025 at
 https://www.army.mil/article/142667/army_closes_chemical_agent_disposal_facility.
27
 See Mauroni, A., 2003, Chemical Demilitarization: Public Policy Aspects, Praeger Publishers, Westport, Conn.
 Also see CMA fact sheets and other information available at http://www.cma.army.mil/home.aspx; ACWA
 information is available at http://www.pmacwa.army.mil/index.html.
28
 PL 102–484, National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 1993, October 1992, Sec. 171 et seq.

agent in ton containers. Congress mandated the study of non-incineration disposal techniques for
assembled chemical weapons (those with explosive charges, such as mines and mortar rounds) in
1996,29 also directing that the Army set up a separate office for oversight of the assembled
weapons program. The Assembled Chemical Weapons Assessment (ACWA) program became
the Assembled Chemical Weapons Alternatives program, retaining the same acronym as the
program moved from study into implementation. The Program Executive Office (PEO) for
ACWA chose a neutralization and biotreatment program for Pueblo in 2002, and neutralization
followed by supercritical water oxidation for Blue Grass in 2003. Both facilities also employed
an explosive destruction process (static detonation chamber [SDC]) for a subset of the munitions
that could not be processed in the main facilities. The Pueblo Chemical Agent-Destruction Pilot
Plant (PCAPP) began operations in 2016, completing them in 2023, and the Blue Grass
Chemical Agent-Destruction Pilot Plant (BGCAPP) began operations in 2020, also completing
them in 2023.

2.4.2 Spring Valley Cleanup

Another event that influenced the developing CSEP Program was the January 1993 discovery of
buried World War I-era chemical munitions at a construction site in Spring Valley, a suburb of
Washington, D.C. The Army assembled a task force of specialists and designated a chemical
service response force (SRF) to manage and execute the cleanup. The SRF included technical
personnel to perform the cleanup, plus environmental experts, attorneys, claims specialists,
public affairs personnel, and others to interact with neighbors, local authorities, and the larger
public. Cleanup operations required temporary evacuations and raised issues such as work
planning, hearings, neighbors’ expenses, and even police overtime. While not exactly a CSEPP
rehearsal, the experience highlighted some of the realities of dealing with multiple levels of
government and a displaced and concerned public. The event also provided valuable insight to
guide subsequent SRF exercises. After the initial assessment and cleanup by the SRF (completed
in February 1993), responsibility for continuing restoration was turned over to the U.S. Army
Corps of Engineers (COE). The COE completed soil remediation in 2021, and other remediation
work was ongoing as of February 2025.30

2.5 Legislative and Regulatory Mandates
The CSEP Program was a creature of Federal law, initiated and continued through provisions
inserted in U.S. Department of Defense (DOD) funding bills. Funding and direction for both
CSEPP and the CSDP were provided through this mechanism. CSEPP and the CSDP also were
aligned with and affected by two other legal mechanisms: the CWC and the U.S. Federal
regulatory system for hazardous chemicals. This section discusses the effects that these legal and
regulatory mandates had in shaping CSEPP.

29
 PL 104–201, National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 1997, September 1996, and PL 104–208,
 Department of Defense Appropriations Act, 1997, September 1996.
30
 U.S. Army COE webpage, Spring Valley Overview, available at https://www.nab.usace.army.mil/Home/Spring-
 Valley/ , accessed September 2025.

 19

2.5.1 Defense Authorization and Appropriations Acts

The Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year (FY) 1986 (PL 99–145), Section 1412
established the chemical stockpile disposal program and CSEPP. Subsection (c)(1)(A) required
that the Secretary provide “maximum protection for the environment, the general public, and the
personnel who are involved in [such] destruction.” This provision was often referenced as the
original mandate for the CSEP Program.

There was little in the legislative history of this statute to illuminate the intent of “maximum
protection.” The maximum protection language was not part of the original bill but was inserted
during the course of debate in the U.S. House of Representatives. Representatives Hopkins
(R-Kentucky) and Anthony (D-Arkansas) spoke briefly in favor of “maximum protection” on
June 26, 1985. The chairman of the House Armed Services Committee, Representative Aspin
(D-Wisconsin) supported it, and the amendment passed the House that same day. The
United States Senate later agreed to the bill as amended, and it was signed into law on
November 8, 1985, as PL 99–145. The House Conference Report on the bill contains only one
brief paragraph regarding Section 1412, and it does not mention maximum protection. Thus, the
only discussion of the amendment on the floor of either the House or the Senate consisted of two
brief supporting speeches by Reps. Hopkins and Anthony when it was first introduced. Their
speeches emphasized the danger posed by the stockpile and the need for its elimination; both
characterized it as “an accident waiting to happen.” The only reference to safety or
environmental protection associated with the destruction process came in Rep. Anthony’s
remarks, in which he said, “The goal would be to ensure that the destruction of this dangerous
stockpile is done in a way which is consistent with local, State, and Federal laws as well as
public health and safety standards.”

Congress may not have contemplated the establishment and growth of the CSEP Program with
this initial language. However, the continued annual appropriations after 1985 were an indicator
of congressional support.

After 1985, Congress tweaked the CSDP many times and CSEPP a few times. Changes to the
CSDP included the following:
 • Extensions of the deadline for destruction (several times);
 • Provision for study and then adoption of alternative (non-incineration) technology;31
 • Prohibition of interstate transport of stockpile materials;32
 • A requirement that support measures be in place prior to initiation of “demil”
 (demilitarization) operations at a given site, including security and surety program
 requirements and environmental permits from State regulatory agencies. States were
 eligible to receive grants to offset the costs of processing the permits;33

31
 PL 102–484, 1992, Defense Authorizations Act for FY 1993, Section 174, and several subsequent public laws;
 see generally Public Laws Authorization and Appropriation Acts 1969–2007 for the U.S. Army Chemical
 Materials Agency, available at http://www.dtic.mil.
32
 PL 103–337, Div. A, Title I, Section 143, October 5, 1994. Codified at 50 United States Code (USC) Sec. 1512a.
33
 PL 104–208, Department of Defense Appropriations Act, 1997, Sec. 101(b). Codified at 50 USC Sec. 1521(b).

 20

 • Prohibition of post-demil re-use of demil facilities, unless the host State consents;34
 • Establishment of citizen’s advisory commissions.35
 • Requirement of a quarterly briefing of Congress on the progress of demil.36
 • A technical amendment in the National Defense Authorization Act for FY 2020
 transferring responsibilities of the Under Secretary of Defense for Acquisition,
 Technology, and Logistics to the Under Secretary of Defense for Acquisition and
 Sustainment.37

Congress also addressed emergency preparedness specifically:
 • Authorizing the Secretary of Defense to “make grants to State and local governments
 (either directly or through FEMA) to assist those governments in carrying out functions
 related to emergency response in connection with the disposal of... lethal chemical agents
 and munitions... ”38
 • Requiring use of IPTs. The Defense Authorization Act for FY 1997 directed the
 Secretary of the Army to submit a report within 120 days on implementation of site-
 specific IPTs as a management tool for CSEPP.39
 • Directing FEMA to aid State and local governments, in coordination with the Army, and
 requiring annual CSEPP reports to Congress by the FEMA Director.40
 • Defining the endpoint for CSEPP at a given site as stockpile destruction is completed.
 Assistance could be provided until the completion of all grants and cooperative
 agreements or 180 days after the completion of demilitarization activities, whichever
 came first.41

Congress reorganized and updated the legislative mandate for stockpile destruction and the
CSEP Program in the defense authorization act for FY 2011.42 The consolidated version,
reflecting the points described above, may be found in the U.S. Code at 50 USC Section 1521.

2.5.2 Chemical Weapons Convention

The CWC is an international treaty that prohibits the stockpiling of chemical weapons. The
United States ratified the Convention in 1997 and so was obligated to destroy its stockpile. Note

 21

that the U.S. demilitarization process began well before this international obligation took
effect.43

The inventory of chemical weapons and the destruction process were subject to verification by
international inspectors, and each CSDP site received regular visits from them. The treaty
established disposal deadlines, which were adjusted several times, each time requiring Senate
approval and formal notification to other treaty signatories. However, treaty obligations did not
otherwise impact the CSEP Program. The CWC did not require or otherwise affect measures for
emergency preparedness per se, although it did provide that, “[e]ach State Party, during
transportation, sampling, storage and destruction of chemical weapons, shall assign the highest
priority to ensuring the safety of people and to protecting the environment. Each State Party shall
transport, sample, store and destroy chemical weapons in accordance with its national standards
for safety and emissions.”44

2.5.3 Hazardous Material Regulations

Many hazardous chemicals are produced, stored, shipped, and used in the United States on a
daily basis. Several Federal statutes have been enacted to provide for the safety of hazardous
materials workers and the public, including the Clean Air Act; Clean Water Act; Occupational
Safety and Health Act (OSHA); Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and
Liability Act (CERCLA) (also known as the Superfund Act); Oil Pollution Act; Resource
Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA); and Emergency Planning and Community Right to
Know Act (EPCRA) (also known as the Superfund Amendments and Reauthorization Act
[SARA] Title III). These statutes and their implementing regulations affected how CSEPP was
conducted, in terms of both on-post and off-post procedures. Although some of the
considerations and hazards posed by chemical weapons are unique, in many respects,
preparedness for a chemical weapons accident was similar to preparedness for other hazardous
materials accidents. CSEPP was in some sense a “special case” of the more general preparedness
scheme required by these statutes.

CERCLA and its implementing regulation, the National Oil and Hazardous Substances Pollution
Contingency Plan (NCP), EPCRA, RCRA, and OSHA were the main statutes of interest and are
discussed below.

2.5.3.1 CERCLA and the National Oil and Hazardous Substances Pollution
 Contingency Plan (NCP)

CERCLA Section 104(a)45 authorizes the president to respond to protect the public and the
environment in the event of a hazardous materials (HAZMAT) spill. The roles, responsibilities,
and mechanisms for HAZMAT-spill response are spelled out in the NCP.46 An On-Scene

43
 The CWC was preceded by a bilateral treaty with the Soviet Union. President George H.W. Bush and Soviet
 Union leader Mikhail Gorbachev signed a bilateral treaty on June 1, 1990, to end chemical weapons production
 and start destroying each of their respective nation’s stockpiles. See Wikipedia entry on chemical warfare.
44
 CWC, Article IV, Section 10.
45
     42 USC Section 9604(a).
46
 40 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) 300.

 22

Coordinator (OSC) directs NCP spill response. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency
(EPA) and the U.S. Coast Guard (USCG) maintain staffs of trained OSCs ready to direct spill
response from industrial or transportation accidents. However, Executive Order (EO) 12580
delegated the response function to DOD and U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) for HAZMAT
spills that occur “on or from” their facilities.47 DOD, in turn, through the Army, delegated OSC
responsibilities to Army installation commanders for events taking place at their installations.

Under Army Regulation (AR) 50-6, in the event of a chemical agent accident or incident on-post,
the installation commander would be the OSC. Army implementation of the NCP was also
referenced in AR 200-1, Environmental Protection and Enhancement.

The NCP addresses actions in the short term to reduce immediate hazards and in the long term to
assess the extent of contamination and complete cleanup. The NCP also provides mechanisms to
obtain other Federal resources, such as Regional Response Teams (RRTs), for response
assistance. The EPA and USCG have implemented actions under the NCP hundreds of times;
both have a cadre of trained and experienced OSCs, as well as staff and contractors ready to
respond on short notice. Together, they provided a reservoir of expertise and experience
available to the Army in the event of a chemical agent emergency. These agencies participated in
training and exercises associated with the CSEP Program.

2.5.3.2 EPCRA

Also known as SARA Title III, EPCRA reinforced preparedness for those hazardous materials
considered to pose immediate threats to the local community. Under EPCRA, states and local
communities set up State Emergency Response Commissions (SERCs) and Local Emergency
Planning Committees (LEPCs). LEPCs are responsible for developing emergency response
plans. Facilities that have materials designated as “extremely hazardous substances” 48 must
report their inventories to the LEPCs and must participate (or offer to participate) in local
emergency planning efforts. Facilities also must immediately report releases of hazardous
substances to the LEPC point of contact.49 Executive Order 12856, Federal Compliance with
Right-to-Know Laws and Pollution Prevention Requirements (1993), directed compliance with
these requirements at DOD facilities.

2.5.3.3 RCRA

The impact of RCRA on the CSEP Program was twofold, although both aspects stemmed from
the same requirement — hazardous waste treatment facilities, such as the chemical disposal
facilities, required a permit from the State environmental agency. First, in the event of an
accident or incident involving release of chemical agent, the State could use its RCRA permitting
authority to require cleanup and oversee the cleanup process. Restarting disposal operations
would depend on State approval. Thus, RCRA and CERCLA cleanup requirements are
substantially parallel. The EPA, in recognition of this fact, issued policies encouraging the use of

47
 Executive Order 12580, 1987, Superfund Implementation, Section 2.d.
48
 Regulations implementing EPCRA, including a list of the designated extremely hazardous substances, are found
 at 40 CFR 355. Chemical agents are among the substances on the list.
49
 Releases must be reported if they are over the threshold quantity set for that particular substance.

 23

interagency agreements to resolve issues and develop a unified cleanup plan satisfying all
requirements.50

The second and more substantial impact of RCRA for CSEPP purposes was that State agencies
could make emergency preparedness a condition for issuing and maintaining a disposal facility
permit. Under RCRA regulations, hazardous waste facilities must have an emergency
coordinator, a contingency plan, arrangements with local emergency response authorities, and
other emergency preparedness measures in place.51 The authority issuing the permit enforces
these requirements and may also add “[such] terms and conditions as the Administrator or State
Director determines necessary to protect human health and the environment.”52 This authority,
along with similar permitting authority under the Clean Air Act, was used to link aspects of the
CSEP Program to disposal permitting.53 For example, the Umatilla Chemical Agent Disposal
Facility permit from the Oregon Department of Environmental Quality specifically required
semiannual reports on the status of the CSEP Program:

 The Permittee shall submit by January 31 and July 31 of each year until all
 chemical agent at UMCD [Umatilla Chemical Depot] has been destroyed, a
 written progress report to the Department on the status of the Chemical Stockpile
 Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP). The report shall evaluate CSEPP’s
 readiness for responding to an incident at the Umatilla Chemical Depot and
 should address at a minimum, status of community emergency sirens and
 distribution of tone alert radios of the Alert Notification System (ANS); the ability
 to provide offsite chemical agent monitoring and decontamination during an
 incident, offsite triage and treatment of casualties; and, the state of enhanced
 sheltering and positive pressurization of buildings, such as schools and hospitals,
 where substantial numbers of persons can be expected to gather daily.54

In addition, facility operations could not be conducted unless the permittee “...has received
written notification from the Governor of the State of Oregon, or his designee, that an adequate
