2.6 Program Antecedents
Many elements of CSEPP evolved from existing emergency preparedness programs. On the
Army side, as long as there were chemical weapons, there was chemical surety — a set of
doctrines and practices adopted to ensure that the weapons were managed safely. On the off-post
side, the REP program offered the best analogy to CSEPP — fixed facilities that posed an
esoteric, statistically unlikely, but potentially significant hazard to the surrounding community.
These two programs are described below.

2.6.1 Army Chemical Surety

When the Army first began to acquire chemical warfare materiel, it was stockpiled in much the
same way as were conventional (explosive-filled) munitions with regard to safety and security.
Much of the bulk chemical stockpile was stored outdoors, mostly in one-ton containers, and
chemical-filled munitions were stored mostly in warehouses and earth-covered igloos in
conventional ammunition storage areas. Operating procedures and facilities, personal protective
clothing and equipment, and worker-level emergency response capabilities and procedures were
military WWII/Korean War-era vintage for the most part. Belgian hares (rabbits) were used to
detect nerve agent vapors during operations — similar to using a canary in a coal mine. And
canaries were actually used in some laboratories. There were no formal plans for emergency
response beyond the worker protection provisions contained in the standard operating procedures
(SOPs) for an operation. None of the chemical storage sites had EOCs to coordinate an overall
installation response. The fire department and industrial hygiene clinic represented most of the
on-post response capability beyond self-aid and buddy-aid, and the security desk was the primary
point of contact with local off-post security forces to provide notification of an emergency that
might affect local communities.

Unserviceable and excess chemical materiel was buried, dumped at sea, incinerated in the open
air, or neutralized chemically in batches large and small. Chemical munitions were evaluated in
the open air. Screening and training activities for the workers handling this materiel were
minimal. Occasional exposures of workers to agent over the years added up to hundreds of
events in all. A worker died at Rocky Mountain Arsenal from exposure to phosgene in
November 1965. A number of sheep died in Skull Valley, Utah, in March 1968 from a nerve
agent open-air release; this incident led, about a year later, to the cessation of open-air testing.
Few Army officials gave serious consideration to the potential for a catastrophic release that
would threaten communities beyond the installation boundary because such an event was
believed to be so unlikely, and perhaps because so few tools were available to describe what

55
 Ibid.

 25

such an event would look like other than the various nomograms used to calculate battlefield use
of chemical munitions.56

The presence of chemical warfare agents and munitions was common knowledge in communities
near these storage sites, but there were no more plans or preparations for a chemical agent
accident or incident in off-post areas than there were for any other nearby technological hazard.
These sites represented good jobs for the surrounding populace and were not perceived to be a
problem or a threat. Details about the chemical stockpile, including the particular stocks at any
given facility, were classified.

2.6.1.1 Surety Management

This situation began to change during the late 1960s. The Army Chemical Surety Program57
began in 1968 as a unilateral decision by the DA to apply selected surety management concepts
to the chemical weapons program, similar to the DOD Nuclear Surety Program that began about
4 years earlier. The U.S. Army Nuclear Weapons Surety Group at Fort Belvoir became the U.S.
Army Nuclear and Chemical Agency and the principal agent for implementing this change. This
organization prepared the definitive Army guidance on chemical surety program management in
AR 50-6, supplemented later by DA Pamphlet 50-6.

In 1969, the Army Materiel Command (AMC), responsible for weapons development,
production, and most chemical weapons storage, directed its chemical installations to produce a
chemical accident/incident response plan as an annex to the installation Disaster Control Plan.
Soon thereafter, the AMC directed that selected officials in communities near these sites must be
given For Official Use Only (FOUO)-level information about the chemical munitions stored on-
site to help them plan to protect their communities. This protocol was already being carried out
unofficially at most places. In the same year, AMC also began a program of annual compliance
inspections for all AMC chemical storage sites. Thus began a slow but steady upgrade of Army
chemical operations even as the stockpile was moving toward obsolescence and, ultimately,
disposal.

The implementation of surety management practices met with resistance in some quarters,
especially among senior chemical corps officers who advocated maintaining a military capability
for offensive use of chemical weapons. Those in favor of maintaining such a capability did not
want constraints placed on their programs, while those opposed to chemical weapons warfare
pointed to the risks to workers and nearby communities posed by storage of these materials.
Many believed the military utility of chemical weapons was questionable from the beginning and

56
 For historical context, during this time, 80-column punch cards were high-tech data-processing tools, cell phones
 and computers were not available, and there was a general lack of public awareness around environmental
 concerns.
57
 The term “surety” is used in a military context to refer to safety, security, and reliability. AR 50-6 defines a
 “chemical surety program” as “[a] system of control measures designed to provide protection to the local
 population, workers, and the environment by ensuring that chemical surety operations are conducted safely; that
 chemical surety materials are secure; and that personnel involved in those operations meet the highest standards
 of reliability.” The term “chemical surety operation” refers to any operation involving chemical surety materials,
 and the term “chemical surety material” refers to chemical agents and chemical weapons. See AR 50-6,
 Chemical Surety, July 28, 2008, Glossary.

 26

that these weapons were being made obsolete as modern conventional weapons systems became
more effective and exacted a lesser logistics burden. The compromise at the time was to improve
safety, security, facilities, and operational practices while maintaining the stockpile as a
bargaining chip in international disarmament talks.

2.6.1.2 Evolution of Response Capability

For approximately 20 years prior to the formal beginning of the CSEP Program in the mid-
1980s, there was a slow but steady upgrade of Army chemical operations emergency
preparedness, even as the stockpile was moving toward obsolescence and demilitarization.
During this period, there were no specific laws or regulations that compelled a more robust
response posture at the chemical stockpile storage sites; however, various events brought to light
the need for a more robust response capability.

Installation-level Requirements

Planning. As noted, in 1969 AMC mandated that sites where bulk agent and agent-filled
munitions were stored must produce a chemical accident/incident response plan as an annex to
the installation’s Disaster Control Plan. Installations shared FOUO-level information about the
stockpile and its hazards with local off-post officials to help them plan for community protection.

Personnel. Another Army initiative, the Chemical Personnel Reliability Program (PRP), was
developed by AMC in late 1968 and subsequently published as a stand-alone AMC Regulation.
The PRP was eventually refined and codified in AR 50-6 (effective 1 January 1977).

Facilities. AMC published requirements for setting up an EOC at each chemical storage site to
coordinate operations and serve as the primary hub for communications with higher headquarters
and off-post communities.

Inspections. AMC began a program of annual Surety and Operational Inspections (SOIs) of all
AMC chemical storage sites in 1969 and expanded this initiative to include all other chemical
operations in AMC soon thereafter. The inspections examined safety, security, PRP, munitions
serviceability, and accident response capabilities. The inspections also included dynamic
exercises of emergency response capabilities by a dozen or more subject matter expert (SME)
evaluators. In 1983, AMC extended the scope of these inspections to include external support to
stockpile storage sites. The visibility of the results of these inspections and exercises within the
command prompted publication of new and better guidance and allocation of resources to fix
systemic problems. In 1988, the Army Inspector General Technical Inspection Division began
formal annual inspections of Army chemical facilities, which included examination of chemical
emergency response preparedness. AMC adjusted its surety management oversight from SOIs to
Surety Management Reviews, while maintaining interest in ensuring chemical emergency
response capabilities.

 27

Development of Augmentation Capabilities

In 1971, AMC conducted a table-top exercise to explore the capability of a chemical storage site
to sustain emergency response operations in the days following a significant release of agent.
This exercise, Bald Eagle I, quantified, in general terms, what augmentation might be needed and
led to internal AMC plans to deploy augmented personnel and equipment resources from one
chemical installation to help at another in an emergency. Bald Eagle II, which took place
18 months later, examined how an installation might notify nearby off-post communities and
what assistance could be provided in the event of a chemical accident.58 These exercises led to a
few memoranda of agreement (MOAs) and MOUs and led to measures to augment capabilities
by drawing on the larger Army’s resources.

Examples of augmentation capabilities included additional qualified trained chemical workers
with sufficient PPE to support field operations on a 24/7 basis; medical experts to support
extended field operations and provide advice to local off-post medical treatment facilities; public
affairs officers to develop and sustain an effective emergency public information program;
claims specialists; extra security forces; legal and environmental advisors; chemical warfare
agent hazard analysts; historians; protocol officers; information technology (IT) experts; and
environmental sampling and monitoring teams. Augmentation capabilities also included
administrative and logistical support, communications, transportation (both air and surface),
facilities, and specialized equipment.

In addition, the Army formed a rapid deployment Technical Escort Unit based at Edgewood.

Chemical Service Response Force and Service Response Force Exercises

Many of the senior managers at DA and AMC who were responsible for the Army’s chemical
weapons program also managed its nuclear weapons program, so it was logical that there would
be cross fertilization of ideas and guidance between these programs. Experience suggested that,
for a catastrophic event such as the release of radioactive material over a large area, a tailored
task force response would be needed. The task force, called a Service Response Force, would be
led by a flag officer from the service having custody of the weapon. This concept was
successfully applied by the US Air Force to cope with nuclear weapons accidents in Palomares,
Spain, in 1966 and in Thule, Greenland, in 1968 and was codified in DOD nuclear accident
response planning. The Nuclear Weapons Accident Exercise series (NUWAX-79, 81, and 83)
provided many lessons that were applicable to the Army’s chemical weapons program.

DA and AMC nuclear and chemical weapons managers who participated in the NUWAX
exercises came away believing that a service-level task force would solve two vexing problems
associated with chemical accident/incident response. These challenges involved addressing
(1) how to assemble and deploy all the assets (skilled personnel and essential equipment) needed
to mitigate and recover from a chemical event quickly, given that no single installation possessed
all of the assets that would be required; and (2) how to most effectively provide assistance to
citizens in nearby communities and repair environmental damage. These challenges remained the

58
 Note that this was a one-dimensional exercise; no off-post participation was involved.

 28

two main priorities for deploying an SRF, although the tools used to satisfy these priorities
changed over the years.

Chemical program managers developed an arrangement similar to the nuclear SRF, but modified
to fit the needs of the chemical program. Thus was born the Army Chemical Service Response
Force, with the SRF Commander (a general officer) reporting to DA HQ if the Chemical SRF
stood up. The Commanding General, AMC, would establish and maintain the contingency SRF.
Essentially, as a contingency force, the Chemical SRF consisted of a list of personnel with
appropriate qualifications who could be called upon to respond to a chemical incident if needed.
This arrangement was incorporated in AR 50-6 in November 1986. The requirement for a
general officer reflected the belief of planners that in a significant event, sending a senior officer
would signal that the Army took the situation seriously and was committed to assisting the
community.

Army Chemical SRF Contingency Plans were tested during the first Chemical SRF exercise,
SRFX-87, which took place in August 1987. This multiday, full-scale Army exercise was
conducted at a fictional Midwest Army Depot but with responders from BGAD. AR 50-6, dated
November 1986, was the standard for this exercise. Many Army units participated, along with
representatives from the EPA and FEMA. State, county, and city officials from the
Commonwealth of Kentucky and Madison County represented their jurisdictions, although there
was no off-post exercise field play. SRFX-87 yielded two main lessons: better planning and
training were needed for the SRF Commander and staff when working with other Federal, State,
and local entities and in implementing the NCP; and a single Army Chemical SRF Emergency
Response Plan was needed, rather than allowing each Commander to develop his/her own plan.

The Assistant Secretary of Defense (Installations, Logistics, and Environment) took an interest in
SRFX-87 as a means to explore what might be involved in implementing the NCP for a chemical
accident on a DOD installation. The Assistant Secretary, two EPA Regional Administrators, and
Army and FEMA HQ officials were onsite during SRFX-87 to learn about the impact of such an
event and determine what a DOD OSC would face in implementing the NCP.

One result was an agreement among DOD, DA, and AMC that special provisions were warranted
to ensure effective implementation of the NCP if chemical warfare agents were released in an
accident or incident. These special provisions (an exception to the basic DOD arrangement to use
regional DOD OSCs) included preselection (by position) of a qualified senior military officer to
be the OSC and preplanned support (personnel and equipment) to help the OSC discharge these
responsibilities. Deputy OSCs would be designated at each chemical stockpile storage site; these
would be senior civilian executives whose important qualification would be their extensive
knowledge of the local community. These civilian deputy OSCs would provide continuity of
preparedness as military commanders rotated. It seemed reasonable to incorporate this approach
into the contingency mission of the Army Chemical SRF, because the SRF could also be the
mechanism to provide assets to help the DOD OSC implement the National Contingency Plan.
Thus, the Initial Response Force (IRF) commander would become the OSC by default, and the
SRF commander would become the OSC if the SRF stood up.

 29

The second Chemical SRF exercise, SRFX-89, was conducted in June 1989 at PBA. This
exercise was another multiday, full-scale 24/7 Army exercise, but was conducted at an actual
chemical storage site. This time, the exercise included scenario activity involving off-post testing
that local responders had to address. Participants in SRFX-89 included Army higher HQs; the
full range of Army technical responders, crisis management staff, and controllers from more than
40 military units deployed to the site; EPA and FEMA regional responders; and a complete
complement of State, county, and city officials. The exercise was covered extensively by local
news media. Lessons learned from SRFX-89 enabled progress in developing DA guidance that
was subsequently published in 1991 as DA Pamphlet 50-6.

The next biennial exercise of the chemical contingency task force was SRFX-91, conducted in
June 1991 at TEAD, Utah (later Deseret Chemical Depot, or DCD). Exercise activities were
driven by a multi-event scenario that posed challenges to the installation and the surrounding
community. All of the DOD elements that would be involved in responding to such events
participated in SRFX-91, as did other Federal agencies and State, county, and city governments.
Hundreds of players were involved, and there was extensive TV, radio, and print news coverage.

SRFX-91 also served as the pilot exercise for the CSEPP exercise program. It was the transition
to a whole new era in chemical emergency management exercises that included demonstration of
play in off-post jurisdictions, as well as on the Army installation.

The Army chemical exercise program operated by AMC before CSEPP contained many features
that were carried forward into the CSEPP exercise program: management and control of
exercises at a higher level than that of the installation; evaluation of player responses by non-
installation SMEs; formal assessments (written reports) that included a corrective action plan to
track findings; full field play involving realistic props; a robust scenario that demanded
demonstration of the full spectrum of player capabilities; use of a Simulation Cell to measure
player interaction with agencies and organizations outside of the installation; and the conduct of
these exercises on an annual basis.

Integration of Chemical Agent Response into Hazardous Material Response

Beginning in 2010, the biennial SRFXs were replaced with a quarterly modular training program
focusing on cleanup authorities, available Federal assistance, legal issues, and other aspects of
long-term cleanup and recovery from a chemical event. The Army also began extensive
discussions and planning to discontinue the chemical SRF and apply Army resources to a
chemical event through normal channels of authority, as it would for any other hazardous
materials incident.

With DOD and DA adoption of the National Incident Management System (NIMS) and the
Incident Command System (ICS) for managing emergencies, bringing them in alignment with
national response policies, DA rescinded the requirement for an SRF through Army Directive
(AD) 2013-03, Chemical Accident or Incident Response and Assistance (CAIRA), April 4, 2013.
From that point forward, response to a CAI event would follow concepts provided in AR 525-27
(Army Emergency Management Program), DA PAM 525-27 (Army Emergency Management
Program), and the NCP. The directive also rescinded the requirement for an installation IRF,

 30

although commanders remained responsible for maintaining planning and capabilities consistent
with the Army’s emergency management, safety, and security guidance and regulations.

AD 2013-3 also rescinded DA Pam 50-6, which contained specific CAIRA guidance. The AD
directed the Deputy Chief of Staff (DCS), G-3/5/759 to incorporate specific CAIRA planning and
exercise guidance into AR 50-6. Pending revisions to AR 50-6, the AD provided general
guidance on chemical emergency planning and exercises, meteorology supporting hazard
analysis, and medical support. As of this writing, the most recent version of AR 50-6 is the
November 2022 version.60 Note that this version of AR 50-6 established new terminology: the
term for a chemical event was changed from chemical accident or incident (CAI) to chemical
incident or mishap (CIM), and the name for response plans and quarterly installation exercises
was changed to chemical incident or mishap response and assistance (CIMRA) (previously
CAIRA).

2.6.2 Radiological Emergency Preparedness Program

As described above, over time, the Army developed capabilities, guidance, and internal
regulations to help prepare for response to a chemical event. Similarly, for off-post communities
and FEMA, experience with the REP program served as a model for building off-post chemical
preparedness. The REP program had many parallels with CSEPP.

The REP program was established following the 1979 Three Mile Island nuclear power plant
accident. The purpose of the program was (and continues to be) to improve and maintain the
state of emergency preparedness in communities near nuclear power plants. Under the program,
jurisdictions within about a 10-mile radius of a power plant are responsible for planning and
preparation for a radiological emergency at the power plant. State and local (county or
municipal-level) government agencies prepare and submit radiological emergency response
plans to FEMA for review and approval. FEMA continuously monitors preparedness through
review of submissions by State and local governments and evaluation of periodic exercises.
Every 2 years, each nuclear power plant site holds a major “joint exercise” that includes power
plant personnel and offsite authorities.61 FEMA and the NRC evaluate these exercises, and their
findings become part of the regulatory process that allows operation of the power plant. Since
1981, hundreds of joint REP exercises have been held.

The parallels between the REP program and CSEPP were considerable. Both programs involved
preparedness for an incident at a large, fixed facility that presented a potentially far-reaching
hazard to the surrounding community. While officials believed that the probability of a major
hazardous event was low, preparation activities were considered worth the effort because of the
possible scale and unusual nature of the hazard. In addition, not only was the public unfamiliar
with the hazard (compared with, for example, hazardous weather conditions that occur

59
 This code refers to Army command staff positions; the G indicates a high level (General Officer) and the
 numbers represent subject matter areas such as logistics or operations.
60
 Headquarters, Department of the Army, 2022, “Army Regulation 50-6, Nuclear and Chemical Weapons and
 Materiel, Chemical Surety,” Nov. 13, available at https://milreg.com/File.aspx?id=279, accessed January 2024.
61
 FEMA, 2023, Program Manual, Radiological Emergency Preparedness, December 2023. available at
 https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/documents/fema_npd-rpm-2023.pdf, accessed September 2024.

 31

periodically), but it was also out of the ordinary experience of most emergency response
agencies. Both programs involved the following particular needs: special instruments to measure
the extent and severity of the hazard, special PPE for responders, special medical treatments and
precautions for victims, and a specialized vocabulary for understanding and communicating the
hazard.

As noted in Section 2.1.2, the conceptual framework for off-post preparedness in CSEPP was
largely based on REP experience. Many of the basic elements — geographic zone-based
participation, development of expedited public warning capabilities, pre-scripted protective
action options, specialized medical support, emergency worker protection, joint exercises, and
public education — were developed based on REP precedent, and many aspects of the early
planning guidance and exercise practices were borrowed from REP.

Both programs involved coordination between a facility and the surrounding community and
between FEMA and another Federal agency. In the REP program, the power plants are generally
owned by private utilities. NRC licenses and extensively regulates the plants to ensure safe
operation, coordinating with FEMA on evaluation of emergency preparedness. NRC evaluates
emergency preparedness at the power plant itself (onsite preparedness) and FEMA evaluates
preparedness by the surrounding community (offsite preparedness). NRC is also a repository for
technical expertise about nuclear power plants and the hazards they may pose. In CSEPP, the
Army played a similar role, monitoring on-post preparedness, coordinating with FEMA, and
serving as a repository of technical expertise about chemical weapons.

Both the REP and CSEP programs developed extensive guidance, training, and exercise
protocols. Through various reorganizations, the FEMA HQ offices in charge of the two programs
remained close on the organization chart. Some of FEMA’s CSEPP staff at the HQ and regional
level were transfers from the REP program, and many of the staff of major support contractors
also had backgrounds in REP.

 32

 3 Organization of the Program and Agency Roles

One of the main challenges — which ultimately became one of the main strengths — of CSEPP
was the requirement for multiple jurisdictions and agencies to work together. This chapter
describes the roles of the Army, FEMA, other Federal agencies, and non-Federal (State, Tribal,
and local) agencies in the program.

3.1 Army and FEMA Roles

3.1.1 Army

3.1.1.1 Headquarters Level

Prior to 2013, the Army CSEPP resided within the U.S. Army Chemical Materials Agency
(CMA), a major subordinate command of AMC. CMA was formed by combining elements from
the former U.S. Army Soldier and Biological Chemical Command (SBCCOM) and PMCD to
consolidate the Army’s chemical storage and demilitarization functions under a single
organization. Safe storage and disposal of the Army’s stockpile of lethal chemical weapons was
a principal mission of CMA. The CSEPP Office reported to the CMA Director of Stockpile
Operations.

The CMA CSEPP Office missions included the following:
 • Providing support and oversight of chemical emergency preparedness at the chemical
 installations and in nearby communities. This mission was accomplished by providing
 direct technical support from CMA CSEPP staff; contractor support; and funding for
 CSEPP-specific personnel, equipment, and operating expenses.
 • Providing oversight of CSEPP training and exercises on the Army side. This part of the
 mission involved providing training to Army CSEPP personnel, co-directing CSEPP
 exercises, and evaluating Army performance at CSEPP exercises.
 • Coordinating with FEMA on all aspects of the program, including:
 o Analysis of funding requests and provision of funds for community preparedness;
 o Program policy;
 o Execution of exercise programs; and
 o Execution of meetings and workshops.

In 2012, as the demilitarization process was completed at the non-ACWA sites, CMA was
redefined as the Chemical Materials Activity, an AMC Separate Reporting Activity. In 2016,
CMA moved in the Army organization scheme to become a part of the Joint Munitions
Command.

 33

3.1.1.2 Installation Level

The Army maintains emergency response resources on its installations; in a chemical emergency
at an Army installation, those resources would be the primary ones to provide damage
assessment, rescue, firefighting, and HAZMAT containment. Specially trained and equipped
Army crews were maintained for this purpose; local off-post police, fire, and emergency medical
services (EMS) personnel would not have been requested to respond to the chemical limited
area.

These capabilities predated CSEPP but were expanded once CSEPP began. Installation
participation in CSEPP involved the addition of staff, equipment, and facilities; development of
more detailed plans; and greatly increased communication with the off-post community. The
sequence of activities at ANAD is illustrative.

Staffing and Training

ANAD entered the CSEP Program with a small staff that was considered adequate for the
CAIRA mission at that time. The organization consisted of a Chemical Surety Officer, a PRP
specialist, a secretary, and a part-time military staff member who also had other duties on the
depot. In the early 1990s, a CSEPP Coordinator was hired, and an Argonne staff member was
provided for onsite support. During the 1990s and into the early 2000s, it became clear that
additional staff would be needed to fulfill the installation’s increasing responsibilities, including
24-hour staffing at the EOC and technical staff to operate the hazard analysis system and provide
notification to off-post populations. Ten EOC specialists were hired in the 1996–1998 timeframe.
In 2003, an additional five positions were filled to cover notification requirements for the “Pink
Zone.”62 All positions were trained as Hazard Analysts.

ANAD established the position of Chemical Exercise & Training Officer in 1991 to train the
depot work force on CAI response and to help off-post responders, medical personnel, and
emergency managers become better prepared. The off-post training was conducted in
coordination with a training contractor hired by the Alabama Emergency Management Agency.

CSEPP also highlighted the need for additional public affairs activity. ANAD initially hired
contract support to develop the first Public Affairs Plan and Joint Information Center (JIC) plan.

Facilities and Equipment

At the beginning of the program, emergency response coordination staff operated out of a small
EOC in the depot HQ building. As the program matured, the EOC was moved to a larger space
in building 363 and staff acquired additional equipment and capabilities, including telephones,
radios, a CSEPP hotline, computers and software to support hazard analysis, on-post siren and
tone alert radio (TAR) activation, and communications with off-post officials. Just before demil
operations began, as part of the pink zone MOA, CSEPP staff added equipment to allow initial

62
 The Pink Zone refers to a subset of the Anniston IRZ, consisting of close-in areas where warning time would be
 minimal in the event of an emergency.

 34

activation of pink zone sirens and TARs. The ANCA EOC was also given an emergency alert
system (EAS) encoder to allow it to initiate an EAS message for the pink zone.

A separate facility was designated for use as a turnkey JIC and provided with communications
and presentation equipment and limited equipment for use by the media. The JIC facility was
located in nearby Fort McClellan, just outside of the IRZ in a location considered unlikely to be
affected by a chemical agent release.

ANAD updated meteorological monitoring equipment and added towers, including three towers
on-post and two on Pelham Range, a training area located just north of the Depot. The tower
instruments measured wind speed, wind direction, temperature, atmospheric pressure, and
humidity. The data were measured at two distinct levels, 10 meters (m) and 30 m above the
ground, and fed directly into the hazard analysis model, along with other local data and weather
forecast information. Armed with these data, the model was continuously ready to produce an
estimate of the area potentially affected by a release.

Planning and Coordination

With the advent of CSEPP, the Depot’s first task was an outreach program to educate the
community and first responders about the chemical agents, their effects, how to treat exposures,
and ANAD’s response capabilities. Initially, much of the information regarding the types and
quantities of the agent stored was still classified and remained so until the information was
released through the chemical weapons treaty process. Thus ANAD personnel could not be too
specific in their initial briefings to the off-post agencies. The information that could be shared
was provided to fire departments, law enforcement, elected officials, medical personnel, and
church and other civic organizations. This outreach was part of the long, slow process of
building trust between ANAD and the community.

CSEPP also generated some changes to the overall Army surety mission, which were reflected in
changes to AR 50-6 and the publication of DA Pamphlet 50-6. These changes, in turn, resulted in
major revisions to the installation’s CAIRA Plan to reflect CSEPP’s role.

During this same early phase, ANAD personnel began working with local and State emergency
management agency (EMA) offices on development of coordinated plans and procedures for
notification and response in the event of an accident on the Depot. The Army and its civilian
counterparts had different ways of operating, which at first led to some frustration. For example,
there was no common terminology when the process began. Despite these obstacles, by the time
SRFX-93 was held, initial plans and procedures were in place for the Army and the community
to operate together during the exercise.

Between 1994 and 2000, considerable time and effort went into defining procedures and
protocols for making Protective Action Recommendations (PARs) and Protective Action
Decisions (PADs). Although software to predict the path of the chemical agent plume was
improving, its results were not tied to a system for making PARs and PADs. During this period,
on- and off-post experts formed the Alabama Hazard Analysis Work Group (HAWG) to
coordinate this work. Out of this group came two workable (but sometimes time-consuming)

 35

manual methods. One was the Alabama Reference Number System book, which provided a PAR
based on plume direction and distance. Alabama also developed a system for hazard analysts to
determine whether it was safe for first responders to rescue people before the plume arrived and
provided safe, time-dependent routes for responders to do so.63

The Alabama CSEPP IPT was established in 1997. Although this group had an outside
moderator, the early years of the IPT proved contentious. By 2000, as the IPT formed working
IPTs (WIPTs), a better working relationship was established, with plant construction coming to
completion and demil operations looming closer. Two key working groups for the installation
were the HAWG, mentioned above, and the Automation WIPT.

Because Hazard Analysis and Automation were closely related, the HAWG and Automation
WIPT often met together to propose a single solution to the IPT. ANCA took on the de facto
responsibility of assessing each new automation system or improvement and, along with the off-
post EMAs, evaluating the software for its utility in making an acceptance decision. As
automation and communication systems were upgraded and became more complex, the full-time
staff spent more time training part-time responders to the EOC in the use of the systems. Staff
often conducted mini-exercises with the off-post EMAs to ensure proper operation of new
software and systems and develop procedural changes to incorporate into the new systems.

Public information and education were other key elements of the installation’s CSEPP during the
latter part of the 1990s until the beginning of plant operations in 2003. On post, the CSEPP
Public Affairs Officer (PAO) provided information to Depot personnel about the nature and
effects of the agents and how to take protective action in an emergency. The PAO also provided
general information about chemical agent effects to the off-post community, but off-post Public
Information Officers (PIOs) were responsible for educating the public on how to protect
themselves.64

As demil operations began, installation staff worked with off-post authorities through the
Alabama CSEPP IPT to solve problems or upgrade capabilities in communications, public
information, recovery planning, and other topics. This process worked well during the demil
sustainment years from 2003 to 2009. Around 2009, attention became focused on program
closeout, including what the legacy of CSEPP would be, and how to close out the funding with
the least impact after 20 years of successful operations.

3.1.2 FEMA’s Role

At FEMA, CSEPP resided within the Technological Hazards Division (THD) of the National
Preparedness Directorate (NPD) within the FEMA Resilience office. The THD encompassed two
main programs: the CSEP and the REP Programs (see CSEPP Antecedents in Section 2.6). The

63
 The formation and efforts of the HAWG are detailed in the “Lesson Learned on Hazard Analysis Working
 Group” in Volume 2 of this document.
64
 As a matter of terminology: emergency response officials with specific responsibility for communications with
 the public and media are generally referred to as PIOs in the civilian community and PAOs in the Federal
 government, including the Army.

 36

THD organization scheme endured through multiple FEMA reorganizations, including FEMA’s
consolidation into the U.S. Department of Homeland Security (DHS) in March 2003.65

FEMA took the lead in assisting, promoting, and evaluating the preparedness of the off-post
CSEPP communities. FEMA’s role was executed through a national office within THD and
through personnel in each affected FEMA Region. Participating FEMA Regions originally
included Regions III, IV, V, VI, VIII, and X; for the last dozen-plus years of the program, FEMA
Regions IV and VIII remained active, serving the Pueblo and Blue Grass CSEPP communities,
respectively.

FEMA’s CSEPP missions included the following:
 • Providing support and oversight for chemical emergency preparedness of State, Tribal,
 and local governments in CSEPP communities. FEMA CSEPP staff provided direct
 technical support; contractor support; and funding for CSEPP-specific personnel,
 equipment, and operating expenses.
 • Administering grant funding to State and Tribal governments for CSEPP. Money was
 transferred from the Army to FEMA for pass-through to off-post CSEPP communities;
 FEMA managed the process by which off-post communities applied for and received
 funding and monitored the progress of funded activities.
 • Developing and implementing appropriate training for off-post responders.
 • Co-directing and evaluating CSEPP exercises on the off-post side.
 • Coordinating with the Army on all aspects of the program, including budgeting and
 funding, program policy and strategic planning, execution of training and exercises, and
 coordination of meetings and workshops.

3.1.3 Army-FEMA MOU

FEMA’s and the Army’s respective roles in the program were defined through a series of
interagency MOUs. The Army-FEMA CSEPP MOU was first signed in 1988 and was updated in
1993, 1997, and 2004. The 2004 MOU described the agency roles (similar to the descriptions
above) and defined the following areas of cooperation to which both agencies committed:
 1. Jointly developing a readiness posture at the stockpile storage installations and in
 the surrounding communities on the basis of assessments, validated requirements,
 and available resources at the earliest practicable date.

3.2 Other Federal Agency Roles
Other Federal agencies played a role in the CSEPP program directly or indirectly. Three agencies
in particular developed standards or guidelines that directly affected how CSEPP operated: the
Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), the EPA, and OSHA. Numerous Federal
agencies participated in SRFXs both before and after the SRFXs were joined with CSEPP
exercises.

3.2.1 CDC

CDC had a longstanding tasking to review and oversee DA plans to dispose of or transport
chemical weapons, established by Federal law.68 In carrying out this responsibility, CDC issued
guidelines in 1995 on medical preparedness for communities near chemical installations.69 The
guidelines were developed in cooperation with a working group of recognized experts in the
fields of emergency medicine, disaster preparedness, nursing, chemical stockpile emergency
preparedness, and pre-hospital emergency medical systems. The guidelines touched on the
following, among other things:

67
 DA/DHS, 2008, CSEPP Programmatic Guidance, June, p. 13.
68
 U.S. Congress, 1970, Public Laws 91–121 and 91–441, Armed Forces Appropriation Authorization of 1970 and
 1971. This mandate was included in PL 99–145 also.
69
 CDC, 1995, CDC Recommendations for Civilian Communities Near Chemical Weapons Depots: Guidelines for
 Medical Preparedness, 60 Federal Register 33308 (June 27).

 39

 • Integration of medical preparedness planning;
 • Training of medical responders and other first responders;
 • PPE for emergency responders;
 • Protective actions for the public in the event of a chemical agent release; and
 • Pre-hospital, medical transport, and hospital treatment of contaminated or potentially
 contaminated patients.

3.2.2 EPA

The EPA’s Office of Prevention, Pesticides, and Toxic Substances established the National
Advisory Committee for Acute Exposure Guideline Levels (AEGLs) for Hazardous Substances
to develop guidelines for short-term exposure to hazardous substances. As stated by the
committee,

 The primary purpose of the AEGL Committee is to develop guideline levels for
 once-in-a-lifetime, short-term (not repeated chronic) exposures to airborne
 concentrations of acutely toxic, high-priority chemicals. AEGLs are needed for a
 wide range of applications in chemical emergency planning, prevention, and
 response programs.70

The AEGLs are defined at three levels — AEGL-1 through AEGL-3 — ranging from “minor
discomfort” to “life threatening.” The effects of exposure depend on air concentrations and
duration of exposure. The AEGLs are expressed in terms of air concentrations in milligrams per
cubic meter (mg/m3) for exposure times ranging from 10 minutes to 8 hours. AEGLs for the
nerve agents were proposed in May 2001.71 The final AEGLs for nerve agents and sulfur
mustard were published in 2003 in the EPA’s Acute Exposure Guideline Levels for Selected
Airborne Chemicals, Volume 3.72

The Army’s adoption of AEGLs for chemical agents led to changes in the zone concept for
protective action planning, for example the adoption of priority “pink zones,” in Alabama, and
changes to the PAZ and addition of host counties in Kentucky. (See Section 2.1.2.1 above for
discussion of the zone-based protective action planning concept.)

3.2.3 OSHA

OSHA establishes regulations for workplace safety, including rules for when PPE is needed and
what types may be used. OSHA rules governing emergency workers who may be exposed to
toxic materials are found in the Hazardous Waste Operations (often shortened to

70
 EPA, AEGL website, available at https://www.epa.gov/aegl/access-acute-exposure-guideline-levels-aegls-
 values, accessed June 2025.
71
 National Advisory Committee for AEGLs for Hazardous Substances, 2021, Proposed AEGL Values, 66 Fed.
     Reg. 21940 (May 2).
72
 EPA, 2003, Acute Exposure Guideline Levels for Selected Airborne Chemicals, Volume 3, available through the
 National Academy at https://nap.nationalacademies.org/catalog/10672/acute-exposure-guideline-levels-for-
 selected-airborne-chemicals-volume-3, accessed January 2024

 40

“HAZWOPER”) rule (29 CFR 1910.120). The interpretation of this and other related OSHA
rules in the context of workers responding to a chemical event was the subject of considerable
discussion in CSEPP. In the mid-1990s, OSHA contributed to the discussion through letters
interpreting the HAZWOPER regulations.

A letter dated July 6, 1995, from Joseph A. Dear, OSHA Assistant Secretary for Labor, written
in response to a request by Lewis D. Walker, Deputy Assistant Secretary of the Army (DASA)
for Installations, Logistics and the Environment, addressed recommended PPE for off-post
emergency workers. This letter presented OSHA’s views regarding the Army’s recommendation
for PPE to be used by local responders from communities near the TEAD in Utah. The letter
stated that certain PPE ensembles identified in Walker’s request would meet OSHA
HAZWOPER requirements provided they were not used in (1) areas where concentrations were
unknown or (2) areas where concentrations were known to be immediately dangerous to life and
health (IDLH). Other OSHA letters regarding CSEPP addressed use of powered air-purifying
respirators (PAPRs), use of plume dispersion modeling to identify hazard areas, air monitoring
requirements, and operations in IDLH areas.73

3.3 Role of State, Tribal, and Local Governments
America’s constitutional foundation of federalism places significant trust and responsibility in
the hands of State and local governments to protect the American people. In accordance with this
principle, the CSEPP Strategic Plan was built upon the foundation that State, local, and Tribal
governments were in the best position to understand their communities and provide first response
to a chemical emergency.74 State, local, and Tribal governments develop and implement plans to
protect the public from all hazards, including hazardous materials from any source.

Local agencies — such as county or municipal law enforcement, fire, EMS, HAZMAT teams,
and public works — are generally the first to respond to any type of emergency because they are
closest to the problem. If local resources are overwhelmed, the town or county may request
assistance from neighboring local jurisdictions and from the State. The State maintains a variety
of emergency response resources such as an EMA, law enforcement (State police), and public
health organizations. The State also typically has some specialized resources that may not exist
at the local level, such as environmental sampling and analysis capabilities. In addition, the
governor may activate local National Guard units to assist with response.

State and local emergency response roles and authorities are defined by State law. State
emergency management statutes generally specify that the governor and, at the local level, the
top county executive may exercise particular emergency powers, including the power to declare
a State or local “state of emergency.” A state of emergency declaration empowers the declaring
authority to implement protective actions and generally allows the authority to implement special
powers (e.g., declare a curfew) and to bypass normal procedures and protocol for taking action

73
 Most of these letters came in response to correspondence with Cheri Bandy Faust of ORNL; see Faust, 1999,
 How Do I Know? A Guide to the Selection of Personal Protective Equipment for Use in Responding to a Release
 of Chemical Warfare Agents, ORNL-TM-13343.
74
 CSEPP Strategic Plan, 2004.

 41

(e.g., normal procurement rules for equipment and supplies). The exact contours of these special
powers and who may wield them varies from state to state.

In addition, Federal law (Stafford Act) assigns to State governors the role of requesting Federal
assistance. Acting on such a request, the president may declare an emergency or disaster,
activating Federal disaster relief programs to assist affected individuals, businesses, and local
governments.75

In a chemical emergency, local authorities would have had primary responsibility for taking
immediate measures to protect the nearby off-post population, including public warning,
protective action instructions, blocking access to the potential hazard area, managing evacuation,
and providing initial reception and shelter for evacuees. The state’s role would likely be to assist
with personnel, equipment, and resources to support local functions; provide emergency powers
and authorities through an emergency declaration; request a presidential declaration under the
Stafford Act; and provide other state resources for managing long-term protective actions and
recovery processes, if needed.

Forty counties, ten states, and one Tribal nation76 participated in CSEPP.77 Table 3.1 lists them.
Thirteen of the counties included areas within 10 miles of the chemical stockpile locations. Each
of these jurisdictions developed and maintained a plan pertaining to chemical emergency
response. The close-in counties received support for facility upgrades and CSEPP-dedicated
staffing. The further-out counties also received support for activities depending on their
particular response roles — as potential host counties, evacuation, or medical support, and so on
— as well as for participation in training and exercises. In some cases, the further-out counties
also received support for facilities, equipment, and personnel.

75
 The president also may declare an emergency and provide Federal assistance independently of any request, if “an
 emergency exists for which the primary responsibility for response rests with the United States . . .” 42 USC
     Section 5191(b).
76

 within the Umatilla PAZ; however, the IRZ and PAZ did encompass some officially recognized Tribal sacred
 sites and river-rights areas.

 4 Program Management and Budgeting

The number of agencies involved in CSEPP meant that program priorities, policies, and budgets
had to be negotiated among multiple parties. All parties generally agreed that an effective
program was needed to protect the public and that the sooner the stockpile was eliminated, the
better. But each jurisdiction and agency brought its own culture and agenda to the table. It took
years for the program to reach an equilibrium in which all sides had similar expectations and
general agreement on their respective roles. This chapter traces the evolution of interagency
program coordination and budgeting mechanisms throughout the program.

4.1 Program Management
No one could possibly have envisioned CSEPP as a multi-billion-dollar undertaking78 at the
signing of the first Army/FEMA MOU in 1988. The Army was charged with disposal of the
chemical stockpile. The State, Tribal, and local governments had responsibility for the safety and
welfare of their residents and control over environmental permitting for the disposal facilities.
This diverse group of participants had to interpret and define the program mandate for
“maximum protection” and build effective working relationships. Management processes
evolved to alleviate the natural tensions and build trust among local, State, and Federal partners,
while blending the command-and-control orientation of the Army with the cooperative perspective
required of FEMA in working with State, Tribal, and local governments.

In the earliest days, from approximately 1989 through 1992, CSEPP was managed by a Steering
Committee co-chaired by the Army and FEMA. Although the Steering Committee brought together
a broad representation of local, State, and Federal stakeholders, its size (more than 50 members)
hampered deliberation and decision-making on important programmatic matters. The Steering
Committee established subcommittees to address different functional areas.79 Even supported by its
subcommittees, the Steering Committee was not able to resolve many issues. As one observer
noted,

 FEMA could not provide timely budgeting information required for DA to request
 appropriations for the program. Technical issues were next to impossible to resolve...
 achieving agreement on programmatic policy was difficult at best. State and local
 communities complained that their views and concerns were not being addressed.
 Little, if any, information returned to the storage communities.80

In reaction to Congressional direction, the Army and FEMA began to design a new management
structure. The new structure was outlined in a Joint Memorandum for the Record (JMFR),
Restructuring the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, dated February 24, 1994.

78
 Just over $2.8 billion was awarded for CSEPP through FY 2026.
79
 The 1990 CSEPP Management Plan established the following Subcommittees, each of which were co-chaired by
 the Army and FEMA: Planning (also the CSEPP Steering Committee), Re-entry/Restoration, Training, Exercise,
 Public Affairs, Automated Emergency Management, and Simulation Modeling.
80
 Hewett, P., 2011, Communication from Argonne National Laboratory.

 45

The JMFR eliminated the Steering Committee and subcommittee management system in favor of a
CSEPP Core Team consisting of both Army and FEMA personnel.

In December 1994, the Chemical Stockpile Disposal Program was designated a DOD Acquisition
Category I Acquisition Program, which moved all of its activities under the PMCD, including
CSEPP. As a result of this consolidation, the CSEPP Core Team was placed under the Project
Manager for Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness (PM, CSEP) and renamed the CSEP
Office. The CSEP Office provided both the Army and FEMA the staff required to manage the
program at the national level. Previously, management in both organizations was essentially a one-
person operation. Because of the additional staff and focused effort, the CSEPP Core Team/Office
was able to break the log jam of delayed projects and technical guidance and bring financial control
to the project.

4.1.1 Integrated Product/Process Teams

The Defense Acquisition Program had successfully used IPTs to reduce delivery times, obtain early
stakeholder involvement, and thereby cut or contain costs. The IPT approach was applied to CSEPP
beginning with an overarching joint Army-FEMA IPT, which then formed a Financial IPT in 1995
to coordinate program funding. Use of IPTs was expanded after Congress mandated the approach in
the Defense Authorization Act for FY 1997, which directed the Secretary of the Army to submit a
report within 120 days on implementation of site-specific IPTs as a CSEPP management tool. In a
section entitled “Contingent Mandated Reforms,” the statute further specified that if the Army and
FEMA were unsuccessful in implementing the IPTs, the Army was to “assume full control and
responsibility for the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (eliminating the role of
the Director of the Federal Emergency Management Agency as joint manager of the program)...”81

A new Army/FEMA JMFR established a comprehensive system of IPTs for managing the CSEP
Program in May 1998,82 stating that, “[t]he parties agree that IPTs will serve as a central
management tool within the program for planning and issue discussion, and agree to utilize IPTs to
the maximum extent possible subject to Federal regulations and policy-making limitations.” The
JMFR did not establish particular IPTs but outlined guidelines for establishing site-specific IPTs,
functional IPTs, and a national IPT.83

IPTs were established at each site to facilitate local, State, and Federal communication and
coordination. In addition, the Army/FEMA established national-level functional IPTs on the
following topics:84

81
 PL 104–201, National Defense Authorization Act for FY 1997 (1996), Section 1076.
82
 ARMY/FEMA Joint Memorandum for the Record, 1998, Subject: Use of IPTs as a management tool within the
 CSEPP.

 For details on CSEPP’s implementation of the IPT process, see the “Lesson Learned on Use of IPTs for
 Interagency Program Management,” in Volume 2 of this document.

 The list is from Kunz and Company, Inc., 2005, CSEPP’s Teams: Best Practices and Lessons Learned,

 46

 • Automation. Co-chaired by county/State and Army depot representatives; identified and
 investigated automated tools and supported the development and implementation of
 WebPuff, the CSEPP hazard analysis and response tool.
 • Exercise. Co-chaired by Army and FEMA representatives; designed an exercise program to
 raise the level of exercise activity, used integrated performance indicators to support site
 readiness assessments, provided feedback, and continued to build response capabilities.
 • Medical. Led by State and county co-chairs; consolidated several closely related medical
 teams and groups to develop and integrate medical planning and operations guidance and
 conduct medical workshops and training of trainers.
 • Public Affairs. Co-chaired by Army and FEMA public affairs specialists and public affairs
 representatives from all of the CSEPP sites; tasked with ensuring that the public could and
 would act appropriately upon notification of an emergency at a chemical installation through
 a comprehensive communications strategy.
 • Protective Actions. Led by an interim FEMA chair; created to translate the findings of the
 Shelter in Place Working Group into CSEPP guidance and to broaden the perspective
 beyond SIP to include all protective actions.
 • Program Management Team/Overarching IPT. Jointly led by the Army Chief, Chemical
 Stockpile Emergency Preparedness and the FEMA CSEPP Branch Chief, this IPT worked
 on programmatic planning and budgeting policy and processes. It was responsible for
 overseeing the preparation of the CSEPP strategic plan, programmatic planning guidance,
 and the establishment of programmatic benchmarks.

A 2005 report commissioned by DHS/FEMA and the Army found that the IPTs were an effective
management system:

 CSEPP’s national teams differ significantly in structure, purpose, representation,
 and output. Taken together, however, the IPTs are important at the program,
 community, and site levels. IPTs are significant at professional and individual
 levels as well. CSEPP’s teams have provided common solutions and consistency
 of approach across the eight sites, and they have been the platform for problem
 and solution sharing among otherwise disparate and independent jurisdictions.
 The IPTs have served both as communities of practice and of functional
 integration, providing opportunities for professional expansion and growth. They
 have also produced products of immediate value not only to the CSEPP
 community, but to the broader emergency preparedness field as well, at a point in
 time when models of inter-jurisdictional collaboration, and the products and tools
 they create, are increasingly appreciated in the comprehensive homeland security
 environment.85

85
 Ibid., p. 6

 47

4.1.2 GAO Reports

Evolution of the CSEPP management process was also impacted by organizations outside the
installation communities. Every year from 1993 through 1997, and then again in 1999 and 2001,
the GAO issued a report criticizing some aspect of the CSEP Program. In 2002 and 2004, the
GAO recognized progress and improvement in the program, and some subsequent reports
referred to aspects of CSEPP that might serve as examples or lessons for other programs. The
findings of these reports are summarized below.

Management mechanisms and financial controls were GAO’s primary concerns in the first few
studies. In the summer of 1993, GAO officials before the House Subcommittee on Environment,
Energy and Natural Resources Committee gave the following testimony:

 Even though CSEPP has used $187 million in funding, the program has made
 little progress in achieving its main objective of helping communities prepare for
 emergencies involving chemical agent release. The lack of progress is partly
 because of program management weaknesses such as fragmented authorities and
 responsibilities and weak controls over funds, leading to missed program
 milestones and delays in issuing guidance. As a result, communities near the eight
 chemical warfare agent storage sites are not prepared to respond to a chemical
 emergency. While participating communities have developed emergency response
 plans and participated in major emergency response exercises, they lack several
 important capabilities.86

GAO reported continuing concerns 18 months later:

 [CSEPP’s] management is complicated by the need to work with various State,
 local, and Federal agency officials. However, the Army’s overall management
 approach has not been effective. Specifically, its approach of sharing management
 responsibility for various activities such as training has resulted in unclear
 responsibility, uncoordinated activities, and at least until recently weak controls
 over funds. The conditions have contributed to delays in achieving program
 objectives.

 Communities are unable to complete their plans and preparations for responding
 to a chemical emergency because CSEPP has not completed all the guidance and
 standards they need. The remaining portions of this guidance will not be issued
 until the Army completes an analysis of the risk of chemical agent deposition
 beyond Army installation boundaries. The analysis has taken more than 2 years
 and is only now approaching completion. Guidance for 4 of CSEPP’S 13 planning
 standards is on hold awaiting the outcome of the study.87

86
 U.S. General Accounting Office (GAO), 1993, Chemical Weapons Storage: Communities Are Not Prepared to
 Respond to Emergencies, GAO/T-NSIAD-93-18, July 16.
87
 GAO, 1994, Chemical Weapons Stockpile: Army’s Emergency Preparedness Program Has Been Slow to
 Achieve Results, GAO/NSIAD-94-91, February.

 48

The latter (1994) GAO report recommended that the Army determine whether existing
milestones were realistic and establish new baselines for each initiative. GAO also recommended
that the Secretary change CSEPP’s committee structure to establish a single focal point of
accountability within the Army to implement the program and to coordinate with other agencies
as needed. This finding became a major stimulus for the reorganization of CSEPP’s management
processes.

Financial controls were next on GAO’s list. A 1995 report88 criticized the tracking of funds and
accountability for funds in relation to the program goals. The report noted that, in some cases,
funds had not been promptly expended and the purpose for which the funds were allocated — for
example, purchase of communications equipment — was unfulfilled.

The report also expressed concerns that FEMA’s planned changes to the cooperative agreement
(CA) process would make tracking of CSEPP funds even more difficult. FEMA planned to
eliminate program-designated funding and replace it with functionally designated funding across
programs. The GAO recommended tightening controls and keeping CSEPP money separate from
other funding:

 We recommend that the Secretary of the Army (1) consolidate financial as well as
 programmatic functions at the new joint CSEPP Office in the Chemical and
 Biological Defense Command and (2) strengthen the new office’s procedures for
 financial reporting and accountability, including requiring regions, States, and
 counties to report financial data consistent with the format used to award
 allocations.

 We recommend that the Director of FEMA move CSEPP from the agency’s
 comprehensive cooperative agreements umbrella and administer the program
 separately, not commingled with other agency programs.89

A 1997 report90 noted continuing difficulties with restructuring of the program and specifically
with implementation of the IPT concept. GAO’s impatience was evident in the report’s
background summary:

 As we have reported since 1992, CSEPP’s slow progress has been due largely to
 long-standing management weaknesses, including disagreement between the
 Army and FEMA over their respective roles and responsibilities. The FEMA
 Inspector General, Members of Congress, and State and local officials have also
 expressed concern about these management weaknesses.

The report documented what appeared to be the low point in Army/FEMA relations. Each
agency proposed that the other have a reduced role:

88
 GAO, 1995, Chemical Weapons: Army’s Emergency Preparedness Program Has Financial Management
 Weaknesses, GAO/NSIAD-95-94, March.

 GAO/NSIAD, 1997, Chemical Weapons Stockpile: Changes Needed in the Management of the Emergency
 Preparedness Program, GAO/NSIAD-97-91, June 11.

 49

 In September 1996, FEMA’s Deputy Associate Director for Preparedness,
 Training, and Exercises reported that the Army and FEMA had vastly different
 management styles and philosophies and that the current approach was not
 working. She concluded that attempts to combine Army and FEMA approaches in
 developing off-post preparedness capabilities have resulted in delays and
 conflicting messages to participating states. Additional FEMA correspondence
 indicates that the agency continues to want to manage all off-post activities with
 little or no Army involvement.

 In October 1996, the Army Program Manager for Chemical Demilitarization
 agreed that Army’s and FEMA’s management styles were different and added
 that relationships were strained and leadership was less effective than desired. The
 Program Manager reported that maintaining the current management structure
 would continue regional and State confusion over the program’s leadership and
 prolong the program’s problems. He concluded that FEMA’s participation in
 CSEPP was preferred but suggested that FEMA’s role and personnel involved in
 the program be reduced...91

GAO recommended that the differences be settled at the executive level and in accordance with
Congress’s direction to implement site-specific IPTs:

 [W]e recommend that the Secretary of the Army and the Director of FEMA work
 together to complete the mandated assessment of the implementation and success
 of integrated process teams by July 15, 1997. We also recommend that, as part of
 this assessment, the Secretary, and the Director reach agreement on a long-term
 management structure for CSEPP that clearly defines the roles and responsibilities
 of Army and FEMA personnel.92

This advice was taken, and the results are described in Section 4.2.

In 2001, GAO examined the program once more, this time focusing on the state of readiness at
the program sites. Like many programs, CSEPP cost estimates had ballooned over time and
deadlines for acquisition of critical items had been pushed back. The report noted improvements
since the last review, but much work remaining: “All the locations we visited indicated that their
program has improved since our June 1997 report. In 1997, none of the 10 states had attained all
of the program’s critical items considered necessary for emergency preparedness; now 3 of the
10 states have.”93 GAO expressed concern that slow progress on preparedness might lead to
delays in startup of demilitarization facilities. GAO recommended increased attention to
guidance, training, and technical assistance to expedite State and local preparedness efforts,
especially in the “three states with long-standing issues...” (The report called out Alabama,
Indiana, and Kentucky as lacking critical plans, equipment, and staffing.) Reentry guidance was
specifically mentioned as an area needing attention.

This report noted the inequality in funding among CSEPP states (Figure 4.1), but did not critique

Two other reports by the GAO during this period focused on specific sites. A 1996 report

Washington.95 The latter report noted progress on issues such as sirens, communications
systems, and over-pressurization systems but also pointed to unfinished items, such as
distribution of tone-alert radios and SIP kits.

practices on an Internet site, shares lessons at national meetings, and does coordinate with the
Lessons Learned Program, especially for outreach and public relations efforts.” 96 This report
also noted that earlier issues had been addressed: “In August 2001, we reported that the
Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program did not share its lessons effectively.
During this review, the program’s management demonstrated steps that had been taken to
address this issue.”97

A 2001 GAO report on terrorism preparedness cited the roles of CSEPP and REP in advancing
State and local emergency preparedness. The report described aspects of CSEPP, including
interagency cooperation and funding provided for State and local preparedness, training,
guidance, and exercises.

 Many lessons have been learned from the exercises. For instance, FEMA has
 learned that communication between installations and nearby communities has
 improved considerably over the years and that assessing threat and meeting
 notification times for nearby communities has been difficult. The information
 gained from post-exercise reports allows planners to focus exercises on areas
 requiring greater attention. Every exercise evaluation ends with a meeting in
 which exercise evaluators provide immediate feedback to the community. Further,
 a 45-day review and comment period is provided prior to finalization and
 distribution of the exercise report, which consists of a plan negotiated by regional,
 State, and local officials to correct deficiencies and identify responsibility for
 corrective actions. Problems noted during exercises are addressed in future
 planning and training activities.98

A 2004 post-hearing memorandum to Jim Saxton, Chairman of the House’s Subcommittee on
Terrorism, Unconventional Threats and Capabilities, addressed a question from Rep. Mike
Rogers about the Alabama portion of the program. The memo summarized the GAO’s past
assessments of the Alabama program and provided general assurance that previously identified
issues had been or were being resolved.99

Finally in 2006, a GAO report on preparedness for evacuation of transportation-disadvantaged
populations mentioned that lessons learned from the CSEPP and REP programs could be
distributed more widely to assist with preparation for evacuation of transport-disadvantaged
populations. “[F]ederal evacuation studies of, and lessons learned from, the chemical stockpile
and radiological emergency preparedness programs could also help state and local officials
prepare for these populations. . . [for] example, a Department of Energy National Laboratory
study on emergency preparedness in Alabama includes information on how to address the needs
of transportation-disadvantaged populations in evacuations.”100

A 2023 DHS Office of Inspector General report on FEMA’s Technological Hazards Division
found that the CSEP and REP Programs both capably assisted the preparedness efforts of the
affected State and local governments.101 The report cited CSEPP’s Strategic Plan
implementation, updates to guidance documents, use of the Integrated Public Alert and Warning
