4.2 Budgeting Process
As the CSEP Program developed, effective mechanisms were needed to distribute funds to the
many entities involved while preserving accountability and tracking. As with many other aspects
of the program, these mechanisms evolved over time and took shape through a cooperative effort
among jurisdictions at various levels. The first funds were made available for distribution to the
states in 1988; just over $2.8 billion had been awarded through fiscal year 2026.102

4.2.1 Early Phase: Budget Outgrows Coordination Mechanisms

As noted in Section 2, in 1987, the U.S. Army released a Draft Emergency Response Concept
Plan, which presented a basis for development of local emergency response programs and
examined various methods of emergency planning. The Army also prepared a Chemical
Stockpile Disposal Implementation Plan and requested funds to implement enhanced emergency
preparedness on- and off-post for all chemical stockpile sites. FEMA joined the Army in
implementing the CSEPP through an MOU signed in August 1988.

FY 1988 was the first fiscal year in which CSEPP funding was programmed and budgeted as a
separate project in the Chemical Demilitarization Program. Initial cost estimates for CSEPP were
around $114 million for the entire program through FY 1994 (the projected completion date for
the program). The Army funded only initial emergency preparedness studies in FY 1988. In
FY 1989, the Army provided $6.6 million in support for the 10 CSEPP States and FEMA and
$4.6 million for Army organizational support.

In the early years, all CSEPP funding requirements were considered and approved in the Office
of the Deputy for Chemical Demilitarization, Assistant Secretary of the Army for Installations,
Logistics and Environment. Army commands and FEMA managed the program by submitting
requirements for CSEPP funding to the Office of the Deputy for Chemical Demilitarization for
disposition. When that office approved the funding requests, they sent a memo to the Chemical
Demilitarization Resource Management Office at Edgewood, Maryland, requesting that a
Military Interdepartmental Purchase Request (MIPR) be processed to convey the funding to the
receiving organization. To fund CSEPP State and local governments, FEMA — once it received
the MIPR from the Army — would process CAs to convey the funding to the CSEPP States,
which, in turn, would fund State and local government CSEPP requirements, processing the
funding through their respective accounting systems.

102
 Spreadsheet compiled by Argonne National Laboratory from official CSEPP budget document; see Table 4.3.

 53

In the first few years of CSEPP, when the program was relatively small and required relatively
modest funding, managing the program funding in this top-down fashion was feasible, although
neither CSEPP nor the States considered it totally satisfactory. As the program started to expand
rapidly (see Table 4.1), the top-down approach to managing the funding became problematic,
especially for managing off-post funding, where the greatest expansion was occurring.103

 Table 4.1 Early 1990s CSEPP Budgets
 On-Post Funding Off-Post Funding
 Fiscal Year
 ($ million) ($ million)
                           1990               12.2             31.4
                           1991                9.6             27.8
                           1992               11.8             28.4
                           1993               31.2             55.8

For the FY 1994 CSEPP budget, staff from the DA and FEMA HQs formed a Joint Program and
Budget Review Panel to review and approve both installation and State-originated CSEPP
budget requests. A total of $48.1 million was awarded for off-post funding and $22.7 million for
on-post funding in FY 1994.

In 1995, CSEPP budget decisions were moved from the Office of the Deputy for Chemical
Demilitarization in the Pentagon to a central CSEPP office at the U.S. Army Chemical and
Biological Defense Command (CBDCOM) in Edgewood, Maryland. The CBDCOM CSEPP
Office had been established under the 1994 revision of the Army/FEMA Joint Memorandum.
This joint CSEPP office, referred to as the CSEPP Core Team, was established in October 1994
to provide a central focus for CSEPP within the Army, implement the program on Army
installations, and coordinate and integrate on- and off-post activities. Both Army and FEMA
civilian personnel staffed the office, with FEMA HQ personnel splitting their time between the
Edgewood office and FEMA HQ in Washington, D.C. The CSEPP office in CBDCOM had
integration teams and site teams. The integration teams were organized by functional areas
including planning, budget, exercises, training, automation, public affairs,
modeling/meteorology, demilitarization, security liaison, research and development, engineering,
and contracts. There were four site teams, each consisting of an Army and a FEMA
representative, assigned to the eight program locations. The Director of the CSEPP Core Team
was an Army employee, and the Deputy Director was a FEMA employee. The integration and
site teams made budget recommendations for their respective functions and sites. The Director,
assisted by the Deputy Director, made the final award decisions and was responsible to the
PMCD for managing CSEPP funding.

103
 Processing of on-post funding for CSEPP installation support and other Army organizational support was
 relatively stable and non-contentious compared with the problems encountered with processing off-post funding.

 54

Congress had mandated “maximum protection for the environment, the general public and the
personnel involved.” The challenge was to balance the translation of that mandate into CSEPP
requirements while containing the cost of the program. The Army and FEMA did not always
agree on which CSEPP off-post requirements were valid, but the Army made the final decisions
about what would be funded. Many CSEPP States began to push back on the denials of some of
their stated requirements. Some CSEPP States would have preferred block grants with minimum
Federal government involvement in their emergency preparedness programs.

Two funding processes came into play in CSEPP budgeting. One was the Army’s Planning,
Programming, Budgeting, and Execution System (PPBES), prescribed by Army Regulation 1-1,
which made up the Army component of the DOD Planning, Programming, Budgeting, and
Execution process. The other funding process was FEMA’s uniform administrative requirements
for grants and CAs to State and local governments, as outlined in 44 CFR Part 13. This process
was replaced in December 2013 by 2 CFR Part 200, Uniform Administrative Requirements, Cost
Principles, and Audit Requirements for Federal Awards, which was updated in 2024 and
renamed Office of Management and Budget (OMB) Guidance for Federal Financial
Assistance.104

The PPBES is a continuous process that results in the annual submission of the DOD budget,
which is included as part of the President’s budget submission to Congress. There are at least
two phases of PPBES ongoing at any given time: the Future Years Defense Program, which
looks ahead 5 years, and the current budget execution year. During the budget execution year,
DOD defines obligation goals and monitors the obligation of funds to ensure that the funds are
used efficiently and that no surplus funds accumulate.

A Federal grant is financial assistance provided by a Federal agency such as FEMA to a State or
local government for a public purpose. A CA is used instead of a grant for the same purpose
when there will be substantial interaction between the Federal agency and the State or local
government. The grant/CA process was executed on an annual basis after funding for the stated
purpose had been appropriated. The process did not usually involve advance planning and
estimating for future years’ funds, requiring only a financial report 30 days after the end of each
quarter for all grants/CAs in which the total amount expended, and the balance remaining, was
reported to FEMA Region Assistance Officers.

To be effective, the CSEPP funding process had to address both DOD and FEMA requirements
while fulfilling the Congressional mandate for maximum protection. Understanding, integrating,
and conforming to each of these requirements was a challenge to Army and FEMA management,
as well as the CSEPP State and local governments.

104
 OMB, Guidance for Federal Financial Assistance, 2024, 89 FR 30046, April 24, available at
 https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/04/22/2024-07496/guidance-for-federal-financial-assistance,
 accessed March 2025.

 55

4.2.2 Application of Benchmarks and Life Cycle Budgeting

As the CSEPP program matured, a process evolved to effectively manage CSEPP funding that
was objective and acceptable to all participants. The initial attempt occurred in 1993 when the
Army and FEMA defined a set of nine national benchmarks to serve as a basis for development
and assessment of CSEPP capabilities at each site. The goal was to achieve full compliance with
each benchmark and to sustain this capability throughout the program. CSEPP Program
Managers tracked the obligation of their CSEPP resources by these national benchmarks.
Managers assessed benchmark capabilities using the principle of “functional equivalency,” that
is, it was not necessary to provide every jurisdiction with identical assets, provided their basic
emergency capabilities met CSEPP guidance.

In addition, FEMA established 16 different CSEPP budget classifications, which included the
benchmarks plus other categories to use in tracking CSEPP funds. FEMA developed
spreadsheets for each State that broke out the funding requests by fiscal year, State agency or
county, functional area, and CSEPP class for each requested item. There were three columns for
funding amounts for each item: “State Requested” amount; “FEMA Recommended” amount;
and “Army/FEMA Award.” Items were categorized as a first, second, or third priority. These
spreadsheets, however, were not universally used by the CSEPP States to develop their annual
budgets. GAO noted this inconsistency, among other things, in two reports released in February
1994 and March 1995 that were critical of the process used to budget and account for funds
awarded to the CSEPP States.105

These were just two of several GAO reports critical of the Chemical Demil Program (see
Section 4.1.2). To impose more rigorous management and cost control, in December 1994 DOD
announced that the Chemical Demil Program was an Acquisition Category I Defense Acquisition
Board Program requiring development of LCCEs for all Chemical Demil projects, including
CSEPP. The LCCE requirement provided an opportunity for CSEPP managers to develop a
standardized approach to managing the CSEPP funding process.

The Director of the CSEPP Core Team initiated the CSEPP LCCE process in early 1995 to
identify requirements for destruction of all chemical weapons at each stockpile site. Argonne was
tasked by the CSEPP Core Team Director to create a “Roadmap Process” to be used by all
CSEPP organizations to build a baseline LCCE for the initial Chemical Demilitarization
Acquisition Program. A representative from the U.S. Army Cost and Economic Analysis Center
(CEAC) observed the process as part of the Army’s required independent validation process. The
CSEPP baseline LCCE for FYs 1996–2004 submitted to the Chemical Demil office included
inputs from States and installations, reviewed and filtered by the CSEPP Core Teams at each
site, along with inputs from the Core Team integration team for support items such as risk
assessment, data automation, medical support, protective actions, exercises, training, and public
affairs. The States and counties developed long-term program and cost plans on the basis of their
current capabilities, experience, jurisdictional requirements, and CSEPP policy and guidance.
However, not all their requests were accepted by the CSEPP Core Team, and some were

105
 GAO/NSIAD, 1994, Chemical Weapon Stockpile: Army's Emergency Preparedness Program Has Been Slow to
 Achieve Results, NSIAD-94-91, February 22; and GAO/NSIAD, 1995, Chemical Weapons: Army's Emergency
 Preparedness Program Has Financial Management Weaknesses, NSIAD-95-94 March 15.

 56

excluded from the CSEPP LCCE. The CSEPP LCCE was updated in 1997 and 1998 without
direct input from the States and counties, heightening the conflict between some States, FEMA,
and the Army about what and how much should be included in the LCCEs.

Also in 1995, FEMA management was proposing to reform the comprehensive CA process by
eliminating program-designated funding and replacing it with functionally designated funding
across all programs. To avoid being included in this process and further reducing the program
managers’ limited ability to relate expenditures to specific program objectives, FEMA CSEPP
management decided to have its own set of guidelines and functional worksheets for preparing
comprehensive CA budget submissions.

FEMA and the Army created a CSEPP Financial IPT to produce these guidelines and functional
worksheets. With the CSEPP State Directors’ concurrence, representatives from FEMA
Headquarters, FEMA Regions, the CSEPP Core Team, the Army CEAC, and some of the
CSEPP States formed the Financial IPT. The IPT was led by Leo Coonradt, the Deputy Director
for the Utah Division of Comprehensive Emergency Management, and met five times between
June 7, 1995, and February 5, 1996. The Financial IPT was a big step forward in building trust
between State and Federal government participants in the CSEPP budgeting process.106 The
guidelines and worksheets developed by the IPT were subsequently approved by Army and
FEMA CSEPP management and the CSEPP State Directors. Argonne was tasked to automate the
guidelines and functional worksheets, and the resultant product became the initial CSEPP CA
software application, CSEPP CA Database Module. The Module was first used for the
submission of the FY 1997 CSEPP CA applications.107

The software allowed States to build their CA requests on worksheets by entering their funding
requirements by line item and explaining in narrative blocks how the line items would help meet
their program objectives. Each line item had a unique, computer-generated identification (ID)
number. The software would group the line items by functional area and provide a summary of
the total funding requested by each county or State agency.

The funding process for off-post activities was initiated at the county or State agency level. The
completed request was passed up to the State for review and approval, on to the FEMA Region
for review and recommendation, then to FEMA HQ and the Army for approval. FEMA HQ and
the Army would approve the amount of funding for each item, and after all the line items were
considered, send the approved budget request back to the FEMA Region and State for
completion of the CA. Narrative comments could be made in the software by the reviewers and
approvers, resulting in a transparent process for all involved parties. State and local governments
could view the status of their requests, which was not an option before. This software evolved
over the next decade as the CSEPP financial process evolved, and become a valuable tool in
managing the CSEPP funding process for the CSEPP State, Tribal, and local governments.

106
 CSEPP’s successful experience with IPTs is further detailed in Lerner, Zadra, and Hewett, Using Integrated
 Process Teams for Interagency Coordination, The Public Manager Vol. 42, No. 4 (Winter 2013) and in the
 “Lesson Learned on Use of IPTs” in Volume 2 of this document.
107
 The process of developing a funding process based on benchmarks and LCCEs is detailed in the “Lesson
 Learned on Distributing Funding to Diverse Sites to Achieve Functionally Equivalent Capabilities,” in Volume 2
 of this document.

 57

Although the initial LCCE process and the Financial IPT involved greater participation by the
CSEPP States than previous CSEPP financial processes, there was still friction between some
counties and States, FEMA, and the Army about funding some of the State and county
requirements. Some States indicated that they would not grant permits for chemical demil to start
until they had acquired the desired capabilities. Within some of the CSEPP States, some counties
believed their requirements were being ignored by the State as well as FEMA, and they worked
to enhance their participation in the funding process deliberations. There was even a
recommendation made within the Army and supported by at least one CSEPP State to manage
the CSEP Program without FEMA. However, the Secretary of the Army did not accept that
recommendation. Instead, he worked out an agreement regarding roles and responsibilities with
the Director of FEMA. This agreement resulted in another revision of the Army-FEMA CSEPP
MOU in October 1997, which cleared the way for a more community-oriented approach to
processing and funding CSEPP off-post requirements. A year later, the agreement was
incorporated into law in the Defense Authorization Act for FY 1999 (PL 105–261 [1998]).

In the revised 1997 MOU, FEMA agreed to assume total authority, responsibility, and
accountability for working with and administering funds to State, Tribal, and local governments
for developing and maintaining their off-post emergency preparedness. The Army agreed to
provide an annual funding amount to FEMA at the beginning of each fiscal year that was based
on the FEMA-validated and -approved off-post portion of the CSEPP LCCE, adjusted for the
actual Congressional appropriation. The CSEPP Core Team, which had been operating since
1994, was replaced with an Army CSEPP Office established within CBDCOM, which became
the Chemical Materials Agency in 2003. This office continued to be located at Edgewood but
was staffed only with Army military and civilian personnel. FEMA personnel who had been part
of the CSEPP Core Team now worked solely at their FEMA HQ Office, which had its CSEPP
operations consolidated into a supporting role. CSEPP operational responsibility and additional
workforce positions were assigned to the FEMA Regions, making the Regions accountable for
the CSEP Program objectives of State, Tribal, and local governments.

Both the Army and FEMA committed to using IPTs as a collaborative approach to decision-
making and problem solving. An Army/FEMA JMFR signed in May 1998 spelled out the use of
IPTs as a management tool within the CSEP Program, including the authority to discuss financial
and budgetary issues and make recommendations on budgetary changes. But, even after FEMA
assumed responsibility for the off-post portion of the program, problems continued regarding the
adequacy of CSEPP funding for several CSEPP States. During FY 2000 budget negotiations,
FEMA was challenged by the State of Oregon, with the backing of some of its Congressional
representatives, to add more requirements to its LCCE.

At a meeting in Washington, D.C., in November 1999, Russ Salter, Director of the FEMA
Chemical and Radiological Preparedness Division, proposed a system for addressing what
Oregon perceived to be deficiencies in its CSEPP capabilities. His solution was simple and
involved using a consensus-building-approach to making budget decisions. He asked the State to
review the CSEPP benchmarks and list the following: (1) what was currently budgeted for each
item meant to fulfill the benchmark; (2) what the estimated cost was to complete the item to
fulfill the benchmark; (3) what the annual estimated cost would be to maintain the existing items
needed to meet each benchmark; and (4) what additional items were needed to fully meet each

 58

benchmark. This approach enabled a more open process for budget negotiations that involved all
the stakeholders, setting a precedent that FEMA decided to use with the remaining CSEPP States
in updating the CSEPP LCCE in 2000. Argonne devised a spreadsheet to record and track these
requirements during budget negotiations, and the spreadsheet continued as the standard format
that all CSEPP States and Tribal and local governments used to record and negotiate their
LCCEs and annual budget requests.

4.2.3 Consensus-Building and Automation

Beginning in the spring of 2000, with the new consensus-building approach to the budget
negotiations process in hand and an expansion of the CSEPP National Benchmarks to 12 distinct
capabilities (see Table 4.2) FEMA visited each of the CSEPP States to review and update their
respective LCCEs. With the State’s concurrence, most local governments were allowed to
participate in these reviews and advocate for their local requirements. While FEMA was
responsible for conducting these negotiations, the Army did have a representative from the
CEAC attend most of the negotiations to better understand and validate the process to the
Army’s Chemical Demilitarization Program Cost Review Board. This revised process
established a precedent and true community involvement in determining funding for CSEPP off-
post communities. Finally, the FEMA Regions and State, Tribal, and local governments saw that
cooperation could be an effective way to negotiate the resources required to protect the public,
workers, and the environment. Although State and local governments did not always obtain
everything they requested, their perception (for the most part) was that negotiations with FEMA
were changing from a hierarchical process to a more open process, resulting in a reasonable
distribution of CSEPP funding.

As the IPT process took hold as the primary management tool within the CSEP Program, some
States began to use their IPTs to build and/or review their annual budget requests and LCCE
updates. The State of Oregon continued to have annual face-to-face meetings with FEMA
representatives to update its LCCEs. As demil plants approached their dates to begin operations,
CSEPP budget negotiations intensified. Most State or county emergency managers had to certify
that they had the capabilities needed to protect the public so that State regulatory agencies would
allow the destruction of chemical weapons to begin. From the Army’s perspective, this meant it
had to provide more funds for CSEPP, but the system removed impediments to demil operations.
In other words, the risk of having operational demil plants sitting idle because the community
was not satisfied that it had adequate emergency preparedness capabilities outweighed any
potential cost savings from taking a hard stand on CSEPP requirements.

Attaining the capabilities defined by the CSEPP National Benchmarks had become the working
definition of “maximum protection.” However, the self-assessments by State and local
emergency managers were always somewhat subjective because they were based on the political
realities they faced, which included the need to get the process of destroying chemical weapons
started. In the final analysis, compromise was the best solution for all.

 59

Table 4.2 CSEPP Benchmarks108
 No. Benchmark
 1 (ADM) Administrative support for each CSEPP installation, State, and county necessary to
 support their CSEPP preparedness activities.
 2 (A&N) Functioning alert and notification system extending across the installation and
 appropriate off-post jurisdictions to communicate protective actions and other critical
 response information to the public.
 3 (AUT) Functioning automation system for rapid exchange of chemical hazard modeling and
 protective action analysis between the CSEPP installation, State, and counties.
 4 (COM) Functioning communications links between the CSEPP installation, State, and county
 EOCs, and the JIC to support coordinated implementation of response plans.
 5 (COP) Coordinated plans for response to hazards from the chemical weapons stockpile for
 each CSEPP installation, State, and county.
 6 (EOC) Functioning EOCs at each CSEPP installation, State, and county to support
 coordinated implementation of response plans.
 7 (EX) An exercise program that effectively evaluates integrated response capabilities and
 preparedness.
 8 (MED) A medical program to support on- and off-post medical preparedness among first
 responder and receiver organizations for a chemical weapons accident or incident.
 9 (PER) Specialized personnel, such as CSEPP manager, PIO, planner, and IT specialist, to
 support activities at CSEPP installations, States, and counties.
 10 (PRO) Protective action strategy for each jurisdiction that is based on the threat from the
 stockpile, consistent with response plans, and conforms to established CSEPP guidance.
 11 (POE) A program for coordinated emergency public information and education, including a
 public outreach/education program to enhance CSEPP awareness and familiarity with the
 protective action strategy.
 12 (TNG) Training programs, consistent with CSEPP guidance, State and local training plans
 (for off-post jurisdiction personnel) and Army certification requirements (for installation
 personnel) that maintain proficiency of emergency services providers and CSEPP staff.

While the CSEPP community had reached consensus on how to develop the LCCEs and annual
budgets, FEMA still faced a challenge in managing and accounting for the funding that had been
distributed to the CSEPP States and, beginning in FY 2002, one Tribal nation. That meant
holding the States and Tribe accountable for CSEPP funding obligations, expenditures, and
unliquidated obligations.109 FEMA also needed accountability for the State, local, and Tribal

108
 CSEPP Policy Paper Number 18, CSEPP National Benchmarks, pp. 1–2. The Benchmarks were based on
 disaster preparedness capabilities similar to those used for the National Fire Protection Association
 1600 standards. Wording of the benchmarks was adjusted in the November 2013 CSEPP Strategic Plan.
109
 Unliquidated obligations are funds that have been obligated for something, but not expended. Because the
 funding that the Army sends to FEMA is considered obligated on the Army’s books, any funds that FEMA staff
 have not expended on their books is designated by the Army as unliquidated obligations.

 60

government personnel who were being paid with CSEPP funding and some kind of accounting of
the results (i.e., performance reporting). Obtaining these data had been a problem from the
beginning of the program. While the requirement was ingrained in the on-post CSEPP
community as part of the Army and DOD way of doing business, it was mostly foreign to the
off-post CSEPP community. This issue was further complicated by the CA reporting process,
which did not require detailed accounting for awarded funds. The CA reporting requirements
were far less complicated than those of the CSEP Program. Also, there was a concerted effort by
the Federal government to reduce reporting burdens on the States. However, the Army and
FEMA were responsible to Congress for ensuring that CSEPP funding was used only for valid,
needed CSEPP purposes.

The line items from the standardized CSEPP Benchmark spreadsheet were integrated into the
automation software used to administer CSEPP CAs to ensure continuity between the off-post
CSEPP LCCEs and their annual budgets. The original software application was designed only
for the development of CA applications; FEMA also needed to manage the funding after the
awards had been made. Innovative Emergency Management, Inc. (IEM) developed an expanded
CSEPP CA Database Module for FEMA to meet this need.

In 2002, FEMA introduced a new CSEPP CA software application called CA Tools that allowed
users to prepare work plans for off-post CSEPP employees; add indirect costs to State/Tribe and
county budget requests; generate and use the Report for Reconciliation of Grants and CAs;
generate and use the Report of Government Property; use the Region negotiations checklist and
generate award documentation; generate Quarterly Financial and Performance Reports;
reallocate funds between FEMA-approved line items; and produce CA closeout reports. CA
Tools continued to evolve through three different versions that were used until 2007, when the
software was replaced by CSEPP WebCA.

Each user had to install the CSEPP CA Database Module and CA Tools applications on their
computer, then create the databases at each level of the CSEPP off-post funding process. A
database file had to be exported to the next user level and imported into that user’s database to
achieve commonality. The process then had to be reversed after the budgets were approved by
FEMA HQ to deliver the awards down to the users who had initiated the requests. With each
updated version of the software application distributed across four distinct levels of users, this
process became more cumbersome as the software applications expanded and the size of the
databases increased. CA Tools users became more vocal in their criticism, and State Directors
demanded that the application be improved. A CA Tools User Group met in January 2005 to
identify and prioritize user requirements for the next release of CA Tools; the group also began
the requirements process to develop an Enterprise Version (later named CSEPP WebCA). The
obvious solution was a centralized application with a single integrated database that could be
shared by all users.

With the advent of web-based technology, such an application was possible. IEM demonstrated a
prototype of the web-based version at the August 2005 meeting of the CA Tools User Group.
After further development, review, and testing, the initial web-based version was launched as
CSEPP WebCA in May 2007. The CSEPP CA software was an important tool in facilitating the

 61

negotiation, award, and results associated with State, Tribal, and local government CSEPP
budgets.

Guidance for preparing and managing CSEPP CAs was evolving concurrently with evolution of
the financial software. CSEPP CA preparation and management began with the guidelines and
functional worksheets that the Financial IPT developed in 1995 and evolved until, in FY 2000,
the FEMA CSEPP Office published three sets of specific instructions with firm oversight from
FEMA’s Office of Financial Management (OFM):
 1. Preparation Instructions for Submitting CSEPP CA Applications — intended for all users
 submitting CSEPP CAs.
 2. Regional Office Instruction for Awarding/Amending CSEPP CAs — intended to guide
 Regional CSEPP Program Managers and Assistance Officers in administering the awards
 phase of the CSEPP CAs.
 3. Regional Office Instruction for Outlays, Performance Reporting, Budget Deviations,
 Cash Reconciliation, Property Management and Closeouts for Grants and CAs —
 intended to guide Regional CSEPP Program Managers and Assistance Officers in
 administering CSEPP CAs after the awards had been made.

Together, the three documents contained a total of 278 pages of instructions and sample forms.
The content (and length) was heavily influenced by FEMA’s OFM. These documents were
updated and issued annually for FY 2001 through FY 2005.

In FY 2006, FEMA completed a major revision of the CSEPP CA guidance, trimming it down to
a total of 35 pages and eliminating redundancy between the guidance and information already
contained in the CSEPP CA software application. However, FEMA expanded the CSEPP
National Benchmark section that defined allowable costs, unallowable costs, and cost
apportionment items for each benchmark, addressing many previous questions about what
FEMA considered a legitimate CSEPP requirement for off-post capabilities. The CSEPP CA
guidance was issued in this format for FY 2006 through FY 2010. For FY 2011 the format was
changed to conform to standard grant format guidance required by the OMB and FEMA, but the
content of the guidance was basically unchanged. The new format was continued in CSEPP CA
guidance in FY 2012. In FY 2013, the format was modified again to conform to the Homeland
Security Grant Program Funding Opportunity Announcement format, which was used again in
FY 2014.

On December 26, 2013, OMB published the Uniform Guidance: Uniform Administrative
Requirements, Cost Principles, and Audit Requirements for Federal Awards in the Federal
Register. The Uniform Guidance was the result of two presidential directives and the culmination
of years of collaboration among Federal agencies, recipients, and other stakeholders in the grants
community. The goal was to streamline regulations and requirements affecting grants
management. The Uniform Guidance was published in 2 CFR 200, consolidating eight grants
circulars into a single document. For CSEPP, the Uniform Guidance became effective with the
issuance of the FY 2015 awards. To conform to the Uniform Guidance, the previous year’s
CSEPP CA guidance document was replaced with the DHS Notice of Funding Opportunity,
Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, with appendixes for the Articles of

 62

Agreement and Program-Specific Guidance including a table for allowable costs. Although the
format was different for the FY 2015 award guidance, the substance of the guidance was mostly
unchanged. The Uniform Guidance was updated in 2024 and renamed OMB Guidance for
Federal Financial Assistance.110

4.2.4 Summary

In summary, the off-post portion of CSEPP funding involved development of policy and
guidance, implementation of procedures, and development of tools to facilitate the process.
Although there were conflicts among jurisdictions, with time and an evolution to more open
negotiations, participants eventually built trust and made durable agreements. The off-post
CSEPP funding process effectively integrated the requirements of the Army’s PPBES with
FEMA’s administrative requirements for grants and CAs to State and local governments.

The on-post funding process was much simpler because all parties involved used the Army’s
PPBES. In 2004, the Army adopted the 12 CSEPP National Benchmarks for use by the CSEPP
installations and technical support functional managers in their respective LCCEs. However,
other than grouping their requirements under these categories for their LCCEs and annual budget
requests, the on-post funding process followed the Army’s PPBES process without any major
problems.

Indian Reservation. These numbers reflect the funds expended for closed accounts and the final
amount of funds projected to be expended for those accounts still open for the Blue Grass and
Pueblo Communities, DHS/FEMA support, and Army support.111

4.3 CSEPP Guidance
With multiple sites and a broad mission to bolster capabilities for response, it was soon clear that
the CSEP Program would require internal guidance on a variety of emergency management
topics. Joint Army/FEMA guidance documents were developed either at the respective national
offices or under the oversight of an IPT. Guidance documents consisted of two types: short (one-
to five-page) policy papers, establishing policy direction from the national offices, and longer
documents with more comprehensive guidance on particular aspects of the program. The
following short policy papers were issued over a 10-year period beginning in 1991:112
 1. Definition of Maximum Protection
 2. Environmental Sampling to Determine Chemical Agent Contamination
 5. County Public Information Officers for CSEPP
 6. Interim Policy Regarding Off-post Meteorological Towers for CSEPP
 7. Review of CSEPP Exercise Initiating Events
 8. Public Information in Connection with CSEPP Exercises
 11. Compensation for Volunteers in Exercises
 12. 1994 Update to Exercise Policy
 13. Elimination of Distinction between Direction and Control Exercise and Full-Scale
 Exercise
 14. Designation of Public Affairs as CSEPP Core Objective
 15. Revised Policy for Developing Off-Post Medical Preparedness Capability
 16. 12-Month Modified Exercise Schedule
 17. Protocol for Communicating with SBCCOM Technical Staff
 18. CSEPP National Benchmarks
 19. Amending CSEPP Exercise Policy
 20. Adoption of AEGLs

In 2004 and 2005, a working group of program officials113 consolidated the planning guidance,
policy papers, and some other program guidance into two documents: a planning guide and a
programmatic guide. The programmatic guide included technical and institutional background
information and covered program management functions such as funding, personnel, training,
and reporting requirements. The planning guide covered emergency planning for CSEPP. The
planning and programmatic guides were first issued in 2006 and were updated in 2008. In 2012,
they were combined into one document, the CSEPP Program Guide, which was updated for the
last time in March 2019.

The final Program Guide emphasized the program’s integration with national preparedness
doctrine. As stated in the preface,

 The guidebook reflects national doctrine, including the FEMA National
 Preparedness System and National Preparedness Goal. The benchmarks ensure a
 unified approach across the five mission areas and 32 core capabilities outlined in

112
 Note: the policy paper numbers are not in sequence because some numbers were reserved or were assigned to
 papers that were later superseded or were never finalized.
113
 The Planning and Programmatic Guidance Steering Committee.

 65

 the National Preparedness Goal and all components of the National Preparedness
 System. Alignment to national doctrine is described in the base document and
 illustrated in Appendix M: Program Alignment to the National Preparedness
          System.114

In addition to the general planning and programmatic guides, CSEPP developed a number of
other guidebooks and workbooks to address specific subject areas that required more detail,
including the following (in some cases, they were revised several times as the program
progressed and practices changed):
 • Exercise Guidance (aka the “Blue Book”) (last updated in February 2021)

4.4 CSEPP Strategic Plan and Reports to Congress
As recommended by the GAO,115 the Army and DHS/FEMA jointly developed a 6-year Strategic
Plan defining CSEPP’s mission,116 goals, and objectives for the program, including actions
necessary to achieve the goals and performance measures used to track and report outcomes to
Congress. The 12 goals specified in the Strategic Plan correspond to the benchmarks for funding
justifications and serve as the basis for reporting programmatic progress to Congress.117,118

Annual reports, provided to Congress from FY 2001 onward, reviewed the overall status of the
program nationwide, including program management, significant political or social events

    GAO, September 2003, pp. 25–26.
116
 As defined in the Strategic Plan, CSEPP’s mission is to “enhance existing local, installation, Tribal, State, and
 Federal capabilities to protect the health and safety of the public, work force, and environment from the effects
 of a chemical accident or incident involving the U.S. Army chemical stockpile.”

year. The Army and DHS/FEMA reported achievement of the benchmarks at the programmatic
level; Figure 4.2 shows a benchmark-achievement table from the FY 2005 report. The annual
reports also included summaries of activity at each site, including funding amounts, recent
accomplishments and challenges, and benchmark status.

The Strategic Plan was issued in 2004, revised in 2007,119 and revised and reissued in November
2013120 and July 2019.121 The 2013 revision included changes reflecting the reduced number of
sites, the program’s new emphasis on leaving a preparedness legacy, and new processes for
budget management and cost estimation. It also included revisions to the program benchmarks
reflecting evolutionary change in program methods and goals. The 2019 revision focused on two
strategic goals: (1) maintain and sustain enhanced preparedness, and (2) achieve successful
closeout, defined as actions taken by the storage installations and CSEPP partners to cease
program activities while leaving a legacy of preparedness.

4.5 The CSEPP Portal

4.5.1 Overview

The CSEPP Portal was an online collaborative and file-sharing space for members of the CSEPP
community and an information source about the program for the public. Members of the
password-protected section of the site could share documents in online libraries and workspaces,
search the site for contact information for community members, find program-related
documentation, and join online discussion boards, among other things. The public-facing side of
the CSEPP Portal shared links to information sorted by relevant audience type. Figure 4.3 shows
the Portal entry page as of 2011.

 Figure 4.3 CSEPP Portal Sign-In Page

The Portal provided quick access to a variety of topics (Figure 4.4), as well as tabs containing
links and files related to Benchmarks, Communities, IPTs and Workgroups, Contacts, and
Support. Key functions of the Portal included the following:
 • Information repository. The Portal provided a repository of program guidance and
 reference materials, IPT reports and meeting minutes, exercise reports, public
 information materials, and other materials. These collectively constituted a library of
 reference materials and examples for program staff.
 • News and announcements. The front page of the Portal generally highlighted recent
 developments such as new or updated guidance documents, information about upcoming
 meetings and workshops, and other recently posted documents, plus news clippings about
 the installations and communities as reported in local news outlets.

 68

 • Program calendar. The calendar function showed upcoming meetings, teleconferences,
 exercises, and other events across the program.
 • Support to subgroups. The Portal included pages designed to support the efforts of
 specific CSEPP communities (i.e., sites) or working groups (e.g., medical, public affairs,
 automation, or program managers). Access to these pages was sometimes limited to
 specific group personnel. These pages provided project-specific reference libraries,
 announcements, and file-sharing.
 • E-mail listserv. The Portal registration list served as an email list for distributing
 announcements. This function was used sparingly.
 • People finder. The people search function on the Portal served as a networking tool for
 staff seeking contact information for a particular person or a particular position in the
 program.

 Figure 4.4 CSEPP Portal Topics Page

4.5.2 Portal Development Process

By 1999, the Army had a CSEPP website (http://csepp.apgea.army.mil) hosted on an Army
server that included a public area with a program description, links to CSEPP Army staff and
offices, IPT minutes, program calendar, and bibliography. The site also had a separate area for
CSEPP personnel that included a CSEPP resource guide, automation section, and “newsgroups”
to support several CSEPP sub-communities, such as the Emergency Management Information

 69

System (EMIS) and Federal Emergency Management Information System (FEMIS), exercises,
and hazard analysis.

In 2000, FEMA developed a secure website designed to facilitate communication and
information sharing among CSEPP planners. The CSEPP planners’ website allowed the planning
community to share forms, notifications, MOUs, policy documents, plans, and ideas. Additional
sections were soon requested to support programmatic IPTs including Exercise and Training,
Budget/CA, and Public Affairs IPTs. The FEMA planners’ site and the Army website operated in
parallel for a time.

In 2002, FEMA’s CSEPP planners’ website was migrated and upgraded to a SharePoint server to
further expand the capabilities and tools available. SharePoint was a Microsoft software tool
designed to support Internet-based collaboration, file sharing, and web publishing. At that point,
the website was renamed the CSEPP Portal and additional information and features were added,
including programmatic documents, policies, guidance, regulations, statutes, contact information
for program personnel, and current news items. The separate Army website was discontinued.

In 2005, IEM updated the CSEPP Portal to take advantage of current industry standard web
technologies. The static web pages were converted to a dynamic, database-driven web
application capable of integrating future functions such as document collaboration. IEM further
enhanced the Portal in 2007 to take advantage of the updated version of the software (SharePoint
2007). In this timeframe, the CSEP Program also designated site-specific content managers
responsible for developing and maintaining site-specific libraries and calendars.

In 2010, the public-facing side of the CSEPP Portal was redesigned to incorporate an audience-
based navigation system to share reference materials and links to documentation and websites
relevant to site visitors, who could choose options based on potential interest areas (e.g.,
emergency manager, family, training) or location (site). Each choice led to a list of resources
(e.g., guidance documents or news stories) and/or links to resources.

By 2012, the Portal had been in use for 10 years. The 2012 CSEPP Report to Congress described
its utility:

 For 10 years, the Portal has provided access to a vast library of information,
 including presentations from stakeholder meetings, site event calendars, news
 articles, current and archived photos, and Federal guidance on program
 benchmarks. The Portal also provided a platform for the CSEPP communities,
 IPTs, and working groups to collaborate remotely on preparedness projects.
 Visitors can also find CSEPP-related social media links organized by community,
 and an aggregated blog page bringing together multiple RSS123 feeds. In FY 2012,
 the CSEPP Portal was moved to a new hosting environment with upgraded and
 improved hardware, software, security, and redundancy.124

123
 An RSS feed is a format used to aggregate and publish links to frequently updated content (e.g., news articles,
 blog posts).
124
 FEMA, CSEPP Fiscal Year 2012 Report to Congress, p. 28, accessed from Portal. Subsequent reports to
 Congress noted that the Portal had more than 1,000 registered users.

 70

Through the end of the program, IEM continued to manage the Portal to upgrade security and
redundancy, adapt to platform changes, improve the user experience, and maintain its Authority
to Operate from DHS. The Portal was eventually migrated to the Federal Risk and Authorization
Management Program certified cloud services to reduce overhead and provide more robust
disaster recovery and continuity of operations planning (COOP) functions.

4.6 Meetings and Workshops
As a diverse and geographically spread-out program involving many agencies and jurisdictions,
CSEPP required periodic meetings and workshops to coordinate action and share information.
CSEPP participants held thousands of local staff meetings, interagency meetings, and site IPT
meetings to coordinate plans, exercises, agreements, training, and other topics on a site-by-site
basis. There were also numerous meetings involving staff from multiple sites and program HQs;
regular meetings and workshops on a national scale to share information and best practices,
provide feedback, and solve problems; and national topic-specific workshops, conferences, and
IPT meetings. Other sections of this report document the work of the IPTs; the following
subsections summarize some of the more significant cross-cutting meetings and workshops.
Section 4.6.1 covers the long series of general-purpose meetings, including national workshops,
Army/FEMA staff meetings, and Army Planning Working Group (PWG) meetings. Section 4.6.2
summarizes some of the function-specific meetings that had a significant impact on the program.

4.6.1 General-Purpose Meetings

4.6.1.1 National Workshops

National workshops were held almost every year from 1989 to 2013.125 They typically included
300 to 400 participants from a cross-section of the program, including representatives from every
participating jurisdiction, agency, and contract support organization. The workshops were held in
each of the 10 CSEPP States on a rotating basis and they lasted 2 or 3 days, generally with
several pre-meeting sessions (described below) the day before the formal workshop began. The
workshops began and ended with general-interest plenary sessions (Figure 4.5); in between the
plenaries, breakout sessions or practicums addressed specific topics of interest to subsets of
program staff.

The plenary sessions included perspectives from senior program managers and presentations on
topics of overarching interest such as demil progress, reorganizations within the Federal
agencies, and developments in national emergency management policy. Plenary sessions also
included opportunities to highlight significant achievements and awards within the program and
honor program participants. The latter activities contributed to a sense of community and pride
among program personnel.

125
 The exception was 2008, when a Closeout Forum was held in Denver, Colorado, in lieu of the national
 workshop.

 71

 Figure 4.5 Plenary Session, 2009 National Workshop (photo by R. Norville, FEMA)

Although breakout sessions were held in parallel, generally each topic would be covered more
than once, so that workshop participants would have flexibility to attend the sessions that
interested them. Typical breakout session themes included the following:
 • Medical preparedness
 • Automation
 • Communication and warning systems
 • Public affairs
 • Training and exercises
 • Closeout
 • Finance and budget processes

Breakout sessions were also used to introduce new guidance documents and planning or
response tools, such as the reentry and recovery workbook, post-emergency compensation guide,
chemical agent monitoring and sampling plan, CA guidance, and protective action guidance and
software. Practicums provided an opportunity to introduce new software tools to support
communications, training, budgeting, and emergency planning.

Notetakers documented the plenary and breakout sessions and compiled the session notes into a
workshop report. In later years, the workshop presentations were collected and made available
via the CSEPP Portal.

 72

To save travel expenses and to take advantage of the presence of many program personnel in one
location, additional meetings would be held the day before the workshop. Typically, the day
before the workshop would include meetings of:
 • State Directors and Program Managers
 • Elected Officials and County Directors
 • Citizen Advisory Commissions (CACs)
 • National IPTs such as the medical, public affairs, automation, and exercise IPTs.

National workshops were discontinued after 2013 as the program shrank to two active sites.
Thereafter, program information and innovations were shared through IPTs, smaller program
meetings, and direct contacts among the Kentucky and Colorado CSEPP communities.

4.6.1.2 Army/FEMA Strategic Planning Meetings

Key Army and FEMA headquarters program staff met annually to review program progress and
discuss funding and budget planning. These meetings involved a review of the status of each site
and generally included several presentations about ongoing CSEPP projects such as website
development, training activities, planning for the next national workshop, software and template
development, and evaluation of PPE. Some presentations touched on CSEPP’s relationship to the
larger world of emergency management and homeland security, such as the Homeland Security
Exercise and Evaluation Program (HSEEP) and the National Level Exercise program. These
meetings provided an opportunity for a joint review of program initiatives and issues of concern
to both the Army and FEMA, ensuring a joint understanding of issues and a coordinated
approach to addressing them.

The last Army/FEMA Strategic Planning meeting was held at the Edgewood Area of APG on
January 28, 2025. The meeting addressed issues relating to program closeout, including specific
measures to be completed in Kentucky, schedules, organizational shutdowns, associated
budgeting processes, and documentation of the program’s legacy. The meeting included two
representatives from the Office of the Secretary of Defense, which was directing the program to
terminate activities and cap expenditures.

4.6.1.3 Army Planning Working Group Meetings

Army CSEPP PWG meetings were held annually, usually in January. These meetings were
primarily by and about the Army CSEP Program and included representatives from CMA
CSEPP, AMC, HQDA, installation CSEPP staff, chemical activity commanders, and Army
contract support staff. The meetings allowed Army staff involved in the program to confer,
compare notes among sites, and obtain updates on program initiatives. In later years, FEMA
would send a representative as well. These meetings took place over 2 days and featured
program status reports from each installation and review/discussion of major program initiatives
and issue areas. For example, the 2000 PWG meeting agenda included site status reports and

 73

“issues and updates” reports on atmospheric modeling, exercises, training, reentry/recovery,
personnel, budget, public affairs, response, automation, and demilitarization.126

4.6.2 Function-Specific Meetings

4.6.2.1 Legal Workshops

CSEPP planning and exercises repeatedly raised legal issues, mainly concerning application of
environmental laws and regulations, governmental powers and authorities, potential for liability,
and agreements between governmental organizations. To address these issues, CSEPP held a
series of law-oriented workshops and conferences during the 1990s.

The first legal workshop, held in Arlington, Virginia, in September 1993, was limited to Army
and contract support personnel. It included both legal and operational personnel from the major
commands and policy centers associated with the CSEP Program. Participants gave presentations
on various legal issues, prompting discussion and proposals for action. Issues discussed included
application of environmental regulations to chemical warfare materials, notification requirements
in the event of a release, post-emergency claims resolution, permissibility of Army response
actions off-post, preservation of evidence, and MOAs. Proposed actions primarily involved
preparation of guidance for chemical installations, technical escort units, and other relevant
Army personnel. Some items were followed up with subsequent exercises, guidebooks, or
workshops (see below). Workshop deskbooks were provided to all participants with copies of the
presentations and appropriate reference materials.

The second legal workshop was held at Argonne National Laboratory in May and June 1995. It
was attended by 89 participants from the Army, FEMA, other Federal agencies, and state and
local governments. The participants again included attorneys and emergency managers.
Workshop discussions addressed claims, MOAs, evidence preservation, and lessons from
private-sector HAZMAT incident response. This workshop also included a reference deskbook
with excerpts from various appropriate statutes, Federal regulations, Army regulations, and
journal articles.

These workshops were followed up with two activities directed at particular legal topics. The
first was an accident-investigation tabletop exercise (TTX) conducted at Edgewood in September
1996 as a follow-on activity after a regular CSEPP exercise. The TTX explored the various
investigations that would be expected after a chemical event. The participants were primarily
from the Army but also included FEMA, EPA, and State and local personnel. The 1.5-day
exercise included presentations on chemical agent characteristics, dispersion modeling, sampling
and analysis, and exposure symptoms. Presentations also covered other kinds of investigations
that would be expected to occur for different purposes: collateral (Army procedure), safety,
claims, and environmental (record of decision). Following the presentations, a panel conducted a
mock investigation using technical reports, operating procedures, witness statements, damage
reports, medical records, and other simulated evidence generated to support the exercise.
126
 U.S. Army Soldier, Biological and Chemical Defense Command, AMSSB-OCS Memorandum for Record, 2000,
 Subject: Minutes of the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program Planning Working Group
      Meeting, June 13–14, dated July 5, 2000.

 74

Information from the exercise was processed into a guidebook, the Chemical Stockpile Accident
Investigation Guide issued in May 1997.

The second follow-up legal activity was a workshop conducted in August 1999 to review use of
interagency agreements for CSEPP. This workshop followed the guidebook issued in May 1999,
and included a presentation on the guidebook and several case study presentations describing
specific uses of agreements in CSEPP. These included the Anniston A&N Agreement and
Tooele County’s agreement with the National Weather Service on TARs. Presenters explained
limitations imposed by the Posse Comitatus Act and the Anti-Deficiency Act and, on the second
day, led several breakout sessions to discuss agreements on particular subjects: public
information, protective-action decision making, public notification, chemical-agent monitoring,
medical support, and agreements for providing support to a depot from other military
organizations.

4.6.2.2 Planning Conferences

A survey taken prior to the 2000 National Workshop identified 33 key unresolved planning
issues in the program. These included issues relating to public A&N, protective action decision-
making, coordination between on- and off-post planning, information sharing among CSEPP
communities, and reentry and recovery, among others. Resolution of these issues led to several
program developments including a series of planning conferences, formation of additional
working groups, and preparation of guidance documents.

The planning conferences were held each December from 2000 through 2003. They resembled
the national workshops in that they included personnel from the spectrum of CSEPP jurisdictions
and involved parallel breakout sessions on specific topics, but they were smaller than the
national workshops. The first (2000) planning conference included 69 planners and program
managers. At that conference, the group formed several work groups to address particular subject
areas: the SIP Work Group, Plan Coordination/Integration Work Group, Planning Website Work
Group, and Reentry/Recovery Work Group. Progress on these issues was reviewed at the 2001
planning conference, which had a total of 124 attendees including 16 from the Army, 25 from
FEMA, 30 State personnel, 27 county personnel, 25 contract support personnel, and 3 others
from private organizations or universities.

The planning conferences were tightly focused on chemical emergency planning and on
resolving technical issues that planners had encountered. The sessions went into considerable
depth about technical issues involved in chemical emergency response; participants were asked
to identify key problems and propose solutions. Discussions at these sessions were captured and
compiled into a report.

 75

4.6.2.3 Medical Conference

The 1999 CSEPP Medical Conference was held November 17–18, 1999, in San Antonio, Texas.
The goals of the conference were to improve medical preparedness at each of the CSEPP sites,
share medical preparedness best practices developed at each CSEPP site, and share the CSEPP
lessons learned to foster improved medical preparedness in the United States.127

The conference featured presentations by medical experts on aspects of CSEPP medical
response. Following these plenary presentations, participants were divided into four groups, each
supplied with a professional facilitator, a recorder, and a team of SMEs. The groups discussed
medical preparedness, hospital preparedness, equipment, and interoperability, respectively. The
objective was to develop a framework for medical preparedness and response plans for
integration into their communities’ overall CSEPP plans, by discussing the medical planning
base, mission, and goals in each area in the context of hypothetical scenarios devised for the
workshop.

Early in the conference, participants objected to the conference design and expressed a
preference for actual case studies as opposed to hypothetical scenarios. As a result, the
conference agenda was changed after the first day. Each of the four groups was allowed to
determine its own method for addressing the overall conference goals of improving medical
preparedness, sharing best practices, and identifying lessons learned.

In the end, conference participants agreed on a set of four recommendations for future action:
 1. Maintain an ongoing program of meetings and workshops to foster communication
 among the CSEPP medical community.
 2. Continue to emphasize the need for a scientifically defensible basis for CSEPP medical
 plans to ensure that preparations are based on realistic and credible threats.
 3. Focus future conferences on the dual themes of case studies and problem resolution.
 4. Give greater emphasis to the medical preparedness function in CSEPP drills and
 exercises, with particular emphasis on designing and evaluating exercises that challenge
 the communication, coordination, and integration of medical preparedness programs.

127
 Proceedings of the 1999 CSEPP Medical Conference, 2000.

 76

 5 Program Components and Innovations

As discussed in Section 4.2, CSEPP established 12 national benchmarks that collectively
accounted for the essential elements of a fully functional chemical emergency preparedness
program. This section details the program’s major accomplishments and innovations in each of
the 12 benchmark areas. In addition, this chapter addresses two areas of emergency management
that cut across several benchmarks: preparedness for special populations and reentry/recovery.

5.1 Alert and Notification
Early in the program, Army and FEMA officials determined that a quick and effective warning
system would be an essential element of protection for on-post and off-post populations. The
nature of the hazard placed a premium on speed: a chemical agent release could happen
instantaneously, and the agent could drift downwind into populated areas in a matter of minutes.
Thus, the chemical installations and surrounding communities had to be equipped with reliable
and effective A&N systems to warn workers and the public of a chemical event.

Meeting this challenge required two elements working in concert. First, physical systems were
needed that were capable of alerting the affected populations and disseminating instructions.
These systems employed a variety of communication methods including sirens, TARs, public
address systems, highway message-display signs, automated audio telephone calls and text
messages, and others. Second, the human element needed to be in place, in the form of (a)
procedures, agreements, and trained personnel to carry them out, and (b) an educational effort
that allowed at-risk populations to recognize, understand, and act on a warning. These elements
had to function together to confirm an incident, alert people to the emergency, direct them on
how best to respond, and inform them when the incident was safely resolved.128

5.1.1 Background

The warning process for a chemical weapons event would begin with the installation recognizing
a situation involving an actual or potential release of chemical agent, assessing the potential
hazard and area affected, and notifying off-post authorities. The installation and off-post
authorities, together or separately, would determine the appropriate protective action(s) to be
implemented and activate communication systems to warn on-post and off-post populations.

A warning process has two components: alerting and notification. Alerting refers to attracting the
intended recipient’s attention. A siren’s wail and the familiar buzzing sound at the beginning of
an EAS message are examples of alerting. Notification refers to providing appropriate
information and instructions concerning protective actions, such as an instruction to evacuate or
shelter in place.

128
 Further information about the A&E process is documented in the “Lesson Learned on Planning for Immediate
 Alert and Notification,” in Volume 2 of this document.

 77

5.1.2 Public Warning Procedures and Policies

The Army used various monitoring and observation systems to detect a chemical leak at a
stockpile location. During operations that involved handling chemical weapons, on-scene
personnel were responsible for reporting any incidents to the installation operations center (OC).
Once the installation OC was aware of a chemical accident or incident (or in later terminology,
mishap), CSEPP policy required the installation to notify the off-post emergency management
community within a few minutes.129 The initial notification had to include a brief description of
the situation, including the identity of the agent and the predominant wind direction; the zones
where the population was at risk (if any); and an appropriate initial PAR (i.e., evacuate or SIP)
for each affected zone in the IRZ. The installation was also responsible for categorizing the
emergency according to a four-stage scale.130 CSEPP policies also required dedicated and
redundant lines of communication to the off-post warning points. In addition to voice
communications, an automated hazard modeling program (WebPuff) was used to provide an
initial PAR to the off-post community.131

Coordination of communication systems, warning points, protective actions, and warning system
activation required an unprecedented level of cooperation between the installations and the
surrounding communities. In general, coordination procedures were spelled out in local
agreements. The need for quick action required that some delicate issues be addressed in advance
regarding choice of protective actions and activation of warning systems. Often, the community
had to rely upon the Army’s recommendations for protective actions, as there would be little
time to review and ponder the best course of action. The Army installation, in turn, had to agree
to provide recommendations to the public, an action extending beyond the Army’s usual area of
responsibility. In addition, at some sites the Army agreed to undertake responsibility for
activating the sirens, TARs, and other warning systems.132

Another important procedural component of the warning process was the use of pre-scripted
instructional messages. Again driven by the need for speed, important instructions on protective
actions were developed ahead of time, so that in an emergency, the appropriate set of instructions
could simply be chosen rather than having to be developed on the spot. This practice was
adopted from the REP Program.

129
 Either 5 or 10 minutes, depending on the site. Planning Guidance for the Chemical Stockpile Emergency
 Preparedness Program, 1996, Section 8.4.1.
