 Limited Area Emergency, Post Only Emergency, and Community Emergency. CSEPP Planning Guidance,
    2008, Section 5.A.
131
 The automated system was developed and approved after extensive testing under the direction of the Automation
 IPT; see Section 5.7.
132
 The Army could provide direct notification and instruction if requested by local authorities and detailed in an
 MOA, as stated in Memorandum from DA Deputy Chief of Staff for Operations, Plans, and Training for the
 Commander, AMC, Subject: CSEPP Public Alert and Notification Policy, dated June 22, 1994, with
 1st Endorsement from AMC dated July 15, 1994. Direct activation of civilian warning systems was also
 provided for under DA Pam 50-6, Sections 3–5.c(5) (March 2003). It is worth noting that the program had
 established a level of trust sufficient to allowed communities to entrust this vital public-protection function to the
 Army installation.

 78

5.1.3 Public Warning Systems

In recognition of the maximum protection mandate, CSEPP officials determined that CSEPP
public warning systems would need to address both indoor and outdoor populations.133 This
objective was achieved by coupling outdoor systems such as sirens with indoor warning devices
(TARs) in the IRZ. Full siren coverage was required for all inhabited areas of the IRZ and in
high-density or special-use areas in the PAZ. TARs were required for all inhabited structures in
the IRZ. All warning devices, indoor and outdoor, would be capable of providing an audible alert
signal and a spoken emergency notification message.

Warning systems varied from site to site and changed over time as new technologies became
available. The main systems used are described below.

5.1.3.1 Outdoor Alerting and Notification

Sirens

CSEPP sites used a network of sirens, placed to ensure coverage of the required area to a certain
decibel level. Depending on the proximity of the sirens to a building, they could be heard from
inside, but they were primarily designed to alert people who are outside. CSEPP sirens provided
both a warning tone and a pre-recorded or live message with specific information about the
emergency and what protective action the listener should take. Sirens were placed to provide
complete coverage on and near the installation.

Route Alerting

Route alerting involves using loudspeakers mounted on vehicles to alert residents. Emergency
responders drive a specific route through the community and announce emergency instructions
over loudspeakers, particularly in areas not equipped with outdoor sirens and indoor TARs.

5.1.3.2 Indoor Alerting and Notification

Indoor Alerting Systems

CSEPP provided TARs and/or National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA)
weather radios to homes, schools, and businesses closest to the Army installations. TARs are
similar to weather alert radios (in some cases they are also weather alert radios) and use a tone
alarm. When activated, a tone (alerting signal) sounds, followed by an instructional message.
The instructional message may contain protective actions (shelter or evacuate) or may advise
listeners to stand by and monitor the EAS for further information.

The three types of indoor alerting systems offered were NOAA all-hazards weather alert radios,
specially designed TARs that offered programmable local control for selecting geographic or

133
 See, for example, Planning Guidance for the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, May 17,
 1996, Section 8.7 and Appendix F.

 79

common-interest groups, and specialized EAS-capable TARs with the added capability to
receive NOAA weather alerts. Some indoor A&N activation systems included base station
operation, in which an operator or dispatcher could select from a menu of tones signifying
different emergencies and pre-recorded messages, as well as make live public address
announcements. Most alerting systems offered special equipment option devices for those with
special needs, such as strobe lights to alert those with hearing disabilities.

TARs were delivered, and often installed in homes, by contracted companies, volunteer and city
fire departments, emergency management agencies, and the U.S. Postal Service. Details of the
distributions varied by site as follows:
 • Tooele and Utah counties ultimately distributed NOAA all-hazard weather radios to
 1,100 households during a 13-year effort.
 • Pueblo County, Colorado, offered and installed 1,600 TARs in residences, businesses,
 and public buildings within the IRZ and PAZ. Later, the County distributed weather
 radios to IRZ and PAZ residents.
 • TARs were offered to Newport community residents within the three Indiana CSEPP
 counties.
 • In the Blue Grass community, a second round of TAR offerings in 2011 (to replace
 existing TARs) extended the previous 6.2-mile radius to a distance of 9 miles. The new
 TARs were delivered to eligible residential addresses by the U.S. Post Office to ensure
 accuracy; special-population facilities were provided with TARs regardless of their
 location within Madison County.
 • For the PBA community in Arkansas, a second round of TAR distributions, with a
 NOAA all-hazard weather alert capability, culminated in the installation of TARs in
 approximately 15,000 residences, businesses, and special facilities in the IRZ within a
 9.5-mile radius.
 • Approximately 24,000 households, businesses, and special facilities in the UMCD IRZ
 and PAZ received hand-delivered TARs; strobe light attachments were offered to those
 with hearing impairments and for use in high-noise commercial areas.
 • The first round of TARs for the ANCA Alabama community was distributed to all
 residences, businesses, and special facilities in the IRZ out to a distance of 11.4 miles;
 registered persons with special needs residing beyond the 11.4-mile radius, were
 provided TARs that included strobe and vibrator-alerting accessories as needed. In a
 second round of TAR distributions to replace the initial county-programmed CSEPP tone
 alert receivers, approximately 130,000 NOAA all-hazard weather alert radios were
 mailed to ANCA IRZ and PAZ residents, businesses, and special facilities.

Emergency Alert System

The EAS was created by the Federal Communications Commission (FCC) to alert members of
the public to potential dangers in their community and to provide them with specific instructions
to help ensure their safety. EAS includes broadcasters, cable television systems, wireless cable
systems, satellite digital audio radio service providers, and direct broadcast satellite providers.

 80

EAS has broad reach, including essentially anyone in their home, car, or outdoors who is
listening to the radio or watching television. Moreover, most people are familiar with it through
the monthly testing program and from alerts broadcast for severe whether events. CSEPP
communities incorporated EAS activation as a public A&N method for a chemical event and
practiced activation procedures during CSEPP exercises.

Integrated Public Alert and Warning System

IPAWS provides emergency alerting, warning, and notifications to the public through a variety
of media. Messages to the public can be initiated at the Federal, State, or local level and can be
distributed through broadcast, dish, and cable networks for radio and television and through
weather radios and mobile telephones. IPAWS enables authorized alerting authorities to
simultaneously send a wireless emergency alert (WEA) to mobile devices such as cell phones; an
EAS alert to media platforms, such as radios and television; and an alert message through the
NOAA system to weather radios — all using a single origination system. Authorized by an
Executive Order134 and Federal legislation, the IPAWS took several years to develop, test, and
implement.

With its well-established cooperative relationships among Federal, State, and local emergency
management agencies, and longstanding emphasis on quick alerting and notification, CSEPP was
a logical test bed for adoption of IPAWS.

CSEPP formed an IPAWS working group in early 2013, a subsidiary of the Automation IPT
(AIPT). The new group had three subcommittees: Governance, Messaging, and Technology.
FEMA HQ also coordinated closely with the FEMA IPAWS Program Management Office. The
Governance subcommittee was tasked with establishing State and/or local alerting authority to
send messages to the public. The Messaging group developed message templates for various
types of alerts, and the Technology group discussed the technology associated with IPAWS
messaging and reviewed messaging templates for adherence to specifications. In 2014, these
separate work groups were merged into the existing public affairs and automation IPTs.
Participants included the Army CMA, FEMA CSEPP offices at the national and regional level
(Regions IV and VIII), CSEPP State and local jurisdictions (Colorado/Pueblo County; Kentucky/
Madison, Lexington-Fayette, Garrard, Laurel, and Estill Counties), and support contractors
including IEM, Argonne, and mission critical partners (MCP).

The CSEPP IPAWS working group had two main accomplishments. First, the group conducted
an IPAWS test over 2 days in December 2014 using the IPAWS Lab housed at the DOD Joint
Interoperability Test Command (JITC) test center in Indian Head, Maryland, to practice message
origination and distribution. Second, the group incorporated lessons learned from the December
2014 test, along with other information, to develop a CSEPP IPAWS Implementation Guide. The
Guide was published in 2015 and updated in 2019.135

134
 President George W. Bush, 2006, Exec. Order 13407, Public Alert and Warning System, June 26.
135
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 2019, Guide to Implementing the Integrated Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS)
 Version 2, February. See also “Lesson Learned: IPAWS Testing and Development,” January 29, 2021, in
 Volume 2 of this document.

 81

The testing process resulted in a number of lessons learned, which were incorporated into the
IPAWS Guide. The lessons included a number of specifics regarding messaging format, time-
stamping, software, and other system features and protocols, and also the need for user-friendly
instructions, manuals, job aids, and training.

The Colorado and Kentucky CSEPP communities integrated IPAWS into testing and exercises
and used it for actual local alerts and emergencies. As noted in the CSEPP annual report to
Congress for FY 2019, “The Pueblo County Sheriff’s Office, Emergency Services Bureau
(PCSOESB) successfully demonstrated their IPAWS capabilities by conducting an out-of-
sequence exercise to notify the community of a simulated railroad incident. PCSOESB also uses
IPAWS for public warning . . . Madison County, Kentucky, also tested their IPAWS during the
annual CSEPP exercise and sent at least one actual IPAWS message during the year.”136

5.2 Communication Systems
The CSEPP communications program had three main goals:
 • Direct, reliable, and redundant communication between the Army on-post EOC and the
 off-post EOCs (both primary and alternate) of all IRZ counties and States,
 • Reliable inter-jurisdictional EOC communications for all affected off-post areas, as well
 as links to the State emergency services or related agencies, and
 • Reliable communications between all off-post EOCs and their field units.

A complex network of communication systems was required to achieve these capabilities. A
requirement for two independent modes of communication meant that CSEPP communities were
able to acquire and install state-of-the-art communications systems. Another of the program’s
early principles was that CSEPP communication links must be integrated with the public alert
and warning system. Thus, CSEPP deployed a two-way, on-post/off-post radio system
augmented by the capability to activate radio-controlled sirens, EAS broadcasts, indoor alerting
devices, monitors, pagers, and related hardware. A separate communications support system,
used solely for data transfer, linked terminals on the CSEPP automation system network, thereby
avoiding dual use of the same dedicated telephone lines or radio channels for both voice and
automated data transfer.

Because of the longer lead times available before response action would need to be taken beyond
the IRZ, program managers determined that notification of the PAZ counties could be performed
via the State EOC using various existing communications systems. They also determined that
other State and Federal agencies could be notified, and information could be shared, using
existing communications systems.

Because emergency information had to be transmitted quickly and accurately, the emergency
communication system incorporated both high reliability and redundancy. A multi-system
solution offered immediate efficacy while ensuring that single points of failure would not derail

136
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 2020, Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program Fiscal Year 2019 Report to
 Congress, February 27, p. 17.

 82

the overall response. Dedicated, non-public telephone lines were incorporated as an effective
means of on-post to off-post communication. Because dedicated lines could have reduced
function as a result of distances, weather, line damage, potential system overload, and facility
limitations, other communications systems supplemented the phones.

Radio links using dedicated frequencies offered another effective means of communication. A
communications network consisting of redundant telephone and radio systems would ultimately
link each Army installation with the EOCs and notification points of the corresponding IRZ
counties and States. Whether the telephone or radio system was designated the primary method
of communication, the other system would serve as a backup, and both primary and alternate
systems would have high reliability.

As communications technology evolved, many upgrades were made to CSEPP systems. Notable
among these were 800-MHz radio system upgrades; high-capacity, two-way radio systems;
microwave antennae; and web-based IT, including WiFi high-speed connections. While the
methods of communication varied from site to site, and while minor problems with the systems
occurred from time to time, States and local communities met benchmark requirements for
communications early in the program. Some of the most lasting and important legacies of the
program were the improvements to emergency communication and IT.

Figure 5.1 illustrates the communication network that served the Washington State CSEPP.

incorporating an extensive list of components, support systems, and tasks.

To ensure the continuing availability of essential communication systems, and to address
increasing national concerns about infrastructure resilience, the Colorado CSEPP community
undertook a series of activities in 2015 and 2016 to enhance the resilience of emergency
communications. There were three phases: first, key emergency-communication systems and
their underlying infrastructure were identified, and their vulnerabilities were analyzed. Second, a
workshop was held to establish a common foundation of understanding between emergency
managers, information technology (IT) staff, and communication service providers regarding
emergency communications. A planning working group was convened to discuss ways to
minimize the impact of any communication system disruptions. Third, a tabletop exercise was
held to test existing plans, capabilities, and agreements against a variety of scenarios involving
communication system breakdowns.

The Colorado activities were motivated by concern that a disaster might disrupt the very systems
that underlie public warning and emergency response. A storm, fire, earthquake, or other event
could affect physical infrastructure such as cell towers, microwave antennas, fiber-optic cables,
or power supplies that are essential for local communications networks.

 84

The Colorado activity addressed key systems supporting CSEPP-related communications,
including the physical systems carrying WebPuff communication traffic; on/off-post hotline;
landline telephones; email; paging and dispatch systems; radio communication channels used by
law enforcement, fire and EMS; and systems involved in delivering public warnings such as
sirens, tone-alert radios, and the EAS. These capabilities in turn depended on local
communications infrastructure including fiber-optic networks; server rooms in county and
municipal buildings; towers with microwave and radio repeaters; and other equipment located on
rooftops, in underground vaults, or on poles.

As noted, the activity included a vulnerability assessment, workshop session, and TTX. The
exercise was held in June 2016 with a mix of participants including IT professionals, emergency
planners, emergency managers, and service providers (vendors). A lead controller presented a
series of brief scenarios for discussion: a utility pole being knocked over at one of the key
facilities, a fire in a server room, a construction accident that severed cables, and an earthquake
that caused numerous cascading infrastructure failures, as well as other types of damage.
Participants discussed the impacts of these scenarios on critical communications channels and
how to assess, compensate for, and quickly repair disruptions.138

5.3 EOCs and JICs
The potentially large scale of a chemical event, and the potential for nationwide media interest,
drove development of facilities that could accommodate large numbers of people operating in a
complex response situation. EOCs and JICs were the key types of facilities identified for CSEPP
response. The program provided support to upgrade existing facilities, and in some cases new
facilities were built.

5.3.1 Emergency Operations Centers

EOCs are the hub of emergency response communications, resource support, and decision-
making. Generally, in an EOC, key decision-makers and staff from all responding agencies
gather to coordinate and support response actions conducted in the field.

In 1993, the Army and FEMA agreed that each Army installation and IRZ county should have a
functioning EOC where authorized officials would gather to direct and coordinate emergency
operations, speak with other jurisdictions and emergency response officials in the field, and
make PADs. All of the involved States and many of the counties had an EOC at the beginning of
the program, but some counties and municipalities did not, and many of the existing EOCs were
determined to need upgrading to support a chemical event response.

The existing EOCs varied widely in size, equipment, and operational capabilities, depending on
community resources and exposure to natural and technological hazards. For example, counties
with strong emergency management programs were typically equipped with effective
communication systems, sleeping accommodations for emergency workers, meeting rooms, and

138
 For additional details see the “Lesson Learned on Assessing and Exercising Emergency Communications
 Resilience” in Volume 2 of this document.

 85

a large operations room with separate workstations for a number of local agencies that would
assemble for emergency response. Counties with smaller or no formal emergency management
organization typically used a multi-purpose location, such as the apparatus floor of a fire station,
which could be turned into an EOC with portable tables and telephone drop-lines for a few local
agencies.

To provide a uniform level of preparedness, CSEPP developed standards for EOCs serving IRZ
counties.139 A CSEPP county EOC was expected to provide a command-and-control center for
chemical emergency response, as well as for other potential emergencies identified in the
community’s hazard assessment. The multi-hazard capability was deemed necessary because
other hazards, such as an earthquake or tornado, could be a factor in initiating a chemical agent
release.

In 1996, the Army and FEMA published EOC guidelines incorporating recommendations from
the following FEMA civil preparedness guides (CPGs):
 • Federal Assistance Handbook: Emergency Management, Direction and Control
 Programs (CPG 1-3);
 • Emergency Operating Centers Handbook (CPG 1-20); and
 • Objectives for Local Emergency Management (CPG 1–5).

The guidelines focused on six categories of physical aspects to be considered when designing a
CSEPP EOC facility: location, size and layout, protection and security, communications
capabilities, information processing capabilities, and miscellaneous equipment and supplies.

Within these guidelines, States and local communities were afforded both flexibility and latitude
in designing an EOC to meet their needs. Upgrades to existing facilities and, in a number of
cases, new construction required both time and significant resources. It was not until 2008 —
12 years after publication of the 1996 guidelines — that all CSEPP States achieved the EOC
benchmark and were in sustainment status. Figure 5.2 shows an example county EOC in
Colorado.

EOC building did not come to a halt once the benchmark had been achieved. After 2008, CSEPP
provided funding for new or upgraded EOC facilities in Alabama (Cleburne and Talladega
Counties), Arkansas (upgrades to Grant County EOC), Colorado (new EOC/JIC for Pueblo
County and upgrades to State EOC), Kentucky (renovated Commonwealth, Madison County and
Lexington-Fayette County EOCs; new EOCs for Clark, Estill, Garrard, Rockcastle, Powell,
Jackson, and Laurel Counties), Utah (upgrades to State EOC), and Washington (upgrades to
State EOC).

139
 Army/FEMA, 1996, Planning Guidance for the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, May 17,
 Appendix B.

 86

 Figure 5.2 Pueblo County EOC140

With the arrival of SARS-CoV-2 (severe acute respiratory syndrome – coronavirus 2) (aka
COVID-19) in the United States in 2019, the emergency management community was
challenged to effectively respond, both to the pandemic and to all other types of emergencies and
disasters, while maintaining the safety of response staff. Because EOC staff were at risk of
disease transmission while working in close proximity, CSEPP implemented a process of
planning and executing adjustments to operations using IT to allow close coordination of
response operations while minimizing risk to staff — termed a “distributed EOC.” Army and off-
post CSEPP communities in Colorado and Kentucky implemented protocols for staffing and
used distributed electronic systems (meeting software and WebEOC, among others) to
coordinate response while minimizing risk of disease transmission among response personnel.141

140
 U.S. Army PEO ACWA news release, Practice Drills Improve Emergency Response Capabilities, June 10,
 2015. Accessed March 2026 at https://www.peoacwa.army.mil/2015/06/10/practice-drills-improve-emergency-
 response-capabilities/.
141
 For further information see “Lesson Learned: Using a Distributed EOC,” January 2022 (in Volume 2 of this
 document) and Hewett, P.L., J. Dactelides, R. Sharp, and J. Short, 2020, Using Distributed Management Systems
 for Direction, Control, and Coordination of Chemical Accident or Incident Response Activities (CAIRA),
 Argonne National Laboratory White Paper, November.

5.3.2 Joint Information Centers (JICs)

Just as EOCs are the hub of emergency response activities, a JIC as part of a Joint Information
System (JIS) can facilitate delivery of accurate, timely, and coordinated emergency public
information. A JIS is an information network that allows PIOs and PAOs from multiple agencies
in different locations to work together using agreed-upon procedures so that messages to the
public and news media are clear and consistent.142 A JIC facilitates operation of a JIS by
establishing a central location where PIOs from Federal, State, and local jurisdictions, as well as
volunteer and nongovernmental agencies, can meet to coordinate the release of emergency public
information. CSEPP planning guidance issued in 1996 required each community to develop a
plan that, among other things, provided for the coordinated dissemination of emergency public
information through a JIC.143

A JIC is organized, equipped, and staffed to gather, produce, and disseminate information to the
public and media during an emergency using all means available. JIC staff members monitor and
analyze news media and social media coverage of the event, with a rapid response team
addressing identified gaps in information and/or misinformation or unconfirmed information
(i.e., rumors or speculation) that may hinder response and recovery efforts or cause undue public
concern or confusion. Information flow out of a JIC should be multi-directional, to allow for
collaboration among all interested and affected parties. A JIC should be located as close as
possible to the best sources of information, such as the incident scene and the EOC, without
compromising security or safety. The facility should be large enough to accommodate
anticipated surges in JIC staff, as well as visits from the news media. Planning should also
provide for the establishment of an alternate JIC, in the event that the primary JIC is unusable.

While it is ideal for public information staff to collocate in a central physical location during an
emergency, in reality it is unlikely that all representatives would be able to come together in a
JIC, and extremely unlikely that this gathering could take place before the need for effective
emergency public information arises. Therefore, a JIS is set up to coordinate information
activities among the various response organizations prior to (and then in addition to) activities at
the JIC. As with a JIC, a JIS should have pre-established operating procedures, organizational
structures, position descriptions, and MOAs or MOUs in place to guide JIS-member
participation. A JIS is a cooperative effort that does not dictate what participating agencies can
and cannot say. Rather, it provides a mechanism for PIOs to share information and resources
while working together in gathering and disseminating information.

At the program’s outset, the primary focus was on establishment of functional JIC facilities.
While all of the CSEPP sites did develop fully staffed and equipped JICs and regularly tested
them during drills and exercises, many communities operated JICs on an ad-hoc basis in the
early years. Examples of early JIC locations include a county courthouse, an area restaurant, a
National Guard armory, and a vacant building on-post. These facilities were makeshift; they
were multi-purposed and not always available to the community on short notice. For any kind of

142
 See Section 5.11 for more information on JISs.
143
 Army/FEMA, 1996, Planning Guidance for the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, May 17,
      Sec. 8.14.2.

 88

activation (real-world, training, or exercise), the JIC had to be set up from scratch with supplies
and equipment such as telephones and computers.

As the public affairs program evolved, and dedicated funding became available, communities
began to identify facilities better suited for regular JIC use. Anniston, for example, renovated a
building in the old Fort McClellan area to create a facility that incorporated a JIC along with
training and meeting facilities. The towns of Tooele and Pine Bluff renovated spaces in their
downtown districts. Umatilla included a JIC in the design of its new county EOC and jail, while
Madison County, Kentucky, constructed a new building for its JIC and 911 call center. Pueblo
moved into space at the Colorado State University Pueblo campus and made plans to use the
Pueblo City/County Health Department building as an alternate location. Subsequently Pueblo
used space in the new Pueblo County Sheriff’s Office Emergency Services Center for the JIC.
Newport used the Army National Guard armory in Rockville, Indiana, and Edgewood eventually
moved from its original restaurant location and utilized Maryland’s new JIC facility collocated
with the State EOC.

Evolution of new communication and information-sharing technologies allowed public
information staff to expand the reach and scope of the JIS/JIC. Communities developed web-
based status boards to share information within a JIC facility while employing social media tools
to gather and disseminate information. PIOs recognized the potential for using other CSEPP sites
in mutual aid agreements to augment limited JIC staff and resources. The Virtual JIC concept
allowed public affairs staff at one site to assist in an emergency at another site. Formal mutual
aid agreements were put in place and practiced during drills and exercises. Also developed
during this time was the Mobile JIC concept (also known as JIC-in-a-Box), which identified and
organized communication equipment for use as an alternate JIC, as required by CSEPP planning
guidance.144

5.4 Medical Preparedness and Response
Medical support and preparedness for communities around chemical stockpile sites evolved over
the course of the program. In the early days, the CDC National Center for Environmental Health
(NCEH) became involved in CSEPP through taskings in PLs 91–121, 91–441, and 99–145. As
the program matured, medical preparedness became the focus of a national-level IPT that held
workshops, issued guidance, and developed training. Medical preparedness was incorporated
into CSEPP exercises and evaluated on-post and at local hospitals.

5.4.1 Early CSEPP Medical Training and Conferences

Initial training in CSEPP medical aspects was led by State EMAs, with involvement from the
CDC/NCEH, DA, and FEMA. As CSEPP evolved, the CDC developed and presented medical
preparedness courses to medical personnel serving populations adjacent to the eight installations.

144
 Army/FEMA, 2008, CSEPP Planning Guidance, June, Section III.E.

 89

The first joint CSEPP Medical Symposium was held in June 1992 in Salt Lake City, Utah. It
addressed pre-hospital and hospital patient care issues as they pertained to off-post chemical
agent casualties and was hosted by FEMA, the Army, and the CDC.

In June 1993, civilian off-post medical personnel were invited to attend the Army’s Toxic
Chemical Training Course at the Chemical Demilitarization Training Facility in the Edgewood
Area of APG.145 Course attendees also included civilian first responders (pre-hospital) and first
receivers (hospital personnel) who were supporting the non-stockpile recovery of chemical agent
rounds found at the Spring Valley site in Washington, D.C. A member of the Army Surgeon
General’s Office taught the course, along with Johnston Island personnel who had firsthand
knowledge of the chemical agent incineration process. The course topics addressed the medical
aspects of recognizing and treating nerve and blister agent intoxication, heat stress associated
with wearing PPE, and an orientation to PPE worn at the demilitarization sites. The highlight of
this course was a real-world drill that evaluated the students’ understanding of the content. The
course was offered in the spring and fall for medical support personnel (physicians and nurses)
both on-post and off-post.

During the fall of 1993, the CDC hosted a CSEPP working group meeting that included
leadership from the Army, FEMA, and the CDC, as well as medical representatives from Israel
who had worked as healthcare personnel during the Gulf War. The working group developed
minimum standards for medical preparedness and included the current requirements for hospitals
as identified by the Joint Commission. These guidelines were published as CDC
Recommendations for Civilian Communities Near Chemical Weapons Depots: Guidelines for
Medical Preparedness.146 The guidelines did not supersede existing medical or public health
practices or requirements. They were divided into pre-hospital and hospital sections, and they
included recommendations that all local medical emergency response plans related to the release
of a chemical agent be integrated into the all-hazards State and local disaster response plans.

Using these guidelines, local health and emergency management officials were encouraged to
work with Army personnel to analyze the nature of possible releases at each installation,
determine what kinds of toxic effects and what level of contamination might occur, and match
local or regional resources to the potential task.147 This coordination marked the beginning of the
community’s integration with local emergency management and public health agencies in a
coordinated planning and response effort.

CSEPP held a medical conference on November 18–19, 1999, in San Antonio, Texas, attended
by 172 persons including CSEPP staff from FEMA, the Army, and every CSEPP State, plus
CSEPP contractors; and medical SMEs from the Army, SAIC, CDC, and other organizations.
The goals of the conference were to improve medical preparedness at each of the CSEPP sites,
share the medical preparedness best practices developed at each CSEPP site, and share the

145
 Guidance entitled Medical Management of Chemical Casualties was issued by the United States Army Medical
 Research Institute of Chemical Defense, Aberdeen Proving Ground, Maryland, in 1992.
146
 The guidelines were initially published on July 27, 1994, with a request for comments, and finalized on June 27,
 1995, in the Federal Register, 60 FR 33308.
147
 Symptoms and treatment protocols differ for the different chemical agents; because some sites had only one kind
 of agent and others had multiple agents, medical preparedness efforts had to be tailored to each site.

 90

CSEPP lessons learned among preparedness programs to foster improved medical
preparedness.148 The conference featured a combination of general presentations and group
workshops in the areas of medical preparedness, hospital preparedness, equipment, and
interoperability.

The conference highlighted medical themes that played out over the next decades of CSEPP.
Conferees identified the following needs: better communication between the CSEPP medical
community and the emergency management community; risk-based, scientifically defensible
hazard analysis; and greater medical participation in drills and exercises. The conferees also
noted that most of the issues facing CSEPP medical preparedness were not unique to CSEPP, but
would also apply to other natural and technological hazards; thus there was enormous potential
for the CSEPP solutions to be integrated into overall community medical preparedness. This
theme was reflected in subsequent development of training materials and evaluation guides.

5.4.2 Medical Preparedness

Medical guidelines were adopted and supported by installation-specific MOUs with area
hospitals and their communities. The MOUs addressed each party’s responsibilities with respect
to pre-hospital treatment, administration of nerve agent antidote, and training of personnel.
Subsequent MOUs addressed communication between the Army installation, the community,
EMSs, hospitals, and public health departments.

Specific elements of the CSEPP medical program developed over time include the following:
 • Civilian responders initially used the DA battledress overgarments and PAPRs with a
 combined high-efficiency particulate and organic-vapor cartridge to protect themselves.
 As the program evolved, commercially available PPE ensembles were used to provide
 responders with OSHA Level C protection. The protection scheme included development
 of specific work procedures and training for personnel designated to use the PPE. Work
 procedures followed established standards from the National Fire Protection Association,
 as well as those established through OSHA.
 • First responders (pre-hospital) and first receivers (hospital personnel) were trained in
 decontamination to prevent secondary contamination and chemical injury to medical and
 rescue personnel.
 • CSEPP developed patient triage guidelines that included ID bands to identify “clean”
 (decontaminated) patients and patients who had been administered nerve agent antidote.
 • EMS personnel coordinated their plans with the hospital first-receiver communities.
 CSEPP-specific decontamination guidelines moved away from using bleach to using only
 soap and water as a primary skin decontaminating solution.
 • The CSEPP program provided 24-hour access to an emergency communication network
 linking the chemical depot, local and regional EMS, and all potential receiving hospitals.

148
 U.S. Army and FEMA, Proceedings of the 1999 CSEPP Medical Conference, accessed on the CSEPP Portal
      April 2010.

 91

 • Medical response personnel were specifically trained to assess, decontaminate, treat, and
 manage patients exposed to chemical agents through training programs offered off-site in
 Aberdeen, Maryland, and later in each community.
 • Hospitals developed CSEPP-specific annexes in their emergency preparedness plans.
 CSEPP personnel planned and evaluated exercises that included coordination with local
 health and EMA personnel on an annual basis to achieve specific objectives. CSEPP
 exercises and plans later became part of an all-hazards planning focus for each
 community.
 • Medical breakout sessions became standard at the annual CSEPP workshops.
 • Healthcare providers participating in CSEPP exercises (Figure 5.3) were evaluated using
 medical evaluation guidelines (MEGs) developed by the Medical Integrated Process
 Team (MIPT). Federal evaluators included peer experts from other CSEPP sites who
 collaborated with the community medical participants. Specific medical evaluation
 elements were incorporated into CSEPP exercise evaluation guidance.

5.4.3 The Medical Integrated Process Team

Discussion among medical experts in the program began early on, and medical groups operated
under various names and charters. A Medical Quality Improvement Team was established in
2001, and in 2004, it was converted to the MIPT, which included representatives of FEMA;
Army CSEPP and Army Public Health; CDC; State and county emergency and public health
services; hospitals; and supporting contractors including Argonne, Battelle, IEM, Oak Ridge
Institute for Science and Education (ORISE), and ORNL. The MIPT charter was updated in
March 2006. Later, the MIPT was renamed the Medical Working Group.

The MIPT developed training materials, evaluation guides, and other medical-response-related
products to address chemical agent hazards, specifically, and other technical hazards and medical
response elements such as biological and radiological hazards, decontamination, mass casualty
response, and PPE. It also served as a resource to CSEPP generally for all medical-related issues,
providing input to the medical components of CSEPP programmatic, exercise, and closeout-
related guidance and hosting medical sessions at the annual CSEPP national workshops.150

Training materials included medical self-study training videos, such as “Don’t be a Victim” and
“Exposure and Contamination.” These materials prepared first responders for chemical stockpile
events; they have also proven to be applicable to medical responses outside of CSEPP. All 10
CSEPP States achieved the medical benchmark before the end of the 2001 Federal fiscal year. A
multi-hazard medical “train-the-trainer” course developed and delivered in 2009 included
sections on chemical agents and other hazardous chemicals, as well as biological and
radiological hazards, decontamination, mass casualty response, and PPE. The course was
updated in 2012 and designated as the CSEPP Multi-Hazard Medical Curriculum.

The MIPT developed an electronic resource guide for hospitals, made available through the
CSEPP Portal. An MIPT-specific web page on the CSEPP Portal served as a resource for all
members, providing access to meeting minutes, guides, training materials, and other documents.

As noted above, the MIPT developed all-hazards, comprehensive MEGs for hospital and pre-
hospital care and updated them multiple times, with the latest version published in December
2016.

In 2016 and 2017, the MIPT conducted a comprehensive review of medical capabilities in the
Kentucky CSEPP community, at the request of the Kentucky CSEPP IPT, in anticipation of the
impending start of chemical weapons destruction operations at BGCAPP. As described in the
resulting report, 151 “The project involved distribution of self-assessment survey questionnaires
and related guidance to the pre-hospital response agencies and hospitals that voluntarily agreed
to participate in the interest of enhancing their capabilities. Questionnaires and instructions were

    554. Published online Oct. 1, 2020, doi: 10.1016/j.cnur.2016.07.013.Available at
 https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7094223/, accessed January 2024.

distributed to participating organizations, completed, and returned for analysis. A team of
CSEPP partners, including representatives of Army Public Health Center (APHC) and CSEPP
medical support contractor Battelle then scheduled and conducted follow-up site visits to clarify
responses and gather additional information.” The review generated several recommendations to
enhance emergency response preparedness and capabilities in the Blue Grass medical response
community.

5.5 Protective Action
Responsibility for protecting the on-post population from a chemical agent release (or any other
hazard) falls on the installation commander. Local and State officials are responsible for
protecting off-post areas. From the beginning of the program, CSEPP personnel recognized that
the process of choosing protective actions was too complex and time-consuming to be performed
after an emergency began. Although the options were limited to evacuation and SIP, methods to
quickly determine or pre-determine the optimum mix of protective actions for downwind areas
were needed. The CSEPP personnel conducted research, developed tools, established policies,
and fostered cooperation among all responsible parties to help ensure that protective actions
could be quickly identified and implemented.

5.5.1 Protective-Action Decision-Making

A 1990 ORNL technical study152 concluded that, because evacuation provides maximum
protection wherever it can be completed before arrival of the toxic chemical plume, it should
always be the principal protective action for the general public in the PAZ, although other
actions may be appropriate for special populations. Within the IRZ, the study determined that
evacuation should be recommended for the general population in specific areas when it can be
completed in time, but for areas of the IRZ that cannot evacuate in time, SIP should be
recommended.

The 1996 CSEPP Planning Guidance described protective actions-related decision making as
follows:

 Selection of appropriate protective actions for a given event must consider all
 relevant aspects of the release, meteorological conditions, and characteristics of
 the surrounding population. These factors interact to define the populations at risk
 from a particular release, the concentration and duration of the toxic plume from
 which they will require protection, and the time available for them to implement
 protective actions. Pertinent release characteristics include the type and amount of
 agent and the duration of the release. Relevant meteorological conditions include
 wind speed, wind direction, and atmospheric stability. Pertinent population
 characteristics include the distance between the location of the release and

152
 Rogers, G.O., A.P. Watson, J.H. Sorensen, R.D. Sharp, and S.A. Carnes, 1990, Evaluating Protective Actions for
 Chemical Agent Emergencies, ORNL-6615, April.

 94

 populated areas, the distribution and density of population, and the locations of
 institutions and special population groups.153

The 1996 Guidance also referred to protective action planning based on the three designated
geographic zones (IRZ, PAZ, and PZ) and designated subzones within these zones. The subzone
concept divides a given zone into subareas or sectors, generally based on direction from the
source of the hazard. Figure 5.4 shows an example of subzones at the Umatilla site. Subzones
allowed CSEPP personnel to determine the appropriate protective actions and communicate them
to the public based on the expected hazard area; for example, at Umatilla, if winds were from the
north, protective actions would be a priority for subzones E and F in the IRZ and R and Q in
the PAZ.

 A

 Figure 5.4 Umatilla IRZ and PAZ with Letter-Designated Subzones

To ensure that the appropriate set of protective actions is selected in the shortest possible time
during an emergency, CSEPP personnel divided the overall protective action decision-making
process into two phases:
 1. During the planning phase, numerous release scenarios were analyzed to determine the
 best protective options for affected areas based on expected conditions downwind.

153 Planning Guidance for CSEPP, 1996, p. 164.

 95

 2. If an event occurred, conditions would be quickly assessed to identify which pre-
 determined sets of protective actions should be implemented.154

A hazard assessment and protective-action decision support model was developed and refined by
CSEPP specifically for the chemical warfare agents. Results obtained from the model established
the technical basis upon which civilian officials determined the appropriate protections for their
communities, considering both the plume projection and protective actions that would be
recommended by the Army.

The achieve the fastest possible response during an emergency, an Army installation could
initiate protective actions as a part of its emergency notification process and communicate the
results to the jurisdictions. Army regulations155 required that, when an installation was authorized
to initiate protective actions on behalf of civilian authorities, they be coordinated and formalized
in written MOUs, with the jurisdictions specifying exactly which actions the installation would
perform under what conditions.

Real-time protective action decision-making was integrated into the automated hazard analysis
system, so that the system made recommended protective actions (evacuation or SIP) for each
designated zone at the same time that it provided a hazard plume projection. See the discussion
of D2-Puff and WebPuff in Sections 5.7.2 and 5.7.3. An algorithm available in WebPuff allowed
users to apply Shelter or Evacuate depending on when the chemical agent plume would arrive —
so that, for example, if the plume would arrive in a given zone within an hour, residents would
not have time to evacuate, so they would be advised to SIP.156

5.5.2 Protective Action Implementation

As a part of preparedness planning, each CSEPP jurisdiction analyzed the protective actions it
expected to use and identified ways to expedite or improve the effectiveness of those actions.
Planners were encouraged to identify the equipment and personnel needed to assist the public
and ensure that those resources would be available in an emergency.

CSEPP jurisdictions developed evacuation plans with designated routes leading away from the
hazard area and toward facilities where evacuees could receive support services such as medical
evaluation and temporary lodging. They also designated alternate evacuation routes, depending
on wind direction; conducted evacuation time studies; and made provisions to support evacuation
with traffic control points, staffed by first responders, to facilitate unidirectional traffic flow.

154
 The importance of predetermining actions and implementing them with great speed was reinforced by systems
 analysis studies performed by IEM; see Section 5.8.2 for details.
155
 DA Pamphlet (Pam) 50-6 and AR 50-6. See also Memorandum from DA DCSOPS for the Commander, AMC,
 Subject: CSEPP Public Alert and Notification Policy, dated June 22, 1994, with 1st Endorsement from AMC
    dated July 15, 1994.
156
 Communication from Jeff Davis, DETech, May 8, 2025. Mr. Davis also noted that at Blue Grass, the large off-
 post population and limited road network led the community to establish “normal shelter” as a default protective
 action for all areas due to the perceived impracticality of rapid evacuation.

 96

Analysis of the effectiveness of different protective actions led to an emphasis on SIP for
populations in the closest areas, where the risk of toxic exposure would be highest and the time
to act would be shortest. Program guidance defined four types of sheltering: normal, expedient,
enhanced, and pressurized. Pressurized sheltering uses blowers and filters to create higher air
pressure in a building, preventing infiltration of toxic vapors. Equipment was installed to provide
this protection at many IRZ schools. Normal sheltering refers to the simple act of going inside,
shutting windows, and turning off ventilation systems. Enhanced sheltering and expedient
sheltering involve improving an existing building (such as a home) to make it a better shelter —
either ahead of time (enhanced) or on the spot (expedient). Over the years, CSEPP sponsored
several studies on effective SIP practices. For example, one study looked at the effectiveness of
duct tape and plastic to seal a “safe room” in response to a chemical event. 157

Beyond studies and planning, CSEPP communities provided residents and businesses with
specialized equipment to enhance protection. All communities provided indoor alerting systems
of some sort (TARs or NOAA weather radios), and some communities provided residents with
equipment to enhance the effectiveness of sheltering at home.

5.5.3 Sheltering Enhancements

Some communities distributed equipment to households to enhance their ability to shelter from
toxic vapors at home. Sheltering equipment (depending on location) might include the following:
 • SIP kits containing pre-cut plastic sheets and tape that could be used during an
 emergency to seal off windows and doors.
 • Respiratory protection devices providing a filtered air supply to an individual user.
 • Portable room air cleaners (PRACs) or recirculating air filters (RAFs) that circulated the
 air within a chosen “safe room” and filtered it with charcoal to reduce chemical
 concentrations.

All of the equipment included instructions. A detailed video demonstrating proper SIP
techniques was also developed and distributed widely both within and outside of the CSEPP
community. In some cases, CSEPP even provided home repair and home improvement services
to nearby residents to reduce a home’s draftiness and improve its ability to resist vapor
infiltration.

The contents of SIP kits were individually developed by EMAs for their communities. All SIP
kits provided rolls or sheets of plastic to place over doors and windows, duct tape or painter’s
tape to seal the plastic, and a pair of scissors; other items that might be offered included a towel,
battery operated AM/FM radio, flashlight and batteries, videotape of sheltering practices, and
literature on sheltering. SIP kits were distributed through several means, including door-to-door
distribution in concert with TARs; after a training session at an EMA office; at designated
distribution centers; via a saturation mailing; or by mail after the return of a postage-paid reply
card or following a phoned-in request. Some communities offered instruction on SIP kit use to

157
 Sorensen, J.H., and B. Vogt, 2001, Will Duct Tape and Plastic Really Work? Issues Related to Expedient
 Shelter-In-Place, ORNL/TM-2001/154, Oak Ridge National Laboratory, Oak Ridge, Tennessee, August.

 97

those with special needs at senior centers, housing authority agencies, and other selected
locations.

PRACs and RAFs were distributed in the Umatilla and Anniston communities, respectively.
Approximately 1,200 RAF units were distributed to residents of Irrigon, Oregon, and members
of the CTUIR; because the units were distributed door to door, officials were able to identify
those with special needs and accommodate their needs. The RAF units could be used as
conventional indoor air cleaners (i.e., for non-emergency purposes) if desired, but in case of
chemical emergency, everyday filters were to be replaced with a special charcoal filter that was
shrink-wrapped and kept in a separate box. The Anniston CSEPP Community offered PRACs for
each IRZ residence, commercial establishment, and special facility for pick up at a Fort
McClellan distribution center making special provisions for delivery and other options for
special-needs individuals.

Anniston also offered respiratory protection devices providing a filtered individual air supply
(donned for limited periods while sheltering in place or evacuating) to those in the IRZ subzones
immediately adjacent to the site (pink zone). Residents picked up their devices at a distribution
center where a nurse could check them to gauge their ability to use the hood and then be fitted
and trained in its use. People who could not travel to the distribution center could arrange for a
home visit.

The U.S. Army Corps of Engineers completed safe room enhancements at Umatilla and
Anniston, evaluating 218 residences in the Anniston pink zones that were determined to offer
inadequate places to shelter; 167 residents accepted the offer to make their safe rooms more
effective by installing and replacing doors and windows, replacing window-mounted air
conditioners, and repairing walls. At UMCD, simple improvements were made to three homes
belonging to individuals who had registered in the special-needs program.

5.5.4 Protective Action Working Group and IPT

Two national-level groups examined protective-action issues. The first was the SIP working
group (SIPWG), formed in February 2001. After the SIPWG completed its work, the Protective
Action (ProAct) IPT was formed in 2003 to expand upon and formalize the work of the SIPWG.

5.5.4.1 SIPWG

The SIPWG defined its mission as assessing the current “state of the art” for SIP as a protective
action, identifying how SIP should be used in CSEPP, and developing guidance and policies to
implement best use of SIP. These issues were addressed in the Report of the SIPWG, published
in December 2001. Some of the most important recommendations in this report were:
 • Avoid fatalities to the maximum extent practicable in all protective-action strategies.
 • Develop a balanced Protective Action Strategy Plan (PASP) in every CSEPP community,
 and formalize it with MOUs among involved jurisdictions and the Army installation.
 • Use dispersion modeling to develop the PASP in the planning phase, and use dispersion
 modeling, along with other data, to make PADs in the response phase.

 98

 • Include a strategy for ending SIP in every SIP strategy.
 • Recognize that any PAD must consider those who will implement a different protective
 action. There will always be some people who cannot evacuate, whom emergency
 managers must consider as being sheltered-in-place and who must be given adequate
 information to protect themselves. Some will always evacuate even if SIP is
 recommended. Emergency managers must consider both groups in their planning.
 • Use dispersion modeling to decide when and how to end temporary SIP when definitive
 monitoring data are not available in time to minimize fatalities among a sheltered
 population.

Under SIPWG direction, Argonne staff developed a proof-of-concept model known as TSIP
(Terminate Shelter in Place), linked to the D2PC and D2-Puff dispersion models, to automate
calculation of the optimum shelter termination times for each subzone.

The SIPWG report also identified many best practices for using SIP as a protective action
strategy, including incorporation of a strong public education component. Ultimately, almost all
of the recommendations and best practices in the 2001 report were incorporated in revised
CSEPP policy and guidance and in community response plans and training programs. A variant
of the Argonne TSIP concept was developed by IEM and incorporated into its plume modeling
and decision support software.

5.5.4.2 ProAct IPT

The CSEPP National ProAct IPT was formed in 2003 to expand upon and formalize the work of
the SIPWG. As with the SIPWG, the ProAct IPT included representatives from local and State
governments, DHS/FEMA, the U.S. Army, and technical experts associated with the national
laboratories. ProAct IPT activities included review of relevant technical studies; development
and review of public education and emergency public information materials, in coordination with
the Public Affairs IPT; development of a PAD-making guidebook; and coordination with other
IPTs on incorporating protective action guidance into exercises and planning guides. The ProAct
IPT continued to provide these services until it was deactivated in August 2007.

ProAct IPT products included the protective action game, “Stay or Go, Do You Know?”; the
Shelter-In Place Protective Action Guide Book; a white paper on Protective Action
Considerations for Special Needs Populations; an Interactive Emergency Evacuation Guide
Book; presentations about protective actions at CSEPP national workshops; input to the CSEPP
Planning Guidance and CSEPP Programmatic Guidance; the CSEPP Protective Action Toolkit
(distributed on compact disc [CD]/digital video disc [DVD]); the Emergency Planners
Companion on Protective Actions (distributed on CD); and training videos about Evacuation
Planning, SIP for Residential Structures, SIP for Business, and Animals in Emergencies.158

158
 SIPWG and ProAct IPT findings were also published in an Argonne technical report: Yantosik, G.D., K. Lerner,
 D. Maloney, and F. Wasmer, 2002, When and How to End Shelter-In-Place Protection from a Release of
 Airborne Hazardous Material: Report on a Decision-Making Concept and Methodology, Argonne National
 Laboratory (ANL/DIS/RP-106770, February) and in the “Lesson Learned on Effective Sheltering in Place,” in
 Volume 2 of this document.
