5.6 Training
Development of CSEPP training began during the spring of 1989 with the formation of the
FEMA HQ Training Team. This team consisted of three program and training specialists and a
support staffer. The goal of the team was to determine the needs of the CSEPP communities and
develop a comprehensive training program to fill those needs.

Initial work consisted of developing a series of field survey questions aimed at assessing current
community knowledge regarding the chemical agents stored in the eight sites and a checklist of
resources (staff, equipment, and facilities) within each State. The training team spent a minimum
of two weeks in each State gathering data and compiling a report that became the foundation for
CSEPP training development.

With the data gathered, the training team developed the first CSEPP training classroom courses
with assistance from staff at ORNL and the U.S. Army Defense Ammunition Center and School
in Savanna, Illinois.

In 1992, the training team issued the first CSEPP Training Management Guidance document.
FEMA and the U.S. Army signed off and released the guidance document to the 10 CSEPP
States. The document outlined the procedures for developing, adapting, and securing training for
the CSEPP communities at the Federal, State, and local levels. Subsequent versions were
released in 1997 and 2005.159 Robert Norville of the FEMA national CSEPP Office played a key
role in developing and supporting CSEPP-related training programs for offpost communities.

5.6.1 ORNL Programs

Oak Ridge National Laboratory began performing work for the Army on the Chemical Weapons
Disposal Program as early as 1983. When CSEPP was initiated, FEMA asked ORNL to develop
a research-based Technical Training Program. This initiative consisted of a series of classroom
courses, including the following:
 • Agent Characteristics and Toxicology First Aid and Special Treatment
 • Chemical Awareness for CSEPP
 • Response Phase Decontamination for CSEPP
 • PPE

ORNL CSEPP training transitioned from classroom courses to training videos because the video
format was more appealing to its audience. Videos included the following topics:
 • Animals in Emergencies Video for Owners (also in Spanish)
 • Animals in Emergencies Video for Planners
 • Business SIP Video

159
 CSEPP Training Management Guidance, August 2005.

 100

 • Residential SIP Video
 • Evacuation Planning Video
 • Don’t Be a Victim — Medical Management of Patients Contaminated with Chemical
 Agents
 • Exposure and Contamination — Factors Affecting the Toxicology of Chemical,
 Biological, and Radiological Agents
 • Operations Level Training: A Refresher for Responders
 • Communicating Public Information in Emergencies
 • Emergency Planning for People with Access and Functional Needs

ORNL also assisted in developing and maintaining the CSEPP Training website and Photo
Image Library.

5.6.2 Argonne Programs

Argonne developed a series of training programs on emergency public information. As public
information functions were integrated into CSEPP, each organization designated a CSEPP PIO
or PAO. In many cases, new hires filled these positions. The newly minted CSEPP PIOs came
from diverse backgrounds; some had experience in public relations or media and some did not,
while some were familiar with chemical agents and some were not. Training the new CSEPP
PIOs quickly became a priority for the CSEPP personnel.160

The first CSEPP-sponsored PIO training course was a JIS/JIC Workshop offered by Argonne in
1994. Argonne also developed a Spokesperson Training Course for elected officials, military
commanders, and other key decision-makers, first offered in 1997 and later expanded to include
PIOs and PAOs.

As new needs were identified, Argonne’s public information training program grew to include
new topics and techniques.161 In recognition of the growing importance of social media, Argonne
designed a New Communication Technologies Workshop to provide instruction and hands-on
training in the use of the latest communication technologies and platforms. The Laboratory also
developed training specifically aimed at preparing spokespersons for media interviews, including
interviews conducted remotely from their home or office. Public information training continued
essentially until the end of CSEPP, with more than 400 public affairs training sessions attended
by approximately 5,500 participants.

When the COVID-19 pandemic hit in late 2019/early 2020, it became clear that the risk of
disease transmission would preclude instructors or trainees from traveling to and attending in-
person training sessions. In addition, many potential trainees were involved in pandemic

160
 For further information on CSEPP PIO training, see Section 5.9.2.
161
 For further information on training program development, see the “Lesson Learned on Emergency Public
 Information Staff Training” in Volume 2 of this document.

 101

response duties, limiting their time or energy for training sessions. All in-person training courses
scheduled in the Blue Grass and Pueblo CSEPP communities during the 2020 calendar year were
canceled. Argonne developed new remote, virtual, and “on demand” training formats, along with
a broader range of training topics, to allow training to continue throughout the pandemic.
Argonne produced a series of webinars, interviews, and short informative “how to” videos
addressing specific elements of risk and crisis communications. The shorter length (ranging from
5 minutes to about 1 hour) and video format, delivered online, meant that busy communications
officers and public officials could tune in to receive training on their own schedule.

In addition to these changes, existing longer-format training was adapted to a remote-delivery
format. Planning and testing were required to find ways to preserve the interactive aspect of
training during remote delivery. However, some advantages of the format became apparent.
First, travel time and cost were eliminated. Second, to some degree it made it easier for trainees
to commit, since they did not need to travel to a different location or be absent from their offices
for an extended period. Third and perhaps most important, it meant that participants could be
drawn from personnel at various locations and organizations to create a full roster.162

Argonne also developed a program to train Army OSCs. As previously described in
Section 2.5.3.1, under the NCP the Army had primary responsibility for responding to hazardous
substance releases (or potential releases) on or from Army facilities. In accordance with Army
regulations, the installation commander was delegated this responsibility, and in turn, could
designate one or more on-post OSCs to coordinate spill response. Chemical installations
typically had two or more designated OSCs — often an executive agent and the chief
environmental officer — in addition to the commander.

Initially, OSC training was obtained through USCG, which had already developed training for
USCG personnel (OSC is a career-track position at USCG). A number of chemical-installation
commanders and other personnel attended USCG training. However, the USCG course was not
ideally suited to Army OSCs because it was focused primarily on maritime and coastal zone oil
discharge response and was intended for personnel who would become full-time OSCs. In
addition, access to the USCG training was limited; over time, it became harder for Army
personnel to access the available courses. These factors, coupled with the rescission of DA Pam
50-6 and the adoption of NIMS by the Army to address chemical weapon event response, led
CSEPP leadership to recognize the need for an in-house, Army-specific OSC training course that
would better serve the storage installations and their surrounding communities.

Argonne developed a 5-day training course specifically tailored to the execution of OSC duties at
chemical installations. The course included material to familiarize trainees with the legal,
regulatory, and doctrinal background of an Army OSC role, as well as technical aspects of spill
response and the public information aspects of the OSC role.163 Instructors included Argonne
personnel and EPA and USCG OSCs with extensive experience in actual hazardous substance
release emergency response. As the scope of the training program changed to include other

162
 For further details see “Lesson Learned: Crisis Communication Training in a Pandemic” (January 29, 2021), and
 “Lesson Learned: News Media Interview Training and Exercising” (January 29, 2021) in Volume 2 of this
 document.
163
 For additional details, see “Lesson Learned on OSC Training” in Volume 2 of this document.

 102

military departments, instructors were also drawn from private sector entities such as railroads
and pipelines that interface with military installations.

The initial Army-specific OSC training session was offered in 2011 as part of the AMC’s
Service Response Force Training Program. That 90-minute session served as the foundation for
the CSEPP-provided Army OSC course, which was pilot-tested in late 2015. Four additional
courses were provided before CSEPP support for the course ended in 2023. At the program’s
end, the OSC course had trained 60 Army and six Navy personnel, as well as three industry and
two State environmental emergency response personnel.

5.6.3 ORISE Programs

Oak Ridge Institute for Science and Education (ORISE) designed, developed, and delivered
training for CSEPP beginning in 1998. Based on research, assessments, and input from CSEPP
emergency managers, ORISE training focused on providing participants with hands-on
experiences and skills development using cost-effective, off-the-shelf technologies.

The CSEPP Public Affairs and Emergency Preparedness Course, previously called the Advanced
JIC Training Course, guided participants through the process of using web-based, wireless, and
smartphone applications to better prepare for and respond to emergencies. ORISE training
included collaboration platforms ranging from simple web pages created with Microsoft Word to
more advanced website development tools. These hands-on courses also incorporated
E-Spokesperson, WeB-PIO, and other applications as examples of handheld/smart phone
technologies to enhance the communication and coordination capabilities of emergency
responders.

Beginning about 2008, ORISE training increasingly focused on how to use social media to
enhance communication and collaboration in CSEPP communities. Social media training
allowed participants to use applications such as Twitter (later X), Facebook, YouTube, and the
Google suite of applications to manage emergencies more effectively and efficiently.

The ORISE Medical Information Technology Course was developed to prepare medical
responders at CSEPP sites to incorporate patient tracking technologies into medical response
protocols and processes. The Medical Information Technology Course incorporated the ORISE-
developed WeB-MEDIS and E-MEDIS as web-based and non-web-based technologies for
managing emergency medical patient tracking.

ORISE also conducted training on the CSEPP Exercise and Training Analysis Tool (CETAT), a
database tool to track exercise and training issues and identify trends and solutions.

In the 2010s, ORISE integrated National Incident Management System (NIMS) and HSEEP
requirements into its training to improve communication, coordination, and consistency with
other DHS exercise programs.

 103

5.7 Automated Data Processing
The CSEP Program developed a number of custom software applications to support program
data needs. This section reviews applications developed to address the need to quickly assess the
hazard posed by a chemical agent release, identify the areas at risk, develop recommended
protective actions, and communicate this information to all concerned jurisdictions.

Applications were developed to address other program needs as well, including response
planning, exercise control and evaluation, identification of special populations within the risk
area, crisis communication training and exercising, and other aspects of the program. These
products are described in Sections 5.7.1 – 5.74.

5.7.1 Federal Emergency Management Information System (FEMIS)

In 1993, the Army and FEMA began developing a standard automated information system to
serve the CSEP Program. Pacific Northwest National Laboratory (PNNL) developed the Federal
Emergency Management Information System (FEMIS), which was designed to run on EOC
computers and a Unix server. FEMIS databases were developed in conjunction with IEM and
site personnel at each installation.164 FEMIS included hazard and evacuation models; a
geographic information system (GIS); and planning, database, status board, and communication
functions.

FEMIS underwent extensive operational testing from 1994 through 1996. The system was
critiqued in a 1997 GAO report, which pointed to problems with system speed and reliability.165
It was identified as a preferred but optional system; some communities opted for the Army’s
EMIS (developed by ORNL and Applied Computing Systems, Inc.) and Integrated Baseline
System (developed by PNNL). EMIS and FEMIS initially incorporated the plume dispersion
model D2PC, later upgraded to D2-Puff.

The Automation IPT was originally formed in 1998 to provide input for selection of a CSEPP-
specific information management software package. The AIPT selected FEMIS, which fulfilled
its charter, and the team disbanded in 1999.166 FEMIS, however, was not universally adopted by
the CSEPP communities, and the project was eventually retired. (The AIPT later reconvened, as
described in Section 5.7.2.)

5.7.2 Evolution of D2-Puff and WebPuff

In 1996, in response to some of the limitations of D2PC, the Army tasked IEM with developing a
new plume dispersion model called D2-Puff. D2-Puff could predict dosages and concentrations
under changing meteorological conditions, including wind shifts, and could account for local
topography. Following development of D2-Puff, IEM completed an enhanced, web-based

164
 System Administration Guide for FEMIS Version 1.5.3, PNNL-10610 Ver. 1.5.3 (November 20, 2002).
165
    GAO/NSIAD—97-91 (1997), Appendix IV.
166
 For a more detailed discussion of this first iteration of the AIPT, see the Lesson Learned on Automation
 Integrated Process Team in Volume 2 of this document.

 104

system, WebPuffTM, which incorporated D2-Puff along with additional elements in support of
planning for and response to chemical events. WebPuff, developed using Open Source J2EE
technology, was a secure, scalable, enterprise-distributed emergency management system167 that
was implemented at all the CSEPP sites. In 2003, the AIPT was re-formed and became actively
involved with the development, implementation, improvement, and support of WebPuff.

WebPuff supplied the D2-Puff model with real-time meteorological data from meteorological
instrument towers, surface weather observations from the National Weather Service and other
agencies, and weather predictions from mesoscale numerical weather prediction models.
WebPuff included IEM’s SafePort™ PAR algorithm, which used modeling data and a self-
contained traffic model to determine the efficacy of evacuation versus SIP.

Meteorological instrument towers or “met towers” were installed at each installation, with some
towers on-post and some off-post. Each tower was equipped with instrumentation to measure
wind velocity, air temperature, and humidity. The instruments fed data into the installation’s
automated hazard analysis system to support plume modeling; at any given moment, the system
could characterize the local atmosphere to help predict the speed, direction, and concentration of
a plume from a hypothetical release of chemical agent. Later in the program, the weather data
and plume modeling were also used to determine conditions for operation; chemical agent would
only be transferred to the disposal facility when conditions were relatively favorable. Specially
trained Argonne staff periodically maintained, calibrated, and replaced or upgraded
instrumentation on the towers as necessary.168

Using WebPuff, emergency personnel could:
 • Quickly model and analyze a chemical release (Figure 5.5 shows a sample predicted
 release plume).
 • Issue PARs within 5–10 minutes.
 • Increase the speed and sharing of information across multiple jurisdictions.
 • Enhance emergency response through effective coordination (Figure 5.6 shows a sample
 protective-action status report).

167
 IEM Inc., Case Study: WebPuff – Improving Protection through Science and Innovation, available at
 https://iem.com/webpuff-improving-protection-through-science-innovation-and-a-web-2-0-approach/ (accessed
    July 2025).
168
 The met tower data was also used to monitor conditions for chemical workers in the CLA. Readings from the
 met towers were input to an Argonne-developed model for estimating Wet Bulb Globe Temperature, which
 describes the potential for heat stress among persons performing strenuous work. This model was an important
 part of worker safety in the chemical storage areas. See the “Lesson Learned on Wet Bulb Globe Temperature
 Model” in Volume 2 of this document.

 105

 Figure 5.5 Sample WebPuff Plume Prediction

Figure 5.6 Sample WebPuff Protective-Action Status Report

 106

The WebPuff system evolved in three main phases:
 • Phase I: Implementation of service functionality, such as communication, meteorological
 data collection, and database function. The dispersion model ran on the local work
 station.
 • Phase II: Implementation of additional emergency management tools, such as logs and
 status boards. The ability to display (but not edit) model output via the browser was also
 developed.
 • Phase III: Implementation of the current web-based version, in which the dispersion
 model and its supporting functions and all emergency management tools run in a
 server/browser environment.
 • D2-Puff 1.0 was released in 2000, and was replaced by WebPuff 1.0 in 2004. Several
 new versions were introduced over the years. WebPuff 5.5, deployed in 2023, was the
 final version for CSEPP.

5.7.3 Operation of WebPuff

WebPuff was adopted for use by all of the CSEPP sites and was installed at State, county, and
on-post EOCs. WebPuff was a distributed system, running on servers at multiple locations with
data sharing across linked locations (i.e., the installation, State, and county servers for a given
site). The system had four different “modes” — normal operations, exercise, response, and
training — and served multiple functions during distinct phases of response. The mode status
was visually displayed by screen color (blue, yellow, and red, respectively, for normal, exercise,
and response) so that the system status was readily apparent at any time.

In normal day-to-day operations, on-post hazard analysts input information based on the daily
chemical area work plan. On the basis of the operations (if any) planned for the day, and
meteorological conditions, the hazard analysts would input a source term for the “maximum
credible event” (MCE) — that is, what is the worst thing that might happen today? The system
generated PARs based on the MCE, which would become the default recommendations if an
accident were to occur.

For an exercise or actual event, an on-post hazard analyst would initiate an exercise or response
by selecting “begin event.” The system would ask whether this was an exercise or actual
response. If the analyst selected exercise, there were options to choose the participating
jurisdictions, and the jurisdictions themselves could choose whether to participate. In an actual
response, all workstations at all jurisdictions would be conscripted to response mode.

In an exercise or actual response, the system supported hazard analysis. Information about the
release (e.g., the chemical agent involved, location of release, number of weapons involved,
whether there is fire) was input by the on-post hazard analysts. On the basis of those inputs, the
system projected the extent, severity, and timing of the resultant hazard to human health. From
those projections, the system generated PARs for the preplanned zones. The input information
could be adjusted to create updated hazard projections and PARs as new information was
obtained, or conditions changed (e.g., wind shift).

 107

During exercises at some CSEPP sites, off-post analysts took an active role in generating their
own hazard projections and testing scenarios. Other sites tended to simply rely on the Army’s
analysis and projections.

Off-post jurisdictions could accept the system-generated PARs or override them. Either way,
PADs could be input to the system and shared with all users. That flexibility allowed each
jurisdiction, and also the Army installation, to see what the other jurisdictions had decided in
terms of which areas to evacuate or SIP.

In addition to hazard analysis and protective actions, the system supported storage and sharing of
information about ongoing response efforts. EOC activity logs could be entered from any EOC
and shared (or not) with other users at the logger’s discretion. Information could also be input for
status board display within the EOC. Status boards could be customized to serve particular user
needs; for example, a board could be limited to showing Real-Time Analytical Platform (RTAP)
status.
The system also had a mode for user training. IEM staff members provided WebPuff user
training, and DETech staff members provided hazard analyst training that included how to
perform hazard analysis using WebPuff.

The AIPT oversaw development and deployment of WebPuff. New versions of the software
were developed periodically to keep up with software advances (e.g., new Internet browser
versions) and to incorporate new features requested by users. New versions were evaluated in-
house, then put on a server for testing by CMA and DETech hazard analysts. After those tests
were completed, IEM personnel installed the latest version at the CSEPP sites and tested it on
those particular servers in those particular environments. That step was followed by user training
and acceptance testing. Once those steps were completed, the system becomes operational at the
site, and was run in parallel with the older version for several weeks until there was confidence
in the updated version.

5.7.4 Continuing Role of AIPT

The AIPT continued to meet until 2021, when it became inactive pending critical mission items.
AIPT membership included people from CMA, chemical installations, FEMA, states, counties,
and contractors (IEM, DETech, C3M, MCP). Its scope was expanded in 2011 to include
evaluation of other automation systems (e.g., communications and alert/notification) for the
CSEPP communities in Colorado and Kentucky; it played a key role as a forum and coordinating
body for all IT-related program issues and developments. It addressed programs and projects
ranging from hazard analysis and warning software updates (WebPuff, WebEOC) and critical
communications systems resilience169 to public warning systems (IPAWS)170 and lastly, software
for virtual meetings during the COVID lockdown.

The AIPT helped the program adapt to changes in the computing and communications
environment, which occurred in rapid succession during the life of the program. For example, the

169
 See discussion of Colorado communications resilience activities in Section 5.2 above.
170
 See discussion of CSEPP role in IPAWS development in Section 5.1.3.2 above.

 108

AIPT prepared a white paper on cloud computing in September 2019, explaining the
terminology, meaning, and impacts of cloud-based systems, and their potential applications for
CSEPP.

As the end of CSEPP approached, CMA initiated steps to preserve WebPuff as a legacy item by
adding it to the suite of models included in the Defense Threat Reduction Agency’s (DTRA)
Hazard Prediction and Assessment Capability (HPAC) system. That effort was ongoing as of the
time of this writing, with oversight transferred to CMA’s Recovered Chemical Material
Directorate (see the CMA closeout summary in Section 6.4.9.1).

5.8 Coordinated Response Plans
Army and FEMA personnel identified the need for detailed hazard-specific planning early in the
CSEP Program’s development. The 1987 ERCP stated that:

 Every affected local jurisdiction and the State will have a chemical agent-specific
 response plan or specific annex to an operations plan. Every emergency plan
 developed in support of the Chemical Stockpile Disposal Program, including the
 on-site (CAIRA) plans, will be accompanied by detailed emergency plan
 implementing procedures…. Following each drill or exercise, procedures will be
 revised as necessary to address deficiencies and improve performance.171

At the beginning of CSEPP, each chemical installation had a CAIRA plan in place, but few of
the off-post jurisdictions (with the exception of the State of Oregon and Madison County,
Kentucky) had any corresponding plans or plan annexes. Thus, early in the program, significant
emphasis was placed on developing off-post plans that were coordinated with the CAIRA plans
and addressed the critical public protection functions identified in the ERCP.

The 1991 release of DA Pamphlet 50-6, “CAIRA Operations,” by the U.S. Army Nuclear and
Chemical Agency strengthened the requirement for close coordination of on-post/off-post
planning and formalized guidance regarding off-post notification, a standard emergency
classification system, PARS, and other essential elements of information sharing.

Early CSEPP response plans were typically stand-alone plans, although they were often titled as
hazard-specific annexes to State and local Emergency Operations Plans (EOPs), in much the
same manner as REP plans of the period. Over time, the CSEPP plans became better integrated
into State and local all-hazard plans as Federal all-hazards planning guidance and policy evolved.

Each CSEPP jurisdiction had its own plan, generally supplemented by implementing procedures
for specific functions or response positions. A county CSEPP plan with associated procedures
might comprise several hundred pages. School districts, medical centers, and other entities often
had their own plans. A complete set of printed State and local plans for a site with many
jurisdictions and districts — for example, Pine Bluff — might fill dozens of binders, occupying a
small bookcase.

171
 IEM Inc., 1987, CSEPP Emergency Response Concept Plan.

 109

CSEPP emergency planning was supported through a variety of guidance documents, tools, and
techniques that assisted emergency planners in identifying the resources and procedures required
for effective response to a chemical agent emergency and provided tools for executing and
coordinating the planning process.

5.8.1 Planning Guidance

The first CSEPP planning guidance was issued as an interim-use document in 1990172 and was
finalized in 1996. It was modeled to a certain extent on guidance developed for the REP
Program, with adjustments for the differences in the hazard. It included comprehensive guidance
for off-post communities on the essential elements of planning for a chemical event. Table 5.2
lists topics covered by the 1996 guidance.

 • Protective Actions and Responses • Reentry
 • Public Alert and Notification • Training
 • Traffic and Access Control • Exercises
 • Special Populations • Automation Support Systems
 • Emergency Support Operations

The planning guidance included general information in text form and a checklist for planners of
points to be covered in CSEPP plans. The guidance also included appendices with additional
detail and design criteria for many aspects of the program.

172
 Planning Guidance for the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, Final Interim Draft,
      April 1990.

 110

The next three decades were eventful for the field of emergency management, with the
emergence of new threats (e.g., terrorism and cyber) and increases in the frequency and severity
of familiar threats (e.g., wildfires, hurricanes, pandemics). These events, in turn, led to increased
attention to all aspects of emergency preparedness, and development of new models, systems,
protocols, techniques, and doctrine relating to emergency planning, response, and recovery.
CSEPP both contributed to and conformed to advancements in national guidance for emergency
management.

In response to the terrorist attacks of September 11, 2001, and other factors, in the early 2000s
emergency management began to receive more attention and resources than ever before.
Homeland Security Presidential Directives (HSPDs) 5, 7, and 8; the National Response Plan
(later the National Response Framework or NRF); and the NIMS document all aimed at
improving, standardizing, and integrating emergency planning and response efforts nationwide.
CSEPP planning needed to adapt to this new environment.

In late 2003, the CSEP Program undertook a review of its own planning guidance to determine
whether it satisfied the conditions set forth in the NIMS. Of particular concern was the emphasis
in NIMS on the need for States and localities to develop flexible, “all-hazards” EOPs. This
emphasis presented a problem for CSEPP because the existing guidance issued for State and
local program participants was unique to CSEPP and combined chemical event planning
guidance with directions for CSEPP administration. Revision of the guidance was discussed at
the 2003 CSEPP Planning Conference.

In June 2004, the CSEP Program empaneled a CSEPP Planning and Programmatic Guidance
Revision Steering Committee. Over the next 18 months, this group provided input for the new
guidance. Initial decisions of this group included the following:
 • Emergency planning/preparedness guidance should be separate from program
 management guidance. This approach would allow emergency operations planning
 guidance to be brought into line with NIMS while preserving the program-specific
 administrative requirements of CSEPP.
 • Planning guidance would be restructured to aid CSEPP jurisdictions in developing “all-
 hazards” plans as outlined in NIMS, but with a CSEPP-specific annex.
 • CSEPP should develop an automated all-hazards planning template that CSEPP planners
 could use to migrate their existing plans into the new, all-hazards structure while ensuring
 that CSEPP objectives were met.

 111

CSEPP published revised guidance in 2006. The guidance
comprised two volumes: planning guidance and programmatic
guidance. The new planning guidance reflected many
developments in emergency planning practice and doctrine —
including NIMS and adoption of the Incident Command
System (ICS) as a standard — that had taken place since
issuance of the earlier guidance. To ensure that the new
planning guidance was consistent with NIMS, the CSEPP
Program Office submitted the draft guidance to the NIMS
Integration Center (later the Incident Management Systems
and Integration Division) for comment. The planning and
programmatic guides were revised slightly in 2008, and then
recombined into one program guide in 2012.

CSEPP issued the final program guide in March 2019
(Figure 5.7). The 2019 Program Guide provided, as described Figure 5.7 CSEPP Planning
in the preface, “guidance and best practices for implementing Guidance
the program as described in the CSEPP Strategic Plan. The
guidebook’s intent is to serve as a how-to document and is deliberately descriptive rather than
prescriptive. The content focuses on and is organized by the 12 CSEPP National Benchmarks . . .
[and] also includes several appendices designed to supplement information in the base document
or serve as reference tools.”

The guidebook reflected DHS national preparedness doctrine, including the FEMA National
Preparedness System and National Preparedness Goal. Primarily organized around the 12 CSEPP
National Benchmarks (see Table 4.2 in Section 4) the 2019 Program Guide also included 13
appendices addressing other aspects of the program: history and technical background, risk
snapshots of the two remaining sites (Blue Grass and Pueblo), budgeting and execution,
assessments, communications systems, closeout planning, policy papers (a recapitulation of the
program policy decisions from 1991–2001), training resources, references, acronyms and
alignment to the National Preparedness System.

5.8.2 Software Tools

The program developed several techniques and software applications to aid emergency planning.
Two of them involved application of project-management concepts to emergency response.
Others included an electronic template for drafting plans, an evacuation modeling tool, and tools
to analyze protective action strategies and integrate them into emergency decision-making. These
innovations advanced the state of the art in emergency planning and serve as a valuable model
for addressing other hazards that can result in serious emergencies with little or no notice.

5.8.2.1 Tools Based on Project Management

In the mid-1990s, IEM undertook a planning initiative that involved what became known as the
Systems Analysis approach, in which the defined response actions from CSEPP plans and
implementing procedures were loaded into Microsoft Project® software, and task dependencies

 112

and predecessors were established. Emergency managers were then asked to identify the time
required to execute each of the sequential steps in their emergency plans and procedures, and
these times were added to the project software. These data were used to produce a Gantt chart
showing the amount of time required to execute key actions. IEM overlaid this timeline onto a
timeline showing the amount of time available to implement protective actions, derived from the
accident planning base and historical meteorology for the specific chemical installation.

This process demonstrated, in graphic form, that the traditional emergency management
approach — receiving initial notification of an event, recalling EOC staff and elected officials,
activating the EOC, assessing the information received, and then making PADs — was woefully
inadequate for chemical stockpile-related hazards. Response times based on this traditional
approach exceeded the hazard arrival times by a large interval; the plans as written would be
ineffective in protecting the public.

As a result of the Systems Analysis, additional guidance was issued that emphasized the need for
CSEPP emergency plans to pre-define protective-action strategies for various accident scenarios;
elected officials would pre-approve these strategies so that the individuals at the off-post warning
point could initiate default public warning and protective action strategies immediately upon
notification of a chemical emergency.

Another planning tool that expanded upon the process initiated in the Systems Analysis effort
was the Emergency Response Synchronization Matrix (ERSM) that Argonne developed. The
ERSM adapted a military planning model and associated tool (synchronization matrix) to
emergency response planning, allowing for modeling and synchronization of all plans (for
installations, multiple counties, and multiple states) to identify areas where plans did not connect
properly or where one plan assumed that something would happen that was not addressed in the
corresponding plan.

Argonne grouped response actions into six research-based and supported response operating
systems (ROSs) in the ERSM, each covering a broad, critical function essential to response.
Each ROS was divided into a set of supporting task groups or functions.173 Later, the ROSs were
slightly modified and adopted as the Emergency Response Outcomes (EROs) for the CSEPP
exercise program (see the discussion in Section 5.9.2.4).

The ERSM allowed users to chart tasks against time and link them to other tasks through a
dependence relationship (finish-to-start, where one task must be completed before another begin,
or start-to-start, where one task would start at some interval after the start of the preceding task).
It also charted decision points and allowed for tracking of resources. ERSM allowed linking of
multiple response operations to facilitate tracking of dependencies and resources across response
organizations. Generally, Argonne staff would construct a site ERSM in close consultation with

173
 Hewett, P.L. Jr., J.E. Mitrani, W.C. Metz, and J.J. Vercellone, 2001, “Coordinating, Integrating, and
 Synchronizing Disaster Response: Use of an Emergency Response Synchronization Matrix in Emergency
 Planning, Exercises, and Operations,” International Journal of Mass Emergencies and Disasters, November,
 Vol. 19, No. 3, pp. 329–348. The ROS concept was linked to the Response Stream concept in exercise
 evaluation; see discussion of integrated performance evaluation in Section 5.9.2. See also the “Lesson Learned
 on Using a Synchronization Matrix for Exercise Planning” in Volume 2 of this report.

 113

local planners (or would provide training to local staff on how to construct their own), then
review the results in a “roll-up” session with multiple agencies. These sessions allowed staff to
review the “big picture” time chart of response operations and identify task dependencies, key
decision points, and resource limitations that might affect response efforts.

The ERSM for a site could also be used to chart expected actions during an exercise, serving as a
tool for exercise planning and control.

5.8.2.2 Planning Template

The CSEPP Emergency Operations Planning Template grew out of the effort to bring CSEPP
planning guidance into conformity with the NIMS, allowing planners to re-orient their plans
along NIMS lines. The EOPT used commercial, off-the shelf software in a structure that
permitted the user to generate a customized EOP that met the needs of the community, was
consistent with NIMS, and integrated with plans from other levels of government.

The EOPT came preloaded with sample plan language that users could adapt to their needs,
along with references to appropriate sections of the NRF, NIMS document, and current FEMA
planning guidance. Guiding planners through the process of creating and revising EOPs, the
EOPT was designed to be usable in settings from small jurisdictions, where only a few people
are involved in planning, to large organizations, where planning involved a collaborative process
among multiple agencies. Developed for CSEPP jurisdictions, it was structured for all-hazards
planning and therefore had utility beyond the CSEP Program.

CSEPP released the first version in September 2006. Several updates followed, implementing
changes based on user input and aimed at remaining current with changing doctrine and guidance
documents, as well as other software developments. Version 2.0 employed Microsoft SharePoint
as a mechanism to provide user jurisdictions with a high degree of control of their own planning
sites and to integrate with Microsoft Office applications. Subsequent versions incorporated
revised reference material drawn from updated guidance. The application was eventually adapted
to an online environment and renamed EOPT for SharePoint Online.

During the EOPT development process, the FEMA CSEPP Program Office actively sought input
from all levels of the user community, plus two FEMA divisions: the Incident Management
Systems and Integration Division and the Preparedness Policy, Planning, and Analysis Division.
This input provided the basis for upgrades and improvements. The FEMA CSEPP Program
Office provided users with training and support services to ensure that they could effectively use
the software. Use of the EOPT extended beyond CSEPP; it was also used by several non-CSEPP
states: Alaska, Idaho, Iowa, Virginia, Kansas, and Washington.

 114

5.8.2.3 Additional Tools

CSEPP developed additional tools to assist in the development and analysis of protective action
strategies. These included two automated tools developed by ORNL, the Protective Action
Evaluator for Chemical Emergencies, followed by a more sophisticated tool called Protective
Action Dose Reduction Estimator. These automated tools allowed emergency managers to model
response actions using planning base accident scenarios, different meteorological conditions,
zone-specific evacuation times from evacuation time estimate studies, and warning time
variables to simulate various protection strategies and refine their plans accordingly. Ultimately,
even more sophisticated simulation tools were developed and embedded into the D2-Puff and
WebPuff software to allow it to serve as both a planning and response tool.

ORNL also developed the Oak Ridge Evacuation Modeling System (OREMS), a simulation tool
for evacuation planning. OREMS could be used to estimate clearance times for evacuating an
area, predicting traffic bottlenecks, and evaluating traffic control strategies.174

5.9 Exercises
Exercises were a key component of the CSEP Program from the beginning. Joint on-post/off-
post exercises were provided for in the August 1988 MOU between FEMA and the Army and
reaffirmed in subsequent MOUs and JMFRs. Among CSEPP staff, exercises were widely
considered a valuable and successful aspect of the program.

Each CSEPP site conducted an annual exercise beginning in 1991 or 1992.175 For a time, there
were two tiers of exercises, with more elaborate exercises (Federally Managed Exercises or
FMEs) alternating with simpler, more locally controlled exercises (Alternate Year Exercises or
AYEs). An FME was a mandatory, Federally-evaluated readiness assessment of a community’s
full capabilities to respond to a chemical stockpile accident. An AYE was an option available to
each CSEPP community to use the exercise process to train personnel, evaluate new plans or
procedures, correct known issues, or address other issues. Because few communities chose to
conduct AYEs, the option was removed in the 2009 reissuance of the exercise guidance. Once
the stockpile was eliminated at a site, exercises were discontinued. In all, more than 170 full-
scale CSEPP exercises were conducted.

The scale of CSEPP exercises was considerable. Each exercise required several months of
planning and coordination between the Army, FEMA, State and local response agencies, and
support contractors. Each exercise also entailed participation by hundreds of individuals and
thousands of person-hours of effort. On average about 100 people would travel to the exercise
site to serve as evaluators and controllers and provide administrative support. Exercises were
conducted under the direction of Army and FEMA co-directors.

174
 Sorensen, J., and B. Vogt, 2006, Interactive Emergency Evacuation Guidebook, February.
175
 Except Pine Bluff Arsenal, which began in 1993. Most sites began with a TTX or direction and control exercise
 before moving to a full-scale exercise.

 115

The regularity with which CSEPP exercises were conducted makes the exceptions significant.
Changes to the schedule were not undertaken lightly. On a few occasions, State or local
jurisdictions refused to participate because of ongoing disputes over program policy or
funding,176 and a few exercises were canceled because of disasters. The 1994 exercise in
Alabama was postponed because of the Palm Sunday tornado outbreak that affected parts of
Alabama, Georgia, and the Carolinas. The September 11, 2001, terrorist attacks occurred the day
before the Deseret exercise was scheduled, and the exercise was cancelled. The Pueblo
Community CSEPP exercise, scheduled for May 6, 2009, was postponed because of concerns
about the H1N1 flu virus, and 2020 exercises in Colorado and Kentucky were scaled back due to
the COVID-19 pandemic (see Section 5.9.8).

CSEPP exercise policies and practices evolved over time as the program sought to solve specific
problems, and the exercise program was integrated into the larger process of managing and
gauging readiness. In addition, techniques developed for CSEPP were later adopted on a national
scale for homeland security exercises. Key developments and milestones are detailed in
Sections 5.9.1 through 5.9.8.

5.9.1 Development of Exercise Program and Policy

The CSEPP exercise program drew from at least two independent exercise pedigrees. On the
Army side, there were existing chemical response exercise programs and a developed body of
doctrine on military exercises. On the FEMA side, the REP Program had been developed to
promote and measure preparedness for communities near nuclear power plants (see Program
Antecedents, Section 2.6). Army and FEMA exercise culture differed in some respects, and those
differences had to be bridged for CSEPP. Both organizations regarded exercises as both a
training event and a readiness assessment. The FEMA approach was more assessment-oriented, a
concept derived from the REP Program where a utility’s license to operate a power plant was
conditioned, at least in theory, on adequate community preparedness. The Army’s approach was
more training-oriented as CSEPP exercises were nested among a host of other training drills and
quarterly CAIRA (later CIMRA) exercises. Finding an approach that balanced the two was
critical. Both Army and FEMA exercise programs had previously employed outside evaluators,
and this practice was applied to CSEPP.

CSEPP established a national-level Exercise IPT to engage Army, FEMA, State, and local
program staff in developing exercise policy. The IPT issued Exercise Policy and Guidance for
the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, commonly referred to as the “Blue
Book” within the program because of its blue cover. The program published eight major versions
of the Blue Book between 1992 and 2019, with the last version (Change 1 to the 2019 version)
published in February 2021.

The Blue Book contained guidance on exercise scheduling, exercise planning including pre- and
post-exercise tasks, and expected participation levels, plus detailed evaluation guidance.

176
 In March 1993, for example, Tooele County, Utah, refused to participate in a CSEPP exercise pending resolution
 of an impasse regarding the number of sirens to be located in the county. In 2002, Alabama and six Alabama
 counties did not participate in an Anniston exercise because of a budget dispute.

 116

5.9.2 Innovative Features of CSEPP Exercise Program

Under the direction of the Exercise IPT, CSEPP developed and implemented a number of
significant policy and practice innovations, including use of an extent-of-play agreement (XPA),
simulation cell (SimCell), master scenario events list (MSEL), and integrated performance
evaluation (IPE). CSEPP also undertook an extensive and innovative effort to exercise the crisis
communications aspect of response.177 These innovations are detailed in Sections 5.9.2.1 through
5.9.2.5.

5.9.2.1 Extent-of-Play Agreements

Planning for every emergency response exercise involves setting goals and determining which
parts of the response will be demonstrated, simulated, or not tested. As the scale of the exercise
and the number of organizations and players increases, this process becomes increasingly
complicated. Aside from the greater number of participants, planners must consider the links
between them: one organization’s ability to demonstrate its capabilities may depend on the actual
or simulated actions of another. To address these issues, the CSEPP exercise program
emphasized that representatives from each jurisdiction participating in the exercise should
clearly identify what they wanted to learn from it and hoped to demonstrate in terms of
capabilities. This information was recorded in an XPA that served as a kind of contract between
the jurisdiction and the exercise co-directors. The XPA stated what capabilities each jurisdiction
intended to demonstrate during the exercise and how they intended to do it. The use of XPAs
facilitated accountability and efficient exercise design.178

5.9.2.2 Simulation Cell

CSEPP exercises featured use of a SimCell to stimulate exercise play and enhance realism. A
SimCell is a group of exercise controllers (generally in a central location, but sometimes
distributed geographically and linked through an online system) who interact with exercise
players via telephone and other means (e.g., social media). The SimCell serves as a hub for
exercise stimulation, monitoring, and control. On the stimulus side, SimCell controllers perform
two key functions: calling in exercise injects and portraying non-playing organizations. Both
functions involve role-playing by the controller. To call in an inject, the controller assumes the
identity of a simulated person; for example, a concerned resident (“is my child’s school in the
affected area?”), or a local Congressional staffer calling for a status report. Portraying non-
playing organizations is a more passive role and involves fielding calls that — in a real event —
would go to organizations that are not playing in the exercise. For example, if an exercise
player’s SOP called for notifying the EPA of a hazardous material release, but the EPA was not a
participating organization, the player would call the SimCell to make the notification instead.

177
 For an overview of the development of the CSEP Program’s innovative exercise program, see the “Lesson
 Learned on Exercise Evaluation Design and Guidance” in Volume 2 of this document.
178
 See Lerner, K., and G. Yantosik, 2014, “Exercise Extent-of-Play Agreements,” Domestic Preparedness Journal
 Vol. 10 Issue 2, February (available at https://domesticpreparedness.com/articles/exercise-extent-of-play-
 agreements, accessed July 2025) and the “Lesson Learned on XPAs” in Volume 2 of this document.

 117

In CSEPP exercises, use of a SimCell gave exercise participants an opportunity to work through
their checklists and procedures and allowed them to get a sense of just how busy they might be
during an actual emergency response. Particularly for staff who interacted with outside entities
(e.g., the public, higher headquarters, State or Federal agencies, public officials, or the media),
calls to and from the SimCell provided a sense that the emergency and response would not take
place in a vacuum, and they should expect that in a real emergency, there would be intense
interest from those outside entities.

SimCells also provided a centralized location for exercise control. SimCell controllers would
track the status of exercise actions and identify unusual trends or circumstances. They provided
situational awareness for the exercise directors. That awareness, in turn, allowed for control of
CSEPP exercises to be adjusted “on the fly” as exercise play unfolded.179

Virtual SimCells

A SimCell could be costly to implement if staff or contractors must travel to the site of the
exercise. Alternatively, local staff could be used, but that could take them away from
participating in the exercise, or from other duties. In addition, using local staff could
significantly reduce the team-training aspect of the exercise for participants.

To implement a SimCell without these drawbacks, a virtual SimCell system was developed to
provide SimCell functions from one or more remote locations. This provided the benefit of a
SimCell without diverting local staff from the exercise or requiring large numbers of staff or
contractors to travel. However, to be effective, the exercise controllers implementing the virtual
SimCell had to be able to cross-communicate and share information before, during, and after the
exercise.

CSEPP developed “Virtual SimCell” software to facilitate implementation of a virtual SimCell
for exercises. Virtual SimCell was an application based on the Virtual Community Platform
(VCP) developed at Argonne, allowing SimCells to be established through a digital network.
This approach was developed and tested beginning in 2015 at PCD and BGAD to support
CAIRA exercises (medium-scale exercises primarily limited to Army participation, as opposed
to full-scale, multi-jurisdiction CSEPP exercises). Following testing and refinement, virtual
SimCells were used either as the main SimCell tool or for the Army/Federal table’s use in every
CSEPP exercise. Argonne continually updated and improved the application’s feature set based
on accumulated user experience.

Using Virtual SimCell, controllers would see a status board showing a spreadsheet-like display
of the injects to be used for the exercise, with pre-loaded information such as inject text, caller
persona, who to call and when, etc. Other cells would be left blank for the controller to fill in
when the call was made (e.g., time the call was made and the response or result). Controllers

179
 See Lerner, K., 2013, “Using a Simulation Cell for Exercise Realism,” Journal of Emergency Management
 Vol. 11 No. 5, September/October (available at
 https://www.researchgate.net/publication/259347345_Using_a_simulation_cell_for_exercise_realism, accessed
 July 2025), and the “Lesson Learned on SimCells” in Volume 2 of this document.

 118

could also add new, ad-hoc messages if desired to adapt to developments during the exercise.
Key events, such as public warning messages, could also be logged using the application.

Anyone with access could use the status board to get an overview and monitor progress of the
exercise control process.

The application also allowed controllers to chat using a sidebar, to check in and confirm they
were in communication with the group, get assistance, compare notes, ask questions, and share
information on an immediate basis.180

Student Staffing of SimCells

CSEPP exercises in Kentucky were particularly large and complex because of the number of
local organizations involved. Major participants included the Army installation, Madison County
(where the installation was located), the Commonwealth of Kentucky, and a ring of nine
surrounding counties that were either at risk of being affected or might be called on to host or
support evacuees.

Each jurisdiction generally would receive a few dozen calls during the exercise; in the 2016
exercise, the range was 15 to 109 scripted calls among the participating jurisdictions.181 Between
scripted calls, receiving calls, follow-ups, and ad-hoc calls undertaken in reaction to exercise
events, a Blue Grass SimCell could easily go over 500 calls during the 4 to 5 hours allotted for
the exercise.

To facilitate this extensive SimCell operation in a cost-effective manner, and incidentally
provide a disaster-response educational benefit for students, beginning about 2006 the CSEPP
program supplemented SimCell staffing with students from the nearby Eastern Kentucky
University (EKU). Students from a variety of EKU programs, including fire protection
administration, occupational safety, homeland security, and emergency management participated
as SimCell volunteers in the annual CSEPP Kentucky community exercise. Student volunteers
received read-ahead materials and a 4-hour training session prior to the exercise. Each year about
20 to 25 students would participate. The program was enthusiastically supported by EKU
faculty.182
