5.9.2.3 Master Scenario Event List

The MSEL (usually pronounced “measle”) was a chronological timeline of expected actions and
scripted injects into exercise play. Developed as part of the exercise planning process, MSEL
was discussed and agreed to among the participating organizations at a MSEL conference. The
MSEL was a sort of master script for the exercise, mapping out what scenario events and
information would be provided to the players, where, and when, and also outlining what the
expected player responses would be. A typical CSEPP exercise MSEL contained a couple of
hundred entries. Each MSEL record contained a designated scenario time; an event synopsis; the
name of the controller responsible for delivering the inject; if applicable, special delivery
instructions; the task and objective to be demonstrated; the expected response action; the
intended player; and a note-taking section.

5.9.2.4 Integrated Performance Evaluation

The Exercise IPT developed the IPE methodology for evaluating CSEPP exercises and
implemented it beginning in 1999. IPE emphasized evaluator observation and assessment of the
outcome of play rather than simply checking off a list of expected actions. The performance-
oriented approach relied on the expertise of the evaluator to determine whether the functions and
purposes of emergency response were being successfully demonstrated.183

The 6-year period from 2000 to 2005 featured a continual process of review and revision of the
guidance. Initially, the exercise evaluation was organized according to seven “response streams.”
In May 2003, the response streams were replaced by six ROSs (see Section 5.8.2.1). By
September 2004, the ROSs became eight “emergency response outcomes” (EROs).

Appendix C to the Blue Book contained guides for exercise evaluators, organized around the
ERO framework. Each ERO was broken down into a set of component tasks. Exercise evaluation
guides (EEGs) were included for each task. The EEGs broke down each task into component
steps and provided references and other information.

5.9.2.5 Exercise of Crisis Communications

CSEPP exercises incorporated “crisis communications” from the beginning. The term crisis
communications as used here refers to distribution of information about the emergency to the
public through communications to news media, plus direct communications to members of the
public and public officials who call in, and (later in the program) social media communications.
It is distinguished from direct public alert and notification, covered in Section 5.1.

The program realized early on that a chemical emergency would draw intense media attention.
Effective crisis communication would be key to a successful response and to the public’s
perception of the response. This realization drove development of training, planning, facilities,
and staffing for public information, along with an exercise program designed to mimic the
challenges of dealing with media during an emergency response. Emergency public information

183
 For additional details, see the “Lesson Learned on Exercise Evaluation Design and Guidance” in volume 2 of
 this document.

 120

functions were incorporated into exercises through a number of innovative practices including
use of mock news media reporters and, later, social media platforms to stimulate play. These
practices are described in Section 5.11.

5.9.3 Additional Preparation for Exercises

In addition to the months-long process of planning and preparation described above, CSEPP
exercises required evaluator training; assembly of an online exercise library of reference
documents for evaluator/controllers; and in many cases, preparation of an alternate scenario
package to be implemented in case inclement weather prevented some of the live, hands-on
activities planned for the installation.
 • Evaluator Training. A well-trained corps of evaluators was essential because of the IPE
 method’s reliance on evaluator expertise. IPE evaluator training was developed to
 introduce the concept and practice of IPE; review the policies and criteria included in the
 Blue Book; and teach techniques for exercise observation, recording, and analysis. The
 evaluation course was supported with video from a DCD CSEPP exercise. Video was
 shot at the Tooele County EOC, the DCD EOC, and traffic control points. This training
 was delivered, usually by Argonne staff, a couple times a year from 2005 through 2024.
 • Document Library. Prior to exercises, CSEPP contractor DETech compiled extensive
 information for use by evaluator/controllers, exercise trusted agents, and co-directors.
 This information, posted on the CSEPP Portal, included CSEPP plans and SOPs for all
 participating organizations, including the Army, State and local jurisdictions, and
 hospitals; exercise documents such as XPAs, MSELs, exercise plans, exercise health and
 safety plans; evaluation forms and templates; and evaluations from the previous exercise.
 Controllers and evaluators used this information before exercises to prepare for their
 assignments and afterward to help document their findings.
 • Plan B. At the 2013 Pueblo CSEPP exercise, a thunderstorm moved into the area just
 before the exercise was about to start. The exercise was to begin with on-post play
 depicting a chemical accident involving outdoor activities within the CLA. The timing of
 response and details of hazard projections and protective actions both on-post and off-
 post would all begin with this on-post initiating event. Because those outdoor activities
 could not be conducted during a period of lightning hazard, there was a danger that the
 exercise would be significantly delayed or disrupted. Exercise directors quickly
 developed a workaround to initiate play, and the storm passed fairly soon. But after that,
 the Army determined that a backup plan or “Plan B” should be in place for future
 exercises in case of a similar weather disruption. Plan B preparations were made for
 subsequent CSEPP exercises at Pueblo and Blue Grass. Those preparations included
 special injects mimicking the reports that would be expected to come in from chemical
 workers and security, fire, and medical responders operating in and near the CLA. The
 intent was to portray a normal sequence of radio calls from the field and allow play in the
 EOC, JIC, Security Control Center, and other indoor locations to proceed as normally as
 possible. In addition, videos depicting various accident scenarios were available to show
 in the installation EOC to simulate video from CLA monitoring cameras. These
 preparations were made for each exercise thereafter, but (luckily) never had to be
 implemented.

 121

5.9.4 On-Post Exercise Elements

Because on-post events drove much of the play at CSEPP exercises, including the scope and
timing of off-post response demonstrations, on-post scenarios and exercise control were crucial
to the overall success of exercises. These exercise elements received particular attention, and the
approach evolved over time.

Scenarios

Scenario design was integrated with other aspects of exercise planning, such as determining what
organizations were going to participate, setting exercise objectives, and developing an XPA. The
scenario had to be sufficiently robust to drive demonstration of each organization’s chosen
objectives consistent with the XPA. Scenario design also had to account for two additional
factors. First, plausibility — the scenario had to depict an event in the tail end of the probability
curve of severity in order to drive a full response, yet still be plausible enough to not be a
distraction. Secondly, concern over sensitivity — because any scenario implies some failure of
safety systems, there is always concern over the public-relations aspect of the scenario; it should
not cause unnecessary panic in nearby residents.

A scenario design team with representatives from all key jurisdictions and organizations
developed a number of products, including a narrative description of the scenario, supporting
injects for controllers, identification of associated hazards and safety equipment to be used by
field players, a list of needed props and simulations, simulated meteorological conditions, and
ground truth hazard assessments.184

Control

On-post exercise control had to be exceptionally close and nimble. Over the years the Army
exercise directors experimented with different arrangements to obtain such tight control. They
found that a key factor was to dedicate an exercise controller to track all critical on-post player
activity in real time, especially at the chemical storage facility and in the installation EOC.

On-post exercise control typically involved several evaluator/controller personnel in the field (in
the CLA and in nearby field command post and response areas) and several more in the EOC.
The key innovation to assure effective and response exercise control was to add an EOC/Field
Liaison (EFL) position. The EFL position was instituted by Army CSEPP exercise directors to
monitor exercise progress and address situations with potential safety or security implications or
that threatened to derail exercise demonstrations. The EFL was expected to take the lead in
identifying and fixing these problems, so that lead controllers could continue to focus on their
basic exercise control duties. The EFL was located in the installation EOC but was in constant
radio contact with field controllers.185

184
 For additional detail on CSEPP scenario design, see the “Lesson Learned on Exercise Scenario Design” in
 Volume 2 of this document.
185
 For additional detail on this practice, see the “Lesson Learned on Control of Complex Exercises” in Volume 2 of
 this document.

 122

5.9.5 Exercise Documentation

The CSEPP exercise program was well documented; the evaluation team prepared a written
report after each exercise summarizing the participation, scenario, sequence of events, and
evaluation, including identified strengths and areas of concern. With extensive discussion of
exercise play and other aspects of the exercise, each report typically ran from 50 to 200 pages in
length. Exercise reports for every CSEPP exercise from 1992 until the end of the program were
posted on the CSEPP Portal.

CSEPP also maintained a database of the individuals who served as evaluators, controllers, and
support staff at CSEPP exercises from 1992 on.

To help assess the overall exercise plan and develop the exercise evaluation report, each exercise
evaluation team compiled a significant events timeline. By mapping the time sequence of major
events, evaluators could analyze the timeliness of the response and pin down when and where
delays might have occurred. Including a significant events timeline in the after-action report
(AAR) also helped readers visualize the sequence and timeliness of the events and responses that
the report discussed. Examples of significant event times included the time the accident was first
called in on-post, when off-post jurisdictions were notified, when public warnings went out,
when EOCs were activated, when medical assistance was requested, when news releases went
out, etc.

The process to develop and use timelines incorporated three key steps: identify key times to be
recorded; allocate recording of the times among the exercise evaluators/controllers; and collect,
consolidate, and distribute the time data immediately after the exercise. Identifying key event
times to be recorded was part of the exercise design process; in practice, the key times list was
fairly consistent from one exercise to another. Allocation of responsibility to collect key times
was part of the exercise team process, and after the exercise, the times were collected and
consolidated into a list in time for evaluation activities the next day.186

5.9.6 Community Profiles

Responding to GAO reports and other criticisms, CSEPP managers recognized that some type of
overall community readiness assessment was needed. They developed CSEPP Benchmarks to
assess community readiness, making each CSEPP community IPT responsible for maintaining a
record of its own status of accomplishment and deficits based on the Benchmarks (see
Section 4.2.2). This record was called the Community Profile. Exercise performance became a
component of this profile, which was included in the final report for each annual exercise. The
Community Profile allowed the evaluation team to compare the community’s perspective on its
capabilities to the performance demonstrated at the exercise. It also provided a means for a
community to demonstrate a new capability or to identify an unmet need.

186
 For additional detail on exercise timelines, see the “Lesson Learned on Tracking Exercise Flow with a
 Significant Events Timeline” in Volume 2 of this document.

 123

5.9.7 Relationship to Homeland Security Exercise Program

In the early 2000s, the Federal government began a major initiative to integrate Federal, State,
and local emergency response resources and develop a unifying set of doctrines, plans, and tools
with which to coordinate response to disasters.187 Policy direction was set by HSPDs 5 and 8,
and implementation measures included development of several substantial DHS documents: the
NIMS, NRF, and HSEEP. The latter was in many ways modeled on the CSEPP exercise
program. HSEEP tools and concepts including the MSEL, SimCell, exercise outcomes, and
EEGs were all adapted from CSEPP precedents.

5.9.8 Adaptation to COVID-19 Pandemic

As the COVID-19 pandemic began to affect the United States in March 2020, numerous
measures were put into place to control spread of the disease through restriction of travel and
enforcement of social distancing. To comply with these measures and protect staff, planning and
execution of CSEPP exercises had to be modified. In particular, the program needed to:
 • Reduce the exercises’ impact on local responders to avoid interfering with ongoing
 pandemic response — especially medical providers who were already implementing
 emergency health plans and procedures.
 • Avoid having the exercise itself become a “spreader event” posing a risk to exercise
 participants. This requirement involved changing the planning, evaluation, and control
 processes to protect personnel.
 • Recognize response agencies for ongoing pandemic response activities. CSEPP policy
 already provided for giving response agencies “credit” for recent actual responses;188 this
 provision was widely applied to acknowledge the capabilities demonstrated during the
 pandemic response.
 • Accommodate changes in the way agencies would realistically respond to a chemical
 emergency in a pandemic environment. For example, EOC staffing patterns were
 changed and staffing of mass evacuation centers and shelters was avoided to maintain
 social distancing among persons displaced by the emergency.

CSEPP convened a national-level committee to develop a program-wide exercise health and
safety plan (HASP) in April 2021.189 The HASP provided a safety framework for planning
exercises. It also acknowledged that planning would have to adapt as conditions and
requirements changed over time.

The HASP provided guidelines for pre-exercise travel, exercise arrival and execution, and post-
exercise procedures. It identified COVID-related hazards and spelled out safeguards, reporting

187
 The reasons for this initiative are beyond the scope of this report but certainly include the rise of terrorism as a
 national threat, including the September 11, 2001, terrorist attacks, and development of the ICS as a unifying
 principle to organize emergency response in a scalable way.
188
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 2019, CSEPP Exercise Implementation Guidance, December, Change 1, February 2021,
 p. 15.
189
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 2021 CSEPP Exercise COVID-19 Health and Safety Plan, April.

 124

requirements, protocols for seeking treatment, and available resources for medical treatment in
the event that personnel felt unwell during the activity.

The 2020 exercises in Colorado and Kentucky were dramatically altered in terms of schedule,
scope of activities, and logistics of participation and evaluation. In 2021, as the conditions of the
pandemic improved, the program returned to full-scale exercising in Colorado and Kentucky.
Again, adjustments were made to protect personnel during exercise planning and execution. As
the widespread availability of vaccinations reduced the need for social distancing, exercises in
2022 and 2023 returned to something closely resembling the pre-COVID format.190

5.10 Personnel
A fully staffed and qualified workforce across all program components — FEMA and Army
headquarters, FEMA region offices, Army installations, State EMAs, and affected Counties —
enabled CSEPP to meet the mandates of Congress and the expectations of the stockpile host
communities. Over the course of the program, personnel costs accounted for a little less than
20% of total program expenditures.

Attendance at the 2005 CSEPP National Workshop provides a snapshot of the numbers and
distribution of personnel associated with the CSEP Program. The workshop roster included
385 attendees, shown in Table 5.3 according to organization type.

Table 5.3 Attendees at 2005 CSEPP National Workshop
 Attendees Organization Attendees Organization
 130 County, Local, and CAC 23 FEMA Region
 71 State 15 Army HQ
 69 Contractor 11 FEMA HQ
 30 Army Installation 10 Misc. (Red Cross, GAO, etc.)
 23 Hospital/EMT/Other Medical 3 Indian Tribe (CTUIR)
While everyone at the workshop had some connection to CSEPP, many were not employed on
the program full-time. On the other hand, some CSEPP staff did not attend the workshops; for
example, ANCA never sent any of its 15 EOC personnel.

5.10.1 Ensuring Qualified Staff

Recognizing that the eight stockpile communities had varying levels of preparedness capabilities,
initial CSEPP guidance sought to equalize these capabilities by (1) identifying key staff
functions needed at the State and county levels and (2) establishing desired qualifications for
each position. These guidelines were used in staffing the State and county CSEPP offices with
qualified personnel capable of fulfilling the responsibilities identified for that particular level of
the program.

190
 For further details on the program’s adaptation to COVID, see the “Lesson Learned on Adapting CSEPP
 Exercises to a COVID-19 Environment” in Volume 2 of this document.

 125

As CSEPP offices began staffing up, many State and county positions were filled by individuals
whose qualifications were at variance with established guidance, for a number of reasons: the
need to transfer existing personnel into CSEPP, lack of available candidates with the requisite
skills, and preferences for specific candidates.

In response, CSEPP developed and maintained an extensive program of staff training and
professional development. Training was made available for program personnel (see Section 5.6),
and several CSEPP IPTs provided professional development opportunities in their specific
functional areas. These training and professional development efforts built staff skills, ensured a
greater equivalency of capability across sites, and helped maintain continuity of capabilities
despite staff turnover.

The length of the program meant that staff turnover was inevitable. One aspect of turnover
derived from Army practice: installation commanders typically were replaced (rotated to other
assignments) every two years.

5.10.2 Employment Status of FEMA HQ and Region Staff

At the outset of the program, Army and FEMA officials expected that demilitarization at all eight
stockpile sites would be completed quickly enough that a permanent full-time CSEPP staff
would not be needed at either FEMA HQ or the respective FEMA regional offices. Therefore,
positions not already staffed by existing permanent, full-time Federal employees when the
program started were filled with term employees who would have a fixed period of employment
with limited opportunity for extensions, in accordance with Federal personnel rules. These
employees would be eligible for Federal employment benefits but would face termination at the
end of their employment period. Term employees were hired in the FEMA HQ and regional
CSEPP offices to fill critical program positions.

As it became clear that demilitarization would take considerably longer, FEMA determined that
the term appointments would need to be extended. At the request of FEMA and with approval
from the Office of Personnel Management (OPM), the term employees in the HQ and regional
offices received repeated 3-year extensions.

By 2008, demilitarization had been completed at Edgewood, Maryland, and Newport, Indiana,
and was on track to be completed by 2012 at four of the remaining six sites. Several HQ Program
staff members had also indicated their plans to retire in the next 3 years. Facing this situation, in
2010, FEMA requested one more 3-year extension for all term employees to maintain full
staffing until there were only two sites remaining. This request was denied by OPM, citing rules
limiting the number of term extensions. To maintain the needed staff levels, OPM began
converting the term positions to permanent full-time positions. Term employees were required to
reapply for their positions as they were posted.

5.10.3 Employment at Army Installations and CMA

Each Army installation maintained a staff of approximately 12–18 employees to support CSEPP.
Installation staffing included a CSEPP Coordinator, hazard analysts, public affairs officer,

 126

communications staff, and other staff sufficient to minimally staff the EOC on a 24-hour basis.
The size of staffing at CMA (CBDCOM, SBDCOM) varied over the term of the program.
During the program’s mature phase, it was about 10–12 staff, including some full-time staff and
some matrixed positions. Full-time HQ staff included a program director, contracting/financial
officer, budget analyst, meteorologist, public affairs officer, research and development engineer,
and site coordination staff. Matrixed positions included a medical coordinator and legal advisor.
The site coordination staff oversaw operations and coordinated meetings, agreements, resource
needs, and exercise planning for the installations, as well as participating in CSEPP national IPTs
as needed.

5.10.4 Use of Contractors

Throughout the program, CSEPP relied on government contractors to provide technical support
to supplement program staff capabilities. The breadth of CSEPP emergency preparedness
responsibilities, coupled with the limited number of authorized staff positions, created the need
for outside contract assistance. In the CSEPP communities, contractor assistance enabled CSEPP
to develop and maintain essential communications and public warning systems, assess hazards,
determine optimal protective action strategies, create and support information sharing and grants
management systems, oversee construction of EOCs, manage exercises, create effective public
outreach programs, interact with CSEPP stakeholders, develop program closeout strategies, and
perform many other functions needed to meet the program’s maximum protection mandate.

Contractor support was also used to augment program staff at Army and FEMA HQ, FEMA
regional offices, Army installations, and State EMAs. Contractor employees were placed within
the FEMA Region VI and FEMA Region X offices to assist with program execution. A
contractor also worked in the DASA office to provide coordination and liaison between FEMA
and the Army on CSEPP programmatic issues. Argonne supplied on-post contractor support to
each Army chemical storage installation except Newport, plus Ft. McClellan, which was
adjacent to and affected by ANAD.

Contractor support provided an avenue to access specialized capabilities such as HAZMAT
medical response, crisis communications, meteorological instrumentation, plume dispersion
modeling, and software design. Contractors also allowed the program to meet surge demand for
controllers and evaluators during exercises.

Contract support organizations over the life of the program included Argonne, Applied
Computing Systems, Battelle, BDM, C3N, DETech, IEM, MP3, Northrup Grumman, ORISE,
ORNL, PNNL, SAIC, Schneider Engineers, and TetraTech.

5.10.5 End-of-Program Personnel Transition

As CSEPP ended at each site, and finally nationally, funded positions were eliminated at the
local, State and Federal levels. Part of the closure planning process at each site involved efforts
to anticipate, plan for, and ease the transition process for staff whose positions were being
eliminated. Some retired, others moved on to other positions within the same organization or
another organization — in some cases, to other chemical storage sites. Staffing sometimes

 127

became an issue at a given site as staff moved to other positions prior to the end of CSEPP
(see detailed discussions in Section 6, Program Closeout).

5.11 Public Outreach and Education
Beginning in 1989, CSEPP officials recognized the need for an aggressive public education and
outreach effort to familiarize the communities with the storage and disposal program, associated
hazards, and how to prepare against the possibility of a chemical event. While the stockpiles had
already been present at the installations for many years, residents in those communities had not
been provided detailed information about the chemical agents, the hazards they posed, and the
most effective protective actions to take in an emergency. One reason was Army security
restrictions (later modified) that limited release of information about the stockpile.

Public education and outreach programs were initiated at each of the eight stockpile sites to
enhance public safety and promote understanding of the CSEP Program. Program managers
agreed to fund local PIOs in each CSEPP community. The original guidance ensured funding for
CSEPP-dedicated PIOs at the State level and in the IRZ counties closest to the stockpiles.191 In
the early years of the program, Army and FEMA PIOs began working with State and county
PIOs to develop and refine emergency public information capabilities for their respective sites,
focusing on communicating timely and accurate health and safety information in the immediate
aftermath of an event. During this period, the first local outreach efforts began. Calendars and
brochures with CSEPP information were distributed, websites were launched, and newspaper
inserts appeared on a regular basis. Figure 5.8 shows a CSEPP calendar distributed to residents
in the planning zone for Pine Bluff.

Initially, there was little coordination among the eight CSEPP communities; most public affairs
activities and initiatives were designed and executed locally. Even within a community, there
were sometimes challenges to collaboration. The Army maintained a public affairs program that
was focused on the disposal process, the depot mission, and details about the types of agents and
munitions in storage. At the same time, a different Army public affairs office worked with
FEMA to promote CSEPP and the corresponding emergency public information functions.

191
 CSEPP Policy Paper 5, County Public Information Officers for CSEPP.

 128

 Figure 5.8 Calendar Distributed to Pine Bluff CSEPP Community

This arrangement resulted in separate storage, disposal, and emergency preparedness outreach
messages — although the distinctions among the missions mattered little to the public. Between
1995 and 1998, the Army also established local outreach offices at each site to provide accurate
and timely information to stakeholders and respond to questions and concerns about emergency
preparedness, chemical weapons storage and disposal efforts, secondary waste operations, and,
later, the closure process. These offices were open to the public; staffed with contracted public
affairs specialists; and stocked with brochures, fact sheets, photographs, and cut-away models of
the munitions and the disposal plant to assist in explaining the mission. Yet in some
communities, there was little coordination between the outreach office’s public affairs activities
and county emergency management programs.

In 1993, CSEPP assembled a Public Affairs Subcommittee with representatives from FEMA, the
Army, and the 10 States and 40 counties that were initially part of the program. The goal of the
Subcommittee was to establish and direct a coordinated public education approach in CSEPP
communities. To help with the professional development of Subcommittee members and provide
a forum for sharing lessons learned and best practices, CSEPP held Public Affairs Conferences
on an annual basis. These conferences typically included briefings from site representatives, field
tours, and training workshops, with presentations captured on video and later shared with other
CSEPP staff.

 129

Still, there was not always broad financial or staff support for an ambitious public affairs
program. That began to change in March 1998 with the airing of a 60 Minutes television news
story titled “Whose Chemical Weapons?” which offered a harsh assessment of CSEPP and its
ability to protect the public, with a specific focus on the Umatilla community. In April 2001, a
critical five-part series titled “Umatilla: Mistrust and Money” ran in The Oregonian, which
argued that the community was still unprepared for a chemical event.

After a public awareness survey in 1999 indicated the need to improve knowledge about
protective actions for those living near stockpile sites, a Public Affairs IPT (PA IPT) was formed
in November 2000. The PA IPT defined its mission as seeking to ensure a “public that can and
will act appropriately upon notification of an emergency at a chemical installation.” The IPT
developed a charter, approved by the Army and FEMA CSEPP offices, to ensure appropriate and
equitable representation from all governmental and organizational levels. Army and FEMA
management challenged the IPT to take a more critical look at how the program was managing
public education and outreach and to work in concert to identify and correct weaknesses.

Just prior to formation of the PA IPT, the Umatilla community received funding to initiate a pilot
public outreach campaign that included surveys to measure knowledge of protective actions,
targeted outreach messages, and paid advertising to communicate preparedness messages
directly to residents. The PA IPT reviewed the successes of the Umatilla pilot program and
began to work on a similar plan that could be used for all the sites. In early 2001, the PA IPT
introduced the “Ready, Set…Act!” (RSA) Communication Plan. One of the plan’s key elements
— “focus on the residents, not the program” — was significant in focusing outreach efforts only
on the specific knowledge that residents needed to protect themselves. Outreach products
developed prior to RSA included fact sheets that detailed program funding and the amount of
emergency preparedness equipment purchased. Following implementation of the communication
plan, such inward-looking products were replaced with outreach messages that focused on
community and individual preparedness.

CSEPP communities produced calendars, websites, and promotional items bearing the RSA logo.
The PA IPT developed print, radio, and television advertising templates consistent with the RSA
plan, and standing RSA and Feedback subcommittees composed of PA IPT members were
established to guide the various initiatives. An RSA Notebook was first published in March 2002
to inform other program areas about public affairs activities, and it was updated periodically. The
PA IPT also publicized its work through presentations and product fairs at CSEPP National
Workshops, aided by new public affairs working groups formed at the site level.

CSEPP public outreach included local print, radio, and television advertising campaigns;
coloring and activity books for schoolchildren; CSEPP brochures; dozens of program-wide and
site-specific fact sheets; crisis communication pocket guides; the NewsWriter training CD; PA
legal guidance; and numerous training, outreach, and education videos. A general guide for
PIO/PAOs, the CSEPP Public Affairs Planning Guidance Compendium Workbook, was initially
published in 2005 and later replaced with the CSEPP Public Affairs Guidebook.192

192
 ANL, 2014, CSEPP Public Affairs Guidebook, December, available at
 https://portalfiles.blob.core.usgovcloudapi.net/training/CSEPP%20PA%20Guidebook%2012-15-14.pdf,
 accessed November 2023.

 130

Early CSEPP planning guidance contained suggested position descriptions that outlined minimal
qualifications and experiences for CSEPP PIOs. However, hiring was overseen by the individual
jurisdictions and agencies, and in some cases PIOs were hired without any relevant experience or
specific education that prepared them for the position. Other PIOs entered the program from
previous public relations or media backgrounds. Training a diverse PIO workforce in basic crisis
communication practices and the specific skills required in emergency preparedness became
another priority for the program.

CSEPP PIO training began in 1994 with a JIS/JIC Workshop. The workshop was continuously
updated and offered, and CSEPP was widely credited for its early adoption and expansion of
JIS/JIC concepts, particularly as they relate to the ICS and NIMS. The JIC organizational model
pioneered by CSEPP was later adopted by FEMA and published nationally in FEMA 517: Basic
Guidance for Public Information Officers (November 2007).193

Another training course developed and presented to CSEPP audiences, Spokesperson Training,
was designed for elected officials, military commanders, and other key decision-makers to make
them more effective in communicating with the media and the public in an emergency. The
course was first offered in 1997 and later expanded to include PIOs and PAOs.

In recognition of the growing importance of social media, CSEPP developed two training
courses to provide instruction and hands-on training in using the latest communication
technologies and platforms: the CSEPP Public Affairs and Emergency Preparedness Course and
the New Communication Technologies Workshop.

As CSEPP matured and emergency managers increasingly viewed chemical agents as part of a
broader “all hazards” focus, especially post-9/11, a wide range of emergency responders
regularly participated in CSEPP-sponsored crisis communications training. This strengthened
local preparedness and augmented the number of trained public information staff available to
respond to a chemical agent emergency. During the first 20 years of the program, CSEPP
personnel presented hundreds of training courses to thousands of participants at the sites,
enhancing local practitioners’ knowledge of key PA techniques and improving their ability to
function effectively in an emergency. To augment in-person training, in 2009, CSEPP produced
the Communicating Public Information in Emergencies video, focusing on how to develop a
credible and comprehensive JIS and JIC. This video earned CSEPP its third Aurora Award for
excellence in video production. In addition to CSEPP-specific training, PIOs regularly attended
FEMA-sponsored public information courses locally and at the Emergency Management
Institute in Emmitsburg, Maryland. In 2009 and 2010, CSEPP PIOs participated in curriculum
review groups and pilot tested local-level PIO courses for FEMA, many of which included
CSEPP-originated concepts.

The planning and evaluation of exercises also evolved over the years. Emergency public
information became one of the emergency response “outcomes” evaluated at every exercise.

193
 Superseded in December 2020 by NIMS Basic Guidance for Public Information Officers, available at
 https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/documents/fema_nims-basic-guidance-public-information-officers_12-
 2020.pdf, accessed November 2023.

 131

Exercises were routinely used as opportunities to inform the community by inviting real-world
media to observe.

CSEPP exercises pioneered the use of simulated news media in the form of mock media
reporters, adding greater realism to exercises and improving players’ emergency information
response capabilities. These “reporters” operated as real media would in a chemical emergency,
calling participating agencies, conducting interviews, attending news conferences, and
developing print and video coverage for analysis by exercise evaluators.194 Most exercises ended
with a joint news conference featuring the installation commander and lead off-post officials,
attended by mock media and streamed online within a closed exercise environment. An online
simulated news network, Exercise News Network (ENN), hosted online print and video reports
from mock media in real time during the exercise. ENN was later expanded and re-branded as
the Exercise Training Network (ETN). As described in an Argonne Public Affairs Science and
Technology (PAST) Fusion Academy brochure, during an exercise,

 Based on scenario events and player actions, reporters produce radio, print and
 broadcast news stories and post to ETN for players to monitor and respond to.
 Mock media go to field play locations, conduct interviews, and participate in
 news conferences broadcast live on ETN. Simulated social media messages are
 generated and disseminated during the exercise by SimCell and mock media staff
 to reflect what the public and media would be saying during an actual emergency.
 Some messages are scripted in advance to drive play and are injected at pre-
 determined times. Other ad hoc messages are injected in real-time based on how
 the exercise play is unfolding and in response to player actions.195

In 2012, CSEPP undertook a systematic study of emergency public information issues identified
during exercises. A set of 35 exercise reports were surveyed, five exercises each from seven
CSEPP sites. Exercise evaluations of emergency public information found a few recurring
themes: EAS messages and news releases, coordination of information among participating
jurisdictions, internal information flow, and information provided by call takers.196

In later years, as social media became an important mode of communication, CSEPP
incorporated social media play into the public information component of exercises, establishing a
social media “safe space.” The safe space allowed use of actual social media platforms, but
within a limited-access environment to avoid any potential confusion or alarm to the public.
Safe-space environments were created and used during exercises for the social media platforms
Facebook and Twitter (later X).197 PIOs and PAOs monitored these platforms during exercises

194
 See Lerner, K., and M. Meshenberg, 2024, “Use of Mock Media in Emergency Management Exercises: the
 Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program Experience,” Journal of Emergency Management Vol. 12
 No. 2 (March/April), and the “Lesson Learned on Use of Mock Media” in Volume 2 of this document.
195
 ANL PAST Fusion Academy brochure, The Most Challenging & Realistic Media Simulation, available at
 https://etn.anl.gov/files/etn-mock-media-fact-sheet-2.pdf, accessed May 2025.
196
 See the “Lesson Learned on Common Issues in Emergency Public Information” in Volume 2 of this report; see
 also Lerner, K., and K. Bertram, 2014, “Common Issues in Emergency Public Information as Identified in
 CSEPP Exercises,” Journal of Homeland Security & Emergency Management, 11(1): 155–168.
197
 For details on this effort, see the “Lesson Learned on Social Media Exercising” in Volume 2 of this document.

 132

and participated by posting news releases and responding to messages posted by SimCell role
players.

The social media “safe space” was formalized as ChirpEX, part of the ETN. On ChirpEX,
participants could practice realistic, fast-paced social media interactions. ChirpEX allowed
exercise participants to post, monitor, and respond as on actual social media platforms, with a
broad array of fictional media outlets and public personas available to engage with response
agency accounts. During exercises, responders dealt with a barrage of questions, comments, and
concerns from the “public” and “media” and reacted in real time. From these minute-by-minute
interactions, players were able to experience the pressure and see the challenges of
communicating effectively in a crisis.

ChirpEX allowed users to practice and enhance a variety of tools and skills, including situational
awareness, media monitoring, rumor control, rapid response, information dissemination,
response techniques, visuals and imagery, and social media strategies. ChirpEX was designed to
be flexible and scalable, supporting a range of exercise and training options — from drills,
tabletops, and full-scale exercises to training workshops and classroom sessions. In addition to
its development and deployment for CSEPP public affairs activities, ChirpEX was adapted for
extensive use by other local, State, and Federal agencies.

While fortunately there never was a major incident or accident involving chemical agents at any
of the stockpile sites, CSEPP’s work advanced emergency planning and preparedness in CSEPP
communities and strengthened emergency public information programs across the United States.
There were numerous examples in which local capabilities developed and refined by CSEPP
were effectively used in responding to other emergencies, such as the detection of a possible
intruder at Deseret Chemical Depot in September 2002; a major railroad accident in Talladega
County, Alabama, in January 2006; and the crash of a Navy jet in Umatilla County, Oregon, in
March 2006. In addition, after Hurricane Katrina struck in August 2005, many CSEPP-trained
PIOs were deployed to the area to support response and recovery efforts.

As the CSEP Program closed out, the final function of many CSEPP PIOs and PAOs was
communicating the impact of closure to community members.

5.12 Administrative Support
Like any other public program, CSEPP required oversight, planning, and administration. The
Administrative Support benchmark referred to the administrative functions completed by each
jurisdiction to support preparedness and response activities and ensure program funds were
properly tracked. Key areas of administrative support included these elements:
 • Regulatory Compliance,
 • Proper Execution of Cooperative Agreement, and
 • Ongoing Program Analysis, Evaluation, and Management.198

198
 Anniston CSEPP Community Profile, August 25, 2006.

 133

Planning and management of program costs and budgeting were central components of project
administration and included developing, negotiating, and reporting progress on executing the
CAs that funded State and local preparedness activities. It also included development and review
of the LCCEs that allowed longer-term projection and tracking of program costs. FEMA issued
new CA guidance each year, and the program employed a series of increasingly sophisticated
software processes to allow electronic development and submittal of CAs. After 2011, CSEPP
CAs were managed through a web application called CSEPP WebCA, which generated the
required narrative statements, work plans, funding requests, and financial forms to meet the
requirements of OMB Circular A-102 and its 2014 replacement, OMB Uniform Guidance
(2 CFR Part 200). The required documents were automatically produced and submitted through
this process. Specifically, the software produced the following documents:
 • SF 424, Application for Federal Assistance,
 • SF 425, Federal Financial Report,
 • Program Narrative Statement,
 • FEMA Form 20-16, Summary Sheet for Assurances and Certifications,
 • Work Plans for all CSEPP-funded personnel, and
 • Request for Funding Worksheets.

In addition to execution of these specific budgeting, oversight, and reporting requirements,
Administrative Support included other activities and items involved in conducting an effective
CSEP Program that are not attributable to other benchmarks. For example, costs for office
operations and maintenance, utilities, supplies, contract services, and travel to meetings and
workshops were all allowable under the CA guidance for this benchmark. Administrative staff
supported CSEPP implementation by developing staff work plans, purchasing supplies,
maintaining an equipment inventory, administering contracts, and monitoring projects.

5.13 Inclusion of Persons with Disabilities or Access and
 Functional Needs
As noted in other sections, in the event of a chemical emergency, protective action would have
had to be taken quickly, and for the most part, would involve self-protection actions taken by the
public. From the beginning, the CSEP Program sought to ensure that all persons would be
protected regardless of disabilities or other special circumstances that might affect their ability to
receive a warning and complete a protective action.

Terminology for this aspect of preparedness evolved over time, and various terms were used to
describe the persons served by these programs, including “special populations,” “special-needs”
individuals, people with limited English proficiency (LEP) and people with access or functional
needs (AFN). This document generally uses the term disabilities, access and functional needs
(DAFN)199 to describe the persons served by these programs, but in some cases uses the terms of
art that were in use during the time period being described.

199
 See, e.g,, New York City Emergency Management’s webpage, Community Planning for Those with Disabilities,
 Access & Functional Needs, accessed October 2025 at https://www.nyc.gov/site/em/ready/community-planning-
 disabilities-access-functional-needs.page.

 134

As initially defined in the program, special-needs individuals were those who required assistance
or special equipment to receive notification of an emergency and/or take action to protect
themselves. Special populations included people with perceptual, physical, or mental disabilities;
people not proficient in English; people without transportation (essentially, who did not have a
car); and other categories of people who might need help in an emergency. Special populations
also included those not able to perform protective actions independently due to being in an
institutional setting. The latter included groups as diverse as schoolchildren, nursing home
residents, hospital patients, and prisoners.

Protection for special populations was built into CSEPP policy from the beginning. The 1996
CSEPP Planning Guide had a section titled Special Populations that identified groups at risk and
provided for identifying special needs individuals and institutions with special needs populations
in the CSEPP communities. The Guide also included criteria for ensuring that special
populations’ needs were met through outreach, planning, and specific response procedures and
equipment as appropriate. This policy of inclusion was carried through all the iterations of
planning guidance, up to the final program guidance in 2019. The 2019 guidance provided for
DAFN inclusion in Benchmark 5 (Coordinated Plans), Benchmark 10 (Protective Action
Strategies) (including Strategies for Implementing SIP, Evacuation, and Host Facilities), and
Benchmark 11 (Public Outreach/Education).

Demonstration of service to DAFN populations was also built in to exercise policies. The final
Bluebook version addressed inclusiveness in the construction of XPAs and MSELs (i.e., the
process of setting exercise goals and devising injects to drive play) and in evaluation criteria.
Care of DAFN populations appeared in numerous evaluation criteria including those pertaining
to plan validation, public warning, public information, implementation of protective actions, and
operation of shelters, among others.

The remainder of this section addresses particular aspects of the program’s DAFN inclusion,
specifically legal requirements, and background, inclusion of institutionalized populations,
identification of persons requiring or requesting assistance, associated public-outreach programs,
and late-program activities aimed at inclusiveness at the Colorado and Kentucky CSEPP sites.

5.13.1 Legal Requirements and Background

Federal law requires inclusion of people with disabilities in Federally-funded programs. The
primary legislative authorities are as follows:
 • Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 (Title VI), which prohibits discrimination based
 on race, color, and national origin in Federally assisted programs and activities and
 requires recipients to take reasonable steps to provide persons with LEP meaningful
 access to programs and services; and
 • Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act of 1973 (Section 504), which prohibits
 discrimination based on disability in Federally assisted programs and activities.

These authorities were implemented through an Executive Order and a variety of Federal
regulations and guidance documents. President Clinton issued EO 13166, Improving Access to

 135

Services for Persons with Limited English Proficiency, on August 11, 2000. President Trump
rescinded Clinton’s EO, issuing EO 14224, Designating English as the Official Language of the
United States. However, the latter Order specified that “. . . nothing in this order, however,
requires or directs any change in the services provided by any agency. Agency heads should
make decisions as they deem necessary to fulfill their respective agencies’ mission and
efficiently provide Government services to the American people. Agency heads are not required
to amend, remove, or otherwise stop production of documents, products, or other services
prepared or offered in languages other than English.”200

In 2007, FEMA appointed a disability coordinator as required by the Post-Katrina Emergency
Management Reform Act of 2006.201 The disability coordinator “advises the FEMA
Administrator on issues concerning people with disabilities before, during and after disasters.
The Office of Disability Integration and Coordination was created in 2010 to support the work of
the disability coordinator.” It has policies and guidance documents relating to inclusive services
on its website. 202

In 2017, the DHS Office of Civil Rights and Civil Liberties (CRCL) and the FEMA Office of
Equal Rights (OER) conducted compliance reviews pertaining to inclusive services at the two
remaining CSEPP sites in Colorado and Kentucky. The reviews had “an emphasis on the
obligation to ensure meaningful access for persons with LEP and effective communication,
program access, and physical access for persons with disabilities and others with access and
functional needs.”203 The Office of CRCL found much to praise and also made a number of
recommendations for improvements at both sites.204 Those recommendations, combined with
other factors, led to a renewed emphasis on diversity and inclusion in CSEPP205 (see
Section 5.13.6 for details.)

200

 https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/03/06/2025-03694/designating-english-as-the-official-
 language-of-the-united-states, accessed May 2025.

 documented in the Pueblo Community CSEPP Exercise 2021 Final AAR, July 7, 2021, Community Readiness

 136

5.13.2 Institutions with Special Populations

To identify special population facilities in the IRZ and PAZ, CSEPP emergency planners used an
assortment of commercial directories and area lists. For each location, they collected contact
information and population and facility characteristics data and kept it up to date. As necessary,
they developed measures designed to protect these populations based on their location, facility,
and special needs; protective actions that might be required; and available resources for
assistance. CSEPP provided planning assistance and special A&N equipment to facilities as
needed, in some cases, including special equipment for enhanced SIP, such as air pressurization,
mainly in schools. Air pressurization (or over-pressurization, the term often used) employed
special HVAC equipment to maintain a building at a slightly higher pressure compared with the
local environment, thus reducing or eliminating the infiltration of potentially contaminated air
from outside.

5.13.3 Identification of Persons Requiring or Asking for Assistance

Planning for assistance to DAFN populations began with knowing who and where they were —
identifying the relevant individuals and institutions in the particular geographic area. CSEPP
communities employed a combination of active and passive measures to identify their special
populations.

Seven of the eight CSEPP communities (all except DCD) employed a self-identification process
for individuals to describe their special needs and request assistance. A postage-paid, self-
registration card or form was included in the annual public information mailing. Registrants were
asked to check the applicable descriptive boxes and mail the card or form back. The descriptive
boxes included information to characterize the individual’s needs (e.g., whether sight or hearing
impaired, mobility impaired, and/or in need of transportation or shelter assistance) and allow
local officials to assist.

In three of those communities (ANCA, ECA, and UMCD), the self-registration process was
supplemented with saturation or direct-mail surveys. Baltimore and Kent counties distributed a
single emergency needs survey for the ECA site. Four special-needs-related, direct-mail surveys
were conducted in Morrow County, Oregon, and one in Umatilla County. These surveys,
combined with follow-up telephone calls and/or home visits, resulted in Morrow County
identifying 2% of its population as potentially needing assistance during a chemical emergency
because of a disability or functional need. The registry data were hosted online under a contract
with the InterMountain Education Service District. Emergency planners and responders could
access the data as needed.

In Alabama, the six-county CSEPP community established a baseline registry with a saturation
mailing in 1999 to 152,287 IRZ and PAZ addresses, followed up by annual verification and
saturation mailings through 2011. The verification effort also included intensive data
deconfliction and validation telephone calls. In Calhoun County, the self-registration program

 137

resulted in receipt of approximately 5,000 enrollee responses each year, representing 4 to 4.5%
of the county’s population.206

In all instances, self-registration information was protected in accordance with local procedures
for handling personally identifiable information. Enrollment records were usually maintained in
both paper and electronic form. Alabama used a purpose-designed database tool (Special
Population Planner, or SPP) that allowed users to search registry data by disability type, location,
demographic characteristics, registry history, and type of protection equipment provided. SPP’s
GIS capabilities allowed for further analysis of registry data by risk areas, plume model output,
and proximity to special facilities or shelters. Figure 5.9 shows a GIS map from the SPP.207

In Oregon, Morrow and Umatilla Counties made their registry data available to planners and
responders through a secure website. Users were able to generate reports for planning and
response using search criteria such as planning zone, zip code, or disability.

Some locations chose not to use a registry; rather, they used the “know your community”
approach. For example, Tooele County, Utah, decided to forgo a self-registration process
because of the small number (225) of households in the county. County emergency management
officials felt that they knew the needs of county residents from making periodic visits to each
household. They also factored the community’s culture of self-help into the decision not to use a
registry.

Changes to special-population registration occurred during each community’s two decades or so
of emergency preparedness planning. In Utah, CSEPP officials implemented a statewide special-
needs registry, and the DCD community referred special-needs individuals to it in 2008. The
Tooele County EMA had access to their data through a web-based system. In Oregon, Morrow
and Umatilla Counties replaced their annual calendar self-registration cards with direct-mail
surveys to IRZ residences starting in 2002. In Alabama, the last verification mailing (2010)
asked enrollees whether they agreed to have their personal data transferred to their respective
county EMAs and 911 dispatch centers. For Calhoun County, 100% of the registrants selected
that option, and their data were incorporated into the 911 Center’s computer-assisted dispatch
system. Madison and Estill Counties in Kentucky discontinued the self-registration card in the
annual calendar, replacing it with an open letter to residents asking those with special needs to
call their respective county to be enrolled in the special-needs database. Madison County also
added a self-registration element to its 2010–2011 TAR replacement program; included with the
new TARs was a return-mail postcard for enrolling in the county’s special-needs registry.

206
 The process of identifying and including special needs populations in the Alabama CSEPP community was
 described in “Lesson Learned on Identification of Special Needs Populations” in Volume 2 of this document and
 in two journal publications: Metz, W.C., E.A. Tanzman, L.A. Nieves, and V. Holt, 2005, “Preparing people with
 special needs for emergencies: The Alabama Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP)
 model,” Journal of Emergency Management, July/August (Vol. 3, No. 4); and Metz, W.C., P.L. Hewett, Jr.,
 J. Muzzarelli and E.A. Tanzman, 2002, “Identifying Special-Needs Households That Need Assistance for
 Emergency Planning,” International Journal of Mass Emergencies and Disasters, 2002 (Vol. 20, No. 2).

 in Volume 2 of this document.

5.13.4 Specialized Equipment for Alert, Notification, and Sheltering in Place

CSEPP used specialized equipment to address two issues associated with DAFN individuals:
notification of an emergency, and implementing protective actions. The issue of notification
arose primarily for people with auditory disabilities, given that warning systems generally rely
on an auditory alert (siren or radio-tone). The protective action issue was twofold; first, it might
be difficult or impossible for some individuals to evacuate quickly. Enhanced SIP could be a
preferred alternative for those individuals. Second, some people would have difficulty
implementing SIP and might need special equipment or assistance to shelter effectively.

 139

5.13.4.1 Alerting Systems

Most indoor alerting systems offered options for those
with special needs, including strobe light adaptors
(Figure 5.10) and vibrating devices for those with
hearing disabilities (or for use in noisy environments).
In some communities, indoor alerting systems were
distributed to special-needs individuals or institutions
beyond the zone where general distribution was
made. Also, some communities made follow-up
phone calls to those on their special-needs registries
to verify that they had received a TAR with the
appropriate accessories and that they understood how
it worked and what to do upon receiving an alert. Figure 5.10 Tone Alert Radio with
 Strobe Attachment
5.13.4.2 Sheltering in Place

In communities where air-filtering equipment was distributed to close-in residents to enhance
SIP, provisions were made to ensure that DAFN populations could benefit from the equipment.
At Anniston, PRACs were made available to all IRZ residents. Several measures were
implemented for people with disabilities or access or function needs. First, to make sure they
received the PRACs, the community reached out to registered individuals to recommend they
obtain one and to help with delivery and setup. Second, while the general population was
instructed to keep their 39-lb. PRAC in the delivery box, those with special needs were
encouraged to have their PRACs set up and operational in their designated safe room. Third, the
equipment vendor was authorized to add a remote on/off switch and a small indicator lamp so
that people with hearing disabilities would know whether the unit was operating.

Similarly at Umatilla, approximately 1,200 RAF units were distributed to residents of Irrigon,
Oregon, and members of the CTUIR. Because they distributed the filtration units door-to-door,
officials were able to identify and accommodate those with special needs. Also at Umatilla, SCM
Engineering and Consulting evaluated three residences in the IRZ identified during home visits
as occupied by persons with special needs, and they completed simple, inexpensive fixes to
allow the residents with disabilities (e.g., wheelchair user, emphysema) to adequately SIP in an
emergency.

Another program implemented in the Anniston community provided respiratory protection
devices to those in the Pink Zone immediately adjacent to ANCA. These devices, which supplied
filtered air through a hood, were to be worn for limited periods while sheltering in place or
evacuating. Residents were instructed to pick up their devices themselves at a distribution center
where a nurse could check them to assess their ability to use the hood and then be fitted and
trained in its use. For persons with special needs who could not travel to the center, the
equipment vendor arranged a home visit. A visiting nurse ensured the person could use the
equipment and a trainer provided instruction.

 140

5.13.5 Educational Outreach to Increase Awareness, Preparedness, and Self-Help

All CSEPP communities initiated aggressive public outreach activities, including mailings;
television, radio, and print advertising; information booths and speakers at community events;
local websites; toll-free information lines; and other measures. These outreach activities included
programs aimed at special populations. The communities included information specific to
special-needs preparedness in annual mailings, detailing additional steps to be taken in
developing family disaster plans. Some outreach campaigns also suggested that residents assist
neighbors who might require it and make plans to help them if they had time. Most CSEPP
communities also had a telephone number for persons with special needs to call if they needed
assistance during an emergency.

Most communities distributed special-needs preparedness and planning literature and
instructional videos that were prepared by CSEPP, American Red Cross, FEMA, and DHS.
Local advocacy groups were often brought into the outreach efforts as a conduit to engage their
special-needs constituents in preparedness and response planning activities. Several communities
installed telephone typewriter/telecommunication device for the deaf (TTY/TDD) lines to allow
those who relied on this form of communication to send and receive vital information.

Communities offered one-on-one training and education during door-to-door distribution and
installation of special equipment, and conducted targeted training for groups with special needs
during demonstrations of special equipment at senior centers, housing authorities, and other
facilities housing special populations.

Recognizing the importance of immediate aid and the limited ability of county and local
emergency responders to address thousands of special-needs individuals, the Anniston
community created an extensive program to identify and train volunteer caregivers. The
Alabama Caregiver Training Program was developed with the assistance and collaboration of
local caregivers and the target population. The program included training for both professional
caregivers from service agencies and informal caregivers from volunteer organizations. The
program distributed guides, workbooks, instructional videos, and quarterly newsletters focused
on nine key messages: creating help networks, developing emergency preparedness plans,
building an emergency preparedness kit, knowing the EAS, knowing the CSEPP zones,
acquiring the available CSEPP special equipment, preparing a safe room, taking other protective
actions, and preparing for other hazards. Program officials conducted outreach in English,
Spanish, Braille, and American Sign Language. All materials were available on an Alabama
Special Needs website. Caregiver training was multiplied through a “train the trainer” program
so that representatives of various organizations could carry the message back to their groups.

5.13.6 Late-Program Measures for Inclusion in Colorado and Kentucky

As noted in Section 5.13.1, program reviews by the DHS CRCL and FEMA OER resulted in a
renewed emphasis on policies to ensure an inclusive program. The updated approach to
emergency management programs was that diversity and inclusion must be “baked in” from the
beginning so that they are incorporated into planning, training, and exercising, rather than added

 141

as an afterthought to an otherwise mainstream-oriented program.208 CSEPP adapted its long-
established policies and programs of inclusion to implement the more modern approach.

CSEPP and its constituent jurisdictions pursued new inclusion goals through measures such as
appointing personnel to serve as AFN coordinators, creating an interjurisdictional working group
to review AFN policies, and developing tools to serve diverse populations in the communities
served by CSEPP. These measures ran the gamut of emergency preparedness, response, and
recovery including planning, exercising, public outreach, public warning, implementation of
protective actions, and recovery/remediation.

Ensuring an inclusive program involved a number of organizational and practical elements.
Organizational elements included assigned responsibilities, an inclusive working environment,
and a policy of inclusion. Practical elements included information gathering, planning,
communication/public warning, sheltering and recovery, public education/outreach, and training
and exercises. These elements are described below.

Organizational Elements

Assignment of Responsibility: Each site had a State-wide or CSEPP-wide coordinator and a
working group that met regularly. The State of Colorado appointed an AFN Coordinator within
the Office of Emergency Management. The AFN Coordinator served the entire State and worked
with county, municipal, and regional EMAs through newsletters and virtual meetings; the Pueblo
County CSEPP Coordinator was a regular participant in the monthly meetings. The Colorado
CSEPP IPT established a Civil Rights and Civil Liberties/AFN Advisory Group, which
developed a work plan with 22 specific tasks addressing AFN. The workplan was adopted by
the IPT.209

In Kentucky, there was a CSEPP AFN Coordinator within the Lexington Division of Emergency
Management. The Lexington AFN Coordinator served all of the CSEPP jurisdictions in
Kentucky and worked independently with each county to address the community’s needs and
capabilities.

Inclusive Working Environment: The process of inclusive emergency planning and preparation
involves all agencies and organizations that serve the public in an emergency: public health, law
enforcement, schools, nursing homes, Red Cross, and advocacy organizations that serve relevant
constituencies such as disability organizations and ethnic or immigrant service organizations. In
Colorado, the statewide AFN working group included representatives of State and county EMAs,
human services, public health, and agencies on aging, plus representatives of organizations such
as Disability Law Colorado, the Center Toward Self Reliance (nonprofit based in Pueblo), the
Independence Center (nonprofit based in Colorado Springs), and the American Red Cross.210

208
 Interview with Mark Shull, independent public relations practitioner, November 15, 2021. For further
 information on these late-program measures, see the “Lesson Learned on Creating an Inclusive Service
 Environment” in Volume 2 of this document.

In Kentucky, CSEPP AFN efforts were coordinated with public health departments, public
libraries (which often had programs serving the AFN community), and service organizations
such as the Commonwealth Council on Developmental Disabilities, Kentucky Commission on
the Deaf and Hard of Hearing, Bluegrass Council of the Blind, Kentucky Autism Training
Center, Cedar Lake Foundation, and Bridges of Kentucky.211

Policy of Inclusion: An important aspect of creating an inclusive emergency management
program is the concept of serving the entire community; an understanding that all elements of the
emergency management program — planning, preparation, facilities and equipment, training,
and exercising — must be inclusive as a matter of course. AFN coordinators in Colorado and
Kentucky used CMIST (Communication, Maintaining Health, Independence, Support and
Safety, Transportation) as a policy framework for efforts to serve the AFN community.

Practical Elements

Information Gathering: The CSEPP jurisdictions in Colorado and Kentucky developed
techniques to identify people with access and functional needs, together with their specific
resource needs, in the at-risk area. In smaller jurisdictions, emergency managers were often
aware of the institutions and residents with access and functional needs. Local school districts
had data on students’ native languages and participation in English as a second language (ESL)
programs. Local service agencies, such as meals-on-wheels and visiting nurse programs, had
information on disabled individuals in their service areas. General information on the prevalence
of disabilities and indicators such as age, language, living alone, and poverty (from Census data)
was provided on the Colorado Community Inclusion web page.212

Planning: The Kentucky CSEPP Community created a template for developing an AFN annex to
EOPs.213 Originally developed as part of the Lexington-Fayette County CSEPP Incident-Specific
Plan, the AFN Annex template was intended to foster consistent AFN planning among all ten
Kentucky CSEPP counties.

Communication and Public Warning: As described in the Kentucky County AFN annex
template, preparation for this element includes presentation of public warning messages in both
text and audio formats on public access channels and other alert systems (e.g., weather radio and
mobile phone systems), and translation to other languages as identified during information-
gathering efforts. News conferences used American Sign Language translators and Spanish
interviews, and closed captioning was available.214

Colorado acquired portable Assist2Hear “loop systems” that could be used at local gatherings —
for example, a public education event or a mass care shelter after an emergency — to facilitate
communications with hearing-impaired people. The loop system broadcasts an audio signal

directly to telecoil- (or T-coil) enabled hearing aids, cochlear implants, or headsets with loop
receivers. Each Colorado Department of Homeland Security and Emergency Management

Sheltering and Recovery: The Kentucky CSEPP AFN Annex Template specified that each
American Red Cross shelter in the CSEPP footprint would have access to a Designated
Integration Specialist (DIS). The DIS would be responsible for assisting people with functional
needs, identifying, and requesting durable medical equipment and consumable medical supplies,
and making any other reasonable accommodations. In the absence of a DIS, the Shelter Manager
would make requests for specialized assistance.215

Public Education/Outreach: AFN Coordinators in Colorado and Kentucky cast a wide net to
connect with the DAFN community and distribute information regarding emergency
preparedness. In Colorado, outreach efforts involved representatives of public health and DAFN
service organizations, as well as other community groups such as Councils on Aging and the
Colorado Latino Leadership, Advocacy & Research Organization.216 The Kentucky Community
CSEPP program developed a series of preparedness pamphlets addressing DAFN topics such as
cognitive impairments and hearing, vision, and mobility impairments. The pamphlets, along with
other accessible resources information, were available on the CSEPP Prepare Kentucky
website.217 The website also has a Spanish language version.

The CSEPP Portal also had a web page on whole community inclusion, with information on law
and policy, individual and community preparedness, evacuation, and sheltering and
decontamination.

Training and Exercises: Training and exercises associated with DAFN services included a
recommendation that all CSEPP-funded positions take FEMA Independent Study course IS-368,
Including People with Disabilities and Other Access and Functional Needs during Disaster
Response. In Kentucky, the Lexington AFN Coordinator hosted a day-long AFN Workshop in
September 2021, during which representatives from advocacy groups delivered presentations to
emergency managers from all over Kentucky, including those within the CSEPP footprint. The
Colorado CSEPP community also undertook a number of training, workshop, and exercise
initiatives aimed at inclusion.218

5.14 Reentry and Recovery
Reentry and recovery was recognized as an element of chemical emergency preparedness early
on in the program, and efforts were made to lay the technical groundwork for reentry and
recovery decisions.219 However, recovery received relatively little emphasis compared with
response-phase preparedness, which was seen as more directly related to public health and
safety. As time went on and some of the more urgent response-phase issues were addressed,
reentry and recovery gained attention. Recovery was identified as an area needing attention at the
2000 planning workshop,220 leading to formation of a working group that oversaw development
of reentry and recovery guidance.

5.14.1 Planning Guidance and Workbook

Recovery and reentry were addressed only in general terms in the body of the 1996 planning
guidance document. Although the “planning considerations” chapter included a section on
reentry (8.18), it was designated “to be developed.” However, the appendices contained more
detailed information. The term, “recovery phase” was defined in Appendix L, and Appendix M
was devoted entirely to recovery planning.

Appendix M, “Planning Guidelines for Recovery Phase Activities,” was divided into two main
sections: recovery phase decontamination and reentry and restoration. The decontamination
section offered general guidelines for decontamination planning and discussed the need for
setting priorities if decontamination was needed. The section also contained guidance on setting
up decontamination programs for critical assets, livestock and companion animals, human
remains, drinking water, personal property, real estate and terrain, food, and agricultural
products. The guidance acknowledged that “[t]he potential for off-post agent contamination
during any stage of agent storage and disposal is considered remote.”

The reentry and restoration section introduced a set of terms for particular types of reentry —
including restricted reentry, occupational reentry, emergency reentry, and unrestricted reentry —
to distinguish situations that might require selective reentry by specially trained and equipped
personnel.221 For example, temporary entry might be needed to perform a rescue operation or to
repair critical infrastructure. Topics suggested for reentry and recovery planning included
assessment of residual hazards; control of access to the affected area; collection of extensive data
on land use in the affected area; public information needs during recovery; and provision for
long-term relocation, including provision of social and medical services for displaced persons.

Several sites conducted planning and exercises focusing on recovery. The Colorado CSEPP
Community conducted a series of recovery tabletop exercises and developed a recovery plan, the

 Chemical Agent (GB, VX, or Mustard Agent) (availability announced in Federal Register, 55 FR 28940, July 16).
220
