 145

Colorado CSEPP Team Integrated Emergency Recovery Management Plan, which was revised
five times from 1996-2000. The Alabama CSEPP Community formed a Reentry and Restoration
Work Group (RRWG) in 1998 that included members from the installation, county, state, FEMA
IV and EPA IV. The RRWG developed the Anniston Chemical Stockpile Emergency
Preparedness Program (CSEPP) Reentry and Restoration Planning Guide. This guide was
drafted in 2003, and adjusted over the next few years to incorporate lessons learned from
recovery tabletop exercises, as well as developments in national incident management doctrine.
The final version was published in September 2007.222

At the national level, following the national planning workshop in 2000, CSEPP formed a
recovery working group and developed a workbook for recovery planning, which was published
in May 2003. The workbook recommended developing a community-wide recovery plan that
incorporated the installation and off-post jurisdictions in advance of any emergency. It described
the conditions and concerns that could be expected in the wake of a chemical event. The main
component of the workbook was a model recovery plan with chapters on hazard assessment,
access control, food and water protection, medical services, relocation, social services, public
information, claims and disaster assistance, and environmental remediation — many of the same
topics addressed in Appendix M of the 1996 planning guidance. The model plan was presented
as a simple word-processing document for planners to customize for local application. The
workbook also included appendices with background information on topics relevant to recovery
planning, including chemical agent exposure standards; listings of resources for sample analysis
and other technical services; and discussions of legal procedures associated with environmental
cleanup, disaster assistance, and pursuit of damage claims against the Army.223

The recovery plan workbook was also among the early CSEPP documents to embrace use of the
ICS. The model recovery plan provided position-by-position responsibilities, using an ICS-based
organizational scheme.

5.14.2 Recovery Exercises and Exercise Guidance

Recovery-phase activities were part of the CSEPP exercise process from the beginning, although
they were not emphasized to the same degree as were response-phase actions. Recovery-related
performance evaluation guides were developed under the oversight of the RRWG and the
Exercise IPT in 2003 and 2004 and incorporated into the June 2004 version of the Exercise
Bluebook as Outcome 8, Remediation and Recovery. Outcome 8 included tasks for
environmental removal operations; accident investigation; monitoring and sampling; protective
action decision making (including actions relating to potential food and water contamination);
reentry; public information; and care of evacuees including health screening, temporary shelter,
and financial assistance. Recovery-related evaluation guides were designed for evaluation in
EOCs because field demonstration of recovery tasks was not considered likely. The most
thorough demonstrations of recovery functions took place in TTXs specifically devoted to

222
 For additional detail on the efforts of the RRWG, see the “Lesson Learned on Local Reentry/Recovery Planning”
 in Volume 2 of this document.
223
 The process of developing the Recovery Plan Workbook is detailed in the “Lesson Learned on Reentry and
 Recovery Guidance” in Volume 2 of this document.

 146

recovery. Examples of such exercises include the February 2003 exercise at Pine Bluff, the May
2004 exercise at Pueblo, the March 2007 exercise at Anniston, and the 2015 exercise in Pueblo.

The 2003 Pine Bluff reentry/recovery exercise included 71 participants, support staff, and
observers from the Army, FEMA, and Arkansas State and local agencies. Participation included
the chemical activity commander, the installation commander, and installation support staff;
FEMA regional office staff; and State and local emergency management, public information,
environmental, and health agency staff. Participants were divided into the following groups:
Environmental, Fire/HAZMAT, Law Enforcement, Medical, Public Information, Schools,
Volunteer Organizations, and Policy. A scenario was presented in which there had been an
accidental release of nerve agent the previous day, and key response-phase actions had already
been taken: residents and nonessential on-post personnel were evacuated, decontamination
stations and reception centers were set up, roads were closed, and local air monitoring assets
were deployed. Each group was provided a list of questions to address regarding the next steps.
The groups formulated their responses and provided them back to the whole room. The scenario
and the groups’ responses were summarized in an AAR.224

The 2004 Pueblo exercise followed a similar pattern and included 90 participants from
36 agencies at the local, State, and Federal levels.225 Exercise controllers developed an
abbreviated scenario depicting a post-emergency situation in which approximately 2,000 people
had evacuated, access was controlled to the evacuated areas, about 200 people had self-
transported to area hospitals (including some with mild agent exposure symptoms, as well as a
majority of the “worried well”), and many questions remained regarding the extent of agent
exposure off-post and the potential for residual contamination.

Participating agencies were organized around tables by functional area, including Emergency
Assessment, Accident Site Hazard Mitigation, Emergency Management, Protection, Victim
Care, Evacuee Support, Policy, and Public Information. Each table was provided with a
statement of the issues/situations they were to address and questions to help focus their
discussion. The tables were challenged to develop a PowerPoint presentation addressing their
highest-priority issues, suggested solutions, and points of coordination with other functional
areas. Support staff captured the discussions and assisted with developing the presentations
(Figure 5.11). Each table then gave its presentation to the whole group, with questions and
answers following. The results were summarized in an after-action report.

The Alabama CSEPP Community held a reentry and recovery TTX in March of 2007. The

built on the ACE-07 scenario. In essence, the situation and response was carried forward into the
next day. The TTX followed a series of recovery-related activities in Alabama including revision
of the Anniston CSEPP Reentry and Restoration Planning Guide (May 2004), exercise of
recovery-related issues at the Anniston Community Exercise/Chemical Incident Management
Exercise (ACE/CIMX-06), and development of a recovery plan template for Alabama counties
in January 2007. By agreement of the Alabama IPT, the recovery exercise was not evaluated;
however, a descriptive report was prepared summarizing the activity.227

Participation included 34 personnel from the Army, FEMA, the State of Alabama, and Alabama
CSEPP counties. The exercise began with a situation briefing based on information collected
during the previous day’s response exercise. Exercise controllers asked participants to come
prepared to speak to their response status as if play had continued from the previous day: what
actions they would have taken by now, what resources they would have available, and what
challenges they would currently be facing. Participants then worked as a whole to the address
questions posed in a series of exercise-controller handouts. The handouts addressed elements

In 2014 and 2015, the Colorado CSEPP Community held a series of exercises to validate the
2012 Colorado CSEPP Recovery Plan and to enhance awareness and communication among the
involved departments and agencies regarding various aspects of recovery: establishing a Unified
Command, monitoring and sampling potential chemical deposition, and obtaining medical and
community services to assist and support the recovery process.

The Recovery Series included five exercises:
 1. The January 2014 Recovery Seminar familiarized participants with the Colorado CSEPP
 Recovery Plan.
 2. The June 2014 Recovery Management Workshop concentrated on Command & Control,
 how each organization fit into the Unified Command System, how recovery objectives
 and priorities are set, and who has authority to make reentry decisions.
 3. The November 2014 Incident Recovery Workshop discussed monitoring and sampling
 protocols to restore the environment in the affected area while protecting the health and
 safety of the public and the environment.
 4. The June 2015 Medical and Community Services Recovery Workshop detailed
 coordination between on-post and off-post medical services and provided information on
 community public health, medical, and community services actions during a Colorado
 CSEPP recovery effort.
 5. The Colorado CSEPP Recovery TTX (August 25, 2015) put the individual components
 together as participants worked through a simulated chemical accident at PCD.

Attendees and planners overwhelmingly indicated that their knowledge of the Colorado CSEPP
Recovery Plan, local capabilities, and their recovery partners increased as a result of their
participation in this exercise series.229

5.14.3 Issues in Recovery Planning

Planning for recovery in CSEPP touched many subjects, some of which might have been
controversial at times. In particular, during the recovery period, many legal issues could arise in
terms of authority for cleanup; responsibility for costs; and what compensation may be due to
persons, businesses, and governmental units affected by the event. Another issue was the
decision process for allowing reentry to or reoccupation of evacuated areas. What were the
technical standards for “safe” levels of chemical agent? How would those levels be measured in
the field? What would constitute sufficient data to conclude that an area was safe? The greater
the amount of data demanded, the longer its collection would take; however, the longer it took,
the more the community would be affected by displacement.

CSEPP organizations undertook multiple studies aimed at the technical aspects of determining
residual hazard levels and making reentry decisions, addressing these issues in the Chemical
Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) Off-Post Monitoring Integrated Product
Team (IPT) Report, December 1998, and again in the Final CSEPP Recovery Sampling and
Analysis Plan (RSAP) Protocol for Chemical Warfare Agent Accidents/Incidents, published by
the U.S. Army Center for Health Promotion and Preventive Medicine in 2008.231

230
 Richard W. Brletich, Chief, CSEPP Office, MEMORANDUM For Record, SUBJECT: Observer, Coach/Trainer
 Report for Pueblo Chemical Depot, Chemical Emergency Management Exercise 2018 (CHEMEX 18), AMCSM-
    CS, 6 June 2018.
231
 Both documents are available through a title search on the Homeland Security Digital Library website,
 https://www.hsdl.org/c/.

 150

Another recovery planning issue involved the relative roles of the Army, FEMA, and State or
Federal environmental and health authorities in the recovery and cleanup process. The Army
would be authorized (and obligated) under CERCLA and the NCP to clean up any agent spilled
and assist the community affected by the spill. At the same time, FEMA might be authorized
under the Stafford Act to assist the community — therefore, it was important to know where
each entity’s authority ended and the other began, or whether they would overlap. In addition,
State and local environmental and public health authorities would be exercising their authorities
for public protection. The State could exercise its RCRA permitting authority to oversee
environmental monitoring and cleanup actions.

The Kentucky CSEPP community addressed many of these issues as a group of local, State, and
Federal agency personnel worked together over a 4-year period to develop a detailed framework
for assessment of any potential release of chemical warfare agent. The process resulted in two
documents: a recovery sampling and analysis framework (RSAF) and a sampling and analysis
plan (SAP).

Development of the initial working draft for the RSAF took approximately 18 months of
cooperative effort led by a Kentucky CSEPP community working group and supported by SMEs
from Argonne. The working group held four sessions to present and discuss issues and develop a
path forward. They incorporated input from these sessions into an initial draft RSAF that was
reviewed in a series of conference calls and a workshop held in Madison County, Kentucky, in
January 2018. The final RSAF, completed in November 2018, was tailored to the unique aspects
of BGAD’s storage operation, the chemical agents present at BGAD, the environment and
climate of the Blue Grass area, and the community’s input and preferences.

Upon completion of the RSAF, the working group developed the SAP to guide implementation
of the RSAF, including incident command structure and resources, plus accompanying support
functions such as security, medical surveillance, and public information. The SAP was signed off
in May 2019.232

A notable component of the Kentucky community SAP process was the development of an
Execution Matrix. The group determined that, in light of the complexity of the sampling and
analysis process, the plan should include a simple, high-level, visual overview. This overview
would facilitate situational awareness and give future responders a sense of where they fit in the
overall scheme. The overview was provided by developing an Execution Matrix as an Excel
spreadsheet.

The Matrix was a table of actions versus time. Operational periods were laid out across the top,
and organizations down the left side. The bulk of the table then was filled in with text
descriptions of what each organization would do during each operational period (Figure 5.12).
Over the course of a few printed pages, the total progression of tasks over the life of the
sampling and analysis effort was depicted.

232
 For details on the RSAF and SAP and their development, see the “Lesson Learned on Sampling and Analysis
 Planning,” in Volume 2 of this document.

 151

 Figure 5.12 Sample Execution Matrix Page

Construction of the Execution Matrix required gathering (and coordinating) information from
several agencies regarding their roles and responsibilities after a chemical weapons accident. The
information was gathered using a simple, paper-based, visual system, reminiscent of some
methods of task organization under ICS. Large sheets of paper were posted along the walls
depicting a timeline of operational periods. Tasks to be completed were written on yellow sticky
notes and posted in the appropriate place on the timeline (Figure 5.13). After the information
from the sticky notes was input to a spreadsheet, workshop participants reviewed the draft
Execution Matrix, and a revised version was appended to the SAP. 233

 6 Program Closeout

The CSEP Program always had an expiration date. While the date was pushed back several
times, it was always clear that, eventually, the Army would eliminate the hazard and the
associated emergency preparedness requirement would end. The end was site-specific: demil
operations at Edgewood ended in 2006, at Newport in 2008, and at Pine Bluff in 2010;
operations at Anniston, Umatilla and Deseret were completed in 2011, and at Pueblo and Blue
Grass in 2023. This section reviews how the CSEP Program sought to shut down operations in a
way that was orderly and would leave lasting benefits in the affected communities.

The program underwent a significant
consolidation period from 2006 to 2011.
Edgewood completed demil operations in
February234 2006, Newport in August 2008
(Figure 6.1), Pine Bluff in November 2010, and
then Anniston, Umatilla, and Deseret in
September, October, and November 2011,
respectively. So after 17 years as an 8-site
program, over the course of 5 years, the program
was reduced to two sites, Pueblo and Blue
Grass. After that rapid period of change, the
program entered a comparatively long tail of
lower-level activity as a two-site program.235
Stockpile demilitarization operations concluded Figure 6.1 Newport Chemical Depot Closeout
at Pueblo and Blue Grass in 2023.236 Ceremony, 2008

The Chemical Materials Agency commemorated completion of its demil mission with a brochure
published in May 2012.237 The brochure featured a message from Don Barclay, CMA Acting
Director, and summarized the CMA demil program including the sites and leaders at the site and
HQ level. The brochure also noted CMA’s ongoing missions: continuing chemical storage,
CSEPP, treaty compliance, and the non-stockpile project. With the completion of demil for the
non-ACWA sites, CMA was re-designated as the Chemical Materials Activity, a separate
reporting activity of the Army Materiel Command. In February 2016, CMA was put under
tactical control of the Joint Munitions Command.

The PEO ACWA completed demil operations at PCAPP on June 22, 2023, and at BGCAPP on
July 7, 2023. The PEO ACWA remained active (and remains active as of this report) to complete
closure operations after those dates, including use of SDCs to destroy drained/contaminated
munitions, disposal of secondary wastes, decontamination and decommissioning of facilities and

equipment, disposition of property, demolition of some facilities, and closeout of contracts and
environmental permits. Those processes were anticipated to take 3 to 4 years.238 In a news
release commemorating the conclusion of demil operations, Secretary of the Army Christine
Wormuth commented that, “After years of design, construction, testing and operations, these
obsolete weapons have been safely eliminated. The Army is proud to have played a key role in
making this demilitarization possible.” Hon. Deborah G. Rosenblum, Assistant Secretary of
Defense for Nuclear, Chemical and Biological Defense Programs, praised the leadership and
dedicated workforce that allowed operations to succeed: “The level of determination and resolve
to overcome challenges has been nothing short of outstanding. This achievement demonstrates
our credibility to the international community and has helped move the U.S. government toward
closing this particular chapter of U.S. military history.”239

6.1 Mandate for Closeout
Congress provided the first step in closeout planning by incorporating a funding deadline in the
Defense Authorization Act for FY 1999: “No assistance [to State and local governments
participating in CSEPP] may be provided under this paragraph after the completion of the
destruction of the United States’ stockpile of lethal chemical agents and munitions.”240

The Army interpreted this requirement in a policy paper issued in July 2001, which stated:

 This statute expressly prohibits the Army or FEMA from providing any assistance
 to State and local governments after the completion of the destruction of the
 United States’ stockpile of lethal chemical agents and munitions. Accordingly, the
 Army will not request funding for a CSEPP State in the fiscal year following the
 completion of the destruction of the stockpile affecting that State. . . No additional
 funding will be available to a State in the fiscal year following the completion of
 the destruction of the [associated] installation’s stockpile.

In the transmittal letter to the FEMA Director, the DASA for Environment, Safety, and
Occupational Health requested that FEMA “disseminate this policy to the CSEPP States and
assist them in planning for the closeout of CSEPP activities once the stockpile has been
destroyed.”241

The National Defense Authorization Act for FY 2008 amended the statutory mandate for
program closure to state that “assistance may be provided to State and local governments in
developing capabilities to respond to emergencies involving the storage and destruction of lethal
chemical agents and munitions until the earlier of the following:

238
 PEO ACWA 2023, “U.S. Completes Chemical Weapons Stockpile Destruction Operations,” News Release,
 July 7, available at https://www.peoacwa.army.mil/2023/07/07/us-completes-chemical-weapons-stockpile-
 destruction-operations/, accessed January 2024.
239
 Ibid.
240
    PL 105–261, Sec. 141(a) (1998).
241
 Letter dated July 20, 2001, from Ray Fatz, DASA for Environment, Safety, and Occupational Health, Office of
 the Assistant Secretary for Installations and Environment, to Joe M. Allbaugh, FEMA Director, transmitting
 policy paper.

 156

 1. The date of the completion of all grants and cooperative agreements with respect to the
 installation or facility for purposes of this paragraph between the Federal Emergency
 Management Agency and the State and local governments concerned.
 2. The date that is 180 days after the date of the completion of the destruction of lethal
 chemical agents and munitions at the installation or facility.”242

Although no specific reference to closeout was made in this legislation, from a programmatic
perspective, it was interpreted to apply to expenditures for both closeout and preparedness tasks.
FEMA thus applied this 180-day limitation to the availability of new funding to grantees after
completion of the destruction of the stockpile, but not to the expenditure of funds already
awarded in open CAs. The 180-day period also did not arbitrarily modify (shorten or extend) the
performance period of a previously awarded CA. The performance period was established as part
of the award process and could be extended by the FEMA region upon request from the grantee.

6.2 Closeout Consultation and Guidance Development
In anticipation of being the first site to complete demilitarization, the Maryland Community IPT
began formal closeout planning at its February 2002 meeting, requesting guidance from FEMA
HQ on the FY 2003 budget.

Although the demil schedule was subsequently delayed, the Maryland IPT continued to plan for
closeout and made the first of several national briefings on its efforts at the 2003 CSEPP
National Conference. At the end of the conference, CSEPP management announced the
formation of the Program Closeout IPT. Because of their immediate experience, representatives
from Maryland IPT formed a large part of the original membership of the IPT; membership
expanded over time to include staff from each of the CSEPP communities. The mission of the
Closeout IPT was to complete the following tasks:
 • Identify DHS and DOD regulatory, policy, and guidance materials relevant to program
 closeout.
 • Identify specific program closeout issues for the U.S. Army, FEMA, State, and local
 CSEPP and, for each issue, identify requirements, necessary research, potential
 approaches, and real-world solutions.
 • Identify program activities and suggested approaches to support the transition from a
 CSEPP-funded program to an all-hazards emergency management program.
 • Identify activities to reduce the complexity, length, and cost of the closeout process.
 • Develop program closeout guidance that incorporates the IPT’s work in the areas listed
 above and provide this information to CSEPP personnel in an easily accessible manner.

242
 PL 110–181, National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2008, Section 924 (2008).

 157

The IPT developed a closeout guidebook to serve as a resource for CSEPP communities
approaching the end of the program. The guidebook, last revised in 2010, included guidance on
the following:
 • Planning for closeout;
 • LCCE and budget considerations for closeout;
 • How to deal with personnel, facilities, leases, equipment, and systems;
 • How to maintain strong capabilities through the end of demil operations; and
 • How to close out grant requirements.

The guidebook also contained annexes with information on public affairs planning for closeout
(developed by the PA IPT), alternative funding sources (to help identify emergency management
funding opportunities after CSEPP), a closeout task list, and description of a spreadsheet-based
tool for closeout planning, the Closeout Planning Matrix.

Closeout could be viewed as a two-phase process: a planning phase, in which issues were
identified, resolved, and documented, and an implementation phase, in which decisions were
carried out.243 Just as with preparedness, the programmatic discussion of closeout focused
primarily on the planning phase, while most of the details for the implementation of closeout
were at the discretion of individual agencies and the community IPTs. Each of the other
national/functional IPTs active during the closeout period (Automation, Exercises, Medical,
Public Affairs, and WebCA) also considered closeout and contributed to the resolution of
closeout issues.

Program closeout was highlighted in plenary and/or breakout sessions at each of the CSEPP
Workshops from 2003–2010. In addition, in 2008, a special, 2-day State and local forum was
held in Denver, Colorado, with the theme “Focus on Closeout.” The forum differed from
previous meetings in the prioritization of small group discussions to review and make
recommendations regarding specific closeout issues (those that had been previously identified in
the Closeout Guidebook, along with new issues identified by the forum participants).

The 120+ attendees at the forum included representatives from nearly every CSEPP jurisdiction
and organization. To enable in-depth discussion of closeout issues by stakeholders from different
jurisdictions, the organizers established ten work groups that developed 128 separate
recommendations. A summary report was prepared and turned over to the Closeout IPT for
further action. The IPT reviewed the summary report and provided a briefing on the results at the
2009 CSEPP Workshop. Much of the forum discussion was incorporated and expanded upon in
the August 2010 revision to the Closeout Guidebook.

In accordance with its charter, the Closeout IPT became inactive following the 2010 CSEPP
Workshop based on a joint determination by its membership and program management that no
issues required further IPT input or action. The Closeout IPT page on the CSEPP Portal was
retained as an information repository and resource for CSEPP planners.

243
 From a practical standpoint, however, the two phases overlap significantly.

 158

6.3 Program Closeout Process and Issues
At the national level, FEMA and Army HQ offices faced the problem of maintaining capabilities
and continuity as the scope of the program decreased. The program had leveraged the
multiplicity of sites in two ways. First, response assets (e.g., RTAPS) and personnel from one
site would be available to support another in an emergency. If one site experienced an
emergency, all the other sites would be expected to contribute to the response and recovery
efforts. Second, personnel from each site supported the other sites as exercise evaluators and
through meetings and workshops designed to share experiences and lessons learned. As the pool
of resources and personnel involved in the program shrank, those resources diminished. During
the two-site period from 2011 on, the two facilities continued to support one another. However,
the technical capabilities to provide support were limited because the Pueblo facility had mustard
agent only, while the Blue Grass facility stored both mustard and nerve agent. Training,
equipment, and supplies at the Pueblo depot were focused on mustard agent, although some
personnel had experience with nerve agent at another site.

Another effect of the program contraction was to change the focus of national program
management. On the FEMA side, management of the closeout process involved close
coordination with dozens of State and local jurisdictions. For both FEMA and the Army,
national-level efforts to develop standards and guidance to apply across the sites become less of
a priority. New program management priorities were established in a May 2011 Army–FEMA
memorandum,244 which stated, “The shift to a two-site program will not change the commitment
of the Army and FEMA to maximum protection, but it does provide an opportunity to reassess
program management functions.” The memorandum addressed changes in the national level
(function-specific) IPTs, providing for the continuation of the Automation and PA IPTs with a
new focus on issues pertaining to the remaining sites and discontinuation of other IPTs.
Nonetheless, the program retained its commitment to stay current and align with developments in
emergency management policy and practice, as reflected in higher Army HQ and FEMA national
doctrine.

On the Army side, the winding down of the U.S. chemical arsenal led to closure of the associated
chemical activities and in some cases, the entire installation. Three of the chemical storage
installations — Newport, Umatilla, and Pueblo — were scheduled to close once their chemical
storage and destruction missions were completed. They went through the Army’s established
base realignment and closure (BRAC) process, including transition to a local redevelopment
authority.245 The other installations (Anniston, Blue Grass, Deseret, Edgewood, and Pine Bluff)
continued their other non-chemical missions.

244
 Army/DHS CSEPP, 2011, Memorandum to CSEPP IPT Co-Chairs, May 24.
245
 As of April 2025, Newport and Umatilla were closed; Pueblo was deactivated and operating as the U.S. Army
 Chemical Materials Activity–West to provide health and safety support to PCAPP until conditions are met for its
 turnover to the local redevelopment authority, PuebloPlex (see PEO ACWA, 2024, “Pueblo Chemical Depot
 Deactivation Ends 82-Year Legacy,” September 18, available at
 https://www.peoacwa.army.mil/2024/09/18/pueblo-chemical-depot-deactivation-ends-82-year-legacy/, accessed
      March 2025.

 159

For State and local governments, closeout planning at each site was coordinated through the
community IPT. Considerable effort was applied to plan and implement an orderly closeout
process. For example, the Oregon CSEPP community developed a 69-page Oregon CSEPP
Community Consolidated Closeout Planning Framework. Some of the key elements of the
framework were as follows:
 • Timing. As noted above, the legal mandate for program funding was tied to the demil
 process, so the exact date of demil completion became a particularly important milestone
 (Figure 6.2). Establishing this date required close coordination with the demil facility to
 (a) define completion, and (b) keep refining the estimated completion date. A last-minute
 glitch in the demil process could delay the end date.
 • Personnel. As funding for positions wound down, personnel decisions would be made
 well in advance to allow time for job searches. Retention of key personnel often became a
 problem as the end of the project approached; staffing was needed right up to the end of
 demil, but staff with expiring positions sought employment elsewhere.
 • Equipment. Warning systems, communications equipment, vehicles, trailers, computers,
 etc., could be retained, sold, or transferred to other agencies or jurisdictions. Federal and
 State regulations governed evaluation and disposition of items over a certain value;
 FEMA retained the right to reclaim items valued at more than $5,000 if they would not
 be put to an approved use (Figure 6.2) but preferred not to exercise that authority.

Despite the available guidance, assistance, planning, and information-sharing, some local
jurisdictions underwent painful transitions when CSEPP funding was discontinued. Many of the
CSEPP local jurisdictions had populations of 20,000 or less and limited resources available for
emergency management. Facilities and equipment did not go to waste, but they were often
redistributed to other locations with more resources and more hazards. Some personnel simply
retired at the end of the program; others were able to parlay their CSEPP training and experience
into other positions when their positions were eliminated (in some cases, by transferring to a
different CSEPP site). Section 6.4 details particular issues, events, and strategies used at each site
during its closeout process, along with closeout of the Army and FEMA national CSEPP
offices.246

246
 For further information about the closeout process, see “Lesson Learned on Program Closeout” in Volume 2 of
 this document.

6.4 Site-by-Site Closeout
The following sections describe chemical demil completion and the closure of the CSEP
Program both on- and off-post for each site. The site summaries are in chronological order by
closure date.

These descriptions drew from variety of sources, including interviews with key personnel, Army
and FEMA documents, and newspaper articles.

 161

6.4.1 Edgewood

6.4.1.1 Army

CMA produced a commemorative newsletter marking the completion of neutralization
operations at ECA and the Aberdeen Chemical Agent Disposal Facility.247 The last container was
processed on March 8, 2005. The newsletter featured statements Michael Parker, CMA Director,
and Lieutenant Colonel (LTC) Gerald L. Gladney, ECA Commander. Mr. Parker said,
“Everyone on the project, from the administrative assistants to engineers and research scientists,
to guards, to munitions handlers, and emergency responders, all contributed to this milestone by
keeping safety and environmental protection the top priority while safeguarding, transporting,
and destroying the stockpile.”

6.4.1.2 Community

Among other things, the CMA newsletter discussed the Maryland CSEP Program and its legacy
for the State of Maryland and for Harford, Kent, and Baltimore Counties. The Maryland
jurisdictions benefited from improved facilities (EOCs and JIC), equipment, and training as a
result of CSEPP. Other equipment — such as overpressure systems installed in schools — was
removed.

After CSEPP ended, the Maryland jurisdictions were better able than some to retain personnel
and maintain facilities and equipment acquired through CSEPP. A number of key personnel from
the Maryland CSEPP community were interviewed in 2009 for the CSEPP 20-year anniversary;
all asserted that the community was able to maintain a high level of preparedness post-CSEPP.
Harford County emergency management director Douglas Richmond described a process
beginning years before closure to examine the budget and find alternate funding to maintain key
items after CSEPP ran out. He was proud to report that 100% of his CSEPP personnel were
retained by the County. LTC Gladney, ECA commander during the closure process, likewise
reported that, while the community appreciated the CSEPP funding and used it wisely, “the
community wasn’t really fretting” over its ability to maintain a high level of preparedness post-
CSEPP. Sue Willits, Kent County director of emergency services, remarked that CSEPP “. . .
lives on in the form of call-down systems, communications equipment, staffing, personal
protective equipment, on and on it goes. Automation, the training is invaluable. We were so
lucky to have Federally evaluated exercises because it really ramped up our response and opened
our eyes. It brought so much to our office and to our capabilities . . . and all of our plans and all
of our equipment [including] decontamination equipment that was purchased through the CSEPP
program is still being used.” And Christina Hughes, emergency preparedness coordinator at
Franklin Square Hospital in Baltimore, commented that the training curriculum developed by the
Medical IPT was an excellent tool and had a lasting benefit. She was endeavoring to maintain
contact with the CSEPP medical community after the program ended in Maryland, and

247
 U.S. Army Chemical Materials Agency, 2005, A Safer Maryland Today: Edgewood Chemical Activity &
 Aberdeen Chemical Agent Disposal Facility 2005 Commemorative Newsletter.

 162

recommended others do the same to keep up with the latest information and maintain a network
of contacts.248

6.4.2 Newport

6.4.2.1 Army

A deactivation ceremony for NECD was held on June 17, 2010, including the traditional
retirement of the colors for a closing facility. Ronald M. DeGuire was the master of ceremonies
and there were remarks by Tom Wilkey; Conrad F. Whyne, CMA Director; Carmen J. Spencer,
DASA; LTC William D. Hibner, NECD Commander; and Jack Fenoglio, President of the NECD
Reuse Authority.

CMA produced a commemorative brochure, Newport Chemical Depot: Stockpile Elimination,
published over the signature of Conrad F. Whyne, CMA Director. The brochure contained letters
of congratulation and pride from the commander, LTC William D. Hibner; seven former
commanders; and the site manager, Jeffrey L. Brubaker. It also included brief summaries of the
site’s history and of the demil processes, including the role of contractor Parsons in operating the
Depot. On the human-interest side, the brochure profiled several long-time employees,
highlighting their perspectives and memories of working at the Depot, along with the local
Citizen Advisory Commission. The local CSEP Program was also profiled, noting that “The
efforts of the local, State, and Federal emergency preparedness agencies to prepare and educate
the local communities on response to a potential incident due to stockpile storage have created a
community prepared for numerous emergencies.”

6.4.2.2 Community

The Newport CSEPP community included the States of Indiana and Illinois and Vermillion,
Parke and Fountain Counties in Indiana and Vermilion and Edgar Counties in Illinois. Prior to
CSEPP, the Newport area county EMAs had limited capabilities. CSEPP enabled the
communities to build robust public safety systems that include expanded emergency operations
centers, public A&N systems, interoperable public safety communications systems, and
emergency public information and education programs. CSEPP also enabled the Newport
community to build coordinated plans and expand responder training, annual exercises, and
public education. Regional approaches to emergency preparedness and response were
extensively used during CSEPP and were carried forward after the program’s end.

FEMA assisted Indiana State and county officials to identify equipment to be retained to benefit
the community, including indoor and outdoor warning systems and a mobile command
vehicle.249

248
 CSEPP 2009 legacy interviews with Douglas Richmond, Colonel Gerald L. Gladney, Sue Willits, and Christina
 Hughes. Interview transcripts will be included in the CSEPP collection at the Army Historian’s Office archives.
249
 FEMA CSEPP legacy brochure, FEMA helps Indiana communities plan for closeout of the Chemical Stockpile
 Emergency Preparedness Program, September 2008.

 163

In 2015, interviews were conducted with the emergency directors in Vermilion, Vermillion,
Parke and Fountain Counties to discuss their adaptation to the end of CSEPP. All of these
counties were predominantly rural and had relatively modest governmental budgets and
staffing;250 the end of CSEPP funding required considerable adjustments. Most of the CSEPP-
funded positions were lost, and the counties and States often had to find creative solutions to
maintain facilities and equipment.

In Indiana, Vermillion County and Parke County each had four CSEPP-funded positions, which
were terminated at the end of the program. Fountain County had two CSEPP-funded positions;
one was retained. In Illinois, Vermilion County had no CSEPP-funded staff and had been able to
retain its three emergency management positions as of 2015. Attempts to contact officials in
Edgar County were unsuccessful.

CSEPP-funded EOCs were still in use for emergency management purposes, but with reduced
emergency management staffing, most of them were adapted to also accommodate other uses. In
Parke County, the County Commission began meeting in the EOC conference room, freeing
space in the Courthouse to allow the County Auditor to have a real office instead of working out
of a coat closet. In Vermillion County, vacant offices and storage space were converted for use
by other agencies. NECD’s JIC was located in the Rockville National Guard Armory and so was
not a county asset. Support equipment related to the facility’s JIC use was relocated to another
CSEPP site.

The counties generally found ways to retain and make use of CSEPP-purchased equipment and
supplies, particularly items of direct importance to public safety, such as sirens and highway
message boards. Indiana passed a law in 2008 requiring each county to develop a warning
systems plan by 2010; the CSEPP counties had a head start in meeting this requirement.251 Sirens
installed for CSEPP generally were retained and used for weather warnings.

Vermillion County, Indiana, had a mobile command unit that was left behind in good condition
and was still in use in 2015. Also as of 2015, the County had retained three CSEPP-funded
decontamination (decon) trailers; one was actively in use for training and to support Indiana Task
Force 7 as both a decon asset and a field shower unit for response personnel. Two decon trailers
in Fountain County supported Task Force District 4. Similarly in Vermilion County, Illinois, a
CSEPP-acquired decon trailer supported a hazardous materials response team and was being
used for training volunteer departments around the county.

All of the county personnel interviewed reported that CSEPP-acquired PPE could no longer be
used due to being out of certification.

250
 Vermilion County, Illinois, was the most populous county in the Newport community with a count of 81,625
 residents in the 2010 census. All of the other Newport counties had populations between 16,000 and 19,000.

 FEMA CSEPP legacy brochure, FEMA helps Indiana communities plan for closeout of the Chemical Stockpile

 counties to report on tornado sirens,” at http://usatoday30.usatoday.com/weather/storms/2008-05-27-
 tornado_sirens_N.htm, accessed September 2015.

 164

One county emergency management director reported that because of his CSEPP experience, he
was often consulted by surrounding jurisdictions for planning and preparedness advice. On one
occasion, a much more populous county (over 100,000) asked him how to get started on building
an emergency plan. Years after CSEPP, he was still considered the local “go-to guy.”

6.4.3 Pine Bluff

6.4.3.1 Army

PBCA provided safe and secure maintenance, storage, and transport of approximately 12 percent
of the nation’s original chemical weapons stockpile, including 90,409 M55 GB rockets, 19,608
M55 VX rockets, 9,378 M23 VX landmines, and 3,703 mustard ton containers. PBCA personnel
transported this inventory in 5,879 Enhanced On-Site Container deliveries from storage to the
Pine Bluff Chemical Agent Disposal Facility (PBCDF) for incineration. Incineration operations
began on March 28, 2005, and concluded on November 12, 2010. Figure 6.3 shows a container
of M-55 rockets entering the disposal facility.

 Figure 6.3 Last Enhanced Onsite Container with VX M55 rockets entering PBCDF252

“The elimination of the chemical weapons stockpile at Pine Bluff Arsenal has truly been a team
effort,” said Carmen Spencer, DASA for the Elimination of Chemical Weapons. “Thanks to the
efforts of CMA and Pine Bluff Arsenal, and the support of the local community and the State of
Arkansas, our world is a safer place and our Nation is one step closer to meeting its international
commitment to dispose of its chemical weapons stockpile under the Chemical Weapons
Convention.”

252
 CMA, Pine Bluff, AR, at http://www.cma.army.mil/pinebluff.aspx, accessed August 2015.

 165

PBCDF and PBCA hosted a ceremony commemorating the completion of chemical weapons
disposal operations on Thursday, March10, 2011, at the Pine Bluff Convention Center in Pine
Bluff, Arkansas.

CMA produced an end-of-operations brochure containing messages from Conrad F. Whyne,
CMA Director; Colonel (COL) Franz J. Amann, Commander, Pine Bluff Arsenal; and LTC
Nathaniel W. Farmer, Commander, PBCA. The brochure summarized PBCA’s mission history
and contained statements from longtime Arsenal employees, neighbors, and former
commanders.253

The brochure also described CSEPP in the Arkansas community and summarized its
accomplishments and legacy:
 • Training of more than 2,800 emergency responders including emergency managers,
 firefighters, police officers, medical teams, and hazardous material technicians.
 • Raising public safety awareness through media campaigns, calendars, brochures, videos,
 and other educational material.
 • Installing 63 outdoor warning sirens and highway messaging boards.
 • Procuring other specialized equipment including 2,000 sets of protective clothing for
 emergency responders, equipment for 23 medical facilities that would treat people
 during an emergency, and 26 mobile trailers to decontaminate people who may be
 exposed to chemicals.

PBCA as a unit was terminated in a formal ceremony on August 14, 2012. The Activity colors
were cased to symbolize completion of the mission. Don E. Barclay, acting director of CMA,
and Arsenal Commander COL David L. Musgrave expressed their appreciation to Activity staff
past and present for a job well done.254

6.4.3.2 Community

Lasting benefits of CSEPP for the Arkansas community were detailed in FEMA’s FY 2011
Report to Congress and in an Arkansas Democrat-Gazette story in early 2010.255 Off-post
response organizations were connected through interoperable communications, safeguarded with
protective equipment, and trained to implement protective actions such as evacuation, sheltering,
decontamination, and medical treatment. A network of mutual aid agreements was established
among CSEPP jurisdictions and resource/service providers.

Lonoke and Jefferson Counties built new EOCs. Karen Quarles, Acting Director of the Jefferson
County Office of Emergency Management, was quoted as saying, “At the time it was built it was

 Lee, M., 2012, “Pine Bluff Chemical Activity formally ends 17-year mission at Arsenal,” Pine Bluff

 Linn, M., 2010, “Arsenal plan a future boon,” Arkansas Democrat-Gazette, January 4, at

state of the art — the best one in the State.” Grant County’s EOC was renovated and enlarged.
Other counties obtained communications equipment, storage buildings, pagers, and office
equipment.

In Pulaski County, medical facilities — including Arkansas Children’s Hospital, Baptist Medical
Center, the University of Arkansas for Medical Sciences and St. Vincent’s Hospital — received
communications equipment enabling them to communicate by radio with any hospital in the
State. Arkansas Children’s Hospital used the radio equipment during an ice storm in 2009 to
obtain backup generators.

At the State level, CSEPP funds were used to establish the Arkansas Wireless Information
Network — a cross-agency communications resource. The Arkansas Department of Health
obtained decontamination equipment, medical equipment, and other supplies. The Arkansas
Highway and Transportation Department received support to install highway message boards
that could be used for emergency notices, Amber alerts, traffic announcements, and safety
reminders.

The cost for maintenance of CSEPP-acquired radios was picked up by an interagency
communications authority, the Metropolitan Emergency Communications Association (MECA),
funded primarily by the City of Pine Bluff and Jefferson County. CSEPP-funded maintenance
costs of about $5,000 per month had to be included in the MECA budget after October 2015.256

Tommy Jackson of the Arkansas Department of Emergency Management commented in a 2011
interview, “The CSEPP program has really been a good friend and a good neighbor . . . those
counties are better equipped and better educated in dealing with these sort of things, so despite
the fact the program is ending, its benefits will be felt for a long time to come.”257

6.4.4 Anniston

In a letter dated April 17, 2012, from the Alabama IPT to FEMA and Army CSEPP leaders Terry
Hobbs and Rich Brletich, the authors stated “At the emergency management level, the program
provided many very important and very visible contributions, e.g., jobs and staff; enhanced
EOCs; communication, alert and notification, and protective equipment; training; special needs
data; exercises; and public information materials. But it also made many important but less
obvious contributions too: a more informed and better prepared public for all-hazards;
emergency management leadership, skill, and career development; and a more cohesive team of
local emergency managers and responders.”

6.4.4.1 Army

ANCA completed demil operations in September 2011. A formal closure ceremony was held on
May 7, 2013, featuring speeches by COL Darryl Briggs, CMA Director of Stockpile Operations,
and Jesse Brown, Civilian Executive Director, expressing their appreciation to the ANCA
workforce and noting the demilitarization achievement.258

CSEPP was closed out in the allotted 180-day window after conclusion of demil operations
(Figure 6.4). Chemical activity operations continued for another 20 months as the plant was shut
down and the igloos were cleaned (Figure 6.5).

 mine in the Anniston Army Depot stockpile as it heads down the conveyor.259

 Figure 6.5 Employees inspect empty igloo at ANCA.260

Significant issues for closeout of CSEPP at ANCA included the following.

Personnel. At the peak of CSEPP, ANCA had 18 CSEPP personnel, including 15 EOC staff,
1 CSEPP Coordinator, 1 PA officer, and 1 IT specialist. Five of the EOC staff positions were
eliminated when nerve agent operations ended in December 2008; because risk to the public was
reduced at that point, it was no longer necessary to keep personnel on standby to perform “pink
zone” (close-in, off-post areas) notification. By the end of FY 2009, those five positions were
successfully eliminated through attrition.

Anticipating the need to reassign personnel, ANCA opened a full-time Transition Office, staffed
with a personnel specialist, to assist personnel in finding positions and/or other placement or
retirement options. Of the remaining EOC personnel, one was reassigned, one retired, five were
retained in a pared-down EOC during demil facility decommissioning operations (paid for out of
demil operating funds rather than CSEPP), and the remaining three were subject to layoff
(reduction in force or RIF) or reassignment. The PAO, CSEPP Coordinator, and IT specialist
either left or found other assignments within ANAD.

EOC and JIC Facilities. The ANCA EOC became the Depot EOC. The JIC facility, located on
Fort McClellan, was shut down and turned over to the McClellan Development Authority for
disposition.

Records Management. Staff members reviewed CSEPP files to determine what needed to be
transferred to electronic format and archived. The majority of old files were disposed of
according to accepted procedures.

Meteorological (MET) Towers. CSEPP managed three MET towers on ANAD; the instruments
were removed and the towers taken down following demil.

260
 Photo by Michael Abrams, U.S. Army Chemical Materials Agency Anniston Chemical Activity Weekly Update,
      September 8, 2011.

 169

6.4.4.2 Community

Closeout was coordinated, tracked, and monitored through the Alabama IPT, which developed a
master status document to track outstanding projects and challenges by jurisdiction and CSEPP
Benchmark. Tracked items included the transition to all-hazards operations at the Army Depot
EOC, planning adjustments, personnel changes, public outreach, and disposition of facilities and
equipment. Two aspects of the equipment situation that received considerable attention off-post
were communications systems and public warning devices (sirens).

Communications Systems. After CSEPP funding ran out, State and local authorities continued a
communications system for local responders. The 800-MHz system included 14 radio towers;
5,500 portable radios; and dozens of microwave antennae, computers, and generators. To ensure
continued operation, the Alabama State legislature created a special board to oversee it — the
Alabama Regional Communication System Board — with members nominated by Calhoun and
Talledega Counties. Operating and maintenance costs were allocated on a per-radio basis among
many organizations (county and municipal police and fire departments). As noted in local news
coverage, a damaging tornado outbreak in April 2011 demonstrated the value of interoperable
communications among local agencies, spurring efforts to keep it operating post-CSEPP.261

However, the Regional Communication System Board was unable to raise funds when the
system required updating in 2013. As a result, the system was turned over to county control;
Calhoun County 911 took over the Calhoun County portion of the system, and the Talladega
County Commission took over the Talladega County portion. Calhoun County 911 began a $4.1
million upgrade of the system in December 2013.262

Sirens. The CSEPP counties took varying approaches to maintenance of CSEPP-funded sirens,
now in use for weather warnings. Calhoun, Talladega, Etowah, and St. Clair Counties opted to
continue use of their sirens and assume the cost of maintenance, at least initially — in some
cases, sharing the cost among several municipalities. Cleburne County determined that it could
not afford to keep its siren system intact. Some of its sirens were moved to communities willing
to pay for them, some were taken down and stored for spare parts, and others were turned over to
a volunteer fire department.263 Etowah County agreed to maintain the CSEPP-funded outdoor
sirens for weather warnings, while exploring other technological options for warnings to run in
parallel with them. County officials indicated that the sirens would likely be discontinued if they
fail.264 Maintaining the sirens cost about $1,200 per siren per year, according to various reports.

The community initiated a public outreach campaign to explain to residents that the risk from the
chemical stockpile was removed and educate them about their role in all-hazards emergency
planning, response, and recovery after CSEPP.

 Steele, C., 2012, “Counties’ highly regarded emergency radio system faces crucial transition April 1,” Anniston

 Anniston Star, March 25.

 170

Alabama CSEPP leaders evaluated the lessons learned from their participation in the program
and detailed their observations to FEMA and DA in a letter dated April 17, 2012. These lessons
included the importance of prioritizing public education and EOC development early in the
process. They also noted the importance of maintaining an all-hazard approach to the maximum
extent possible compatible with preparing for the specific hazard involved in CSEPP.265

6.4.5 Umatilla

6.4.5.1 Army

Umatilla completed demil operations in October 2011, and the site closed in 2012. An Operation
Lights Out ceremony marked deactivation of the Depot on July 17, 2012, as the site’s flag was
cased for the last time. Transfer and reuse of the 20,000-acre site was negotiated with the
Columbia Development Authority, the designated reuse authority formed by local, State, and
Tribal governments. The transfer was not concluded until March 3, 2023 (Figure 6.6).

 Figure 6.6 Umatilla Chemical Depot Transfer Ceremony, March 20, 2023266

Rachel Jacobson, assistant secretary of the Army for Installations, Energy and Environment,
outlined the anticipated reuse of the facility: “The conveyance will place part of the
approximately 9,500 acres of property into productive reuse for the community. Another part
will become an important cultural site for the beneficial use of the CTUIR. A third portion will
contribute to the historical preservation of the Oregon Trail.”267

Significant issues for closeout of CSEPP at UMCD included the following.

Personnel. At the peak of CSEPP, UMCD had 13 CSEPP personnel, including a CSEPP
Coordinator, PAO, IT specialist, and 10 EOC staff. As part of closeout and the BRAC process,
the installation worked with CMA to develop and implement appropriate personnel actions to
minimize non-voluntary reductions. UMCD had a Transition Officer assisting personnel in
finding positions and/or other placement or retirement options.

Records Management. Staff members scanned Depot and CSEPP files to transfer appropriate
documents to electronic format and archive them in accordance with standard procedures.

Meteorological Towers. The five MET towers on UMCD were removed, and the instruments
were relocated to other demil sites to serve as backup and replacement items.

6.4.5.2 Community

The closeout process in the Oregon CSEPP community received sustained planning attention
from State and local authorities. A task force including representatives of the Oregon Office of
Emergency Management, Oregon Public Health Division, Morrow and Umatilla Counties, the
CTUIR, and FEMA Region X developed a 60-page Oregon CSEPP Community Consolidated
Closeout Planning Framework.268 The document went through many revisions over several
years; Revision 17 was issued in May 2009 and Rev. 30 in February 2011. The framework noted
that the local CSEPP legacy included the following:
 • The public warning system consisting of 56 sirens (7 on-post, 49 in Oregon), 8 highway
 reader boards, the highway advisory radio, approximately 16,000 indoor tone alert radios,
 and the EAS.
 • Computer networks with email, WebPuff, and video teleconferencing capabilities
 connecting UMCD and State and local emergency operations facilities.
 • Communications systems, including a wireless network consisting of cameras, laptops,
 and hand-held devices extending into southern Washington. Schools and buses were
 provided with ultra-high frequency (UHF) radios.

267
 Tillman, J., 2023, “Land of opportunity: U.S. Army transfers former depot land to Columbia Development
 Authority,” East Oregonian, March 30 (updated April 3, 2023), at https://eastoregonian.com/2023/03/30/land-of-
 opportunity/, accessed November 2023.
268
 With contract support from D&E Technical, Inc. The Umatilla CSEPP legacy is also described in a CSEPP
 brochure, The New CSEPP: Communities Strengthened, Equipped, Protected and Prepared: Emergency
 Resource Guide.

 172

 • Coordinated planning, including an active interstate multi-agency coordination (MAC)
 group.
 • Jurisdictional EOCs and a JIC.
 • Decontamination capabilities at three local hospitals and four fire departments.
 • More than 20 personnel with expertise in communications, exercise and training, finance,
 hazard analysis, IT, logistics, medicine and toxicology, planning,
 management/administration, and public affairs.
 • Protective action infrastructure, including roadside signage and swing-arm barricades,
 cameras at key intersections, positive-pressure air filtration systems at selected schools
 and Head Start facilities, TARs and SIP kits distributed to local households, and PPE
 (provided to fire departments, police departments, hospitals, and EMS groups).

The Framework addressed disposition of personnel, supplies and equipment, real property,
services, plans and agreements, and inter-jurisdictional systems, and included a consolidated
decision/action timeline. It discussed many issues in detail, such as fine points of ownership and
categorization of equipment purchased with CSEPP funds. For example, highway reader boards
for public warning were considered “real property” since they were on poles fixed to the ground.
However if removed from the pole, they became “equipment.”

Appendices to the framework summarized critical questions and issues posed by the task force
and the answers they developed.

Local EMAs expected to lose positions: nine at Umatilla County Emergency Management and
four at Morrow County Emergency Management.269

In both states, the CSEPP A&N system was mostly dismantled. Oregon transferred sirens to
other counties, and in Washington, the sirens were sent to State surplus to be sold. Residents
returned TARs to recycling sites or disposed of them. Certain components of the system were
transferred to the National Weather Service. Electronic signs on State highways in the Oregon
and Washington CSEPP counties were transferred to their respective State departments of
transportation for continued use and maintenance.270

Oregon was able to maintain and upgrade the interoperable radio system serving Umatilla and
Morrow Counties. The system was originally funded by CSEPP and installed in 2001. The two
counties lobbied the State legislature to form the Umatilla Morrow Radio and Data District.
FEMA awarded a $2.7 million grant to upgrade the system to an integrated digital format.271

269
 Mills, E., 2011,“Experts look for silver lining in Depot closure,” East Oregonian, September 23, at

 Motorola, 2012, Two counties, one solution: A proactive partnership keeps a bi-county communications system

 grants/Umatilla_CaseStudy.pdf, accessed October 2015.

 173

CSEPP staff conducted a public outreach study in early 2012, using focus groups from the
community to review the effectiveness of local public information efforts. The focus groups
included two general-community groups, one group of educators (to elicit review of school-
focused public outreach efforts), and one group of seniors (to elicit review of efforts focused on
seniors and people with disabilities). The study included questions relating to closeout
communications and the transition to an all-hazard community preparedness posture. Many focus
group participants considered the local environment to be generally benign and the probability of
disaster low now that the chemical hazard was eliminated. Nonetheless, the participants had a
relatively high rate of family preparedness: more than half had a family emergency plan and
8 of 11 questionnaire respondents had a family emergency kit.272

6.4.6 Deseret

6.4.6.1 Army

The last chemical agent munition in the DCD stockpile was destroyed on January 21, 2012. An
end-of-mission ceremony was held at DCD on April 26, 2012. DCD Commander COL Mark
Pomeroy noted that, “The destruction of chemical agents at Deseret Chemical Depot has made
the world a safer place, and most definitely a safer place for those in the surrounding
communities. Reaching this milestone is truly a credit to the depot and contractor workers, the
support of this community and the resolve of our nation to destroy these terrible weapons.”273

DCD was formally closed as a facility on July 11, 2013, in a ceremony including statements by
Brigadier General Kristin French and CMA Director Don Barclay, who credited communication
transparency across the Depot as a key part of the ultimate success of the mission, helping shift
public opinion away from the early notion of the Army and its contractors as “incinerator people,
evil demil incinerators.”274

CMA also documented the closure of DCD with a brochure, Deseret Chemical Depot End of
Operations. It contained messages from CMA and site leadership; articles about the Depot’s
history, storage, and disposal operations; and articles about the Depot’s relationship to the
community including CSEPP. The brochure noted the good working relationships with State and
local authorities established through CSEPP and the legacy of information, training, facilities,
and equipment provided through CSEPP funding.

6.4.6.2 Community

During the program, like other CSEPP communities, the Utah CSEPP community benefited in
terms of funding for facilities, staffing, equipment, training, and activities. In a 2012 interview, a

272
 FEMA, 2012, Umatilla CSEPP Public Outreach Summary of Focus Groups, March 6–8. Report dated April 18,
 2012.

    August 2015.

 Mesesan, M.W., 2013, “Deseret Chemical Depot closes, transitions installation to Tooele Army Depot,” July 17, at

 174

State health officials noted how the benefits carried over when Utah faced another challenging
emergency preparedness situation, the 2002 Winter Olympics:

 Bob Fowler, medical program manager for the State health department’s Bureau
 of EMS and Preparedness, said the early planning for the demilitarization of
 chemical munitions helped immensely in the State’s preparations for the 2002
 Winter Games.

 The framework for coordination among emergency management, law
 enforcement, public health and other agencies had been well-established by then,
 helping Utah carry out a successful Winter Olympics event in 2002, he said.

 Fowler added that 10 northern Utah hospitals, the Utah Poison Control Center and
 multiple other first-response agencies were beneficiaries of the CSEPP money,
 with dollars directed at professional toxic chemical training, chemical detectors,
 decontamination systems and personal protective equipment.275

The State also benefited from an EOC renovation financed with State and CSEPP funding. Kris
Hamlet, Deputy Director of the Utah Department of Public Safety, Division of Emergency
Management, noted that,

 The addition of a state-of-the-art EOC in Utah has represented one of the most
 significant upgrades to the State’s emergency management efforts. The strategic
 partnership between FEMA and the State of Utah has directly contributed to the
 planning, funding, and construction of this vital facility, which will significantly
 enhance our capabilities to coordinate the information and resources necessary to
 mitigate, prepare for, respond to, and recover from emergencies, disasters, and
 catastrophic events.276

FEMA Region VIII CSEPP personnel worked closely with Utah CSEPP managers to guide them
through the closeout process and the successful closure of cooperative agreements. In May 2012,
the Deseret community participated in a series of focus groups and in-depth interviews to
evaluate CSEPP public outreach efforts. Interviewees repeatedly pointed out lasting benefits
from the program in terms of all-hazard preparedness.277

Nevertheless, at the County level, the emergency management landscape changed drastically
following closeout of CSEPP. The changes were brought on by a combination of the CSEPP
closeout and a major financial crisis at Tooele County.278 The County experienced a major
reduction in revenues from other sources besides CSEPP. To prevent bankruptcy, Tooele County

275
 O’Donoghue, A.J., 2012, “Elimination of chemical weapons stockpile brings end of federal emergency money,”
 Deseret News, January 22.
276
 FEMA, 2013, Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program Fiscal Year 2012 Report to Congress,
    January 30, p. 58.
277
 Ibid.
278
 A newspaper article explored the County’s financial troubles in 2013: McKitrick, C., and P. Manson, 2013,
 “How Tooele County’s boom economy went bust,” Salt Lake Tribune, July 1, available at
 http://www.sltrib.com/sltrib/news/56523171-78/county-tooele-million-2012.html.csp, accessed September 2015.

 175

had to consolidate departments, reduce staffing, and sell or lease county assets. The County’s
financial situation affected the ability to retain CSEPP staff, facilities, and equipment.

As closeout approached, but before the County’s financial situation was apparent, the Tooele
County Emergency Management Director and Deputy Director drafted a plan called Roadmap to
Closure in collaboration with the County Commission. The plan outlined how the county would
be able to retain all CSEPP-funded emergency management employees, and included steps
necessary to retain facilities, equipment and other emergency capabilities. However, due to the
financial crisis, this plan was not followed completely. Toole County Emergency Management
(TCEM) was one of the first departments to be abolished; emergency management became a
division within the County Health Department, although TCEM was later reestablished. The
following section describes the disposition of former TCEM staff, facilities, and equipment.
These detailed descriptions illustrate the scope of effort involved in the transition to a post-
CSEPP situation.

Staff

Of the 12 TCEM staff, most (but not all) were absorbed into other departments. The TCEM
director, deputy director, and communication manager retired, in some cases, earlier than
planned. The PIO, computer network administrator, and communications technician were offered
continued employment with Tooele County. The computer network administrator was moved to
the County IT Department, and the other two employees stayed in the Emergency Management
Division within the Health Department. The 911 dispatch supervisor, a CSEPP-funded position,
was transferred to the Sheriff’s Office. One other TCEM employee, the administrative assistant,
accepted an offer to transfer to the Health Department as the director’s secretary.

The CSEPP planner retired prior to CSEPP closeout and the position was not filled. The CSEPP
A&N technician transferred to the County Roads Department prior to CSEPP closeout. That
position was combined with the communications technician position.

Two employees from the County Health Department were transferred to the Emergency
Management division. One serves as the emergency services coordinator and the other as an
emergency management specialist and administrative assistant.

As of late 2015, with the resignation of the PIO, there were only three full-time employees
working in the Emergency Management Division within the Health Department — two
originally from the Health Department and one originally from the former Emergency
Management department (communications technician).

TCEM was later reconstituted; as of 2023, it had a staff of three, with responsibilities including
emergency planning, disaster response, responder training, and public outreach. It also operated
the Tooele County emergency response communication system, including the microwave system
serving the county, municipalities, Indian reservations, and military facilities.279

279
 Tooele County Emergency Management website, https://tcem.org/, accessed November 2023.

 176

Facilities

EOC: The TCEM building, purchased with both FEMA and County funds, remained in County
ownership and continued to house the Tooele County EOC, TCEM staff offices, and 911
dispatch center. Immediately following CSEPP closeout, the Tooele County Commission
attempted to sell the building but was not successful.

TCEM staff suggested to the County Commission that sale of the building might violate FEMA
grant requirements and could require repayment of the grant funds in the amount of
approximately $6 million. The County attorney contacted FEMA to discuss the issue and was
told that the County could do what it wanted with the building.

Ultimately, the TCEM building was not sold and continued to house emergency management
staff, the EOC, and the dispatch center. To offset costs and utilize the building to its full
potential, two offices within the building were leased to State agencies (the State Health
Department and the Fire Warden). Specific rooms within the TCEM building retained their
original purposes, including the operations room, policy room, and communication room. The
training room continued to be used for emergency management and County government training
and meetings. The bunk rooms still contained beds and recliners. The server room housed all
TCEM computer equipment; the County IT department had plans to move County servers,
routers, and other network equipment into the TCEM server room.

JIC: The Tooele Community JIC, also built as a joint effort of Tooele County and FEMA, was
leased to an outside entity, Valley Mental Health. Before the tenant moved in, TCEM staff
attempted to remove as many of the supplies and equipment items as possible for use in another
JIC in the future. The TCEM offered the equipment and supplies to the Tooele Army Depot PAO
for use in a JIC there, but the offer was not accepted.

As of late 2015, a new JIC location had not been identified and much of the JIC equipment (e.g.,
phones, signage, smart books, staff vests, projectors, office equipment, office supplies) remained
in storage at TCEM. Other equipment such as copy machines, computers and monitors were put
to use by other offices within the Tooele County Health Department.

Communications Shop and Garage: The communications shop remained in TCEM possession
and continued to serve its original purpose, storing microwave radio equipment including radio
repeaters, an amateur radio station, radio repair work stations, and other equipment. The garage
also remained in TCEM possession and housed vehicles, equipment, emergency response
materials, and supplies.

Equipment

Final disposition of CSEPP-funded emergency management and emergency response equipment
varied from retention by TCEM to transfer to other agencies.

Radio System: TCEM retained the CSEPP-funded microwave radio backbone — consisting of
radio shacks, antenna towers, and supplemental power systems (generator and solar) — which

 177

carried the 800-MHz radio system. While considerable discussion occurred regarding selling or
transferring ownership of the microwave backbone and accompanying mountaintop repeater sites
to the State radio agency (Utah Communications Authority), as of 2023, no such transfer had
taken place and Tooele County continued to own, maintain, and operate the microwave radio
backbone.

Following CSEPP closeout, TCEM began charging fees to tenants on the repeater sites, such as
Pacificorp, and also sought new tenants. The fees were used to offset costs of microwave radio
equipment maintenance. (Charging for use of CSEPP-funded equipment was prohibited during
the program.)

Warning Systems – Sirens: The siren system went through a major change. Six sirens were
transferred to Tooele Army Depot to supplement its existing employee warning system. The
remainder were mostly reallocated to higher-population areas or put in storage as spares. Sirens
situated in populated areas were left in place and were maintained and operated by TCEM.

Warning Systems – Highway Message Boards: Highway message boards were treated similarly
to the sirens. Those in remote areas were relocated to more suitable, populated areas of the
county. Two were placed into storage for parts. Sixteen stationery and two mobile highway
message boards were retained for use around the county.

Warning Systems – Other: TCEM retained TAR activation equipment, and allowed residents to
keep TCEM-issued TARs. As of 2015, there were also more unissued TARs still in storage in the
TCEM communications shop. TCEM also retained EAS activation equipment.

Computer Equipment: Most of the TCEM computer equipment remained in the TCEM building,
after being entered into the Tooele County IT inventory and tagged as such. The computer
equipment in the JIC was claimed by and moved to the Tooele County Health Department. The
County IT department will maintain and replace (as needed) TCEM computer equipment.

Personal Protective Equipment: CSEPP purchased substantial amounts of PPE, some of which
was issued to local law enforcement agencies and fire departments. The fire departments retained
that PPE, but most of the law enforcement agencies returned their PPE to TCEM. The returned
equipment, along with all PPE still in storage at TCEM, was transferred to the Utah Army
National Guard for use by its HAZMAT Response Team. Much of it was either approaching or
past expiration dates and was to be used only for training purposes.

TCEM opened its hazardous response supplies inventory and allowed local fire departments to
select and take supplies as needed. TCEM also kept some HAZMAT response supplies for its
own use because TCEM oversaw HAZMAT response in Tooele County.

Vehicles/Trailers: TCEM had purchased several tow vehicles (large trucks with water tank,
pump, and hoses) and decontamination trailers. One truck and trailer each were issued to the
Tooele City Fire Department, Grantsville City Fire Department, and North Tooele County Fire
Department. TCEM retained several other vehicles, including two trucks for the communication
staff, one SUV, and several flatbed and cargo trailers.

 178

Summary

Despite the unexpected County financial crisis, the CSEPP legacy continued in Tooele County.
TCEM, working with County government, used the full countdown time allowed by CSEPP to
complete closeout requirements and develop final dispositions of staff, facilities, and equipment.

While the skills, training, and experience of CSEPP staff did not remain within TCEM beyond
closeout, many staff brought their CSEPP training and experience to other local departments.
Awareness and level of emergency preparedness on the part of Tooele County residents
remained comparatively high for at least several years after program closure.

Four years after closure, the Tooele County EOC was still a state-of-the-art facility. Tooele
County signed a MOA with the Utah Division of Emergency Management designating the
Tooele County EOC as an alternate EOC for the State of Utah.

The warning systems were still in use to notify the public of weather, HAZMAT, or other
threats. Public warning systems built or upgraded with CSEPP funds, including sirens, TARs,
and local EAS capability, were still in place and available to alert and instruct the public in an
emergency. The CSEPP microwave radio system continued to meet Tooele County
communication and emergency response needs and support the State-wide communications
network.

6.4.7 Pueblo

6.4.7.1 Army

The last chemical munition at PCD was destroyed via static detonation chamber on June 22,
2023 (Figure 6.7). PCAPP plant manager Kim Jackson and Site Project Manager Walton Levi
held a news conference to announce the completion of the demil process at Pueblo and the
beginning of closure operations. They expressed their appreciation for the PCAPP workforce,
lauding its mission focus, hard work, and innovative spirit.280

The Army deactivated PCD in a ceremony on September 12, 2024, attended by almost 300
people, including the 40th and final commander, COL Rodney McCutcheon, and four former
PCD commanders: COL John Riley (retired), COL Christopher Grice, COL Michael Quinn
(retired), and LTC Thomas Duncan (retired). COL McCutcheon’s remarks were described in a
PEO ACWA news release:281 “The successful destruction of the chemical weapons at PCAPP,
stored at PCD since the 1950s, demonstrates our adherence and commitment to CWC obligations
and contributes to global chemical weapons disarmament efforts . . . For more than three
decades, the PCD team has strived to make good on the noble cause of ridding the U.S. of
chemical weapons.”
