6.4.7.2 Community

Preparations for Site Transfer Prior to Closure

As chemical demil operations proceeded, the Colorado CSEPP community faced a transition
period during which portions of PCD were transferred to the redevelopment authority
PuebloPlex and were leased to civilian users, while the Army continued to conduct storage and
demil operations on other parts of the site (Figure 6.9 provides a diagram of the phased transition
process). The transfer of ownership and the civilian use of portions of the facility in close
proximity to military operations raised issues of authority, responsibility, and coordination of
response to potential emergency situations.

To address those issues, the Colorado CSEPP community held a series of TTXs from 2017–2019
to explore potential problems and identify follow-up measures to improve and coordinate
preparedness during the transition. Each TTX included participation by both on-post and off-post
response authorities including Pueblo County, PuebloPlex, and FEMA. A scenario was
developed for each exercise, designed to bring out specific issues for discussion, as defined in
exercise objectives. Participants were provided a situation manual (SitMan) beforehand covering
the scenario and objectives for the exercise, and an AAR afterwards summarizing the exercise
results.

284
 U.S. Army Chemical Materials Activity news release, Pueblo Chemical Depot Deactivation Ends 82-Year
 Legacy, September 24, 2024. Accessed March 2026 at
 https://www.army.mil/article/280066/pueblo_chemical_depot_deactivation_ends_82_year_legacy.

 181

 Figure 6.9 Pueblo Phased Redevelopment Plan285

285
 PuebloPlex Redevelopment Authority, 2016, PuebloPlex Redevelopment Plan, February 23, p. ES-16.
 https://puebloplex.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/puebloplex_redevelopment_plan2021S.pdf, accessed
 July 2025.

 182

The TTXs identified areas for follow up to strengthen coordination and capabilities during the
transition period, including clarifying response authorities and responsibilities for specific
geographic areas; use of a unified incident command structure; and measures to increase
interoperability of communications between on- and off-post responders. They also addressed
legal limits on Army authority for law enforcement off-post.286

Closeout Planning

Planning for termination of the Colorado CSEPP community began well in advance. The
Colorado IPT assembled a Closeout Working Group on August 20, 2014. Members included
representatives from the CDHSEM, PCSOESB, Pueblo City-County Health Department, and
FEMA Region VIII. PCD, CMA, and FEMA HQ had ex-officio membership in the Working
Group to facilitate closeout discussions among the on- and off-post communities and
CMA/FEMA management. Other jurisdictions, entities, technical or subject matter experts, etc.,
were invited to attend as needed. The group met periodically throughout 2023, supporting the
collaborative identification and tracking of closeout-related projects, milestones, and deadlines.
Meetings addressed a range of emergency preparedness and response functional issues:
 • Goals for the final CSEPP annual exercises and the future exercise program.
 • Coordination of community closeout projects between CDHSEM and Pueblo County and
 with U.S. Army Depot closure projects.
 • Inclusion of closeout funding in the FY 2022 CA application.
 • Future of CSEPP-funded public safety systems in Pueblo County.
 • Training and professional development for CSEPP-funded employees.
 • Retention and disposition of CSEPP-funded property.
 • Local post-CSEPP mutual aid requirements in Pueblo County.

In addition to its traditional coordination role, FEMA provided technical assistance to support
closeout planning, including best practices from the six previous site closeouts. One key product
was a sustainability study287 conducted by FEMA Technical Engineering Support (FTES). FTES
was “tasked by DHS-FEMA with conducting a sustainability assessment for Pueblo County’s
emergency operations facility post-CSEPP.”288

The study looked at a 20-year period after CSEPP closeout and estimated the costs of
maintaining CSEPP-established capabilities, including facilities, personnel, emergency
communications, and radio systems. The study noted that it was based on sustaining all systems
and capabilities currently in use as of 2021, some of which might not be needed post-CSEPP. It
offered options and, to some extent, recommendations on paths forward to align expected
resources with expected needs. For example, it noted that the County expected to remove the
CSEPP-funded alert and warning sirens.

286
 See “Lesson Learned on Emergency Preparedness During Redevelopment Transition” in Volume 2 of this
 report.
287
 Pueblo County Sustainability Study, August 2021.
288
 Ibid, p. 1.

 183

The study did not consider the current (2021) staffing levels or costs, which were based on the
CSEPP hazard and needs, but rather estimated “what a county of similar size to Pueblo County
would need to have in place.” The study concluded that would be a total of 2.1 full-time-
equivalent staff positions to cover emergency management tasks, such as incident response,
planning, exercises, mitigation, recovery, training, administration, public education, and
technical support. The study urged consideration of diverse options including adding emergency
management duties for existing staff; using part-time staff, contractors, and volunteers; sharing
staff with other jurisdictions; and transferring responsibilities.289 It also recommended keeping
the same level of 911 center staffing moving forward.

The study noted that “Pueblo County’s radio system is part of the greater State of Colorado
Digital Trunked Radio System, which is a system-of-systems from various owning agencies.”290
Estimated costs were considerable, including capital costs on the order of $20 million and
operating costs around $6.5 million over the 20-year study period. The study noted the
possibility of transitioning portions of the system to the State. A public briefing by the County
Sheriff in April 2022 confirmed this as the preferred option.291

The Closeout Working Group developed a closeout planning framework292 as “the roadmap for
final disposition of all personnel, equipment, services, and various plans, procedures and other
supporting documents in accordance with each jurisdiction’s decisions on their post-CSEPP
preparedness posture.” The planning framework described the closeout process and discussed
seven planning elements in detail:
 1. Personnel
 2. Supplies and Equipment
 3. Real Property
 4. Services
 5. Plans, Procedures and Agreements (e.g., EOPs, SOPs, MOUs, intergovernmental
 agreements)
 6. Inter-jurisdictional Systems
 7. Consolidated Decision/Action Timeline

Appendices to the framework contained an expected closeout timeline and a closeout public
affairs plan. Some portions of the planning framework — the plans, procedures, and agreements
section, and the PA plan appendix — were left as “TBD.”

Pueblo County officials also took a proactive role in bringing the approaching closeout to the
attention of local officials. As described by Army CSEPP program director Carmen Kifer,

289
    Ibid, pp. 34–35.
290
    Ibid, p. 52.
291
 CSEPP Closeout Community Leader Briefing, Kirk M. Taylor, Sheriff, April 2022, p. 13.
292
 Colorado CSEPP Community Closeout Planning Framework, April 2017.

 184

 In Pueblo the supportive leadership was the Pueblo Law Enforcement Bureau
 Chief, Sheriff Mark Mears, and the rock stars in Pueblo County were Lisa Shorter
 and Josh Johnson among many other county personnel. Both Lisa and Josh served
 as emergency management coordinators; they were experienced and
 knowledgeable professionals and were both very committed to the program.
 Proactively a year or two before the program was closing out, Lisa put together
 what she called her closeout roadshow briefing, which she presented to the
 community leaders, to remind them what CSEPP was funding. She posed
 questions like, “This is what we've been getting, do you like it? And then, do you
 want to keep it? And then if you want to keep it, where are we going to get the
 funding when we're done?” She asked those key questions, which helped county
 leadership to prioritize, run a cost/benefit analysis and do some out-of-the-box
 thinking to identify continuing funding for those initiatives that the county could
 identify sustainable funding for.293

As a result, some elements of the CSEP Program were preserved that otherwise might not have
been. For example, the County partnered with a private company and the State of Colorado on
their radio tower – the state paid for maintenance, and private companies paid rent to be able to
use the tower. In addition, an emergency management coordinator position was preserved in the
county sheriff’s office through a cost-sharing arrangement with multiple agencies.

As noted above, the last chemical munition at PCD was destroyed in June 2023. Following a
thorough review and inspection of agent-handling equipment and facilities, the PCD Commander
issued an End of Chemical Surety Notification letter to the State of Colorado on August 17,
2023. This action officially ended federally-funded emergency preparedness activities for the
stockpile in Colorado and initiated the 180-day closeout period in accordance with 50 USC §
1521(e)(2)(B).294

Legacy of Preparedness

While the radio tower and emergency management coordinator position were retained, some
capabilities that were determined to be unnecessary or impractical to sustain were eliminated at
the end of CSEPP. For example, five weather towers that had previously held instruments for
determining weather conditions were removed from PCD. The towers had been installed in the
1980s to collect weather data to support decision making about demil operations (sufficient
weather stability to conduct operations) and to support hazard analysis, protective action decision
making, and public warning in the event of a chemical accident or incident. And as noted above,
the public warning sirens were determined to be surplus to the County’s needs (given other
warning systems available) and were removed.295

A Pueblo County Sheriff’s Department posting from August 2023 noted that CSEPP “brought
nearly $100 million into Pueblo County and greatly enhanced the community’s emergency
preparedness and response for more than 30 years . . . .”297

Commission, noted that, “CSEPP has been a huge windfall for Pueblo. While important for the
community to be prepared for an event with the chemical weapons stockpile, the fact that
emergency preparedness can be used for wildfires and other weather emergencies is a significant
asset to our community.”298 Gayle Perez, Pueblo County CSEPP PIO, stated that:

 CSEPP has benefited Pueblo County immensely over the past three decades
 bringing in resources, equipment, training and a new state-of-the-art emergency
 operations center, all of which have enhanced our community’s emergency
 response capabilities. . . The infrastructure and training opportunities that have
 been brought to Pueblo County through CSEPP will continue to benefit this
 community long after the program goes away”299

After the last Colorado community CSEPP exercise in May 2023, a number of program officials

 [E]verything from communications, making sure we all have the radio channels to

 event like this, especially in the real world, knowing that we have drones in the
 air, and water coming from our rural departments who can provide a tanker when
 we do have water issues is really important. . . And just getting together and
 doing this kind of exercise, seeing each other again, working together and making
 sure our verbiage is the same and our tactics are at least unified based on what the
 initial command and assessment is.

6.4.8 Blue Grass

As the last site, closeout of the Blue Grass CSEPP community was closely intertwined with
closeout of the CSEP Program as a whole. This section describes closeout processes for the Blue
Grass installation and Kentucky CSEPP community, but additional perspectives and information
about the Blue Grass closeout may also be found in the descriptions of Army and FEMA
program closeout in Section 6.4.9.

6.4.8.1 Army

Stockpile Destruction

BGAD originally had more than 101,000 chemical agent-filled weapons, including five kinds:
155mm projectiles containing mustard agent, 155mm projectiles containing VX nerve agent, 8-
inch projectiles containing GB nerve agent, M55 rockets containing VX nerve agent, and M55
300
 Reutter, J., 2023, “Pueblo finishes final joint emergency preparedness exercise for chemical stockpile program,”
 The Pueblo Chieftain, May 3, at https://www.chieftain.com/story/news/2023/05/03/csepp-holds-last-joint-
 emergency-preparedness-exercise/70179852007/, accessed May 2025.

 187

rockets containing GB nerve agent. Destruction of the weapons began in June of 2019 and was
completed July 7, 2023.

A PEO ACWA news release reported completion of the demil campaign and noted
congratulatory messages from Candace Coyle, BGCAPP project site manager; Ron Hink,
Bechtel Parsons Blue Grass project manager; COL Brett Ayvazian, BGAD commander; LTC
Tyler McKee, BGCA commander; and Craig Williams, Kentucky Chemical Demilitarization
Citizens’ Advisory Commission and Chemical Destruction Community Advisory Board co-
chair, who said, “Kudos to everyone involved in what can only be seen as a model of
government, contractor and citizen cooperation to accomplish such an important objective.”301

The news release also noted that SDCs were being used for the ongoing destruction of
containerized drained rocket warheads (CRWs), designated as agent-contaminated secondary
waste. The CRWs were considered surety material because they might contain residual chemical
agent; CSEPP would continue until the surety mission was complete.

A public event to mark the end of the destruction process was held on Oct. 11, 2023 at the
Armed Forces Reserve Center in Richmond, Kentucky. It was attended by hundreds and featured
talks by local and state dignitaries including Craig Williams, co-chair of the Kentucky Chemical
Demilitarization Citizens’ Advisory Commission and Chemical Destruction Community
Advisory Board; Madison County Judge Executive Reagan Taylor; Governor Andy Beshear; and
Senator Mitch McConnell, who gave a keynote address.

Destruction of the CRWs was completed on September 11, 2025, ending the chemical agent
threat to the Kentucky CSEPP Community. Reagan Taylor, Madison County Judge Executive,
noted that “This critical step moves us closer to the full closure of the chemical weapons mission
at the Blue Grass Army Depot and reflects decades of dedication, collaboration, and commitment
to safety. I want to thank all the partners, especially our local emergency management teams, for
their tireless work in protecting our community throughout this historic process.”302

A September 11, 2025 PEO ACWA news release described the ongoing process of cleanup and
demolition of BGCAPP: “The areas of the plant that came in contact with chemical agent are
decontaminated and the equipment dismantled. Certain facilities will be demolished. The
disposition of the remainder of the facility has not yet been determined and will be negotiated
among the Department of the Army, Commonwealth of Kentucky, PEO ACWA and BGAD.

Once site closure-related activities are complete, administrative closeout, including the closure of
environmental permits and contracts, will be completed and the project will come to an end. The
depot will continue with its missions of supplying munitions, chemical defense equipment and
Special Operations support to the DOD after the closure of the plant.”303

 188

Adjustments to Installation Emergency Preparedness

With the drastic reduction of the chemical threat, emergency preparedness measures at the
installation were scaled back. On April 8, 2025, CMA Director Kelso Horne sent a letter to
KYEM Director Eric Gibson notifying him that CSEPP closeout would begin on October 1,
2025. The letter noted that FEMA had been notified of the closeout date and that BGCA would
begin closeout of on-post program requirements immediately.

A CMA transition specialist was brought in to assist personnel with planning their futures as
reductions in force were expected. Some planned to retire, some planned to take a separation
package, others would be seeking another position. The reduction in force process was then
disrupted and accelerated by the Deferred Resignation Program (DRP) which was designed to
reduce the federal workforce by offering certain benefits for voluntary resignation.304 Staffing
reductions associated with the end of CSEPP (and the loss of the chemical threat) included EOC
technician positions and positions in installation security, medical, fire protection, and public
affairs. Also, contract support for emergency management (Argonne staff member) was
terminated.

Continuous staffing for the OC was terminated in May 2025, going to workday-only staffing.
Some capabilities associated with chemical response ended, including CCTV coverage of the
CLA and overpressurization capability for the OC. Four onpost warning sirens were retained.
The two meteorological towers onpost were also retained, with instrumentation to be maintained
by Kentucky Mesonet, a statewide weather and climate monitoring program managed by
Western Kentucky University. 305 Mesonet data is used to support local business and industry by
providing improved local forecasts and severe weather warnings, aiding emergency response
efforts, enhancing agricultural productivity, and assisting local utility providers.

The installation maintained dozens of MOA/MOUs with local jurisdictions, departments, and
hospitals as part of the CSEPP preparedness process. With the end of the program, many of these
agreements needed to be canceled or modified; as of January 2026 that process was still ongoing.
The installation would still have a working relationship with at least Madison County in terms of
emergency notifications and mutual aid.

With the end of the chemical weapons storage, BGCA’s mission was complete and it would be
terminated as an Army organizations. A deactivation ceremony for BGCA was scheduled for
June 2026 but as of this writing there are indications it might be delayed.

304
 Often referred to as taking “the fork in the road” in reference to a January 2025 memo from the Office of
 Personnel Management announcing the program.

 Conversation with John Eggum, BGCA CSEPP Coordinator, January 13, 2026; and MEMORANDUM OF
 AGREEMENT Between Blue Grass Army Depot & Kentucky Mesonet, Agreement No.: 2025-DPW-01. The
 latter spelled out obligations of the parties including access, maintenance, permitting, and provision of data.

6.4.8.2 Community

A three-day closeout planning workshop in January 2020 served as a kickoff for the closeout
process in Kentucky. The first day of the workshop featured presentations drawing on the
closeout experiences of other CSEPP sites, including county lessons-learned (presentations by
Joe Whitaker of Fountain County, Indiana and Deborah Gaither from Alabama) and FEMA
lessons-learned (presentations by Bill George of FEMA Region VI and a FEMA Region VIII
representative). The second day focused on matters of property disposition, grant closeout, and
budgeting. The third day included small-group breakout sessions to work on key issues in
closeout planning – key actions and decisions, organizational responsibilities, milestones and
scheduling. The third day also featured a discussion of community messaging from the KY
public affairs working group.

Closeout workshops were also held on December 14, 2023 and November 13, 2024. These
workshops addressed more specific details of the closeout process including the anticipated
schedule for groups of counties; host counties and PAZ counties were expected to close out first,
then IRZ counties. The 2023 workshop also examined specifics of property disposition,
including property such as vehicles, trailers, message boards, medical equipment, network
servers, microwave towers, and IT systems. The 2024 workshop featured a detailed presentation
on the closeout process to be followed by the CSEPP counties and KYEM.

A WBONTV guest columnist summarized some of the community discussion on March 22,
2024:

 In a meeting at the Madison County Extension Office, the Post CSEPP Task
 Force, an intergovernmental work group, briefed officials on the end of Chemical
 Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) funding, which provides
 approximately $1.68 million annually for emergency services operations.

 CSEPP has paid for facilities and emergency equipment for over 30 years,
 including money for the 911 dispatch center, radios for first responders, warning
 sirens throughout the county, as well as fire trucks and other vehicles. . . . Task
 Force Chair Shawn Sandlin said the committee proposed streamlining many
 services to reduce costs. Among other options, the number of outdoor warning
 sirens will be reduced to one per zone, and the FM alert radios distributed to every
 household will be discontinued, since notifications are now available on cell
 phones. Additionally, alert services like the RAVE Mass Notification system will
 be rolled into the county’s 911 system. Individual radios for personnel and
 vehicles, meanwhile, will hereafter be paid for by the three respective
 governments.

 Some systems must remain, since they are crucial to public safety, Sandlin said.
 The 800-megahertz radio system allows for interagency communication among
 all first responders.306

 life-in-madison-county-after-csepp-funds-are-gone-how-much-will-it-cost/.

Throughout the closeout process, the KY IPT met monthly to report progress, share information,
and answer questions. The IPT agendas generally included demilitarization updates,
organizational updates, and a general discussion organized by program benchmarks. Kunz and
Company developed the agendas, moderated the meetings, and produced meeting reports and
“task tracker” documents.

The last IPT meeting was held November 13, 2025 at the Kentucky Natural Bridges State Park.
Originally planned as a ceremonial event to commemorate the Kentucky CSEP Program, much
of the ceremonial aspect did not materialize, largely due to the ongoing federal government
shutdown which prevented attendance by key federal officials and staff.

Kentucky and the Kentucky CSEPP counties continued to use WebCA for financial reporting
and to manage grants, work plans, budget line items, statements of work, and document
retention. As noted in the FEMA CSEPP FY 2024 Report to Congress, “The last capital project
for the Commonwealth was completed in June 2024. No further capital projects are scheduled.
Kentucky Emergency Management, the Federal Emergency Management Agency Region 4, and
the Federal Emergency Management Agency Headquarters, with contract support, are presently
finalizing the file retention procedures, content, and download schedule for the WebCA File
Cabinet.”307 Another software tool, Smartsheet, was also used to help manage the cooperative
agreement closeout process; see the FEMA program closeout description in Section 6.4.9.2 for
details. FEMA Region 4 developed a closeout checklist for tracking progress in cooperative
agreements; disposition of equipment, supplies, and real property; and records retention.

Key closeout issues in the Kentucky CSEPP community included:
 • Staffing
 • Disposition of assets, including facilities, equipment and supplies, sirens and radio
 towers, and school overpressurization equipment
 • Public information and marketing campaign
 • Key lessons learned from CSEPP

Brief summaries of these topics are provided below.

Staffing

FEMA Regional CSEPP staff exited the program through the DRP (federal workforce reduction
program instituted in 2025) or through transfer to other positions. During 2025 all of the FEMA
Regional CSEPP staff left, and another Regional staff person was assigned temporary duty to
manage the rest of the closeout process on the part of the Regional office.

307
 FEMA 2025, CSEPP FY 2024 Report to Congress, September, p. 49.

 191

Kentucky and county CSEPP staff were reduced as a result of the loss of CSEPP funding. In an
interview with local media, Estill County Director of Emergency Management Ronnie Riddell
noted that “My emergency management staff went from 5 or 6 to me.”308

Some CSEPP staff expressed gratitude for the training and development opportunities that
CSEPP offered, as helping them qualify for other positions after CSEPP ended. For example
Stephen Bishop, Lexington-Fayette CSEPP Planner, noted that, “The training, volunteer, and
paid employment opportunities that I experienced positioned me to easily move into a career
with Fayette County Government that will support public safety beyond the life of the CSEPP
Program.” Another former Lexington-Fayette CSEPP Planner, Clayton A. Oliver, commented
that, Serving as Fayette County’s CSEPP planner . . . gave me the opportunity to work with, and
to learn from, a vast array of stakeholders. Those experiences were foundational to my later
career success after making the transition to EM at RI research universities . . . .” 309

Disposition of Assets

Assets acquired with CSEPP funds ranged from large structures such as buildings (EOCs) and
siren towers down to equipment and supplies such as decontamination trailers, roadside reader
boards, mobile satellite communication equipment, vehicles, generators, computers, office
equipment, radios, PPE, traffic control equipment, and antidotes. In general, facilities and
equipment considered still useful for emergency management were to be retained by the state
and counties, with management and accountability to be overseen by the FEMA Emergency
Management Performance Grant (EMPG) program. The EMPG is FEMA’s general program to
build and sustain state and local core capabilities across the prevention, protection, mitigation,
response and recovery mission areas.

Kentucky community jurisdictions acquired 40 real properties with CSEPP funds; in addition to
county EOCs, there were some storage properties and a number of sites acquired for towers
bearing communication equipment, meteorological instruments, and sirens. Final arrangements
for the real properties were still in process as of this writing but expected to be resolved during
FY 2026.

Equipment similarly was expected to be mainly retained for its intended purpose, and tracked
through the EMPG process moving forward. In Madison County, county officials approved a
resolution for decommissioning and transfer of CSEPP assets. As reported in the Richmond
Register,

 The resolution emphasizes the county’s intent to retain specific infrastructure
 purchased with federal funds, provided assurances are given that the facilities will
 continue to serve public safety purposes. County officials expressed their
 commitment to maintaining these assets for the benefit of local residents and

One major project was the removal of overpressurization equipment installed at local schools
and other facilities. This work was to be conducted in two phases under the supervision of the
Army Corps of Engineers (USACE), in coordination with CMA, FEMA, and state and local
authorities. As of January 2026 this work was in process but not yet completed.

Public Information and Marketing Campaign

A public information effort in the Kentucky community included acknowledging the
contributions of CSEPP to local emergency preparedness, but mainly centered on drawing public
attention to the remaining methods for receiving emergency alerts and notifications. In a media
campaign coordinated through the Blue Grass Public Affairs Working Group (BPAWG), this
message was promulgated through a variety of channels: billboards, newspaper ads (Figures 6.10
and 6.11), youtube ads, television ads, social media (Instagram and Facebook), and direct
mailings (postcards). The messaging directed residents to the website PrepareKY.com, which
had a page with information about A&N systems including outdoor sirens, weather radios, and
text and email-based systems. The latter included the national Wireless Emergency Alerts
system, and local county-specific systems such as Smart911.com in Clark County, an Everbridge
system in Estill County, and LEXALERTS in Fayette County.311 Data was collected where
possible on the number of reads (website clicks, video views) and the number of people of who
registered to receive alerts.312

 195

Key Lessons Learned

For the October 2025 KY IPT meeting, in response to an earlier question, FEMA HQ provided a
set of key lessons learned from the Kentucky CSEPP experience, and corresponding best
practices:

Integrated Process Teams:
 • Lesson – IPTs have been critical in fostering collaboration across federal, state, and local
 stakeholders, ensuring alignment of goals and addressing complex programmatic
 challenges.
 • Best Practice - Establish IPTs early in the program lifecycle to address cross-cutting
 issues, share expertise, and ensure alignment across stakeholders. Use regular meetings
 and workshops to maintain engagement and momentum. Leverage IPTs to explore
 innovative methodologies to address programmatic challenges.

Whole Community Engagement:
 • Lesson – Inclusive planning and outreach efforts significantly enhance preparedness for
 vulnerable populations, including individuals with access and functional needs (AFN)
 and limited English proficiency (LEP).
 • Best Practice – Develop tailored outreach strategies to engage various populations.
 Collaborate with local advocacy groups and stakeholders to amplify messaging and
 ensure inclusivity. Use public education campaigns to foster a culture of preparedness.

Training and Exercises:
 • Lesson – CSEPP sponsored training programs and annual exercises, including hospital
 exercise requirements and certifications, were brought to communities that otherwise
 might not have had access to them. These initiatives bridged critical gaps, validated
 capabilities, identified areas for improvement, and drove continuous progress across
 CSEPP communities.
 • Best Practice – Incorporate hybrid training models to increase accessibility and
 participation. Use AARs to inform training priorities and refine emergency response
 plans. Provide professional development opportunities (e.g., leadership training, cross-
 training) to prepare personnel for transitions post-program. Leverage innovative
 approaches to turn challenges into opportunities for meaningful improvement.

Strategic Planning and Closeout:
 • Lesson – Early and detailed planning for program closeout ensures smooth transitions
 and sustainability of critical capabilities.
 • Best Practice – Proactively establish workgroups to strategically plan for personnel
 transitions, equipment disposition, and sustainability. Leverage lessons learned to create
 structured plans, align stakeholders, and prioritize the retention of critical capabilities.

 196

Technology Investments:
 • Lesson – Investments in interoperable communications systems, automation tools (e.g.,
 WebPuff®, WebEOC®), and alert systems (e.g., IPAWS) significantly enhanced
 emergency response capabilities.
 • Best Practice – Regularly assess and upgrade technology systems to ensure readiness and
 cybersecurity compliance. Develop agreements with state and local governments to
 maintain critical infrastructure post-program.

6.4.9 Army and FEMA National CSEPP Offices

As CSEPP wound down to one site and entered a phase of administrative wrap-up, the Army and
FEMA national CSEPP offices reduced staffing and focused on ensuring a smooth termination
process for the program. Planning for closeout began well in advance and was carried out over
the course of a few years.

6.4.9.1 Army

Initial Planning and Phased Approach

In early 2019, ACWA set the initial target date (30 September 2023) for meeting Chemical
Weapons Convention destruction requirements at both PCD and BGCA/BGAD. Accordingly,
CMA established a transition planning guide that included planning dates and milestones for
closing out CSEPP at both storage locations and at CMA. Those dates were subsequently
provided to FEMA and the Pueblo and Blue Grass community IPTs.

Identification of initial stockpile destruction completion target dates triggered development of
the 2019-2024 (and final) CSEPP Strategic Plan. The challenge of this strategic plan revision
was to provide continuing enhanced emergency preparedness while shepherding the program
through its final closeout. Published in July 2019, it identified two strategic objectives and a set
of four supporting objectives that drove the program’s final years, as shown in Figure 6.12.

 Figure 6.12 CSEPP Strategic Plan - Strategic and Supporting Objectives

 197

Concurrent to the strategic plan’s development, CMA and FEMA program managers held a
series of internal and joint meetings that culminated in a program closeout framework. That
framework asked the CSEPP communities to continue to use the program’s 12 benchmarks, self-
assessment, and external exercise evaluation results to measure their ability to respond to a
chemical agent release for the duration of the program. In addition, they were asked to determine
what people, property, and systems currently funded by CSEPP they would want to retain after
the program’s end, considering their ability to afford, sustain, manage, or change those items
without that CSEPP funding. Funding needs for preparedness enhancements and now for
completing closeout requirements would continue to be identified through the LCCE process.
The goal of the framework was to jump start community planning for program closeout.
Figure 6.13 depicts the closeout framework.

Programmatic closeout planning and reporting had to be nested within CMA’s own future state
planning that considered the organizational loss of the two storage sites’ chemical activities and
the CMA CSEPP office. Thus, CSEPP program managers developed the “4P&S” (People,
Property, Plans, Purse, and Systems) briefing/reporting template that reflected similar categories
used by CMA leadership used to inform DA and the Office of the Secretary of Defense (OSD) of
the status of chemical demilitarization activities, including CSEPP. The briefing packet was
designed to let the program managers see where the communities were in the closeout process,
their thinking on end state, and where management assistance was needed.

 Figure 6.13 CSEPP Closeout Framework

 198

The 4P&S template consisted of four elements, often described using an altitude metaphor:
 • The 50,000-foot level provided the jurisdiction’s “vision” of their closeout, including
 managerial goals for three different phases: present state, transition state, and future (end)
 state. For the present state, jurisdictions were asked to provide a high-level snapshot of
 their current CSEPP enhancement posture. The transition state identified what the
 jurisdiction thought it would do during the period from completed stockpile destruction
 through the 180-day closeout period – essentially asking them to describe who would be
 doing remaining CSEPP work and what would be reduced, rescinded, transferred, and
 disposed of. The future state described what the jurisdiction thought it would look like
 after the 180-day post-CSEPP closeout and what CSEPP capabilities they wanted to
 sustain after CSEPP. The expectation was that jurisdictions would change their vision as
 the storage installation moved closer to completed stockpile destruction, as uncertainty
 lessened, and as other factors changed within the jurisdiction.
 • Similar in intent to the Community Readiness Profile (CRP), a Closeout Transition
 Profile (CTP) provided a 20,000-foot overview of how the jurisdiction met their 4P&S
 objectives and CSEPP Strategic Goal 2. The objectives were the closeout elements they
 needed to track, representing the major actions that must be accomplished in the 4P&S
 categories areas. The profile also included a subjective status rating derived from
 completing the Closeout Transition Area (CTA) templates. Using a process similar to
 producing their CRP, jurisdictions would review their CTP at site-specific IPT meetings,
 make any necessary adjustments and then present the CTP at PMT meetings.
 • To provide a 1,000-foot perspective of their closeout planning, jurisdictions were asked
 to use the CTA templates to identify the activities or tasks that they believed critical to
 successful closeout. Within each transition area, jurisdictions provided bullet points that
 summarized their strategy (the Plan), budgetary or logistical requirements (the
 Resources), and activities that needed to be accomplished (the Execution) to meet
 closeout timelines and milestones. For each transition area, the jurisdictions were asked
 to also provide two to three bullets that highlighted major actions already accomplished
 or issues hindering closeout. They were also asked to provide a subjective status rating:
 Green, on track, no issues at present; Amber, potential or slight delay, but working the
 issue internally; Red: address as top priority, need external support.
 • The final template element (the 100-foot level) asked the jurisdictions to identify issues
 where they needed national-level program manager assistance. For each issue, they were
 to provide specific information about the issue, attempts at resolving the issue locally and
 regionally, and the type of help they sought from the program management team.

Examples of the template elements are provided at Figure 6.14. Program participants were
provided the templates in July 2019 to provide sufficient time to have site-specific IPT meetings
and prepare the packet for presentation during the January 2020 PMT meeting.

 199

 Figure 6.14 4P&S Templates

One result of ACWA providing initial target dates for completing stockpile destruction was an
increase in requests for information on how closeout was going to be accomplished while
maintaining preparedness. While more detailed information was provided on a request-by-
request basis, the foundational talking points focused on a two-phased approach, as follows:

Phase 1 (lasts until surety termination approval at second site):
 • Develop CSEPP closeout objectives, plans and metrics for storage installations and federal, state,
 and local government partners. (Accomplished through the Strategic Plan and the Closeout
 Framework.)
 • Continue to provide joint Army/FEMA program administration (Integrated Process Team
 meetings, Program Office Estimate, program execution).
 • Continue to provide enhanced emergency preparedness.
 • Conduct final CSEPP exercise (occurs six to nine months prior to final site surety termination
 letter is signed).
 • End CSEPP assistance to the first site; conduct 180-day CSEPP closeout at the first site.

 200

 • End CSEPP assistance to the second site; conduct 180-day CSEPP closeout at the second site.
 • Initiate CMA HQ CSEPP office, CMA Operations Center, and FEMA CSEPP closeout.

Phase Two (Approval of surety termination, change in activities at the final site):
 • Final site CSEPP 180-day closeout complete.
 • Complete CMA CSEPP Office and Operations Center closeout.
 • Transfer post-CSEPP administrative responsibilities to CMA HQs.

As indicated in the phases, once CSEPP requirements ended at both sites, CSEPP Office closures
at both CMA and FEMA were expected to occur. The closure of FEMA’s program office was
expected to lag by several months beyond the CMA CSEPP Office’s closing to ensure
completion of all grant administration requirements. Any long-term post-CSEPP Army
administrative responsibilities (final contract closeout, line-of-accounting residual billing and
finalization, etc.) were planned to be transitioned to the CMA HQs. Discussion of closeout
messaging to the communities can be found in the section on CSEPP public affairs activities.

Adjusting to Covid-19

Shortly after the January 2020 PMT, the SARS COV-2 (COVID-19) pandemic arrived in the
United States. ACWA’s destruction mission and the Army’s storage mission would not be
paused, meaning that CSEPP would continue operating, though with modifications to ensure the
safety of its participants.

Army and FEMA programs managers quickly switched in-person meetings and training to
virtual settings. The July 2020 and January 2021 PMTs and November 2020 Army/FEMA
Strategic Planning Meeting (AFSPM) were held virtually. Training courses, such as the March
2021 CSEPP Exercise Controller and Evaluator course and On-Scene Coordinator course, were
also offered virtually. (Risk communications courses offered during the COVID-19 period are
described in Section 5.6.2 of this document). As the pandemic waned, meetings gradually moved
from fully virtual to hybrid (in-person with a virtual component; July 2021) to fully in-person
(mid-2022).

Army surety and exercise requirements were not reduced during the pandemic. That meant that
quarterly CAIRA exercises and annual CSEPP exercise would need to take place and would
require external evaluators. As described elsewhere in this document, the CMA CSEPP Office’s
COVID Study group made recommendations to CMA leadership as to whether external
evaluators should travel to these exercises, and if so, under what restrictions. Canceling the
exercises was not an option, as installation response organizations would respond to a chemical
event under any circumstance and needed to practice and assess their response capabilities. Other
tasks, such as maintenance of meteorological towers, also needed to continue. It was the CMA
CSEPP Office’s responsibility to ensure that all travel was processed in accordance with current
(and changing) guidance and that the high-level travel approvals were obtained. By the end of
the COVID-19 restrictions, more than 50 travel instances, with more than 250 total travelers, had
occurred without a single post-travel COVID infection.

 201

Accommodating Changing Demilitarization Schedules

Program managers recognized uncertainty with the weapon destruction schedule as a significant
risk because of its impact on the ability of State and local partners to plan for loss of CSEPP
funding, which affected when their preparedness assistance would end and closeout would start.
As one might expect with an industrial process, schedules could shift right (end later than
planned) or shift left (end earlier than planned). Demilitarization schedules at earlier closed sites
fluctuated often and did not settle until about 90 days before destruction ended. Compounding
schedule confusion were rumors regarding completion dates and inconsistent use of language
that described schedule elements. Thus, transmitting and explaining schedule fluctuations
provided by ACWA gradually became a major focus of the CMA CSEPP office’s closeout work.
The CMA CSEPP Office’s solution was to using multiple communication channels to get
accurate dates to CSEPP participants. First and foremost was repeatedly reminding participants
whenever rumor-based schedule questions arose that the only dates that should be used were the
official dates provided by the CSEPP Office. Approved schedule dates were provided to the
communities through FEMA-to-State channels, at site-specific and functional IPTs, and at site-
specific PMTs.

Highlighting the impact of schedule on CSEPP closeout efforts was the announcement at the
November 2022 AFSPM that weapons destruction at PCD was expected to be completed in June
2023, 90 days earlier than indicated on all planning documents to that point. The biggest effect of
this change was on the annual CSEPP exercise. It remained on the calendar as scheduled (May
2023), but because the stockpile was predicted to be more than 95% destroyed by that point, the
scope of what would now be the community’s final CSEPP exercise was changed from full-scale
to an evaluated table-top exercise. Recognizing schedule risk, the Colorado community CSEPP
calendar retained meeting and CIMRA exercises scheduled through the end of 2023. Some
closeout-specific meetings were added and now included dates for mission completion and
program recognition ceremonies. Stockpile destruction at PCD was competed on June 22, 2023.
Political and fiscal pressures affected final stockpile destruction and funding for the BGAD
community. The CMA CSEPP Office’s focus from November 2022 through January 2025 was in
responding to changing programmatic realities caused by those pressures.

The main pressure on the CMA CSEPP office was predicting the program’s end date, caused by
efforts to meet the U.S. CWC treaty commitment date and subsequent rocket warhead
destruction. The U.S. had its original treaty commitment date extended twice, causing CWC
signees to question why we were not meeting our treaty obligation. Therefore, ACWA, CMA,
and the BGCAPP contractor explored options for meeting treaty commitments. The goal was to
meet the treaty destruction definition (agent destroyed and munition body rendered unusable for
its designed/intended purpose) before the extension expired. The U.S. Department of State
obtained approval from the Technical Secretariat of the Organisation for the Prohibition of
Chemical Weapons that treaty commitments would be met when the M55 rockets at BGAD were
punched, their agent drained, and that drained agent was destroyed. A target date for meeting
treaty requirements was set for 30 September 2023. Punch-and-drain and associated agent
destruction operations were completed on July 7, 2023 and treaty credit was given. All that
remained were the warheads that were removed from the rocket bodies as part of the punch and

 202

drain operation. Each warhead had some residual agent (surety material) and would require
further destruction action.

Further programmatic pressure regarding schedule existed because CSEPP support continued for
Kentucky even though the M55 rockets were considered destroyed, treaty requirements were
met, and the remaining warheads were classified as secondary hazardous waste. The Army
position at the time was that because of the residual agent there was still a surety mission at
BGAD that was tied to the now destroyed stockpile, which meant a continued CSEPP mission.
Thus, the CSEPP office began tracking warhead destruction to better predict potential
programmatic end dates. ACWA’s official schedule had the warhead destruction campaign
ending in late 2024 (November 2024 for VX warheads, December 2024 for GB warheads). That
meant that the latest date for declaring the surety mission complete was expected to be June
2025, with the 180-day CSEPP transition starting in July 2025 and ending in December 2025.

Over time, the destruction campaign experienced delays, primarily caused by unscheduled
maintenance requirements and the need for replacement or repairs of key components of the
destruction chambers. In September 2024, ACWA provided a new schedule indicating the
predicted destruction end date was now September 2025, meaning that the CSEPP 180-day
transition period would run from November 2025 through April 2026. Requests by the Office of
the Secretary of Defense, Threat Reduction and Arms Control (OSD TRAC) for estimates of
possible additional CSEPP funding needs due to further schedule delays added to program
management pressures. As a result, the CMA CSEPP Office began examining the “warheads
only” risk to the Kentucky community, impact of schedule shifts on current and future CSEPP
funding, and the impact of a potential early (before warhead destruction was completed) end of
CSEPP assistance. The ensuing projects included an updated Blue Grass emergency planning
zone assessment based on potential events involving the remaining warheads; community
outreach regarding CSEPP closeout; conducting budget analyses for three-to-six-month
destruction delays, conducting technical, statutory, and regulatory analyses in response to high-
level information requests; and determining what Army preparedness enhancements would
remain as legacy tools when CSEPP ended. These studies and their conclusions are detailed
below.

Updated Blue Grass emergency planning zone assessment. As BGCAPP approached the end of
rocket punch and drain operations, CMA CSEPP conducted an updated EPZ assessment. It re-
evaluated the risk from destruction operations at BGCAPP for the period between BGCAPP
meeting the treaty commitment date and the surety termination date (warheads and all residual
agent destroyed). The assessment assumed that only punched and drained warheads would
remain and considered only the MCE associated with movement of drained M55 warheads to the
destruction facility. The analysis outcomes were risk of fatalities, AEGL-2 & 3 composite
downwind distances, and AEGL-2 & 3 composite downwind hazard arrival times.

The updated assessment indicated that community risks of significant adverse effects (AEGL-2)
and severe adverse effects (AEGL-3) were limited and that the few potential off-post release
scenarios were confined to Madison County. The annual individual risk of fatality was virtually
eliminated – from one order of magnitude (AEGL-2 exposure, 1x10-9) to three orders of
magnitude (AEGL-3 exposure, 1x10-11) less than the 1x10-8 Public Protection Criteria set by the

 203

Madison County Collective Protection Steering Committee (which was also the CSEPP
programmatic criteria). The CMA CSEPP Office briefed the updated Blue Grass EPZ assessment
to the BGCA Commander, the Kentucky Emergency Management Agency, FEMA Region IV,
and FEMA Headquarters in January 2023. The revised assessment led to community and
program management discussions regarding the potential for and impacts of an early end to
CSEPP. Those discussions focused on the following areas, which drove the community outreach
and budget analyses mentioned earlier:
 • The need to determine how the “warheads only” phase’s reduced risk affected the
 community’s preparedness tasks and the need for any further preparedness enhancements
 in the (2023) CSEPP footprint.
 • The need to determine how the overall size of the emergency planning zone was affected
 and whether it should be reduced. For example, since evacuation was not being used as a
 protective action and with the reduced risk/impact, was there still a need for host
 counties?
 • Acknowledgment that the jurisdictions would need to determine if/when/how they would
 implement a potential phased closeout.
 • Acknowledgement that the possibility of an earlier than planned start date for closeout or
 the start of a phased closeout may cause sooner than expected staff losses.
 • General concerns that many participating counties at the time of the briefing had not
 adequately planned for CSEPP closeout.

FEMA CSEPP acknowledged the benefit of closing out select communities before CSEPP ends
to ensure that the 180-day post-CSEPP closeout period restriction can be met.

Community outreach regarding CSEPP closeout. Community outreach focused on two major
areas. First was clarification of terms used by the various principal entities to describe the
process of completing weapons destruction, subsequent removal of the destruction facilities,
identifying the start of CSEPP’s transition period, and CSEPP closeout. The second focus was on
limiting the impact of rumors about destruction schedule and CSEPP end dates.

Clarification of terms was a particularly difficult issue. The two parallel and interlinked
programs (ACWA/BGCAPP destruction program and CSEPP) used similar but distinct terms to
describe elements of their respective programs. While the differences were generally recognized
by long-term program participants, high personnel turnover (particularly in state and local
government agencies) drove the need for a set of easily understood talking points. The language
used by the destruction and preparedness organizations, derived from various statutes,
regulations and memoranda of agreement, could be confusing.

The talking points first described the complex, intertwined processes, roles, and responsibilities
of the organizations involved in chemical weapon stockpile storage, destruction, and community
preparedness. Next, the talking points provided brief descriptions of key storage, destruction, and
community preparedness concepts (chemical stockpile storage, chemical stockpile destruction,
the Chemical Weapons Convention, chemical surety material, and CSEPP).

 204

Most critical in the talking points were the descriptions and definitions associated with meeting
treaty commitments and the end of surety, as presented below:
 • Treaty Commitment Date. The date the United States has pledged to meet CWC
 requirements to destroy the U.S. stockpile of chemical weapons to a point where the
 weapons no longer could be used for their intended purpose. At BGAD, that was when
 the stockpile of GB rockets was punched, drained and the agent neutralized. (That date
 was identified as 30 September 2023.)
 • Storage Mission Completion (SMC). When the last warhead and last leaking rocket/motor
 were destroyed at BGCA.
 • End of Surety. When the BGCAPP Site Project Manager certified that all recoverable
 agents were destroyed, approximately 60 days after SMC.
 • End-of-CSEPP Notification. When the BGCA Commander provided the Kentucky
 Division of Emergency Management (KYEM) a letter informing them of the end of
 storage operations and the elimination of all recoverable agents. The date on that letter
 would start the 180-day period of final CSEPP assistance, in accordance with 50 USC §
           1521(e)(2)(B).315
 • Surety Program Termination. Receipt of Department of the Army approval,
 approximately four months after SMC.
 • BGCAPP Closure. The final phase of the BGCAPP mission, in which the chemical
 weapon destruction facility would be closed in accordance with all applicable
 environmental and safety laws, regulations and requirements.

It is important to note here that BGCAPP closure was different from CSEPP closeout. The
process of BGCAPP closure would involve four major processes: decontamination,
decommissioning, property disposition, and administrative closeout. CSEPP closeout was purely
a financial and administrative process carried out in accordance with 2CFR200 and other guiding
Army and State regulations. Thus, the talking points depicted CSEPP closeout as a phase
comprised of the following elements:
 • Multi-level planning and decision-making. This included retention or transition of CSEPP
 staff, disposition of property, and financial reconciliation activities. The Army, FEMA,
 BGAD, BGCA, KYEM, and the 10 participating counties were asked to identify existing
 CSEPP-funded capabilities, to determine what capabilities must be funded post-CSEPP,
 and to prepare transition plans. Planning toward successful program closeout occurs
 simultaneously with maintaining and sustaining community preparedness. The focus was
 on a gradual progression to a post–CSEPP steady-state of capabilities that support all-
 hazards community preparedness.
 • Public safety continued to be paramount when making decisions on how to transition to
 the end of CSEPP and its associated funding.

315
 Notification to the KYEM was made via letter dated April 8, 2025 from Kelso C. Horne III, CMA Director. The
 letter indicated closeout would begin October 1, 2025.

 205

 • During the 180-Day final CSEPP closeout period, local and state CSEPP partners
 conducted activities to administratively close the program, to include final equipment
 transfers and disposition, funding reconciliation and liquidation, and human resource
 actions.
 • Legacy. The goal was for communities to retain a CSEPP legacy in the form of specific
 resources and capabilities, as well as heightened coordination and planning with
 neighboring jurisdictions.

The talking points and the associated concepts were discussed at each IPT meeting and in
leadership discussions between KYEM, CMA CSEPP, FEMA Region IV, and FEMA HQ.
Additionally, they were used by the Blue Grass Public Affairs Working Group and the various
public affairs/information staffs to keep the public informed of program status.

Final outreach efforts included providing a simplified depiction of the end-of-CSEPP notification
process (Figure 6.15) and coordinating end-of-program recognition activities.

 Figure 6.15 End of Surety / CSEPP Notification Process

 206

Budget analyses for three-to-six-month destruction delays. ACWA demilitarization schedule
variations drove the need for a series of CSEPP budget analyses that included a hard look at
programming requirements and identification of potential excess funding. The analyses were
done in sets of three, three-month increments. The original analysis considered an early
completion date (December 2024), the program executive’s planning schedule date (March
2025), and slippage date (May 2025). As destruction delays occurred and as the schedule
slipped, analyses moved accordingly, with a final analysis conducted for November 2025.

The analyses were conducted by a team consisting of Army and FEMA program managers,
CMA resource managers, and Argonne budget and finance analysts. The analyses considered
many factors:
 • The accuracy of FY 2023 and FY 2024 funding estimates,
 • What, if any, carry-over funding would be available to out-year off-post budgets,
 • The status of FY 2024 through FY 2026 appropriations,
 • FY 2024 and FY 2025 cooperative agreement funding requirements for Kentucky
 evaluated against available funding,
 • Identifying potentially excess FY 2024 funding and options for returning any excess
 funding, and
 • The impact on CSEPP closeout in Kentucky if there were still open cooperative
 agreements when CSEPP ended.

The analyses showed that the program had allocated sufficient funds to meet both on- and off-
post preparedness requirements if the March 2025 destruction completion date was met.
However, and as one might have expected, schedule delays of six or more months would cause a
need for additional on-post and program management funds; the final (FY 2024) cooperative
agreement was found to provide the KY community with sufficient funding to cover their
requirements until December 2025 (end of first quarter FY 2026).

The analyses also found funds excess in both FY 2023 and FY 2024 to meet operations and
maintenance operations and maintenance requirements. Thus, the program requested those
monies, if returned, be reallocated and used to fund any FY 2026 and FY 2027 FEMA program
management costs related to completing final CSEPP administrative and financial closeout for
the Kentucky cooperative agreements and FEMA contracts.

Technical, statutory, and regulatory analyses. As destruction schedule delays occurred, the CMA
CSEPP staff fielded questions from Congressional staff, DOD program managers, and State-
level political leaders regarding technical, statutory, and regulatory aspects of the program. The
personnel turnover that had occurred during CSEPP’s thirty-plus years meant there were few
individuals at all levels with the knowledge of how those three aspects evolved and informed the
program’s political and practical context. Thus, many of the analyses focused on explaining the
history of how the statutes and regulations were interpreted and the subsequent development of
CSEPP policies.

 207

One major effort was explaining CSEPP’s purpose – to assist jurisdictions to enhance their
capabilities to respond to “emergencies involving risks to the public health and safety” caused by
the risks from storage and handling of the chemical weapons. CSEPP staff explained that CSEPP
offered funding to allow participating jurisdictions to hire people, buy equipment, provide
training, provide for public education and outreach, and modernize facilities and systems.

One aspect of the analyses was explaining what the statutory mandate for maximum protection
meant – that it was not setting conditions for zero effects, but rather was achieved through on-
post safety practices and off-post protective action strategies. And the program’s goal: “[T]he
avoidance of fatalities to the maximum extent practicable, should an accidental release of
chemical agent occur,” (CSEPP Policy Paper 1).

As the final demilitarization schedule slipped, the CMA CSEPP office responded to an
increasing number of inquiries regarding the triggers for ending CSEPP and about the
notification process for ending CSEPP. As noted above, the U.S. had fulfilled its treaty
obligations because the remaining rockets were dismantled, their agent destroyed, and they were
no longer useful for military purposes. The remaining risk came from the trace amounts of agent
remaining in the drained but yet-to-be-destroyed rocket warheads. At the time, the Army’s stance
was that CSEPP would continue as long as the warheads were being processed. However,
anticipating a potential change in that position, the CSEPP Office examined the Army’s
responsibilities without a CSEPP program. The analysis led to a simple answer, that the
remaining agent would be treated like surety material at any other installation by following
existing Army regulations dealing with chemical surety, emergency management, and
environmental protection. The position was that Army installations were required to meet those
requirements before CSEPP and would continue to do so post-CSEPP. Blue Grass Army Depot
would still be required to:
 • Hold quarterly response exercises (two of which needed to include community
 participation) (AR 50-6),
 • Coordinate emergency protocols (including establishing mutual aid agreements) and
 conducting biennial emergency response exercises with appropriate Federal, State,
 Tribal, and local response organizations (AR 525-27),
 • Establish and maintain procedures for preventing, mitigating, and responding to
 hazardous substance releases and for coordinating with local emergency planning
 authorities (AR 200-1),
 • Establish mutual and reciprocal aid agreements with the surrounding communities
        (AR 420-5), and
 • Initiate written agreements with civilian medical treatment facilities that describe patient
 referral and transport protocols and mutual disaster support (AR 40-3).

Recognizing that ending CSEPP before warhead destruction was completed would be a political
decision, the CSEPP Office prepared an analysis of the political and practical environment.
CSEPP guidance placed responsibility with the site IPT for analyzing and discussing any
proposed risk-related program reductions, with a goal of joint Army/FEMA acceptance of any
reduction action. The analysis indicated that Kentucky was following CSEPP guidance (and

 208

precedent from previous location closeouts) by reducing the number of jurisdictions participating
based on identified risk. At the time of the analysis, Kentucky had already closed-out or was in
the process of closing-out four counties. Furthermore, the analysis also pointed out that short of a
political/policy decision regarding whether meeting treaty commitments started the 180-day
CSEPP closeout period, there was little program managers could do to affect the pace of the
Kentucky CSEPP community’s risk-based closeout processes. Staff also found that Kentucky
was demonstrating good stewardship of federal funding by conducting early closeouts.

Post-CSEPP Legacy Tasks. CMA CSEPP staff focused on four legacy-related tasks as the
program wound down. First was to add CSEPP’s hazard analysis application, WebPuff, to the
suite of models included in DTRA’s HPAC system. That effort was ongoing as of the time of
this writing, with oversight transferred to CMA’s Recovered Chemical Material Directorate.
There was a prototype available; however, technical and user tests were still required, and further
revisions might be required before final release.

The second task was coordinating the transfer of two CSEPP meteorological towers at BGAD to
the Kentucky Mesonet (KYMN). This was accomplished with an agreement between BGAD and
KYMN (see description in Section 6.4.8.1 above).

The third task was to produce a process chart showing how to establish a joint military/civilian,
multi-jurisdiction emergency preparedness program, built from the lessons learned during the
CSEP Program. That chart was completed in December 2024, was provided to FEMA and other
Army program partners, and was added to the CSEPP Archive in digital form.

The final task was to document the history of the CSEP Program. This involved two subtasks.
The first was to consolidate, catalog, and transfer the 50,000-plus-item CSEPP archive to the
University of Delaware, Disaster Research Center’s, E. L. Quarantelli Resource Collection. The
archive makes historical program materials available to emergency management practitioners
and researchers. The materials include, but are not limited to, interview transcripts, surveys,
photographs and audio-visual materials, policy documents, exercise reports, physical items (such
as shelter-in-place kits and escape masks), and other materials collected during CSEPP’s thirty-
seven years of existence. The second subtask was to complete this report.

The Final Days

The last Army/FEMA Strategic Planning Meeting was held at the Chemical Demilitarization
Training Facility at Aberdeen Proving Ground on January 28, 2025. At that meeting, OSD
TRAC announced that the CSEP Program would not be funded past 30 September 2025. The
DOD Office of General Counsel had determined that the last chemical weapon in the
BGAD/BGCA stockpile was destroyed on July 7, 2023 and that the 180-day CSEPP transition
period should have ended on January 3, 2024; therefore, no additional funding would be
provided for emergency preparedness operations beyond the periods covered by existing
cooperative agreements. The last cooperative agreement for KY provided funding through
FY 2025, meaning that CSEPP would end on 30 September 2025. The formal notification to the
Commonwealth of Kentucky was made by the CMA Director on April 8, 2025. That notification
indicated that the program closeout would begin October 1, 2025. The letter thanked the

 209

Commonwealth and the Counties for their leadership and support, and expressed an ongoing
commitment to safety and coordination with the community.

Closeout of the CMA CSEPP office moved quickly. Through reassignments and retirements, the
office staff was reduced to one individual, with additional required closeout support provided
through the CMA HQs’ resource management and public affairs staff. The CMA CSEPP office
was scheduled to close completely in early 2026, with any remaining closeout tasks being
addressed by CMA HQ staff.

6.4.9.2 FEMA

Like the Army CSEPP Office in CMA, the FEMA HQ CSEPP Office drew down staffing over
several years as the program wound down. In May 2009, the FEMA HQ CSEPP office featured a
branch chief and eight more personnel covering program integration, training, exercises, site
representative duties, PPE and decontamination, closeout, public affairs, and budgets & grants.
By late 2025 the office was down to two personnel covering all remaining tasks.

FEMA’s closeout process involved a complex set of interlocking requirements as the agency
oversaw the completion of numerous cooperative agreements with state and local agencies. Even
after all of the other program activities such as exercises, committee meetings and training were
finished, the administrative, budgetary, and recordkeeping requirements to close out the
cooperative agreements with Kentucky and the Kentucky community counties had to be
completed. That in turn required a certain amount of FEMA staff time.

The closeout process was managed using three main tools: WebCA (grant management system
developed for CSEPP and used for many years; see Sections 4.2.3 and 5.12); Smartsheet (a
cloud-based project management and collaborative work software product); and a closeout plan.

Use of WebCA During Closeout

WebCA was used for tracking closeout of grants to State and local governments and for
archiving the associated documents. As described in FEMA’s FY 2024 Report to Congress,

 As part of the closeout process, grant managers used the Chemical Stockpile
 Emergency Preparedness Program WebCA electronic filing cabinet to archive
 grant-related documents. While each Federal Emergency Management Agency
 region’s office maintains an official grant file for each recipient, the electronic
 filing cabinet provides a quick and convenient means of accessing grant
 information. As the remaining Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness
 Program site goes through closeout, packages of files are exported from the
 system and provided to the states and regions.316

316
 FEMA, 2025, CSEPP FY 2024 Report to Congress, September, p. 27.

 210

Closeout Smartsheet

FEMA managed closeout of remaining agreements and contracts using Smartsheet, a cloud-
based management platform that had been used successfully during the Fiscal Year 2023 Blue
Grass Army Depot exercise. Smartsheet features included interactive workflows, detailed
reporting, and user-friendly dashboards to facilitate monitoring and control. A guide was created
and updated periodically to facilitate using Smartsheet for the closeout process. As described in
the FY 2024 Report to Congress,

 To support all stakeholders in navigating this new system, a comprehensive . . .
 Program Closeout Dashboard Guide was created. This guide serves as a crucial
 resource, clearly outlining the step-by-step closeout work process and detailing
 the pertinent cooperative agreement closeout regulatory requirements. It is
 regularly updated to incorporate enhancements and new features within
 Smartsheet, ensuring that all users have access to the latest information and tools.
 Updated versions of the guide were distributed in January, May, and August
 2024, reflecting the commitment to continual improvement and support for all
 entities involved.317

Closeout Plan

The FEMA closeout plan detailed the requirements to be met, approvals to be obtained, and
expected timelines along with required staffing. The plan outlined administrative and budgetary
closeout processes for the Kentucky CSEPP Counties, the State of Kentucky, FEMA Region IV
and the national FEMA CSEPP Office. Processes included disposition of CSEPP-funded assets
and final reports and accounting in accordance with federal regulations. The plan was updated
many times as events unfolded, work progressed (or was delayed) and schedules had to be
adjusted. As stated in the June 2025 version of the plan (Version 11):

 This plan is intended to:
 • Support Region 4 and the overall program with timely closeout program
 decision-making;
 • Support all Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program
 employees throughout the closeout of the program;
 • Support compliance with the Federal Emergency Management Agency
 administrative and recordkeeping requirements; and
 • Enable a more effective program closeout.318

      Ibid., p. 30.

(Uniform Administrative Requirements, Cost Principles, and Audit Requirements for Federal

program sunset in Region 4 and FEMA HQ in the third quarter of FY2027 (Figure 6.17).

Consistent with other FEMA CSEPP documentation, the closeout plan provided a breakdown of
closeout activities organized by CSEPP Benchmark. Many of the public protection-oriented
benchmarks were simply noted as completed, e.g., alert and notification, communication
systems, exercises, and protective action. Three of the benchmarks were noted as continuing
until the end of the program in FY2027:
 • Administrative Support – as described above, including closeout of cooperative
 agreements, disposition of property, records retention, and other administrative functions.
 • Automation – as described in the plan, “The goal is to have documents/records prepared
 to be transferred to other systems or repositories as soon as the current system is no
 longer needed. . . . Support the export of all permanent [CSEPP] Portal documents and
 ensure that the appropriate agencies/departments receive them for long-term storage.”
 The plan calls for authority to operate through September 2027 for the Portal, EOP
 template, and WebCA systems, to ensure availability of these systems throughout the
 closeout process, and to “Support exporting permanent documents from the web portals
 and ensure that they are sent to the appropriate state or federal records management
 departments for long-term storage.”322
 • Qualified Personnel – this includes ensuring that there are personnel available to perform
 the closeout tasks, and support for FEMA personnel who will be seeking other positions,
 through the FEMA Office of the Chief Human Capital Officer.

Region IV lost its last CSEPP-dedicated position at the end of May 2025 and thereafter relied on
part-time attention from a staff person detailed from another program (REPP) with contractor
support. FEMA Headquarters staffing shrank to two full-time equivalent staff as of June 11,
2025, plus one interagency reimbursable work agreement position.
The closeout plan projected, “. . . sunset of the entirety of the program April 30, 2027, to include
the closeout/completion of all systems, record management and disposition, support contracts,
financial transactions and human resource actions.”323

Benefits to Community Preparedness

FEMA’s FY 2024 Report to Congress noted the benefit to community preparedness that the
program provided. “The program benefits the whole community and builds capabilities to protect
the public from other hazards as well. Due in part to Congressional support of this program and
through the program’s holistic approach to emergency preparedness, these communities are
better prepared to respond in an integrated and coordinated manner. For example, several CSEPP
communities faced severe weather and other emergencies. During these events, the . . .
communities used interoperable mobile radio systems supported by CSEPP investments and
continued to use the Integrated Public Alert and Warning System to send emergency alerts to
ensure the public was notified of any necessary action. Facilities and equipment provided by the
program were used for recent human-caused hazards, including the Rockcastle County train
derailment and other disaster responses . . . .”324

    Ibid., p. 24.

 FEMA, 2025, CSEPP FY 2024 Report to Congress, September, p. iii.
