 7 Perspectives from Program Staff

Approximately 75 interviews of CSEPP staff were conducted in 2009 and 2010 to document the
impressions, recollections, and perspectives of the people who carried out the program.
Interviewees included U.S. Army and FEMA personnel, State and local personnel, and
contractors, including personnel associated with each of the stockpile sites. The 2009 interviews
were videotaped, and excerpts were shown as part of the 20-year retrospective presented at the
National Conference that year. The 2010 interviews were conducted specifically for the CSEPP
History project. Transcripts were prepared of all interviews, amounting to about 500 pages in all.

A few additional interviews were conducted near the program’s end, in 2024 and 2025, for final
perspectives from people with extensive program experience.

The interviewers asked broad questions about the interviewee’s role in the program, particular
achievements, or occasions that stood out in their memories, things they were proud of, and
things they might have done differently. The interviewees brought up a wide range of topics in
response. Reviewing the transcripts, a few themes emerged and are described below. Each theme
is illustrated with interview quotations.

7.1 Interagency and Inter-jurisdictional Relationships
For many program participants, CSEPP was their first and only program that involved reaching
across jurisdictional and agency boundaries. Initially, this element proved difficult because of
differing perspectives and expectations among the various organizations; there was “culture
clash.” As time went on, these difficulties were overcome. Many saw the use of IPTs as a key
contributor to this evolution. Eventually, the level of trust and cooperation became a source of
pride and a strength of the program.

Initially, it was difficult to get people from diverse organizations on the same page. At first,
it was hard for the Army and FEMA to work together because of their differing perspectives
regarding control, missions, goals, and which partners to include as participants.

 “Early on, the Chemical Stockpile Program was a very controversial program.
 There was some very, very difficult times in those early years. There was a lot of
 distrust among local, State, Federal, Army folks, and that created a lot of tension
 in the community. Just a tremendous challenge to finally get those folks to come
 together.” – Delois Champ, FEMA CSEPP Emergency Management Specialist,
 FEMA Center for Domestic Preparedness, Anniston, Alabama; formerly PIO at
 Calhoun County, Alabama

 “I was part of the United States Army as a commissioned officer in the Army. The
 Army is not good at communicating at the local level or at the State level. The
 Army can’t order local communities. It was great to have FEMA come on board
 and understanding the roles and responsibilities, which were another hard point,

 215

 who was responsible for accomplishing what.” – Carmen Spencer, DASA,
 Elimination of Chemical Weapons

 “[T]he infrastructure that it takes to have a program to provide maximum
 protection for the off-post communities had to be built almost from scratch. That
 was a rather difficult transition for the Army to make because we, the Army, was
 running 100 miles an hour to build the program and all of a sudden we found that
 the track we were on was not the track that we thought we were going to be on….
 [T]he organizations had very different cultures. What made it work ultimately
 were the people that were involved, who recognized there’s a need, there’s an
 opportunity, and they took personal initiatives to make it possible.” – George
 Yantosik, Argonne.

Over time, the organizations were able to bridge the gaps. Building trust and confidence,
bridging adversarial relationships, and creating a consensus among Army, FEMA, and off-post
community partners made the program successful.

 “When I first started in 1989 FEMA was just coming on board. We had no idea
 what they were capable of doing, what they were going to be capable of doing and
 I don’t think FEMA trusted the Army much either to be honest with you. . . I see a
 lot more cooperation and respect and trust now[.]” – Larry Orndorff, Army
 QASAS, later Argonne Associate.

 “And, over the years, that trust and confidence developed and really, that was
 what I think paid so many dividends for the CSEP Program. Because, once we got
 the trust and the confidence built up between various groups, we were able to
 move forward.” – David McMillion, Director, Maryland EMA

 “We have earned the trust, now we are working very hard to maintain the trust,
 that we don’t do something stupid that throws that wrench into our gears...
 working very closely together so that we achieve a common mission.”
 – Michael Abrams, PAO, Anniston Chemical Activity

 “I think being open with the public and being accessible to the public is an
 absolute, total, and complete necessity.” – David Holm, Senior Division Advisor,
 Colorado Division of Emergency Management

IPTs were a significant breakthrough in fostering interagency cooperation. Implementation
of the IPT concept, with local, site-specific teams and programmatic-level national teams,
allowed needed dialogue. The IPT originated as an Army concept and was introduced to the
CSEP Program as a process for consensus building. Several participants emphasized that the
IPTs contributed to the success of the program by facilitating interagency and interjurisdictional
cooperation. IPTs blunted the issue of whether the Army or FEMA was in charge, and they
allowed State and local participants to become equal partners with the Army and FEMA, giving
them a voice in affecting key programmatic decisions, allowing collaboration on functional
concerns, and determining benchmark fulfillment.

 216

 “Another success, I think, was the site IPTs... where you had Army and off-post
 representatives sitting together, and they could raise issues and try to reach
 consensus on how to approach solving them. That seemed to give the
 communities more of a voice...” – Keith Alleman, IEM

 “The best thing that happened to the CSEP Program was the creation of the IPT,
 the Integrated Project Team. Getting all the stakeholders together in one room at
 the local levels, at the State levels, at the national levels, and understanding what
 everybody’s role was and what everybody’s expectations are.” –Carmen Spencer,
 DASA, Elimination of Chemical Weapons.

 “As far as managing this program, I would compare the benefits of the IPTs to the
 incident management systems for managing emergencies. I think that has been
 something that really hasn’t been paid a lot of attention to but has been very, very
 successful in helping all of the other successes happen, and I think that’s been a
 wonderful thing...” – David Holm, Senior Division Advisor, Colorado Division of
 Emergency Management.

The program became a model of interagency cooperation. As the initial difficulties were
overcome, in part with the aid of the IPTs, interagency and inter-jurisdictional cooperation came
to be perceived as a program strength.

 “I think the CSEP Programs are basically a model for how interagency
 cooperation should be done...” – Rich Brletich, CMA CSEPP Director.

 “I think at the very beginning, the Army felt they were overall in control of the
 program and tried to direct FEMA, and it took a true partnership on down the line
 and a better definition of roles to get to where we are today, where we have a
 good smooth program with both agencies working together...” – Dan Civis,
 FEMA CSEPP Branch Chief.

 “I think it [CSEPP] really laid a good foundation and a good test-bed to develop a
 system of interagency cooperation both horizontally and vertically from State to
 Federal government. [Y]ou had 10 states, that’s one-fifth of the country, and
 many counties... trying to learn how to really work together...” – Leonard Motz,
 Argonne.

 “I think that the CSEP Program is probably the most comprehensive application
 of emergency management integration strategies amongst different organizations
 that need to each play a specific role in a coordinated manner in order to
 maximize planning and response. A survey was done that I want to say was
 written by the Congressional Research Service in which the authors created a list
 of objective benchmarks of preparedness at the state level. The ten states that had
 the highest level of capabilities were all part of the CSEP Program. That was a
 finding quoted for a number of years as one of the assets that the CSEP Program
 brought to national preparedness. In other words, the CSEP Program became the

 217

 example of what could be done in integrated emergency response to make the
 entire Nation more secure.” – Ed Tanzman, Argonne CSEPP principal
 investigator.

7.2 The Role of Funding
Many interviewees commented on funding: both the upside that program funding built many
capabilities, and the downside that communities faced as program funding was discontinued
once the stockpile was eliminated.

CSEPP funding enhanced preparedness in local communities. Early on, the CSEP Program
was constrained by not having what was considered adequate funding; there were lots of
meetings, but no money for equipment, staffing, writing plans, or facilities. As adequate funding
became available and funding streams stabilized, the program could begin to meet the mandate
for maximum protection by providing the best training, equipment, facilities, and systems.
Beyond the simple fact of establishing adequate funding, it was essential that all participants
become familiar with the budget process, including life-cycle cost estimates, funding
mechanisms, and grant guidelines.

 “First and foremost we had to have adequate funding to be able to accomplish
 results.” – Madhu Beriwal, President, IEM

 “FEMA and the Army were saying, we got this amount of money, we want to
 make sure that we’re using this money the way the States and local jurisdictions
 want to use it... we’ll work with you States and local jurisdictions to determine
 how best to utilize these funds that will do the most good and make sure that
 we’re providing the greatest protection for the people in the region around these
 various sites...” – Dave McMillion, Director, Maryland EMA

 “Probably the two biggest things are, we are dealing with a known focused-threat
 chemical agent and the available funding. There was a lot of money available, and
 it was required because of a mandate to meet maximum protection, and so the
 funding was sufficient for it. The program was successful, I think, because that
 funding could be applied and things could be done right; best equipment could be
 purchased, the best training, the best systems. I mean there’s not many programs
 where you’ve got a known threat and that level of funding available to, for
 example, develop a custom dispersion model...” – Dan Maloney, DETech, Inc.

CSEPP communities face difficult decisions on maintaining capabilities as CSEPP funding
ends. The CSEP Program provided funding for personnel, facilities, and equipment; as the
program ends, sustainability is a significant issue at the State and local levels, particularly the
local level. Some communities may be able to offset the loss of CSEPP funding with grant
funding from other FEMA and DHS programs because they host critical infrastructure, nuclear
power plants, chemical facilities, or other hazards of national-level concern. Others will have to
make tough decisions as they downsize in terms of personnel, facilities, and equipment and seek

 218

to sustain an all-hazard capability at a lower level, making maximum use of what the community
can afford.

 “Some things will go away, some positions will go away, but I think we’re going
 to maintain, for the most part, the capabilities that we’ve had....” – David Holm,
 Senior Division Advisor, Colorado Division of Emergency Management

 “The communities have real tough decisions to make, as to how many employees
 they can keep, what sort of facilities they’re going to have, what are they going to
 do with the sirens and over-pressurization systems that we’ve put in, how do you
 maintain radio systems, those sorts of things.” – Ronald Barker, FEMA CSEPP
 Exercise Manager

 “I’ve seen some communities saying, ‘[Y]ou know, once CSEPP goes away, we
 lose our funding, we are not going to be able to maintain what we have.’ Other
 places, and especially at Pueblo, because of the size of Pueblo and the
 infrastructure that they have, they will be able to continue some of the things that
 CSEPP has brought to the table. But a lot of the smaller States or counties like in
 Alabama and Pine Bluff, that’s going to be very, very difficult.” – Vince Blunn,
 CSEPP Coordinator, Pueblo Chemical Depot

 “[O]ur county EMA staff is dropping by about a dozen and one of the concerns in
 addition to the staff I think is maintenance of the warning system, which is now
 county-wide, again not just for chemicals but for everything. So the sustainment
 costs of the warning system are going to be a problem.” – Walt Studdard,
 Argonne, resident of Anniston, Alabama

 “Proactively a year or two before the program was near closing out, Lisa [Shorter]
 put together what she called her closeout roadshow briefing, which she presented
 to the community leaders, to remind them what CSEPP was funding. She posed
 questions like, “This is what we've been getting, do you like it? And then, do you
 want to keep it? And then if you want to keep it, where are we going to get the
 funding when we're done?” . . . As a result, they kept that critical county
 communication system well-maintained and funded. They are also cost and
 resource sharing with the fire department, the emergency operations center, the
 police force, and the county council to continue to fund [the] emergency
 preparedness director position, which is a key position to the county and the
 program.” – Carmen Kifer, CMA CSEPP Chief, speaking of Pueblo County.

7.3 Dedicated Personnel
The dedication of professionals committed to protecting the public reinforced the CSEPP
partnerships and formed the basis for friendships that extended beyond the CSEPP mission.
Participants in the program were mission oriented, maintained a strong focus, and were very
proud of what they were accomplishing. Executing the program often meant communicating and

 219

collaborating with people outside of one’s typical sphere of contacts. Many interviewees
commented favorably on the people with whom they shared the program.

 “Some of these people at the worker level were just so talented and so dedicated
 in regard to what they were doing. I don’t care if you wanted to get them together
 on weekends, holidays, nights, or what have you, they were there to make sure
 that we were doing everything that we could to advance the CSEP Program, to
 make sure that we were doing everything we could to protect the citizens around
 each one of these sites.” – David McMillion, Director, Maryland EMA

 “It’s not a question of the dollars, although the amount helps, there’s no question
 about that. But it’s been the will. It’s been the national will, and it’s been the will
 of those hardworking people at FEMA, the State, and the local government. Army
 commanders come and go at the site every 2 years. And sometimes less,
 sometimes a little bit more. The hardworking Army civilians that are at each of
 the sites have been working this program day by day… To me, it all goes back to
 people. It doesn’t matter who you work for, whether you’re in government or in
 industry or which company you worked for, it’s about the people. Lots of
 different people from different backgrounds and different motivations have come
 together in this program to make it happen.” – Carmen Spencer, DASA,
 Elimination of Chemical Weapons

 “The heart-and-soul of the program is the people who have made this program
 work.” – Michael Abrams, PAO, Anniston Chemical Activity, CMA

7.4 Ancillary Benefits
Facilities, equipment, and training acquired through the CSEP Program enhanced the
communities’ preparedness for all hazards.

CSEPP Communities enjoyed enhanced capabilities for response to all types of
emergencies. Although the Army was never in a position to specifically fund all-hazard
emergency planning and response preparedness, virtually all of the capabilities — including
equipment, training, exercises, telecommunications, medical services, public information, and
facilities delivered by the program — have benefited all-hazard emergency preparedness in the
participating communities.

 “[T]he sites that have been involved with CSEPP are the best-prepared sites in the
 country. They can handle any circumstances, HAZMAT, terrorism, because you
 respond to an emergency, you don’t really care where it came from or what
 caused it, if the Army caused it or a terrorist caused it, or if it was an industrial
 accident, you still respond the same way, a chemical is a chemical, a response is a
 response. The all-hazard approach that we’ve taken has paid tremendous
 dividends... ” – Rich Brletich, CMA.

 220

 “I believe that the training that emergency management officials, the first
 responders, EMS received, they are in much better shape today than they were
 when this program began. They have achieved a high level of professionalism and
 knowledge, and that’s directly attributable to the CSEPP training, and I think
 that’s one of the biggest things.” – Scott Hamilton, FEMA Region X.

Specific real-world responses benefitted from CSEPP-built capabilities. Several interviewees
pointed to specific examples of emergency responses that were enhanced as a result of CSEPP
training, facilities, relationships, or experience.

 “Several years ago there was a train accident, I believe, in Lincoln, Alabama, in
 which a cyanide product was leaked on the ground. The EMA director there in
 Talladega County thanked FEMA and DHS for the training he and his community
 received prior to that, which helped them to prepare for that accident. Nobody
 was hurt, it wasn’t CSEPP-related, but a lot of the principles that they learned
 were important in containing that accident.” – Ronald Barker, FEMA CSEPP
 Exercise Manager, FEMA Headquarters

 “[T]wo days after 9-11, we had a train collision in Tooele County, an Amtrak
 train that was carrying 263 passengers along with a freight train that they had
 collided with at 5:30 in the morning. We activated our Emergency Operations
 Center and within probably 20 minutes, we had a fully operational center working
 together and the school district was using school buses to transport the passengers
 on the train... [to] a reception center at the senior citizens center. We were
 working well with the law enforcement people there, our people from Tooele
 County were all working together, we had responders from Salt Lake County that
 came on-scene, but our Emergency Operations Center just ran so smoothly, that
 finally when the crisis part of the situation was under control and things were all
 getting taken care of, I just stood back and just looked at everybody... they all
 knew what exactly to do, and I just had one of those “ah hah” moments where I
 was just so proud and this is really what it’s supposed to look like and look where
 we’ve gotten, to be able to see a cohesive team effort, and we were saving lives,
 and it was so gratifying, and I just felt so proud of our community.” – Kari
 Sagers, EMA Director, Tooele County, Utah

 “[W]e had a train accident out at our installation, and because of our relationship
 with Pueblo County built up through CSEPP, and coordinated plans and MOUs,
 and all the stuff we had in place, I believe that’s what saved that woman’s life that
 wrecked into that train. [J]ust everything kind of fell into place because of all the
 coordination and planning that we had done related to CSEPP, and we keep
 seeing this in real-world situations that we’re involved in and worked with Pueblo
 County on. We have a tremendous relationship with them. It’s just phenomenal.”
 – Vince Blunn, CSEPP Coordinator, Pueblo Chemical Depot

 221

7.5 Sharing of Knowledge
A number of interviewees pointed out the benefits of sharing knowledge and best practices
through national workshops and sending evaluators to one anothers’ exercises.

 “Probably one of the biggest parts of the CSEPP program is the national
 workshops, the national conferences where people have a chance to interact with
 other people, find out lessons learned, the same thing happens with the CSEPP
 exercises.” – James Hackett, Public Affairs Specialist, UMCD

 “I think personally I’m most proud of the privilege that I’ve had to find speakers
 for medical education for the national workshop. And the quality of speakers that
 we’ve had sometimes totally amazes me. We’ve had responders from Oklahoma
 City that were intimately involved in those response come and teach us those
 lessons learned. We’ve had physicians from Canada come and educate us in their
 SARS experience, and to learn from that. We have had the director of
 St. Vincent’s Hospital on 9/11 come and tell us what went well and what didn’t
 go so well then. We’ve had the EMS people from 9/11 come and tell us their
 lessons. We’ve had the EMS director from the Pentagon on 9/11 come speak to
 us. We have had Dr. Auf der Heide, one of the nation’s leading disaster
 researchers, come and be part of our program to learn from him... So we have had
 the opportunity to have tremendous education from those who have actually
 responded to disaster situations.” – Sharon Wilcoxson, Preparedness Systems
 Analyst, Battelle

 “I have loved going to different sites... just going around to different places in the
 United States, seeing how they do things, learning from them and sharing boo-
 boo’s and best practices with them of things that we’ve done so that maybe they
 didn’t have to take the same paths.” – Kari Sagers, EMA Director, Tooele
 County, Utah

 “I'm excited that Argonne is helping the Army and FEMA to document the
 CSEPP history, the CSEP Program, and all the valuable informational products
 and ways of enhancing emergency preparedness capabilities. It’s my hope that
 when this archive goes to the University of Delaware’s Disaster Research Center
 that researchers, practitioners, and students have access to what's been
 accomplished here and that, maybe through the work of the Army, DHS, FEMA,
 and the University of Delaware, the current and future emergency preparedness
 community will continue to implement some of the partnerships and realize that
 we're not competitors, we're friends. We're here for a purpose. We're here for a
 common mission. And when we can all take the time to get to know each other a
 little bit better and agree on what our goals are, then great things can happen.” –
 Carmen Kifer, CMA CSEPP Chief.

 222

7.6 Specific Program Strengths
Certain strengths of the program drew frequent comments: exercises and training,
communications technology, public information/education, and service to those with access and
functional needs. The program advanced the art of emergency preparedness in each of these
areas.

Exercises and Training

Exercises and training, much of which was developed specifically by and for the CSEP Program,
enhanced capabilities at all levels. One of the big challenges in the CSEP Program was to
determine and develop an exercise and training program that would establish a standard set of
objectives and requirements derived from different programs, cultures, and positions. Many
interviewees cited skills gained through training and exercises as one of the key and lasting
benefits of the program.

 “The exercise program was, I think, a very well-thought-out process as far as how
 can we take what the Army does, what the off-post does, and how can we
 integrate it together and look at it as one big team because terminology was
 different, procedures were different, chain-of-command was entirely different.”
 – Leonard Motz, Argonne

 “From a training perspective, I think CSEPP is going to leave a wonderful legacy.
 We have developed a number of courses that have been extremely well received
 outside the CSEPP community. For example, the Olympics in Australia, the
 Australian Government contacted the CSEP Program and wanted to use the
 training prior to the Olympics for training their civilian responders because they
 felt the CSEPP training was better than anything the Australian Army had... I
 think so much of our training is useful outside the program. The CSEPP training
 website, for example, has had a couple of million downloads, and the majority of
 that is from outside the community. And it’s being used by governments across
 the U.S.” – Robert Norville, FEMA CSEPP Training Manager, FEMA HQ

 “We’ve used what CSEPP has been able to do with the Master Exercise
 Practitioner Program… We are going to make a Tooele County exercise planning
 team and use all the things we’ve learned through all of these years of exercising
 and designing exercises to get more people involved so that they can help us and
 we can keep this going with [our all-hazards mission].” – Marilyn Candelaria,
 CSEPP Program Manager, Tooele County, Utah

Communications Technology

Enhanced communications technology and automated hazard assessment tools were needed to
manage the chemical weapons hazard. The potential need for an immediate response to an
incident involving the release of chemical agent required that A&N systems, interoperable
communication systems, and automated hazard assessment tools be in place. For many

 223

communities, this reality meant developing a level of capability that would not otherwise have
been attainable.

 “[CSEPP] allowed not just Calhoun County, but surrounding communities and
 agencies within Calhoun County, [to install] an Alert Notification System that
 they had never had before and would not be able to afford had it not been for the
 CSEP Program.” – Delois Champ, FEMA CSEPP Emergency Management
 Specialist, FEMA Center for Domestic Preparedness, Anniston, Alabama

 “CSEPP helped significantly for us to automate and we were pioneers...
 [including] recently with the change-over to digital trunked radios...” – David
 Holm, Senior Division Advisor, Colorado Division of Emergency Management

 “One of the big legacies, I believe, is an interoperable communication system that
 allows us to communicate with each other better...” – Kari Sagers, EMA Director,
 Tooele County, Utah

Public Information/Education

Emergency preparedness was elevated within the CSEPP communities through public
awareness, risk communication, and public education. CSEPP initiated several innovative public
awareness, risk communication, and public education programs. This program element involved
measuring what people knew about protecting themselves and developing targeted media
campaigns and public outreach initiatives.

 “A couple of areas I think CSEPP has really raised the bar in emergency
 preparedness is public awareness, risk communication, public education. The
 whole idea of going out and measuring what people actually know about how to
 protect themselves and then developing a targeted media campaign to address the
 gaps instead of just putting stuff out there and hoping some of it sticks, I think,
 has been great.” – Jack Long, IEM

 “Public outreach and education, some of the things we’ve done there were to say
 the least on the cutting edge... [E]ven though REP had used JIC and JIS before we
 brought it to the forefront, now it’s more common to do that type of thing.”
 – Leonard Motz, Argonne

 “[T]he Umatilla media and outreach campaign has been great. In almost 10 years
 of regular TV, print, and radio advertising in conjunction with outreach activities,
 we have seen the public’s awareness of and ability to make decisions that will
 protect themselves in an emergency increase. In some cases, depending on the
 survey question we ask, 20–30 points of improvement.” – Mark Clemens, PIO,
 Washington State EMA

 “I remember when I first started that you wouldn't see much news coverage on the
 local stations, for example, in the Tri-Cities areas of Washington, which is the

 224

 closest one to Umatilla. You wouldn't see many stories about CSEPP and the
 exercises initially. But over time I remember then, all the three local stations were
 covering CSEPP. They were on site covering the actual exercises. So that to me is
 a manifestation of the great work that happened regarding the outreach about the
 program.” – Adolfo Capestany, PIO, State of Washington

Service to Those with Access and Functional Needs

Serving the AFN communities was identified as a goal in the first guidance documents, and was
carried on throughout the program, including some innovative initiatives and collaboration with
state and local agencies along with disability groups.

 “[T]he work we did developing methods and tools for emergency planning and
 response for people with disabilities, access, and functional needs. . . There had
 always been a heavy emphasis in the state of Alabama on helping people with
 disabilities[.] The work that we undertook in this domain began at the request of a
 retired Army colonel named Jim Bennett, who became Director of the Calhoun
 County Emergency Management Agency. We developed the Special Population
 Planner, which was a software application that was used to support a
 comprehensive method of reaching out to people with disabilities and special
 needs who lived in the emergency response zone surrounding Anniston Army
 Depot, and providing them with customized mechanisms to take protective
 actions. The application was also accessible to emergency responders at a higher
 level so that they could provide a consistent level of “maximum protection” to
 these residents as required by a federal statute. The process of developing the
 software and then administering its use, along with other emergency planning
 tools that we developed, was really a high point of the implementation of the
 “maximum protection” requirement required by federal statute.” – Ed Tanzman,
 Argonne CSEPP principal investigator.

 This page intentionally left blank.

 226

 8 Conclusions

The CSEP Program played a significant role in the evolution of emergency management in the
United States. Before it started, the chemical weapons storage installations followed safety and
security practices for managing the weapons on-post, but because the chemical inventory was
classified, it was difficult for off-post authorities to assess the hazard and prepare for it. In
authorizing the chemical demilitarization program, Congress provided policy direction and
funding to change this situation and develop a coordinated preparedness program involving the
Army, FEMA, and at-risk State and local jurisdictions.

Initially, the program drew on antecedents including the Army’s chemical surety program;
hazardous materials and health and safety expertise from FEMA, EPA, and CDC; and especially
the FEMA/NRC REP Program, which pioneered Federal/State/local cooperative preparedness
for large, fixed-facility technical hazards.

The program established a technical basis from scientific hazard analyses and a policy risk
standard to guide development of preparedness measures. Starting from a relatively low baseline,
the program added staff, analyses, technical capabilities, equipment, and facilities; completed
planning; and developed expertise through training and exercises. Through cooperative effort,
along with steady funding, the program became the standard-bearer for hazardous material
preparedness, and in some ways, for emergency preparedness generally. The program adopted
and integrated proven concepts and practices from both military and civilian emergency-
response services. In other areas, the program built tools and doctrine to better serve public
safety. CSEPP became the model on which national policies and standards for emergency
preparedness were built.

Apart from advancing the art of emergency preparedness, as a practical matter, CSEPP provided
a context of preparedness during destruction of millions of chemical weapons. Through the
operation of CSEPP, the chemical installations and surrounding communities were brought to a
level of capability that allowed the demilitarization process to proceed safely. The fact that the
chemical weapons were destroyed is perhaps the program’s greatest achievement. Because of
CSEPP, along with much other effort, the hazard of stored chemical weapons has been
eliminated.

Seven broad conclusions from CSEPP are:
 1. Congress mandated the destruction of the U.S. stockpile of lethal chemical weapons in
 1985, providing that the destruction should be carried out with “maximum protection
 for the environment, the general public, and the personnel who participate in the
 destruction . . .” The maximum protection mandate was implemented primarily through a
 combination of engineering, technology, and safe practices in the destruction process. It
 is a tribute to the organizational effectiveness of the U.S. Army and its chemical demil
 contractors that the millions of munitions and containers comprising the stockpile were
 safely destroyed. As the chemical agents and munitions were removed from their storage
 bunkers and containers; transported to the destruction facilities; punched, drained,
 disassembled, and incinerated or chemically neutralized, the CSEP Program helped

 227

 provide the “last line of defense.” If all other safety precautions were to fail, the
 community capabilities built through the CSEP Program would be there to provide an
 effective capability to respond to protect the public.
2. The program built on and then evolved away from another fixed-point technological
 hazard program, Radiological Emergency Preparedness. The REP Program enhances
 preparedness in communities near nuclear power plants. Many REP concepts and
 practices (as well as personnel) were initially carried over to CSEPP. Over time, CSEPP
 found its own path, more attuned to the specific hazards of a release of chemical warfare
 agent and better fitted to the methods and preferences of the organizations involved.
3. The program overcame early struggles that stemmed from the need to coordinate many
 levels of government. In the first few years, there were conflicts between organizational
 cultures, schedules, and points of view, leading to slow progress and steady criticism
 from the GAO and Congress. Over time, the program found a way to solve problems and
 foster innovation through the use of Integrated Process Teams. The IPTs were widely
 credited with putting the program on a sound footing.
4. The capabilities built through the CSEP Program were never tested, in the sense that
 there were no accidents or incidents requiring protective action at any of the chemical
 agent stockpile sites. However, the program created tremendous benefits for the local and
 State jurisdictions involved, providing a conduit for funding that allowed local
 emergency agencies to add personnel and upgrade facilities and equipment. It created a
 core of trained staff and advanced many careers. It also provided a way for multiple
 jurisdictions to compare notes and practices. The CSEPP communities became leaders
 and centers of excellence in emergency preparedness, with benefits carrying over to their
 readiness for other hazards.
5. CSEPP advanced the art of emergency preparedness in many ways, sponsoring technical
 and operational research on hazardous materials preparedness, response, recovery, and
 mitigation. It developed techniques for estimating hazards and determining the
 effectiveness of protective action measures. It served as a test bed for many tools that led
 to better planning, crisis decision-making, public warning, and crisis communications. It
 was an early proponent for the Incident Command System for response and for Integrated
 Performance Evaluation of exercises — practices that were later incorporated into
 emergency management programs nationwide. It was an early adopter and testbed for the
 development of IPAWS. CSEPP was also an early adopter and innovator in the
 development of services for those with access and functional needs. Last but not least, the
 CSEP Program sponsored development of numerous training programs that reached a
 worldwide audience through the Internet.
6. CSEPP existed in the political world. Some involved in the program felt that it was an
 over-allocation of resources to address a remote threat, and some believed that funding
 priorities were distorted by political considerations.
7. CSEPP left a legacy of innovation and lessons learned in many fields. The legacy will
 benefit the field of emergency management but also holds lessons with broader
 applications, such as interagency cooperation. Some of the most salient lessons learned
 are compiled in Volume 2 of this document.

 236

 Annex B Glossary

Accident Assessment — The evaluation of the nature, severity, and impact of an accident. In
CSEPP, the Army would be primarily responsible for accident assessment.

Acute Exposure Guideline Levels (AEGLs) — Developed by the National Research Council’s
Committee on Toxicology, the criteria account for sensitive individuals and are meant to protect
nearly all people. The guidelines define the following three-tiered levels:
 • AEGL 1: The airborne concentration of a substance above which it is predicted that the
 general population, including susceptible individuals, could experience notable
 discomfort, irritation, or certain asymptomatic non-sensory effects. However, the effects
 are not disabling and are transient and reversible upon cessation of exposure.
 • AEGL 2: The airborne concentration of a substance above which it is predicted that the
 general population, including susceptible individuals, could experience irreversible or
 other serious, long-lasting adverse health effects or an impaired ability to escape.
 • AEGL 3: The airborne concentration of a substance above which it is predicted that the
 general population, including susceptible individuals, could experience life-threatening
 health effects or death.

Each of the three AEGLs was developed for each of five exposure periods: 10 minutes,
30 minutes, 1 hour, 4 hours, and 8 hours. Areal Locations of Hazardous Atmospheres (ALOHA),
however, will only use an AEGL exposure period of 10 minutes.

Alert and Notification System — A combination of sirens and tone alert radios to be used in
the IRZ and selected portions of the PAZ to provide alert and emergency instructions to the
public.

Blister agent — See Vesicant Agent.

Chemical Accident/Incident (CAI) — An intentional or unintentional event in which chemical
agent is released into the ambient atmosphere and either threatens unprotected personnel or has
the potential to threaten unprotected personnel (AR 50-6, 28 July 2008). (In Army terminology,
an incident refers to an intentional act where security is of concern.) (See related term, Chemical
Event.) Note, this term was later replaced, per the 2022 update to AR 50-6, with the term
chemical incident or mishap (CIM).

Chemical Accident and Incident Response and Assistance (CAIRA) Plan — An
installation’s plan for responding to a CAI. Note, the 2022 update to AR 50-6 replaced this term
with chemical incident or mishap response and assistance (CIMRA).

Chemical Agent — A chemical substance listed in Chapter 6 of AR 50-6 that is intended for use
in military operations to kill, seriously injure, or incapacitate a person through its physiological
properties. Excluded from consideration are industrial chemicals, riot control agents, chemical
herbicides, smoke, and flame (AR 50-6).

 237

Chemical Demilitarization Program (CDP) — The overall DOD program that is responsible
for eliminating all stockpile and non-stockpile chemical agents, munitions, and related materials
in U.S. States and territories. This program includes the Chemical Stockpile Disposal Program,
the Alternative Technologies and Approaches Project, the Non-Stockpile Chemical Materiel
Product, the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, and the Assembled
Chemical Weapons Alternatives Program.

Chemical Event — A term used by the military that means chemical accidents or incidents that
involve chemical surety materiel. Chemical events include (1) chemical accidents resulting from
non-deliberate events where safety is of primary concern and (2) chemical incidents resulting
from deliberate acts or criminal acts where security is a concern.

Chemical Event Emergency Notification System — A tiered system whereby the Army
classifies chemical surety emergencies according to expected downwind hazard distance and
provides appropriate notification to off-post public officials. The system consists of a minimum
of three surety emergency levels (based on the predicted downwind distance of the no-effects
dosage) and one non-surety event level.

Chemical Limited Area (CLA) — The area within a Depot where chemical weapons are stored.
The CLA would have additional security and entrance requirements.

Chemical Stockpile Disposal Program (CSDP) — The congressionally mandated program that
requires the Army to dispose of all its unitary chemical agents. The preferred mode of disposition
is on-post incineration.

Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) — A joint Army and
DHS/FEMA program designed to enhance existing local, installation, Tribal, State, and Federal
capabilities to protect the health and safety of the public, work force, and environment from the
effects of a chemical accident or incident involving the U.S. Army chemical stockpile.

Chemical Surety — Controls, procedures, and actions that contribute to the safety, security, and
reliability of chemical agents and their associated weapon systems throughout their life cycle
without degrading operational performance.

Complete Personal Decontamination — Washing the entire body with soap and water and
completely changing clothing.

Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA) —
A Federal law (42 USC Sec. 9601 et seq.) that deals with hazardous substance releases into the
environment and the cleanup of hazardous spill sites. This act was amended by the Superfund
Amendments and Reauthorization Act (SARA) in 1986.

Concentration — The prevalence of chemical agent in the air, typically measured in milligrams
per cubic meter.

 238

Contamination — occurs when a substance gets on one’s clothing, shoes, hair, exposed skin, or
eyes. Contamination may lead to continued chemical exposure and may expose others to the
same hazard.

CSEPP Community — The combined area of one military installation, its surrounding local
jurisdictions, and the State agencies involved in executing CSEPP for that area.

CSEPP Hospital — A hospital in the vicinity of a chemical installation that has been designated
for participation in CSEPP by the CSEPP Section of the DHS/FEMA Regional office or through
an MOU with an Army installation.

CSEPP Jurisdiction — A State, County, City, or other local unit of general jurisdiction
participating in the CSEP Program.

D2-Puff –– An air dispersion model developed by the Army to estimate downwind hazard
distances from atmospheric releases of chemical agents.

Decibel (dB) –– A measure of sound pressure, which determines loudness. The abbreviation,
“dB,” is often followed by an “A,” “B,” or “C” to indicate the method used to weight sound
frequencies when measuring sound pressure levels.

Decontamination — The process of decreasing the amount of chemical agent on any person,
object, or area by absorbing, neutralizing, destroying, ventilating, or removing chemical agents.

Direction and Control Exercise — An activity in which emergency preparedness officials
respond to a simulated incident by mobilizing emergency management and communications
organizations and officials. Some field response organizations may be involved.

Demilitarization — The mutilation, destruction, or neutralization of chemical surety materiel,
rendering it harmless and ineffectual for military purposes.

Dermal Exposure — Contact with, or absorption through, the skin.

Disability, Access and Functional Needs — This includes people with disabilities, along with
older adults, children, people with limited or no English proficiency, individuals with health
vulnerabilities, and others who may need assistance or support during an emergency.

Disaster — See Major Disaster.

Dosage —Dosage is the exposure to chemical agent accumulated over time (often called the Ct
product) and represents the exposure of an individual to the chemical.

Dose — The quantity of agent absorbed by the body. Often expressed in mass units of agent per
body weight or surface area exposed (e.g., mg or mg/kg). (Compare to Exposure.)

 239

Emergency — Any incident, whether natural or human-caused, that requires responsive action
to protect life or property. Under the Robert T. Stafford Disaster Relief and Emergency
Assistance Act, an emergency is any occasion or instance for which, in the determination of the
President, Federal assistance is needed to supplement State and local efforts and capabilities to
save lives and to protect property and public health and safety, or to lessen or avert the threat of a
catastrophe in any part of the United States.

Emergency Alert System (EAS) — A system created by the FCC as a means of using
communications facilities to alert the public of emergencies. The EAS requires participation by
cable TV systems in addition to broadcast stations and encourages the voluntary participation of
satellite carriers, direct broadcast satellite vendors, and public service providers. It also
establishes new technical standards and operational procedures.

Emergency Operations Center (EOC) — The physical location where information and
resources are coordinated to support incident management (on-scene operations) activities. An
EOC may be a temporary facility or may be located in a more central or permanently established
facility, perhaps at a higher level of organization within a jurisdiction. EOCs may be organized
by major functional disciplines (e.g., fire, law enforcement, and medical services), by jurisdiction
(e.g., Federal, State, regional, Tribal, city, county), or by some combination of these.

Emergency Plan — A plan maintained by a jurisdiction or other organization for responding to
an emergency, typically addressing a wide variety of potential hazards.

Emergency Planning Zone — A geographical area delineated around a potential hazard that
defines the potential area of impact. Such zones facilitate planning to protect local residents
during an emergency.

Enhanced Shelter-in-Place (SIP) — A protective action that is similar to normal SIP except
that it involves taking shelter in a structure to which weatherization techniques have been applied
before the emergency to permanently reduce the rate at which air or chemical agent seeps into
the structure. Effectiveness is improved by going into an interior room. The shelter should be
opened up or abandoned after the toxic plume has passed.

Evacuation — Organized, phased, and supervised withdrawal, dispersal, or removal of civilians
from dangerous or potentially dangerous areas, and their reception and care in safe areas (see the
National Response Framework).

Exclusion Area — The area immediately surrounding one or more receptacles in which
chemical agents are contained. Normally, the boundaries of an exclusion area are the walls, floor,
and ceiling of a storage structure, secure container, or a barrier that establishes the boundary
(e.g., an igloo or fence).

Expedient Decontamination — Removing the outer layer of clothing, washing exposed skin
and hair with soap and water, and providing a suitable replacement for outer clothing

 240

Expedient SIP — A protective action that is similar to normal SIP except that, after going into
the room selected as a shelter during an emergency, the inhabitants take measures to reduce the
rate at which air or chemical agent enters the room. Such measures would include taping around
doors and windows and covering vents and electrical outlets with plastic. Effectiveness is
improved if the room selected as a shelter is an interior room. The shelter should be opened up or
abandoned after the plume has passed.

Exposure — Contact by a person or animal with chemical agent in either liquid or vapor form
through inhalation, contact with eyes or skin, or ingestion of contaminated food or water.
Exposure to agent in liquid form (including droplet and/or aerosol form) can result in
contamination. (Compare to Contamination.)

Federal On-Scene Coordinator (FOSC or OSC) — See “On-Scene Coordinator” below.

First Receiver — Hospital employees, who may be termed first receivers, who work at a site
remote from the location where the hazardous substance release occurred. The location and
limited source of the contaminant distinguishes the first receivers from other first responders
(e.g., firefighters, law enforcement, and ambulance service personnel), who typically respond to
the incident site (i.e., the Release Zone). OSHA recognizes that first receivers have somewhat
different training and PPE needs than do workers in the hazardous substance Release Zone, a
point clarified through letters of interpretation.325

First Responder — Local police, fire, and emergency personnel who, in the early stages of an
incident, are responsible for the protection and preservation of life, property, evidence, and the
environment, including emergency response providers, as defined in Section 2 of the Homeland
Security Act of 2002 (6 USC 101), as well as emergency management, public health, clinical
care, public works, and other skilled support personnel (e.g., equipment operators) who provide
immediate support services during prevention, response, and recovery operations. First
responders may include personnel from Federal, State, local, Tribal, or nongovernmental
organizations.

Full-scale Exercise — An activity in which emergency preparedness officials respond to a
simulated incident by mobilizing the entire emergency organization or its major parts.

GA — See Nerve Agent.

GB — See Nerve Agent.

H — See Mustard Agent.

HD — See Mustard Agent.

325
 OSHA, 2002, Letter of Interpretation Addressed to Mr. Francis J. Roth, Supervisor, Loss Protection, Princeton
 Insurance, Re: Level of respiratory protection required for hospital staff members, Sept. 5, available at
 http://www.osha.gov/pls/oshaweb/owadisp.show_document?p_table=INTERPRETATIONS&p_id=24516,
 accessed September 16, 2011.

 241

Hospital Environments — In medical preparedness guidelines, refers to the hospital emergency
department plus any outdoor areas on the hospital grounds that might be used for triage and
decontamination during a chemical agent emergency, as well as other hospital departments that
might support the hospital’s response.

HT –– See Mustard Agent (HT is mustard agent mixed with a thickener).

Immediate Response Zone (IRZ) –– The emergency planning zone immediately surrounding
each Army installation. Generally, it extends to about 6 miles from the installation’s chemical
storage area.

Incident Command System (ICS) — A management system designed to enable effective
incident management by integrating a combination of facilities, equipment, personnel,
procedures, and communications operating within a common organizational structure, designed
to aid in the management of resources during incidents. The ICS is used for all kinds of
emergencies and is applicable to small,, as well as large and complex incidents. ICS is used by
various jurisdictions and functional agencies, both public and private, to organize field-level
incident management operations.326

Indian Tribes — The United States recognizes Indian tribes as domestic dependent nations
under its protection and recognizes the right of Indian tribes to self-government. As such, tribes
are responsible for coordinating Tribal resources to address actual or potential incidents. When
their resources are exhausted, Tribal leaders seek assistance from States or even the Federal
government.

Ingestion Exposure — Contact with the mouth, throat, and gastrointestinal tract by means of
food or water consumption, or use of tobacco products (e.g., cigarettes or chewing tobacco).

Initial Response Force (IRF) — An emergency action organization tasked to provide first
response to a chemical event at an installation that has a chemical surety mission. Under
command of the Installation Commander, the IRF is composed of command-and-control
elements and emergency teams capable of providing emergency medical services and initiating
those actions necessary to prevent, minimize, or mitigate hazards to public health and safety or to
the environment.

Institutional Populations — People in schools, hospitals, nursing homes, prisons, or other
facilities that require special care or consideration by virtue of their dependency on others for
appropriate protection.

The Joint Commission — An independent organization that provides accreditation to healthcare
organizations, such as hospitals and clinics.

Joint Information Center (JIC) — A facility established to coordinate incident-related public
information activities and serve as a central point of contact for news media.

326
 FEMA, “ICS Resources,” at www.fema.gov/incident-command-system-resources, accessed July 2025.

 242

Joint Information System (JIS) — A system to integrate emergency information and public
affairs into a cohesive organization designed to provide consistent, coordinated, accurate,
accessible, timely, and complete information during accident or incident response.

Lewisite — An organic arsenical blister agent; a brown or colorless liquid that is part of the
unitary chemical stockpile of vesicants.

Liquid Agent — Any chemical agent in undiluted form; includes droplets and aerosols. Only
VX or the vesicant agents (e.g., H, HD, and HT) are likely to be encountered in liquid form.

Local Emergency Planning Committee (LEPC) — The planning body designated by SARA
Title III legislation as the planning body for preparing local hazardous materials plans.

Local Government — A county, municipality, city, town, township, local public authority,
school district, special district, intrastate district, council of governments (regardless of whether
the council of governments is incorporated as a not-for-profit corporation under State law),
regional or interstate government entity, or agency or instrumentality of a local government; an
Indian tribe or authorized Tribal entity; or, in Alaska, a Native Village or Alaska Regional
Native Corporation; a rural community, unincorporated town or village, or other public entity.
See Section 2 (10), Homeland Security Act of 2002, PL 107−296, 116 Statute 2135 (2002).

Major Disaster — Under the Robert T. Stafford Disaster Relief and Emergency Assistance Act,
any natural catastrophe (including hurricane, tornado, storm, high water, wind-driven water, tidal
wave, tsunami, earthquake, volcanic eruption, landslide, mudslide, snowstorm, or drought) or,
regardless of cause, any fire, flood, or explosion in any part of the United States that, in the
determination of the President, causes damage of sufficient severity and magnitude to warrant
major disaster assistance under the Stafford Act to supplement the efforts and available resources
of States, local governments, and disaster relief organizations in alleviating the damage, loss,
hardship, or suffering the disaster caused.

Mass Care Center — A facility for providing emergency lodging and care for people made
temporarily homeless by an emergency. Essential basic services (e.g., feeding and family
reunification) are provided.

Mass Casualty Incident — An incident that generates more patients than available resources
can manage using routine procedures and that will require assistance from outside agencies.

Maximum Credible Event (MCE) — The worst single event that is likely to occur from a
given quantity and disposition of ammunition or explosives, accounting for potential accidental
explosion, fire, or release of a toxic chemical agent. The event must be realistic with a reasonable
probability of occurrence considering the propagation of the explosion, burning rate
characteristics, and physical protection given to the items involved. The MCE evaluated on this
basis may then be used as a basis to calculate effects and predict casualties. (AD 2013-3)

 243

Memorandum of Agreement (MOA) — Interagency agreement that defines general areas of
conditional agreement between two or more parties; what one party does depend on what the
other party does (e.g., one party agrees to provide support if the other party provides the
materials).

Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) — Interagency agreement that defines general areas
of understanding between two or more parties; while they explain what each party plans to do,
the actions of one party do not depend on the actions of the other party.

Mitigation — Activities designed to reduce or eliminate risks to persons or property or to lessen
the actual or potential effects or consequences of an incident. Mitigation measures may be
implemented prior to, during, or after an incident.

Monitoring Teams — Groups of trained individuals in appropriate protective clothing who
could travel through agent-contaminated areas to measure agent concentrations in or on various
environmental media and collect samples for later analysis.

Multi-Agency Coordination (MAC) System — The combination of personnel, facilities,
equipment, procedures, and communications integrated into a common system. When activated,
the MAC system has the responsibility for coordination of assisting agency resources and
support in a multi-agency or multi-jurisdictional environment. (See also Unified Command.)

Mustard Agent — The vesicant agents (H, HD, and HT) that cause blistering. In sufficient
amounts, they can be fatal if inhaled or if not quickly removed from exposed skin.

Mutual Aid and Assistance Agreement — Written or oral agreement between and among
agencies/organizations and/or jurisdictions that provides a mechanism to quickly obtain
emergency assistance in the form of personnel, equipment, materials, and other associated
services. The primary objective is to facilitate rapid, short-term deployment of emergency
support prior to, during, and/or after an incident.

National Contingency Plan (NCP) — Called the “The National Oil and Hazardous Substances
Pollution Contingency Plan” (40 CFR Part 300), the NCP is the Federal government’s blueprint
for responding to both oil spills and hazardous substance releases. Activities under the NCP are
overseen by an On-Scene Coordinator.

National Response Center (NRC) — A communications center for activities related to
hazardous materials response actions, located at Coast Guard headquarters in Washington, D.C.
The NRC receives and relays notices of discharges or releases to the appropriate OSC;
disseminates OSC and Regional Response Team reports to the National Response Team when
appropriate; and provides facilities for the National Response Team to use in coordinating a
national response action when required.

 244

National Response Framework (NRF) — Guides how the nation conducts all-hazards
response. The NRF documents the key response principles, roles, and structures that organize
national response. It describes how communities, States, the Federal government, and private-
sector and nongovernmental partners apply these principles for a coordinated, effective national
response. And it describes special circumstances where the Federal government exercises a
larger role, including incidents where Federal interests are involved and catastrophic incidents
where a State would require significant support. It allows first responders, decisionmakers, and
supporting entities to provide a unified national response (see the NRF).

National Response Team — The group, consisting of representatives of 14 government
agencies (DOD, U.S. Department of Interior, U.S. Department of Transportation/Research and
Special Programs Administration, U.S. Department of Transportation/U.S. Coast Guard, EPA,
U.S. Department of Commerce, FEMA, U.S. Department of State, U.S. Department of
Agriculture, U.S. Department of Justice, U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, U.S.
Department of Labor, NRC, and DOE), that implements the NCP. The NRT coordinates actions
and arranges for resources at the national level; the Regional Response Team coordinates
response in the field.

Nerve Agent –– Lethal, colorless, odorless, and tasteless agents (GA, GB, and VX) that can be
fatal upon skin contact or when inhaled. These agents attack the central nervous system by
inhibiting the production of acetyl cholinesterase, which is essential for proper operation of the
nervous system.

Normal SIP — A protective action that involves taking cover in a building, closing all doors and
windows, and turning off ventilation systems. Effectiveness is improved by going into an interior
room. The shelter should be opened up or abandoned after the toxic plume has passed.

Off-post — The area surrounding a military installation or facility.

On-post — The area within a military installation or facility.

On-scene Coordinator (OSC) — The Federal official pre-designated by EPA or the U.S. Coast
Guard to coordinate and direct Federal responses under subpart D of the NCP, or the official
designated by the lead agency to coordinate and direct removal actions under subpart E of the
NCP. DOD and DOE provide the OSC for response to releases on or from facilities they own.

Pre-hospital Environments –– Term used in medical preparedness guidelines to indicate all
emergency response areas that are outside both the Army installation boundaries and the hospital
grounds.

Preparedness — Actions that involve a combination of planning, resources, training, exercising,
and organizing to build, sustain, and improve operational capabilities. Preparedness is the
process of identifying the personnel, training, and equipment needed for a wide range of
potential incidents and developing jurisdiction-specific plans for delivering capabilities when
needed for an incident.

 245

Prevention — Actions to avoid an incident or to intervene to stop an incident from occurring.
Prevention involves completing actions to protect lives and property — applying intelligence and
other information to a range of activities that may include such countermeasures as deterrence
operations; heightened inspections; improved surveillance and security operations; investigations
to determine the full nature and source of the threat; public health and agricultural surveillance
and testing processes; immunizations, isolation, or quarantine; and, as appropriate, specific law
enforcement operations aimed at deterring, preempting, interdicting, or disrupting illegal activity
and apprehending potential perpetrators and bringing them to justice.

Pressurized SIP –– A protective action that is similar to enhanced SIP except that the
infiltration of contaminated air is effectively prevented by drawing outside air into the shelter
through a filter that removes chemical agent. This filtered air creates a positive pressure in the
shelter so that clean air is leaking out instead of contaminated air leaking in.

Primary Receiving Hospitals –– Hospital that is designated by State or local disaster plans to
provide initial medical care to the civilian population in the event of a chemical warfare release.

Protective Action — An action or measure taken to avoid or reduce exposure to a hazard.

Protective Action Decision (PAD) — Decisions by State and local officials on what protective
action instructions to recommend to the public in the event of a release of chemical agent, based
upon hazard information, specific emergency planning zones, recommendations from the
Installation Commander, and other information.

Protective Action Recommendation (PAR) — Recommendation by the Installation
Commander to off-post community officials in response to a chemical accident/incident. PARs
may include evacuation, SIP, and exit SIP (ending SIP at an appropriate time is often key to its
effectiveness as a protective measure).

Protective Action Zone (PAZ) — The second planning zone beyond the immediate response
zone. Generally, it extends to about 18 to 35 miles from the installation’s chemical storage area
(further at some installations).

Public Alert and Notification System — The system for obtaining the attention of the public
and providing appropriate emergency information. Sirens are the most commonly used public
alert devices but frequently are supplemented by tone alert radios, visual warning devices for the
hearing impaired, and telephone-based A&N systems.

Public Affairs Officer (PAO) — The Army installation person responsible for public affairs.
The PAO is the installation counterpart to the off-post Public Information Officer.

Public Information Officer (PIO) — The person on the emergency management team who is in
charge of public information affairs. The PIO is the counterpart to the on-post Public Affairs
Officer.

 246

Quarantine — A state of enforced isolation or restraint designed to prevent the spread of
contamination, disease, or pests. Activities of persons, transport of goods or animals, and access
to affected or suspect properties may all be restricted.

Reception — A process in which evacuees receive a very quick evaluation for medical needs,
are referred for further medical care if needed, and are offered further information and assistance
such as registration, decontamination, and mass care.

Reception Center — A location where reception functions are performed.

Recovery — The development, coordination, and execution of service and site restoration plans;
the reconstitution of government operations and services; individual, private-sector,
nongovernmental, and public-assistance programs to provide housing and to promote restoration;
long-term care and treatment of affected persons; additional measures for social, political,
environmental, and economic restoration; evaluation of the incident to identify lessons learned;
post-incident reporting; and development of initiatives to mitigate the effects of future incidents.

Re-entry — The entry of persons into an affected area following a hazardous materials incident.
Re-entry can be restricted (entry of monitoring crews) or unrestricted (unlimited public access).

Regional Response Team (RRT) — The representatives of Federal agencies and a
representative from each State in the Federal region. During a response to a major hazardous
materials incident involving transportation or a fixed facility, the OSC may request that the RRT
be convened to provide advice or recommendations on specific issues requiring resolution.

Release — Any spilling, leaking, pumping, pouring, emitting, emptying, discharging, injecting,
escaping, leaching, dumping, or disposing into the environment (including the abandonment or
discarding of barrels, containers, and other closed receptacles containing any hazardous
substance or pollutant or contaminant). For purposes of the NCP, release also means the threat of
release. There are exclusions to this definition (see NCP).

Relocation Points — Areas to which a population or community can be temporarily or
permanently removed in response to an emergency or disaster. Relocation is distinguished from
evacuation in that during an emergency, the potential for a release exists; in contrast, during the
relocation phase, there is no passing plume.

Response — Immediate actions to save lives, protect property and the environment, and meet
basic human needs. Response also includes the execution of emergency plans and actions to
support short-term recovery.

Restoration — Encompasses the efforts and resources needed to return the agent-affected area
to a condition safe for public access and use.

Secondary Contamination — Chemical agent contamination that occurs due to contact with a
contaminated person or object rather than direct contact with liquid agent in the atmosphere;
cross contamination.

 247

Service Response Force (SRF) (military term) — A DA-level emergency response
organization, commanded by a general officer, capable of performing and sustaining the
chemical accident/incident response and assistance mission. The SRF is composed of the Initial
Response Force and follow-on forces, including a staff and specialized teams from various
agencies and organizations involved in the response to and recovery from a chemical event.

Shelter-in-place (SIP) — A protective action that involves taking cover in a building and taking
steps to limit natural ventilation to reduce exposure to a hazard. SIP categories include normal,
expedient, enhanced, or pressurized shelters.

Signs — Objective, physical evidence of a medical condition or disease (e.g., drooling) that is
readily measured or observed.

Site-Specific Emergency Response Concept Plan — A concept plan developed for a specific
chemical agent stockpile location by applying the concepts and methodologies of the Emergency
Response Concept Plan. Each site-specific concept plan categorizes the chemical events that
could occur at that location and examines the topographic, meteorological, and population
characteristics of the area to develop proposed emergency planning zone boundaries and identify
appropriate protective actions.

Special Facilities — Locations with concentrations of special-needs individuals that have
responsibility for assisting those individuals. Examples include schools, daycare centers (for
children or adults), nursing homes, and hospitals.

Special-needs Individuals — Persons who require assistance or special equipment in order to
receive notification of an emergency and/or to take action to protect themselves. Examples
include persons with perceptual, physical, or mental disabilities.

Special Needs Populations — Populations whose members may have additional needs before,
during, and after an incident in functional areas, including, but not limited to, maintaining
independence, communication, transportation, supervision, and medical care. Individuals in need
of additional response assistance may include those who have disabilities; live in institutional
settings; are elderly, children, or from diverse cultures; have limited English proficiency or are
non-English speaking; or are transportation disadvantaged.

Stafford Act — Federal statute establishing and governing the Federal disaster relief program,
including procedures for presidential declaration of an emergency or major disaster, and
available benefits and services. Codified at 42 USC §5121 et seq.

State Emergency Response Commission (SERC) –– The State planning group designated by
SARA Title III legislation as the State coordinating body for hazardous materials activities.

Superfund Amendments and Reauthorization Act of 1986 (SARA) — Public law that
amended CERCLA. Title III of SARA includes detailed provisions for community emergency
planning for fixed chemical facilities.

 248

Surety — See chemical surety.

Symptoms — Subjective evidence of a medical condition, physical disturbance, or disease
(e.g., headache); usually need to be communicated by patient and are not readily measured or
observed.

Title III — The “Emergency Planning and Community Right-to-Know Act of 1986.” A law that
requires the establishment of State and local planning structures (SERCs and Local Emergency
Planning Committee [LEPCs]) for emergency planning to address hazardous materials incidents.
Title III requires (1) location site-specific planning around extremely hazardous substances,
(2) participation in the planning process by facilities storing or using hazardous substances, and
(3) notifications to SERCs and LEPCs of releases of certain hazardous substances. It also
establishes mechanisms to provide information on hazardous chemicals to the public.

Traffic Control Point — A location that is staffed to ensure the continued movement of traffic
inside or outside an area of risk. Traffic control is a temporary function to be implemented at
points where normal traffic controls are inadequate or where redirection of traffic becomes
necessary due to emergency conditions.

Tribal — Any Native American tribe, band, nation, or other organized group or community,
including any Alaskan Native Village, as defined in or established pursuant to the Alaskan
Native Claims Settlement Act (85 Statute 688) [43 USCA and 1601 et seq.], that is recognized as
eligible for the special programs and services provided by the United States to Native Americans
because of their status as Native Americans (see the NRF).

Unified Command (UC) — A structure that brings together the Incident Commanders of all
major organizations involved in the incident to coordinate an effective response while fulfilling
their own jurisdictional responsibilities. The UC links the organizations responding to the
incident and provides a forum for these entities to make consensus decisions. Under the UC, the
various jurisdictions and/or agencies and nongovernment responders may work together
throughout the operation to create an integrated response team. (See also Multi-Agency
Coordination System.)

Unitary Chemical Munitions — Munitions designed to contain a single-component chemical
agent for release on a target.

Vesicant Agent — A chemical agent such as sulfur mustard (HD) or lewisite (L) that induces
blistering and tissue damage.

Virtual Joint Information Center — The off-site public affairs resources that support a
standing JIC operation. Virtual JIC staff work from remote locations and use technological
means to provide a range of assistance that helps the primary JIC meet its information gathering,
production, and dissemination functional responsibilities.

 249

Volunteer — Any individual accepted to perform services by the lead agency (which has
authority to accept volunteer services) when the individual performs services without promise,
expectation, or receipt of compensation for services performed. See 16 USC 742f(c) and 29 CFR
553.101 (see the NRF).

VX — See Nerve Agent.

Weathering — Degradation through the combined actions of sunlight, temperature, moisture,
aeration, and microbial activity. As a decontamination procedure for chemical warfare agents,
NATO recommends weathering for lawns, gardens, pastures, woods, and other similar areas for
contaminated areas not in immediate proximity to occupied buildings. It is simple and requires
few personnel and no special equipment to operate; it is neither precise nor fast and is largely
temperature dependent.

WebPuff –– An automation system that comprises the D2-Puff dispersion model, as well as a
number of other response and recovery tools developed to support CSEPP.

 250
